### [1] [MapTCL: Temporal Consistency Learning via Bidirectional Alignment for Vectorized HD Map Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05209)
*Hyeonseo Kim,Juyeb Shin,Hyeonjun Jeong,Hiwon Shin,Dongsuk Kum*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MapTCL introduces temporal consistency losses (BVCL and RCL) to improve stability of online HD maps with no extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address geometric noise and temporal jitter in per-frame supervised online HD map construction; existing methods rely on frame-wise supervision and lack explicit objectives for temporal consistency.

Method: Auxiliary training strategy with Bidirectional Vector Consistency Learning (BVCL) modeling geometric and semantic discrepancies between past and current vector instances; Raster map Consistency Learning (RCL) stabilizes dense BEV features; jointly trained as a plug-and-play module to improve temporal stability without increasing inference cost.

Result: Experiments on nuScenes and Argoverse 2 show notable gains: +3.7 mAP and +2.8 C-mAP on nuScenes; +3.1 mAP and +2.5 C-mAP on Argoverse 2, with no extra inference overhead and compatibility as a plug-in to baseline models.

Conclusion: MapTCL effectively enforces temporal consistency in online HD map construction by introducing BVCL and RCL as auxiliary losses, yielding improved temporal stability across benchmarks without impacting inference latency.

Abstract: Constructing reliable online HD maps remains challenging in dynamic urban environments due to moving objects and occlusions. While recent works employ feature-level temporal fusion to address this, they rely solely on per-frame ground truth supervision. Consequently, they lack an explicit objective to directly penalize the geometric noise and temporal jitter between consecutive online HD maps. To address this, we propose MapTCL, an auxiliary training strategy that formulates temporal consistency loss between current and past frames via bidirectional alignment. Specifically, Bidirectional Vector Consistency Learning (BVCL) models the geometric and semantic discrepancies between associated past and current vector instances as an auxiliary loss. We also employ Raster map Consistency Learning (RCL) as an additional loss to stabilize dense BEV features. By jointly training with these dual losses, MapTCL improves the temporal stability of generated HD maps. Extensive experiments on two standard benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. As a versatile plug-and-play module, MapTCL consistently enhances existing baseline models, achieving gains of +3.7 mAP & +2.8 C-mAP on nuScenes and +3.1 mAP & +2.5 C-mAP on Argoverse 2 without additional inference overhead.

</details>


### [2] [Innocent Panels, Hateful Stories: Evaluating and Detecting Hateful Intent in Multi-Turn Visual Story Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05210)
*Ye Leng,Junjie Chu,Yiting Qu,Mingjie Li,Yun Shen,Yang Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces HatefulStoryPrompts: a dataset of 330 multi-turn prompts across 55 hateful stories in two languages and three visual styles, evaluating five frontier text-to-image models over 4,950 attempts; finds rapid generation of group-level hate and limited moderation effectiveness, and proposes interaction-aware and post-generation defenses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap in understanding and mitigating group-level hateful meaning in coherent visual narratives produced by modern T2I systems, beyond per-image moderation.

Method: Assembles HatefulStoryPrompts with 330 configurations spanning 55 stories, two languages, and three visual styles; evaluates five frontier models across 4,950 prompts; assesses HatefulVisualStory (969 hateful image sets, 990 benign controls) with existing moderation systems; develops interaction-aware monitoring and post-generation defenses.

Result: Most models complete over 80% of prompts (up to 99.0%); moderation recall is limited (worst 34.9%, best 67.5%); interaction-aware monitor achieves 97.3% recall for prompt-only and 92.6% when first image provided; post-generation analysis reaches 80.2% recall; shows need for stateful safety over image sequences.

Conclusion: Safety for image-generation evolves from per-image moderation to reasoning over interactions and image relationships in visual narratives, with proactive and post-generation defenses to curb group-level hate.

Abstract: Picture books and comics have long been used to disseminate hateful narratives because they are easily understood even by children, as exemplified by the notorious Nazi propaganda picture book \emph{Der Giftpilz}. Recently, frontier text-to-image (T2I) systems such as Gemini and GPT-Image have enabled conversational generation with consistent characters and scenes across turns, making hateful visual stories, namely ordered image groups that collectively convey hateful narratives, cheap and scalable to produce. Although prior work has studied hateful content generation by T2I systems, it focuses on individual images, leaving group-level hateful meaning largely unexplored. We aim to address the gap. Concretely, we introduce \texttt{HatefulStoryPrompts}, comprising 330 multi-turn configurations from 55 hateful stories across two languages and three visual styles, and evaluate five frontier models over 4,950 attempts. Every model completes over 80\% of the stories, with the strongest reaching 99.0\%. We further evaluate existing moderation systems on \texttt{HatefulVisualStory}, a human-labeled dataset of 969 hateful image sets and 990 benign controls, and find that they frequently miss group-level hateful meaning: dedicated safety models achieve at most 34.9\% recall, while a strong vision-language model reaches 67.5\%. Finally, we propose complementary proactive and post-generation defenses. An interaction-aware monitor achieves 97.3\% recall for prompt-only sessions and 92.6\% when the user supplies the first image, while post-generation methods jointly analyzing completed image groups reach 80.2\%. Our work shows that, as image generation evolves from isolated outputs to coherent visual narratives, safety must evolve accordingly, from per-image moderation to stateful reasoning over interactions and image relationships.

</details>


### [3] [StyleComposer: Training-Free Multi-Reference Style Composition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05213)
*Sanghyeok Lee,Jihye Kang,Namhyuk Ahn*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: StyleComposer disentangles color, texture, and structure in diffusion models by routing each attribute through the representation where it separates best, enabling per-attribute strength control without training or inversion and achieving closer alignment to references and prompts than prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reference-guided stylization often treats style as a single signal, which fails to give users control over the separate origins and strengths of color, texture, and structure. No single diffusion-model representation isolates all three attributes, creating a need for a modular, interpretable control mechanism.

Method: Identify attribute-specific representations within the diffusion model that best separate color, texture, and structure. Route each attribute through its best-suited representation and coordinate the routes across the denoising process. Operate zero-shot without training or inversion, yielding one strength slider per attribute. The method targets simultaneous alignment to three references and the prompt.

Result: Zero-shot control yields joint satisfaction of three references and the prompt that exceeds prior methods, while exposing one per-attribute strength slider, enabling independent tweaking of color, texture, and structure.

Conclusion: Proposes a practical, interpretable modularization for multi-attribute style control in diffusion models. StyleComposer delivers improved reference and prompt alignment without training, and introduces per-attribute control sliders, suggesting broad applicability for controllable style editing.

Abstract: The style of a painting is not monolithic: color, texture, and structure may come from different sources. Existing reference-guided methods transfer them as one style signal, leaving each attribute's source and strength outside the user's control. We ask where in a diffusion model one attribute can change while the others hold, and find that no single representation isolates all three. The proposed StyleComposer therefore routes each style attribute through the representation where it separates best and coordinates the routes over denoising time. Without training or inversion, it satisfies three references and the prompt jointly more closely than prior methods, and exposes one strength slider per attribute. Project page: https://lexxsh.github.io/StyleComposer

</details>


### [4] [NeuroAdaptTrainer: A Fiji/ImageJ Plugin for YOLO-Based Neuron Segmentation, InteractiveCorrection and Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05226)
*Daniela Eraso-Casas,Gerard Villarroya-Pique,Esther Serrano-Pertierra,M. Teresa Fernández-Sánchez,Antonello Novellie,Angel Rio-Alvarez,Víctor M. González*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Open-source Fiji/ImageJ plugin NeuroAdaptTrainer integrates a YOLO instance-segmentation model for neuron detection, enabling automatic detection, manual correction in Fiji, transfer-learning adaptation, and external validation within the workflow.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lower barrier to applying deep-learning-based neuron segmentation in microscopy by enabling non-experts to adapt models to new imaging conditions with corrections and to compare base vs adapted models on held-out data.

Method: Integrates YOLO instance segmentation into Fiji/ImageJ as a plugin; supports single/batch image processing; allows manual correction of detections in Fiji; facilitates transfer learning to adapt the model to new imaging conditions; includes an external validation module for quantitative comparison on a held-out annotated set.

Result: End-to-end workflow enabling automatic detection with manual correction and model adaptation, reducing manual effort while preserving expert oversight.

Conclusion: NeuroAdaptTrainer democratizes deep-learning-based neuron segmentation in microscopy by combining automatic detection, user-guided adaptation, and objective external validation within a familiar image-analysis ecosystem.

Abstract: Neuron counting and segmentation in microscopy images of neuronal cultures is a routine and time-consuming task in neuroscience research, traditionally performed through manual inspection or semi-automatic tools. We present NeuroAdaptTrainer, an open-source Fiji/ImageJ plugin that integrates a YOLO instance-segmentation model directly into the microscopist's workflow. The plugin allows a user to run automatic neuron detection on a single image or a batch of images, manually correct the resulting detections from within Fiji, and use those corrections to adapt the model to new imaging conditions via transfer learning. A built-in external validation module allows the base and adapted models to be compared quantitatively on a held-out annotated set. NeuroAdaptTrainer lowers the barrier for non-specialist users to benefit from deep-learning-based segmentation while keeping expert supervision at the center of the workflow.

</details>


### [5] [In-Context Forcing: Uncovering Context Effects in Autoregressive Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05237)
*Lingxiao Yang,Liu Liu,Moran Li,Han Feng,Wenjian Cao,Jiangning Zhang,Ye Shi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A progressive autoregressive video diffusion method called In-Context Forcing uses context frames with decreasing noise to guide denoising, enabling cross-frame parallelism, improving temporal consistency and speed over state-of-the-art on VBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current few-step autoregressive video diffusion models rely on fully denoised frames as context, which leak detailed information and cause shortcuts that harm temporal dynamics. Noisy contexts and masking-based guidance are explored to preserve dynamics and consistency.

Method: Propose In-Context Forcing, a progressive autoregressive paradigm that provides contexts with decreasing noise levels. It applies less masking to distant frames and more masking to adjacent ones, offering adaptive guidance. Decouples dependence on previous clean frames to enable cross-frame parallel denoising, accelerating inference.

Result: Extensive experiments on VBench show significant improvements over state-of-the-art in both visual fidelity and inference speed.

Conclusion: The approach delivers robust temporal consistency and strong inter-frame dynamics while enabling faster inference by relaxing strict dependence on previous clean frames; aligns with diffusion-as-masking perspective.

Abstract: Current few-step autoregressive video diffusion models depend on previous fully denoised clean frames as context for all denoising steps of the current frame. However, these clean frames leak excessive local details, which causes the model to take shortcuts, resulting in compromised temporal semantics and dynamics. Inspired by the perspective of diffusion as masking, we explore the impact of noisy contexts on few-step autoregressive generation. Yet, simply applying contexts with the same noise levels provides insufficient guidance, leading to poor temporal consistency. To resolve this dilemma, we introduce In-Context Forcing, a progressive autoregressive paradigm that utilizes contexts with decreasing noise levels. By applying less masking to distant frames and more masking to adjacent ones, this approach provides adaptive guidance, effectively ensuring both robust temporal consistency and high inter-frame dynamics. Furthermore, by decoupling the strict dependence on previous clean frames, our paradigm enables cross-frame parallel denoising, achieving substantial inference acceleration without sacrificing performance. Extensive experiments on VBench demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms state-of-the-art approaches in both visual fidelity and inference speed.

</details>


### [6] [Grad-CAM for Vision Transformers: A Systematic Taxonomy and Audit of Methodological Ambiguity in Explainable AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05258)
*Casey Wall,Longwei Wang,Rodrigue Rizk,KC Santosh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Synthesizes the state of Grad-CAM adaptations to Vision Transformers, diagnosing a lack of explicit, reproducible methodology and proposing a taxonomy to map the range of adaptation choices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grad-CAM's success in CNNs does not transfer trivially to ViTs due to tokens, attention, residuals, and multimodal dynamics; a systematic audit is needed to improve rigor and interpretability.

Method: Literature audit of 550+ papers; identify 175 applying Grad-CAM to ViTs; develop a descriptive taxonomy detailing feature locations, gradient targets, spatial reconstruction, aggregation; analyze reporting quality.

Result: Many works omit mathematical/implementation details; taxonomy clarifies the diverse, nontrivial choices; reveals Grad-CAM on ViTs is not a trivial extension and affects interpretation and reproducibility.

Conclusion: Calls for standardized reporting and rigorous adaptation schemes; taxonomy provides a framework to compare methods and improve reproducibility in ViT Grad-CAM studies.

Abstract: Gradient-weighted Class Activation Mapping (Grad-CAM) is widely used to visualize model decisions, but it was originally formulated for convolutional neural networks, where spatial feature maps and channel dimensions have clear architectural meanings. Vision Transformers (ViTs) do not provide the same structure, instead representing images through tokens, attention, residual streams, and multimodal interactions. This paper presents a systematic taxonomy and literature audit of how Grad-CAM and related methods are adapted, justified, and reported for ViT-based architectures. From an initial search of more than 550 papers, we identify 175 papers that apply Grad-CAM or Grad-CAM-adjacent methods to ViTs. We find that most papers do not provide a full mathematical or implementation-level account of how Grad-CAM is adapted to transformer representations. To characterize this gap, we introduce a descriptive taxonomy of ViT Grad-CAM adaptations that makes explicit the feature locations, gradient targets, spatial reconstruction steps, and aggregation choices that are often left implicit. This taxonomy is not intended to prescribe a single correct adaptation, but to clarify the range of methodological choices being made. The study shows that Grad-CAM on ViTs is often treated as a trivial extension of CNN-based Grad-CAM, despite requiring nontrivial choices that affect rigor, reproducibility, and interpretation.

</details>


### [7] [A Paragraph is Worth a Thousand Captions: Rethinking Text Supervision for Vision-Language Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05260)
*Mahyar Ghazanfari,Amin Tabrizian,Arsyi Aziz,Binshuai Wang,Peng Wei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Paragraph-level supervision for a frozen-vision CLIP/BLIP-like model yields strong long-text retrieval, matching or surpassing state-of-the-art methods without changing architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether text granularity alone drives long-text image–text retrieval, isolating text-level supervision from model architecture using synthetic data and a frozen vision encoder.

Method: Fine-tune only the BLIP text encoder across 10 configurations while keeping the vision encoder frozen. Generate diverse captions, hard negatives, and quality-scored paragraphs for 500k CC3M images using a synthetic pipeline based on Qwen2-VL and Llama 3.2 Vision. Evaluate on Flickr30k, COCO, ShareGPT4V, and DOCCI; compare against Long-CLIP, focusing on image-to-text retrieval and long descriptions.

Result: Paragraph-supervised models match Long-CLIP-L on ShareGPT4V and outperform by more than 14 points on DOCCI for image-to-text retrieval. Paragraph supervision enables effective use of long token sequences; caption-only training degrades beyond 60 tokens. Increasing caption diversity improves short-caption retrieval with diminishing returns. Paragraph supervision consistently benefits long-description benchmarks; hard negatives are detrimental in text-only fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Text granularity via paragraph-level supervision significantly benefits long-text image–text retrieval even with a frozen vision encoder, with favorable trade-offs across granularity, direction, and description length. Paragraph-level strategies are robust against long-token challenges and outperform caption-only setups in long-text benchmarks.

Abstract: Contrastive vision-language models such as CLIP and BLIP are typically trained on short image captions, limiting their ability to retrieve images from detailed textual descriptions. While methods such as Long-CLIP extend the token limit through positional embedding interpolation, we ask a simpler question: does training text granularity alone determine long-text retrieval performance? We present a systematic study of supervision ranging from single captions to multi-sentence paragraphs for contrastive image-text retrieval. Using a synthetic pipeline based on Qwen2-VL and Llama 3.2 Vision, we generate diverse captions, hard negatives, and quality-scored paragraphs for 500K CC3M images. To isolate the effect of text granularity, we fine-tune only the BLIP text encoder while keeping the vision encoder frozen across 10 training configurations. Our paragraph-supervised models match Long-CLIP-L on ShareGPT4V and outperform it by more than 14 points on DOCCI for image-to-text retrieval, without architectural changes. We further show that paragraph supervision enables effective use of long token sequences, whereas caption-only training degrades beyond 60 tokens. Increasing caption diversity improves short-caption retrieval with diminishing returns, while paragraph supervision consistently benefits long-description benchmarks and hard negatives prove detrimental in text-only fine-tuning. Evaluations on Flickr30k, COCO, ShareGPT4V, and DOCCI provide a comprehensive analysis of the trade-offs between text granularity, retrieval direction, and description length.

</details>


### [8] [Context Matters: Support Set Selection and Failure Detection for In-Context Medical Image Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05333)
*Youssef Gehad,Emmanuel Zerefa,Krish Kabra,Guha Balakrishnan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Similarity-based support exemplar selection improves in-context segmentation performance and reliability over random sampling; a transformer-based predictor can flag likely failures, enabling safer clinical deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how the composition of the ICL support set governs segmentation reliability and to develop mechanisms for selecting exemplars and anticipating failures in medical image segmentation.

Method: 1) Compare random sampling vs similarity-based retrieval of task-specific support exemplars; 2) Train a transformer-based classifier to predict, from the query and support images, whether the segmentation IoU will fall below a chosen threshold; 3) Evaluate on MultiverSeg using DINOv3 embeddings across four benchmarks and three imaging modalities.

Result: Similarity-based exemplar retrieval matches or outperforms random sampling, with the largest gains at small support set sizes; the failure-predictor classifier succeeds above chance across all four benchmarks.

Conclusion: Reliability of in-context segmentation can be enhanced via informed support selection, and potential failures can be anticipated before use, enabling safer clinical deployment.

Abstract: In-context learning (ICL) adapts medical image segmentation models to unseen structures and modalities without retraining by conditioning on a task-specific support set of image-mask exemplars. Because this support set is the model's only task-specific signal, its composition directly influences segmentation performance. In this work, we investigate the support set as a controllable determinant of ICL reliability. First, we compare random sampling against similarity-based selection, where exemplars are retrieved based on their visual similarity to the query image. Second, we train a transformer-based classifier to predict, from the query and support images alone, whether a segmentation will fall below a specified Intersection-over-Union (IoU) threshold. Using MultiverSeg with DINOv3 embeddings across four benchmarks and three imaging modalities, we show that similarity-based selection consistently matches or outperforms random sampling, with the largest gains at the smallest support set sizes. Furthermore, our classifier predicts segmentation failure above chance on all four benchmarks. Ultimately, these results demonstrate that the reliability of in-context segmentation can be both improved via informed support selection and anticipated before use, providing practical mechanisms for safer clinical deployment.

</details>


### [9] [Positive-Unlabeled Preference Optimization For Chest X-ray Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05341)
*Yuta Kobayashi,Pradyun Ramesh,Muhammad Ahmed Chaudhry,Vincent Jeanselme,Judy Wawira Gichoya,Sanmi Koyejo,Kathleen Capaccione,Shalmali Joshi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PU-DPO reframes radiology report generation with positive-unlabeled learning to counter omission noise, using contrastive preference pairs; improves detection of hidden positives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Omission noise in retrospective radiology reports causes clinically present findings to be under-reported. Models trained on such data inherit omissions; need to treat absent mentions as unlabeled rather than true negatives to avoid bias.

Method: Reformulate the learning objective under a positive-unlabeled (PU) framework; create contrastive preference pairs by editing model responses to produce variants that explicitly mention or omit a finding; supervise preferences with generated variants where mentions align with available visual evidence.

Result: PU-DPO yields consistent gains in detection rates and recovery of hidden positives across multiple pathologies in semi-synthetic and real chest radiograph benchmarks, and shows greater robustness to omission noise than prior methods.

Conclusion: Positive-unlabeled preference optimization can mitigate omission bias in vision-language radiology report generation, improving factual completeness without requiring fully labeled negative data; demonstrated robustness across pathologies.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) for radiology report generation are typically trained on retrospective clinical reports, which suffer from omission noise: clinically present findings are left unreported due to the omission of subtle findings. For example, prior studies show that cardiomegaly may be omitted from ICU chest X-ray reports when the imaging request is focused on monitoring support device placement. As a result, models trained with standard approaches inherit these omissions, learning to under-report findings themselves. We propose PU-DPO, a preference optimization framework to prevent omission noise from corrupting the preference signal. We reformulate the objective under a positive-unlabeled (PU) learning framework, treating absent mentions as unlabeled rather than truly negative. Our framework provides preference supervision using constructed contrastive pairs, generated using edits to model responses, producing variants that explicitly mention or omit a specific finding. Generated responses that mention the finding are naturally preferred in the context of visual evidence. Across semi-synthetic experiments and analyses on real-world chest radiograph benchmarks where adjudicated labels are available, PU-DPO yields consistent gains in detection rates and recovery of hidden positives across multiple pathologies, and is more robust to omission noise than prior approaches.

</details>


### [10] [LoDA: A Level of Detection Aware Method and a Multimodal Sensing Benchmark for Object Level Change Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05356)
*Haitian Wang,Xinyu Wang,Sheldon Fung,Xian Zhang,Zichen Geng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: An object-level 3D change-detection pipeline for high-definition LiDAR maps that decouples registration, geometry, and semantics, enabling explicit detection limits and robust object-level labels; achieves state-of-the-art results on LoDA and Urb3DCD-V2 benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D change-detection methods are often tile-based and threshold-driven, yielding per-point changes without explicit object-level limits or consistent object-level labels. Pose uncertainty and density variation across epochs hinder reliable cross-epoch correspondences and false positives.

Method: A decoupled pipeline that uses detection-limit-aware registration, geometry-driven object proxies with rule-based semantic and instance segmentation, and displacement cues in height, volume, and surface-normal to assign five change labels with confidence; propagates pose uncertainty into location-dependent detection limits; builds LoDA, a level-of-detection benchmark with fused multi-temporal maps, semantic instances, and object-level annotations; evaluates on LoDA Subiaco district and Urb3DCD-V2.

Result: On LoDA Subiaco: 95.0% accuracy, 90.8% macro F1, 83.0% macro IoU; beats best baseline by 8.7 IoU points and 4.4 F1 points. On Urb3DCD-V2: 96.81% mean accuracy and 89.52% mean change IoU; improves mAcc by 1.36 and mIoUch by 3.18 over strongest baselines.

Conclusion: Decoupling registration, geometry, and semantics stabilizes cross-epoch correspondences under pose uncertainty, suppresses false changes due to misalignment and density variation, and enables reliable object-level change detection with explicit detection limits; LoDA provides a level-of-detection evaluation framework and benchmarks that support state-of-the-art performance.

Abstract: High-definition 3D LiDAR maps are important for autonomous driving and smart-city services, which require reliable detection of object-level changes in multi-temporal urban LiDAR to keep digital maps aligned with the physical world. Existing approaches from raster height differencing to depth image and point-cloud networks often remain tile-based and threshold-driven, yielding per-point scores without explicit detection limits or consistent object-level labels. We propose an object-level 3D change-detection pipeline that integrates detection-limit-aware registration, geometry-driven object proxies with rule-based semantic and instance segmentation, and displacement cues in height, volume, and surface-normal direction to assign five change labels with confidence. By decoupling registration, geometry, and semantics, the pipeline propagates pose uncertainty into spatially varying detection limits, stabilizes cross-epoch correspondences, and suppresses false changes caused by residual misalignment and density variation. We also present LoDA, a level-of-detection (LoD) aware benchmark for the Subiaco district with fused multi-temporal vehicle-LiDAR maps constructed with LiDAR, GNSS, and IMU support, semantic instances, and object-level annotations. On this benchmark, our method achieves 95.0% accuracy, 90.8% macro F1, and 83.0% macro IoU, exceeding the best baseline by 8.7 IoU points and 4.4 F1 points. On the public Urb3DCD-V2 benchmark evaluated under the official point-wise protocol, it reaches 96.81% mean accuracy and 89.52% mean change IoU, improving over the strongest reported baselines by 1.36 points in mAcc and 3.18 points in mIoUch.

</details>


### [11] [Text-Guided Refinement of Multi-sequence Glioma Subregion Segmentation with a Vision-Language Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05389)
*Zach Eidex,Yu-nong Lin,Mojtaba Safari,Sean Pitroda,Ralph Weichselbaum,Zhen Tian,Xiaofeng Yang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 3D vision-language foundation model VoxTell enables text-guided refinement of glioma subregion segmentation, showing modest DSC gains on internal data and cross-dataset transfer when using correct prompts; performance depends on prompt quality, indicating clinician-in-the-loop potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual glioma subregion delineation is time-consuming and error-prone; existing models may generalize poorly; clinician-directed text-based refinement could improve segmentation and efficiency.

Method: Lightweight VoxTell-based framework; initial masks generated by pretrained VoxTell; oracle prompts encode target, action, location, imaging evidence, edit size, and preservation constraints; frozen Qwen/VoxTell prompt embeddings injected via trainable projections into multiscale decoder conditioning; all other weights frozen; training/validation/testing on BraTS-GLI (901/100/250); cross-dataset transfer evaluation on 100 meningioma, metastasis, pediatric tumor, UPENN-GBM cases.

Result: Internal test (post-contrast T1): Dice similarity coefficient (DSC) for enhancing tumor, edema, and necrotic/non-enhancing core improved from 0.774±0.158 to 0.796±0.137; blank prompts 0.762±0.155; contradictory prompts 0.770±0.163; p<0.001, d_z≈0.48–0.71. Cross-dataset: 0.527±0.287 to 0.550±0.278; contradictory prompts 0.504±0.275; p<0.001, d_z≈0.43.

Conclusion: A 3D vision-language foundation model can perform instruction-guided refinement of glioma subregion segmentations; results show sensitivity to prompt type, suggesting text-dependent contour editing rather than nonspecific post-processing; supports further evaluation as a clinician-in-the-loop tool.

Abstract: Background: Accurate glioma subregion delineation is important for radiotherapy planning and longitudinal monitoring, but manual contour correction is time-consuming. Models such as nnU-Net may generalize imperfectly and lack clinician-directed text correction.
  Purpose: We investigated adapting a three-dimensional (3D) vision-language foundation model for text-guided brain tumor segmentation refinement.
  Methods: We developed a lightweight VoxTell-based framework. Pretrained VoxTell generated initial masks. Oracle prompts derived from segmentation errors encoded target, action, location, imaging evidence, edit size, and preservation constraints. Frozen Qwen/VoxTell prompt embeddings were injected through trainable projections into its multiscale decoder conditioning; other weights remained frozen. Training, validation, and testing used 901, 100, and 250 BraTS-GLI cases. Cross-dataset transfer was evaluated on 100 meningioma, metastasis, pediatric tumor, and UPENN-GBM cases.
  Results: On the internal test set using post-contrast T1-weighted input, correct instructions improved subregion Dice similarity coefficient (DSC; enhancing tumor, edema, and necrotic/non-enhancing core) from $0.774\pm0.158$ to $0.796\pm0.137$. They outperformed blank prompts ($0.762\pm0.155$; Holm-adjusted $p<0.001$, $d_z=0.71$) and contradictory prompts ($0.770\pm0.163$; $p<0.001$, $d_z=0.48$). In cross-dataset testing, correct instructions improved DSC from $0.527\pm0.287$ to $0.550\pm0.278$ and outperformed contradictory instructions ($0.504\pm0.275$; $p<0.001$, $d_z=0.43$).
  Conclusion: A 3D vision-language foundation model can perform instruction-guided refinement of glioma subregion segmentations. Sensitivity to correct, blank, and contradictory prompts suggests text-dependent contour editing rather than nonspecific post-processing, supporting further evaluation as a clinician-in-the-loop tool.

</details>


### [12] [Adapting Vision Foundation Models with Cascaded Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05393)
*Xi Xiao,Xingjian Li,Cheng Han,Tianyang Wang,Lin Zhao,Yunbei Zhang,Guosheng Hu,Runmin Jiang,Xi Li,Xiao Wang,Min Xu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Cascaded semantic priors enhance visual prompt tuning for ViTs by injecting hand-crafted color/texture/shape cues in input space and instance-aware self-attention maps in feature space, enabling strong parameter-efficient adaptation (0.74% of parameters) across 34 datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visual prompt tuning (VPT) uses randomly initialized prompts and ignores rich prior knowledge. Introducing semantic priors—fundamental image cues and attention-based semantics—aims to boost data efficiency and generalization in ViT adaptation.

Method: A cascaded scheme that (1) extracts fundamental image priors (color, texture, shape) with hand-crafted operators and injects them into the input space, and (2) leverages self-attention maps to inject instance-aware semantic information in the feature space, integrated across ViT adaptation with a cascaded pipeline.

Result: On 34 challenging image classification datasets, the proposed method achieves superior downstream adaptation while tuning only 0.74% of ViT parameters.

Conclusion: Injecting complementary semantic priors into VPT substantially improves parameter-efficient ViT adaptation, leveraging both handcrafted input-space cues and learned attention-based semantics to better guide fine-tuning.

Abstract: Prompt tuning, a leading parameter-efficient adaptation paradigm in NLP, has recently been extended to computer vision. Visual prompt tuning (VPT) adapts pre-trained vision transformers (ViTs) by updating a small set of additional prompt parameters. However, existing visual prompts are randomly initialized and do not exploit prior knowledge, such as instructions in NLP. We address this gap by injecting two complementary semantic priors into VPT. Fundamental image priors, including color, texture, and shape, are extracted with classical hand-crafted operators and injected into the input space, while self-attention maps provide instance-aware semantics in the feature space. We further propose a cascaded scheme that integrates both priors throughout ViT adaptation. Experiments on 34 challenging image classification datasets demonstrate superior downstream adaptation while tuning only 0.74% of ViT parameters. Project page: https://xixiaouab.github.io/Cascaded-Semantics/.

</details>


### [13] [Invisible Shortcuts: Why Vision Encoders Know Your Camera](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05424)
*Vladan Stojnić,Ryan Ramos,Giorgos Kordopatis-Zilos,Noa Garcia,Giorgos Tolias*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Models pretrained with strong metadata-semantics correlations in pixels learn to rely on metadata traces, causing robustness and generalization issues under metadata shifts; mitigation can reduce this sensitivity without harming downstream performance and may improve out-of-distribution generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover and quantify how invisible pixel-level metadata traces—arising from image processing and acquisition—become predictive through large-scale supervision, and to assess strategies to mitigate such shortcuts.

Method: Introduce controlled metadata-semantics correlations into training data, measure sensitivity to metadata traces, and evaluate performance under metadata distribution shifts. Examine mitigation strategies applied during and after pretraining, test on unseen metadata, and analyze impact on downstream tasks and generated-image detection ability.

Result: Stronger metadata-semantics correlations increase sensitivity to pixel-level metadata traces and amplify performance degradation under metadata distribution shifts. Mitigation strategies reduce sensitivity to both targeted and unseen metadata while preserving downstream task performance, and can improve out-of-distribution generalization. The work also notes a link between metadata sensitivity and generated-image detection abilities.

Conclusion: Metadata-induced shortcuts are measurable and mitigable. With appropriate interventions during or after pretraining, models can become less sensitive to pixel-level metadata traces without sacrificing downstream accuracy, potentially benefiting OOD robustness and detection tasks.

Abstract: Deep vision models exploit shortcuts, relying on cues that correlate with supervision signals. Prior work has focused on visible biases, such as object-background or texture correlations. We identify a different source of shortcut learning: invisible metadata traces embedded at the pixel level, for metadata such as image processing and photo acquisition. We hypothesize that large-scale semantic supervision, whether through categorical labels (ImageNet) or billion-scale captions (LAION), naturally induces metadata-semantics correlations during pretraining, leading models to convert low-level signals into predictive features. By introducing controlled metadata-semantics correlations, we show that stronger ones produce systematically higher sensitivity to metadata traces and larger performance degradation under metadata distribution shifts. We further explore mitigation strategies applied during and after pretraining that reduce sensitivity not only to targeted metadata but also to unseen ones, without sacrificing performance on downstream tasks. Metadata sensitivity also has a positive side: it partly explains the strong generated-image detection ability of some encoders, while its mitigation can improve out-of-distribution generalization. Code: https://github.com/ryan-caesar-ramos/visual-encoder-traces

</details>


### [14] [MOSAIK: Multi-Patch Content-Aware Spatial Allocation of Image Tokens for Efficient Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05450)
*Mohammadreza Hami,Mohammadreza Samadi,Chao Gao,Negar Hassanpour*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MOSAIK introduces a damage-guided, region-aware patch layout for pixel-space diffusion models to reduce FLOPs and token count while preserving quality, by predicting fidelity loss from coarsening and assigning fine patches to sensitive regions under a token budget. It adapts PixelDiT to heterogeneous patch layouts and uses intermediate denoising features for guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pixel-space diffusion models suffer from quadratic self-attention due to high token counts. Prior efficiency methods use larger patches uniformly, which neglect region-specific fidelity losses when coarsening. A damage-guided, heterogeneous patch layout aims to allocate computation where it matters most, improving efficiency without compromising quality.

Method: MOSAIK extends the PixelDiT backbone to support arbitrary heterogeneous patch layouts. It employs a lightweight predictor that uses intermediate denoising features to estimate the fidelity loss caused by coarsening each region. Given a token budget, the predictor assigns fine patches to sensitive regions and coarse patches elsewhere, thereby reducing FLOPs and token counts while maintaining performance. The framework is evaluated on GenEval and DPG-Bench.

Result: FLOPs reduced by 70% and token count reduced by 83%. MOSAIK matches full-compute PixelDiT on GenEval. DPG-Bench score degrades by only 1.0 point. Outperforms diverse efficiency baselines (e.g., temporal patch scheduling, feature caching) under constrained compute budgets, while maintaining competitive performance across metrics.

Conclusion: Region-aware, damage-guided patch layouts enable substantial efficiency gains for pixel-space diffusion models with minimal loss in generation quality. The approach demonstrates that targeted fidelity preservation in sensitive regions is effective for heterogeneous patching and provides a practical path toward efficient high-resolution image generation.

Abstract: Pixel-space diffusion models avoid the reconstruction ceiling of latent diffusion models by generating directly in image space. However, their substantially higher token count makes generation expensive due to the quadratic complexity of self-attention. Several existing efficiency methods reduce this cost by using larger patches at selected denoising steps, thereby representing the image with fewer tokens. Yet, each step still uses a single patch size uniformly across the entire image, overlooking that different regions suffer different fidelity losses when coarsened. We introduce MOSAIK, a damage-guided framework that varies patch size across regions and denoising steps. MOSAIK adapts the PixelDiT backbone to generate arbitrary heterogeneous patch layouts, and a lightweight predictor uses intermediate denoising features to estimate the fidelity loss caused by coarsening each region. Given a token budget, our damage-guided layout predictor assigns fine patches to sensitive regions and coarse patches elsewhere. Remarkably, while reducing FLOPs by 70% and token count by 83%, MOSAIK matches the full-compute PixelDiT on GenEval and its DPG-Bench score drops by only 1.0 point. Compared to diverse efficiency paradigms, including temporal patch scheduling and feature caching, our approach delivers highly competitive performance at moderate budgets and consistently outperforms these baselines in highly constrained compute regimes.

</details>


### [15] [CDSeg: A Renderable Gaussian Carrier for Image-to-3D Label Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05482)
*Wentao Sun,Yiping Chen,Zhengsen Xu,Jonathan Li,John S. Zelek*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CDSeg enables cross-domain label transfer from 2D segmentation masks to 3D scenes using Gaussian splatting, without training a task-specific 3D segmentation network, by embedding labels in Gaussian primitives and using visibility-driven rendering and multi-view fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reuse 2D segmentation masks across 3D data and multiple representations (point clouds, Gaussian scenes, image views) while avoiding task-specific 3D segmentation training and enabling scalable labeling of large scenes.

Method: Use Gaussian primitives as renderable label carriers; external 2D masks guide labeling; instantiate the label carriers by either completing each input point into a Gaussian or reusing native Gaussians; determine visibility-derived label assignment during rendering; record pixel–primitive associations; fuse multi-view masks via voting and a local filter; outputs can be assigned back to points, retained on the Gaussian scene, or rendered into other views; supports promptable, automatic instance/semantic/LiDAR segmentation settings and scales to millions of primitives.

Result: Achieves 92.35% mIoU on DesktopObjects-360, 95.89% on NeRDS-360, and 65.77% on the full ScanNet-v2 validation split using provided 2D annotations.

Conclusion: CDSeg offers a single interface to reuse 2D masks across 3D point clouds, Gaussian scenes, and image views without a dedicated 3D segmentation network, handling large-scale data efficiently.

Abstract: Modern image models provide strong cues about \emph{what} should be segmented in each view, but their masks do not by themselves determine \emph{where} those labels should persist in 3D. We present Cross-Domain Segmentation via Gaussian Splatting (CDSeg), a label-transfer interface that requires no task-specific 3D segmentation training and uses Gaussian primitives as a renderable label carrier. An external mask source supplies the labels, while renderer-derived visibility determines which 3D primitives receive them. The carrier is instantiated either by completing each input point into one Gaussian, preserving its index, or by reusing the native primitives of an optimized Gaussian scene. CDSeg records pixel--primitive associations during rendering and fuses multi-view masks through voting and a local filter. The resulting labels can be returned to the original points, retained on the native Gaussian scene, or rendered into other views. CDSeg covers promptable, automatic instance, semantic, and LiDAR settings and processes scenes with millions of primitives in seconds. It obtains 92.35\% mIoU on DesktopObjects-360, 95.89\% on NeRDS-360, and 65.77\% on the full ScanNet-v2 validation split using the provided 2D semantic annotations. CDSeg thereby provides one interface for reusing 2D masks across point clouds, Gaussian scenes, and image views without a task-specific 3D segmentation network.

</details>


### [16] [VideoArgus: Agentic Rubric-Grounded Unified Evaluation for Video Generation and Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05485)
*Ziyun Zeng,Zixuan Wang,Yongsheng Yu,Hang Hua,Jiebo Luo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoArgus introduces a unified, rubric-grounded framework for evaluating video generation/editing across five settings, generating per-instance rubrics to drive evidence-grounded scoring; paired with VideoArgus-Bench and a human-alignment set, it improves correlation with human judgments and shows robust performance across backbones; code/data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitations of existing video evaluation benchmarks—fixed content, limited settings, and sparse evidence—by providing a single, rubric-based evaluation framework that yields evidence, rationales, and diagnostic reports.

Method: For each input, generate a sample-specific rubric used to evaluate all candidate videos. Rubrics define criteria, scoring rules, failure modes, and evidence plans. Visual tools and VLM QA produce criterion scores, rationales, and diagnostic reports. Build VideoArgus-Bench (1,026 inputs from 653 images and 416 videos) with pre-generated rubrics; evaluate on a 1,260-video human-alignment set. Compare correlations with human judgments across five tasks and across rubric-generation and evaluation backbones; release code/data.

Result: VideoArgus achieves higher within-input Spearman and Kendall correlations with human judgments than benchmark-specific evaluators across all five tasks. Model rankings remain largely consistent across rubric-generation and evaluation-VLM backbones.

Conclusion: A unified, rubric-grounded evaluation framework and associated benchmark improve alignment with human judgments and demonstrate robustness across methods, supporting standardized, evidence-grounded video evaluation; all resources are released.

Abstract: Evaluating generated videos remains challenging because existing benchmarks rely on fixed evaluation content, cover only a subset of generation and editing settings, and provide limited evidence for their scores. We introduce VideoArgus, a unified rubric-grounded framework covering five video generation and editing settings. For each input instance, VideoArgus generates an output-blind, sample-specific rubric once and reuses it to evaluate all corresponding candidate videos. The rubric defines concrete criteria, scoring rules, failure modes, and evidence plans, which guide criterion-specific VLM QA and visual tools to produce evidence-grounded criterion scores, rationales, and a diagnostic report. We further construct VideoArgus-Bench, containing 1,026 curated input instances built from 653 high-quality images and 416 high-quality videos, with all benchmark rubrics pre-generated, frozen, and released. On a separate 1,260-video human-alignment set, VideoArgus achieves higher within-input Spearman and Kendall correlations with human judgments than the corresponding benchmark-specific evaluators across all five tasks. Model rankings also remain largely consistent across different rubric-generation and evaluation-VLM backbones. All code and data are released. Visit our project page: https://zzzmyyzeng.github.io/VideoArgus

</details>


### [17] [APQF: Agentic Profiling-Guided Structured Pruning and Mixed-Precision Quantization with Adaptive Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05499)
*Sadegh Jafari,Mohiuddin Bilwal,Fan Zhou,Brian Gelder,Ali Jannesari*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: APQF automates structured pruning and mixed-precision quantization with LLM-guided planning and profiling to tailor per-layer compression, achieving large compute reductions with minimal accuracy loss across CNNs and vision transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep networks are expensive and resource-intensive. Manual pruning/quantization and uniform compression lead to suboptimal accuracy. There is a need for an automated, architecture-agnostic, training-aware pipeline that uses profiling data to optimize per-layer compression.

Method: A profiling agent measures cost distribution and pruning sensitivity; LLM planners propose per-layer pruning ratios, per-layer bit-widths, and recovery strategies. The pipeline integrates this with training-aware pruning/quantization for CNNs and ViTs and validates with six LLM planners (including open-weight ones). Evaluation on ResNet, VGG7, ViT, DeiT, Swin; datasets: ImageNet-1k and CIFAR-10.

Result: On ImageNet, compute is reduced to 5.6-7.7% of original bit-operations (13-18x), with accuracy close to baseline. Under a 200k-image budget, Top-1 is roughly 17 points higher than existing joint pruning-quantization methods. On CIFAR-10, compression surpasses the comparison method on four of five architectures. For VGG7, 93.15% accuracy is achieved with only 0.41% of baseline bit-operations, the only method at that compression level to exceed its full-precision baseline. Ablations show uniform compression hurts accuracy more at matched compute, and withholding profiling data degrades performance. Six planners achieve 97.4-97.9% on Swin-Tiny; profiling data is essential.

Conclusion: APQF is the first framework to combine LLM-guided decisions with profiling-grounded, fully training-aware pruning and quantization for both CNNs and vision transformers. Profiling per-layer sensitivity and tailored decisions significantly outperforms uniform compression and non-profiling approaches, demonstrating broad effectiveness and potential for edge deployment.

Abstract: Modern deep neural networks achieve strong performance, but their scale makes them costly and slow, especially on resource-constrained edge devices. Pruning and quantization address this, but rely on manual, expert choices and on algorithms that are hard to apply across architectures. Uniform settings also ignore how differently individual layers respond to compression, which costs accuracy. We introduce APQF, an agentic profiling-guided framework that combines structured pruning, mixed-precision quantization-aware training, and accuracy recovery in one automated pipeline. A profiling agent measures how cost is distributed across the model and how sensitive each part is to pruning, and this evidence drives per-layer pruning ratios, per-layer bit-widths, and the recovery strategy, all proposed by LLM planners and validated before execution. To our knowledge, APQF is the first framework to combine LLM-guided, profiling-grounded decisions with a fully training-aware pruning and quantization pipeline for both CNNs and vision transformers. We evaluate APQF on ResNet, VGG7, ViT, DeiT, and Swin using ImageNet-1k and CIFAR-10. On ImageNet it cuts compute to 5.6-7.7 percent of the original bit-operations, a 13-18x reduction, while keeping accuracy close to the baseline, and under a 200K-image budget it stays roughly 17 points higher in Top-1 than existing joint pruning and quantization methods. On CIFAR-10 it compresses further than that method on four of five architectures. On VGG7 it reaches 93.15 percent using only 0.41 percent of baseline bit-operations, the only method at that compression level to improve on its full-precision baseline. Ablations show that uniform compression loses the most accuracy at matched compute, and that withholding profiling data from the planner hurts every model. Six LLM planners, including free open-weight ones, all reach 97.4-97.9 percent on Swin-Tiny.

</details>


### [18] [DynaPix: Can Vision-Language Models Identify the Exact Future?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05505)
*Thong Nguyen,Vinh-Hien Do,Quynh Vo,Cong-Duy Nguyen,See-Kiong Ng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DynaPix is a checkable-prediction benchmark where models must pick the true future frame from close candidates or a large gallery, given a video truncated before a key event in physics-simulated scenes; reveals a temporal-anchoring gap in current models and partly mitigated by training on simulator-derived true records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations often tolerate plausible or realistic outputs without verifying the exact future state. A checkable benchmark forces models to align predictions with the true subsequent state and time, exposing whether they truly understand temporal dynamics and physics.

Method: Use physics-simulated scenes. Provide a video truncated before a key event and a question about a later moment. The model selects the true future image from a set of near-identical candidates or a large gallery. The true image and its timestamp are known; distractors are deliberately similar. Training uses scene accounts drawn from the simulator's true record rather than teacher guesses. Compare performance on elapsed-time cues versus event-driven cues, and on gallery search.

Result: Models succeed when a visible event marks the target moment but perform near chance when only elapsed time marks it. Gallery search is even harder. Humans handle elapsed-time items well, indicating the challenge lies in models, not question design. Training on simulator-derived scene accounts improves performance, especially for shorter elapsed times, but not fully for long elapsed times, revealing a temporal-anchoring gap where models tether predictions to events rather than to time itself.

Conclusion: DynaPix exposes a temporal-anchoring gap in current models for dynamic scene prediction. The benchmark provides a path to drive improvements by using true-record training data and by focusing on temporally anchored predictions, guiding future model development toward exact temporal state prediction.

Abstract: Acting in a physical scene requires knowing its real later state, not a plausible one. Current evaluations often accept words or a realistic-looking image, so the predicted state is never checked against the true one. We introduce DynaPix (Dynamic Pixels), a benchmark that makes prediction checkable. Given a video clip that stops before a key event and a question about a later moment, a model must pick the true future image from close candidates or a large gallery. The scenes come from a physics simulator, so the correct image and its time are known exactly and the wrong options are deliberately similar. Models often succeed when a visible event marks the target moment, but are near chance when only elapsed time marks it. Gallery search is harder still, as the true image rarely ranks first. People handle the elapsed-time items well, so the difficulty lies with the models, not the questions. Training on scene accounts drawn from the simulator's true record, not a teacher's guess, repairs much of this but not the longer elapsed time case. DynaPix thus exposes a temporal-anchoring gap: models attach a prediction to an event far better than to time itself.

</details>


### [19] [HERA: Historical Evidence Routing Adapter for Physical Prediction in Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05523)
*Yuanruyi,Yue Cao,Haojia Gao,Guanqiu Guo,Ziyuezhang,Shangqin,Junbo Tan,Bokui Chen,Zhuo Zou,Xueqian Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces HERA, a Historical Evidence Routing Adapter, to selectively retrieve and route past evidence into a frozen latent video predictor; instantiated as RRPM with a structured memory, improving predictive accuracy on IntPhys2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Occlusion challenges in physical video prediction require historical evidence that is preserved and accessible; existing methods enlarge context or store features, but do not enable selective retrieval into a frozen predictor.

Method: Propose HERA framework; instantiate RRPM with Structured Memory Bank, Memory Registers, Workspace Registers; routing mechanism to feed past evidence into the predictor without altering its latent workspace.

Result: On IntPhys2 Main, HERA+RRPM improves pairwise AvgSurprise from 52.57% to 54.35% for V-JEPA 2-G; subgroup improvements for fixed-camera continuity (46.15% → 57.69%) and immutability (46.15% → 63.46%).

Conclusion: Historical evidence routing is a viable adaptation for latent world models; RRPM provides lightweight, effective memory units; encourages further retrieval-based enhancements in video-based physical prediction.

Abstract: Predictive video models have emerged as promising world models by learning latent visual dynamics from large-scale video. Yet these models remain challenged by physical events under occlusion, where later predictions may depend on object evidence that is no longer available in the current view. Addressing this challenge requires historical evidence not only to be preserved but also to remain accessible when it becomes relevant to a subsequent prediction. Existing approaches mainly enlarge the temporal context, cache generic video features, or impose explicit object-centric states, thereby improving the capacity or structure of retained history. However, they do not directly address how relevant historical evidence can be selectively retrieved and integrated into a pretrained predictor without interfering with its native latent workspace. Accordingly, we introduce HERA (Historical Evidence Routing Adapter), a framework for routing retained historical evidence into a frozen latent predictor, and instantiate it with Register-Routed Patch Memory (RRPM), a lightweight adapter comprising a Structured Memory Bank, Memory Registers, and Workspace Registers. On the IntPhys2 Main split, HERA with RRPM improves the pairwise AvgSurprise accuracy of V-JEPA 2-G from 52.57% to 54.35%. Subgroup analysis shows particularly strong improvements on fixed-camera continuity, from 46.15% to 57.69%, and fixed-camera immutability, from 46.15% to 63.46%. These results support historical evidence routing as a practical adaptation strategy for physical prediction in latent world models.

</details>


### [20] [OmniMech: All-in-one Multimodal Mechanical Benchmark for 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05539)
*Taiting Lu,Runze Liu,Ziwei Dong,Sisong Bei,Jingying Zeng,Mingjia Wang,Zhenghao Li,Kaiyuan Lin,Yi-Shan Wu,Yangshoudu Zheng,Hongxing Pan,Kai Zhang,Guoliang Shi,Ling Ma,Yifan Yang,Jiaying Lu,Qi He,Sung-Liang Chen,Yi-Chao Chen,Yincheng Jin,Mahanth Gowda*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: OmniMech benchmarks executable CAD generation from industrial drawings at a million-scale, introducing four evaluation tasks and a rich multi-representation dataset to reveal gaps in current VLMs and CAD models for fine-grained toleranced design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between vision-language models and the precision requirements of industrial mechanical CAD, providing a large, diverse dataset with dimensioned drawings, native CAD models, and multiple representations to evaluate and drive progress in executable CAD synthesis and constrained geometric reasoning.

Method: Assemble OmniMech with 251k+ fully dimensioned and toleranced 2D drawings paired to native CAD models, plus multi-view renders, mesh, STEP and B-rep representations, and rich semantic annotations. Define four tasks: (1) parametric CAD program synthesis from engineering drawings; (2) diagram-to-3D reasoning for geometrically/structurally consistent reconstruction; (3) annotation-grounded reasoning over dimensions, symbols, callouts, and constraints; (4) tool-augmented agentic reasoning using visualization, measurement, CAD execution, and verification tools. Release benchmark data, evaluation code, and tool interfaces.

Result: Empirical results indicate existing vision-language and CAD-specialized models struggle with executable CAD synthesis, fine-grained 3D reconstruction, and enforcing stringent dimensions and tolerances.

Conclusion: OmniMech sets a new benchmark standard for VLM-enabled CAD generation in industrial contexts and provides data/tools to guide future work toward robust, toleranced, and verifiable executable CAD generation.

Abstract: Recent vision-language models (VLMs) can generate executable CAD programs from images, but existing methods mainly target coarse, general-purpose 3D objects and rarely address the fine-grained geometry and millimeter-level tolerances required in industrial mechanical design. We introduce OmniMech, the first million-scale benchmark for evaluating VLMs on executable CAD generation from industrial manufacturing data. OmniMech contains more than 251,000 fully dimensioned and toleranced 2D orthographic drawings, paired with native CAD models, multi-view renderings, mesh, STEP and B-rep representations, and rich semantic annotations. The benchmark includes four tasks: (1) parametric CAD program synthesis from engineering drawings; (2) diagram-to-3D reasoning for geometrically and structurally consistent reconstruction; (3) annotation-grounded reasoning over dimensions, symbols, feature callouts, and manufacturing constraints; and (4) tool-augmented agentic reasoning using visualization, measurement, CAD execution, and verification tools. Experiments show that current VLMs and CAD-specialized models still struggle with executable program synthesis, fine-grained 3D reconstruction, and reliable enforcement of dimensions and tolerances. We will release the benchmark data, evaluation code, and tool interfaces to support future research.

</details>


### [21] [Hierarchical Flow Matching for 3D Point Cloud Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05557)
*Linhao Wang,Qichang Zhang,Ye Su,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HFM presents a bilevel flow matching approach for unconditional 3D point clouds, combining Latent Flow Matching for global shape in latent space and Conditional Point Flow Matching for fine details, enabling fast sampling (~15 Euler steps) and competitive SOTA on ShapeNet/ModelNet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CNFs are computationally heavy due to ODE solving and trace estimation; diffusion models require many denoising steps; 3D points have hierarchical structure (global topology and local geometry) that is not exploited by single-level generation. A bilevel, OT-based framework aims to model global structure efficiently while refining local details.

Method: Two-level optimal-transport flow matching: Latent Flow Matching models the global shape manifold in a compact latent space; Conditional Point Flow Matching reconstructs detailed point clouds conditioned on the latent code. Both components trained with simple MSE losses. The approach yields straight OT paths enabling sampling with around 15 Euler steps per flow; the latent space supports downstream tasks such as classification.

Result: Experiments on ShapeNet and ModelNet show that HFM is competitive with, and often surpasses, prior SOTA methods in 3D point cloud generation; efficient sampling with few Euler steps; structured latent space facilitates downstream analyses.

Conclusion: Hierarchical OT-based flow matching effectively decomposes 3D point cloud generation into global and local components, offering computational efficiency, competitive quality, and useful latent representations for tasks beyond generation.

Abstract: Generating high-quality 3D point clouds requires capturing both global shape topology and local geometric details. Existing flow-based methods rely on continuous normalizing flows (CNFs) that demand expensive ODE solving and trace estimation during training, while diffusion models require hundreds of iterative denoising steps. Moreover, most approaches adopt single-level generation directly in point space, disregarding the hierarchical structure natural to 3D shapes. We propose Hierarchical Flow Matching (HFM) that extends flow matching to bilevel structure for unconditional 3D point cloud generation. HFM decomposes the task into two levels via optimal-transport flow matching: a \textit{Latent Flow Matching} models the global shape manifold in a compact latent space, and a \textit{Conditional Point Flow Matching} reconstructs detailed point clouds conditioned on the latent code. Both flows are trained with simple MSE regression losses. The resulting straight OT paths enable efficient sampling with as few as 15 Euler steps per flow, while the structured latent space supports downstream tasks including classification. Extensive experiments on ShapeNet and ModelNet benchmarks demonstrate that HFM achieves competitive or even best performance compared with prior state-of-the-art methods.

</details>


### [22] [From Sports to Safety: Benchmarking Proactive Risk Inference in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05560)
*Jiawei Qiu,Yichen Xu,Jianzhe Ma,Mingyang Yu,Wenbin Zhu,Yang Han,Pinzheng Lv,Wenxuan Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPRINT introduces a large, real-world sports video benchmark (2,888 clips) for proactive hazard inference, showing MLLMs detect hazards well but struggle to ground them in causal explanations; danger-query prompts induce many false alarms; data/code will be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the gap in proactive safety for multimodal language models in dynamic physical environments. Existing evaluations focus on harmful content or general risks, while true proactive prediction requires early, cause-grounded reasoning under spatiotemporal cues; sports offer diverse injury causes and rich cues akin to autonomous driving and fall detection.

Method: Construct SPRINT with 2,888 real-world sports videos (2,440 accident, 448 safe controls) across 14 sports and 3 environmental settings. Provide fine-grained annotations for early hazard cues, accident timing, and hierarchical causes. Evaluate state-of-the-art MLLMs under varied prompts and temporal windows. Conduct diagnostic experiments to assess false alarms triggered by explicit danger queries, even on non-hazard videos.

Result: Top models achieve >95% hazard signaling accuracy but <50% in identifying causes, revealing a gap between detection and causal understanding. Danger-query prompts cause severe false alarms on safe videos. Overall, MLLMs show superficial proactive safety with weak, grounded early warning.

Conclusion: Current MLLMs lack stable, cause-grounded early warning in dynamic physical environments; SPRINT provides a benchmark and resources for advancing reliable proactive safety; data and code will be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Timely anticipation of physical hazards is essential for real-world safety, yet existing MLLM evaluations focus on harmful content or general risks, leaving proactive physical hazard prediction underexplored. Sports provide a well-suited testbed: accident causes span diverse injury dimensions and pre-accident spatiotemporal cues draw on reasoning capabilities shared with broader safety domains such as autonomous driving and fall detection. We introduce SPRINT (Sports Proactive Risk INference Testbed), a benchmark of 2,888 real-world sports videos (2,440 accident, 448 safe controls) spanning 14 sports and 3 environmental settings. Accident videos feature fine-grained annotations of early hazard cues, accident timing, and hierarchical causes; safe videos are manually verified as accident-free and serve to diagnose prompt-induced false alarms. Evaluating state-of-the-art MLLMs under diverse prompts and temporal windows reveals a sharp gap between hazard sensitivity and understanding: the best model exceeds 95% in signaling hazards yet falls below 50% in identifying their causes. Diagnostic experiments further show that explicit danger queries trigger severe false alarms even on hazard-free videos. These findings indicate that current MLLMs exhibit only superficial proactive safety, lacking stable, cause-grounded early warning, and underscore the need for reliable proactive safety in dynamic physical environments. Data and code will be open-sourced upon acceptance.

</details>


### [23] [EffectLearner: World-Aware Object-Effect Reasoning for Real-World Video Object Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05565)
*Feier Wu,Wanke Xia,Xu He,Zilang Zhou,Si Chen,Dongxia Liu,Liyang Chen,Qimeng Wu,Zhengbo Zhang,Wenming Yang,Zhiyong Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A semantic-reasoning framework (EffectLearner) integrates a VLM-based Object-Effect Reasoner with a DiT Video Eraser, guided by a structured effect-analysis prompt, plus motion-aware guidance and a new EffectWorld dataset, achieving strong removal and temporal coherence, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video object removal methods rely on predefined effect categories and fixed data distributions, limiting generalization to complex real-world scenes with compositional, spatially detached or weakly correlated effects, long-tail phenomena, and evolving interactions.

Method: A VLM-based Object-Effect Reasoner performs cross-modal reasoning on a target-highlighted video to extract compact effect-aware context, which guides a DiT-based Video Eraser to remove the object and its induced effects. A structured effect-analysis prompt steers reasoning. Motion-aware mask guidance and motion-consistency supervision enhance removal coverage and spatiotemporal stability. EffectWorld, a paired video dataset with complex object-induced effects, supports a progressive training curriculum combining common supervision with complex-effect data.

Result: EffectLearner outperforms existing baselines on ROSE-Bench for most metrics and shows clear advantages on EffectWorld-Eval and EffectWorld-Wild, demonstrating high-quality video object removal in complex real-world scenes.

Conclusion: The framework delivers robust object removal and effect suppression in complex scenes, with strong generalization to real-world dynamics, validated by a purpose-built dataset and a progressive training curriculum.

Abstract: Video object removal must eliminate not only the target object but also its induced effects while maintaining high-fidelity and spatiotemporally coherent restoration. Existing methods mainly learn object-effect correspondences implicitly from predefined effect categories and fixed data distributions, limiting their generalization to complex real-world scenes involving compositional effects, spatially detached or weakly correlated effects, long-tail physical phenomena, and dynamically evolving interactions. We propose EffectLearner, a semantic-reasoning-enhanced framework that combines a VLM-based Object-Effect Reasoner with a DiT-based Video Eraser. Guided by a structured effect-analysis prompt, the Reasoner performs cross-modal reasoning over a target-highlighted video and extracts compact effect-aware context, which guides the Video Eraser toward comprehensive object-effect removal. Motion-aware mask guidance and motion-consistency supervision further improve removal coverage and spatiotemporal stability under object motion and evolving scene dynamics. To fully exploit the framework in challenging real-world scenarios, we further construct EffectWorld, a paired video dataset specifically designed for complex object-induced effects, and introduce a progressive training curriculum that combines common supervision with complex-effect data. On the standard ROSE-Bench, EffectLearner outperforms existing baselines on most metrics and achieves clear advantages on both EffectWorld-Eval and the challenging EffectWorld-Wild, demonstrating its ability to deliver high-quality video object removal in complex real-world scenes.

</details>


### [24] [CoordRefer: Coordinate-Aware 3D Visual Grounding from Multiview Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05569)
*Haijie Li,Jiaxin Zhang,Dave Zhenyu Chen,Youyu Chen,Yanmin Wu,Jian Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CoordRefer decouples coordinate frame selection from 3D grounding by learning a coordinate-aware, frame-conditioned box predictor and aligning the two stages with a Group Relative Policy Optimization objective using 3D IoU rewards; on ScanRefer with Qwen3-VL-2B, it gains 11% Acc@0.25 and 7% Acc@0.5 over a coordinate-agnostic baseline, with a geometrically refined variant even beating some explicit 3D-input methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The same 3D box can be represented differently under multiple coordinate frames, causing coordinate-relative ambiguity and degraded grounding when coordinate frame selection and box regression are trained jointly. Decoupling these processes aims to reduce representation ambiguity and improve alignment with downstream grounding quality.

Method: 1) Coordinate frame selection: pick a reference frame to define the coordinate system. 2) Coordinate-conditioned grounding: predict 3D boxes conditioned on the chosen frame. 3) Coordinate-aware supervised fine-tuning to instantiate the two-stage setup. 4) Group Relative Policy Optimization with 3D IoU-based rewards to harmonize both stages and optimize grounding quality.

Result: Experimental results on ScanRefer with Qwen3-VL-2B show 11 percentage-point improvement in Acc@0.25 and 7-point improvement in Acc@0.5 over a coordinate-agnostic baseline. A geometrically refined variant surpasses methods that use explicit 3D inputs.

Conclusion: Decoupling coordinate frame selection from coordinate-conditioned grounding via coordinate-aware fine-tuning and policy optimization yields robust, coordinate-consistent 3D grounding and can outperform coordinate-agnostic baselines and some explicit-3D-input methods.

Abstract: Multiview image-based 3D visual grounding predicts a coordinate frame to define a coordinate system and then regresses a 3D bounding box for localization. However, existing methods jointly optimize coordinate frame selection and box regression, leading to coordinate-relative box ambiguity and degraded grounding performance. This ambiguity arises because the same box admits different numerical representations across coordinate frames, creating multiple optimization targets and yielding invalid compromise predictions. To tackle this challenge, we propose CoordRefer, a coordinate-aware framework that decouples coordinate frame selection from coordinate-conditioned grounding. CoordRefer first selects a reference frame to define the coordinate system and then conditions 3D box prediction on the coordinate system. We perform coordinate-aware supervised fine-tuning to establish coordinate frame selection and coordinate-conditioned box regression, followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization with 3D IoU-based rewards to align both stages with downstream grounding quality. On ScanRefer with Qwen3-VL-2B, CoordRefer achieves gains of 11% in Acc@0.25 and 7% in Acc@0.5 over the coordinate-agnostic baseline, while its geometrically refined variant surpasses methods using explicit 3D inputs.

</details>


### [25] [Beyond Frame Selection: Rethinking Long-Video Understanding with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05592)
*Ziling Huang,Shin'ichi Satoh*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VideoRouter (VR) coordinates global and local evidence views for long videos via a hierarchical, question-agnostic segmentation and a verification-guided router to select the best-supported view for final answers. It improves long-video reasoning beyond frame-selection baselines, e.g., +2.9 points on VideoMME with a LLaVA-Video-7B backbone; code to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Token limitations in Multimodal LLMs hinder capturing temporally sparse evidence in long videos. Uniform sampling or frame selection trades off global storyline context against fine-grained evidence, limiting performance.

Method: Construct a question-agnostic temporal hierarchy that partitions video into coarse-to-fine segments, yielding two complementary views: a global view for coverage-oriented reasoning and a local view for detail-oriented evidence. Introduce a verification-guided router to determine which view is better supported by the chosen evidence and select the final answer.

Result: Empirical validation shows the verification-guided router effectively coordinates global and local reasoning, with VR outperforming state-of-the-art frame-selection methods by 2.9 points on VideoMME under the LLaVA-Video-7B backbone.

Conclusion: A principled approach to long-video understanding that balances global storyline context and local evidence through hierarchical views and a verification-guided routing mechanism; promising improvement over frame-based sampling baselines; code release planned.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong progress in video understanding, yet it remains challenging because the token limitation makes MLLMs difficult to capture temporally sparse evidence. Existing methods typically rely on uniform sampling, or frame selection, but these strategies usually optimize either broad temporal coverage or local relevance, making it difficult to preserve both global storyline context and fine-grained evidence. We propose VideoRouter(VR) that rethinks long-video understanding as coordinating complementary evidence views rather than selecting a single subset of frames. It first organizes each video into a question-agnostic temporal hierarchy, which partition the video into coarse-to-fine temporally coherent segments.
