Motivation: Existing benchmarks emphasize local spatial perception from limited viewpoints; there is a need for global, long-horizon spatial understanding and the ability to map egocentric observations to global top-down representations.

Method: Construct GST-Bench from 6,790 minutes of synthetic video with human-verified questions requiring cross-view, global spatial inferences; evaluate 22 state-of-the-art VLMs; create GST-Bench-Local to assess local spatial reasoning; provide GST-Train as a supplementary dataset to foster advancement in global spatial reasoning.

Result: The strongest zero-shot model scores 42.68, far below the human score of 79.08; GST-Bench-Local shows that models struggle to consolidate long-horizon observations into a globally consistent scene; GST-Train is released to facilitate future research.

Conclusion: GST-Bench, together with GST-Bench-Local and GST-Train, highlights a persistent gap in global spatial reasoning for AI agents and provides benchmarks and data to drive future improvements.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence is fundamental to embodied agents, yet existing benchmarks focus on local spatial perception from single or few viewpoints, overlooking global spatial awareness over continuous, long-horizon visual streams. To address this limitation, we introduce the Global-Spatial-Temporal Benchmark (GST-Bench), a VQA benchmark for global spatial intelligence in video understanding, comprising human-verified questions derived from 6,790 minutes of synthetically generated video. It requires models to perform accurate spatial inference from novel viewpoints unseen in the input video and to map egocentric observations onto global top-down images. A comprehensive evaluation of 22 state-of-the-art VLMs exposes a striking gap between models and humans: the strongest zero-shot model attains only 42.68, far below the human score of 79.08. To probe the cause of this gap, we construct GST-Bench-Local and find that models, despite strong local spatial understanding under the same task formulation, still fail to consolidate long-horizon observations into a globally consistent scene representation. We further provide GST-Train, a dataset for global spatial reasoning, as a complementary resource to facilitate future research on this challenge.

</details>


### [52] [Beyond Relevance: Bayesian Evidence Acquisition for Agentic Whole-Slide Image Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05757)
*Bryan Wong,Xun Xu,Huazhu Fu,Nancy F. Chen,Mun Yong Yi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: BEACON reframes whole-slide image reasoning as Bayesian evidence acquisition, using expected information gain to selectively acquire patches and a simple evidence controller to decide on answering, acquiring more data, or inspecting higher resolution. It is training-free, built from off-the-shelf foundation models, and shows strong zero-shot performance across five WSI-VQA benchmarks with improved evidence efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In WSI reasoning, existing training-free agentic methods rely on semantic patch relevance, which can be diagnostically non-discriminative due to overlapping morphological patterns among competing diagnoses. This leads to redundant evidence collection and unresolved uncertainty. A principled, uncertainty-aware approach is needed that directly targets information gain rather than semantic relevance.

Method: BEACON maintains a probabilistic belief over competing diagnostic hypotheses and sequentially acquires patches by maximizing expected information gain (EIG). An evidence controller then chooses to answer, continue collecting evidence, or perform higher-resolution inspection. Built entirely from off-the-shelf foundation models; requires no additional training or fine-tuning.

Result: Zero-shot experiments on five WSI-VQA benchmarks show that BEACON achieves the strongest overall performance among training-free agentic frameworks and substantially improves evidence acquisition efficiency, establishing Bayesian evidence acquisition as a principled paradigm for uncertainty-aware WSI reasoning.

Conclusion: BEACON demonstrates that Bayesian evidence acquisition is a principled, plug-and-play paradigm for uncertainty-aware WSI reasoning. The framework is training-free, leverages existing foundation models, and can be readily deployed for efficient diagnostic reasoning in pathology.

Abstract: Whole-slide image (WSI) reasoning requires an agent to sequentially acquire visual evidence before answering a diagnostic question. Existing training-free agentic frameworks formulate this process as iterative patch retrieval based on semantic relevance to the question. However, semantic relevance does not necessarily imply diagnostic informativeness in computational pathology, where competing diagnoses often exhibit similar and overlapping morphological patterns, making many patches semantically relevant yet diagnostically non-discriminative. Consequently, relevance-based retrieval may acquire redundant observations and leave diagnostic uncertainty unresolved. We propose BEACON, a plug-and-play agentic framework that reformulates WSI reasoning as a Bayesian evidence acquisition problem. BEACON maintains a probabilistic belief over competing diagnostic hypotheses and sequentially acquires patches by maximizing expected information gain (EIG) to reduce diagnostic uncertainty. An evidence controller then determines whether to answer, acquire additional evidence, or perform higher-resolution inspection. Built entirely from off-the-shelf foundation models, BEACON requires no additional training or fine-tuning. Extensive zero-shot experiments across five WSI-VQA benchmarks demonstrate that BEACON achieves the strongest overall performance among training-free agentic frameworks while substantially improving evidence acquisition efficiency, establishing Bayesian evidence acquisition as a principled paradigm for uncertainty-aware agentic WSI reasoning. The code is available at https://github.com/bryanwong17/BEACON

</details>


### [53] [Flow-Map Distillation on Relation Manifolds for Image Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05769)
*Zihao He,Songhua Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FoRM introduces a flow-map distillation approach on relation manifolds to model dynamic knowledge transfer in image restoration, using a flow operator to predict relation states over time with semigroup consistency constraints and endpoint anchoring, yielding improved performance and reduced training variance across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional distillation uses static targets for intermediate features/relations, neglecting the dynamic, trajectory-like nature of knowledge transfer. This can lead to suboptimal supervision and error accumulation.

Method: Learn a flow-map operator F_theta(z, t, s) that maps the relation state from time t to s on the relation manifold, enabling continuous trajectory supervision. Enforce global self-consistency with a safe semigroup consistency constraint using ground-truth bridge states, and apply an endpoint anchoring loss to prevent drift from teacher targets. Train and evaluate across five restoration tasks with various backbones.

Result: Consistent gains over state-of-the-art distillation baselines across backbones on five tasks (super-resolution, deraining, denoising, deblurring, low-light enhancement). Reduction of training variance by ~50% versus naive flow matching distillation; superior restoration quality.

Conclusion: FoRM provides a dynamic, trajectory-aware distillation paradigm that improves knowledge transfer in image restoration, with mechanisms to enforce global consistency and anchor endpoints, demonstrating strong, task-general gains.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation for image restoration typically aligns intermediate features or relation matrices between teacher and student networks as static targets, ignoring the dynamic structure of the knowledge transfer process. In this paper, we propose Flow-Map Distillation on Relation Manifolds (FoRM), which reformulates relation-based knowledge transfer as a continuous flow mapping problem on the relation manifold. Rather than regressing a constant velocity field between student and teacher relation states, FoRM learns a flow map operator $\mathcal{F}_θ(\mathbf{z}, t, s)$ that directly predicts the relation state at any target time $s$ given the current state at time $t$, enabling richer trajectory-level supervision. To ensure global self-consistency of the learned flow map, we introduce a safe semigroup consistency constraint that enforces compositional agreement using ground-truth bridge states, eliminating phantom-state error accumulation. An endpoint anchoring loss further prevents the operator from drifting away from the teacher target. Extensive experiments on five image restoration tasks, including super-resolution, deraining, denoising, deblurring, and low-light enhancement, demonstrate consistent gains over state-of-the-art distillation baselines across multiple backbone architectures, reducing training variance by approximately 50\% compared to naive flow matching distillation while achieving superior restoration quality.

</details>


### [54] [HyTBE: Hyperbolic Target-Background Expert Model for Cross-Domain Infrared Small Target Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05771)
*Aohua Li,Jin Kuang,Yubing Lu,Pingping Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HyTBE combines a Target-Background Relation Intervention with hyperbolic modeling to improve cross-domain infrared small target detection. It expands observed relation patterns and adjusts features via a Hyperbolic Relation Modeling in a Poincaré ball, coupled with a Hyperbolic-guided MoE Adapter, achieving better leave-one-domain-out generalization on NUAA-SIRST, NUDT-SIRST, and IRSTD-1K.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-domain degradation in IRST detectors arises when models learn restricted target-background relation patterns from limited source domains. Unseen domains may exhibit relation patterns not seen during training, weakening discriminative capability.

Method: 1) Target-Background Relation Intervention perturbs targets or backgrounds to broaden the observable relation patterns during training. 2) Hyperbolic Relation Modeling maps multi-scale visual cues into a Poincaré ball and encodes relation for each feature token via distances to target and background anchors. 3) Hyperbolic-guided MoE Adapter uses these relations to calibrate multi-scale features and aggregate expert-specific corrections. 

Result: Leave-one-domain-out experiments on NUAA-SIRST, NUDT-SIRST, and IRSTD-1K show HyTBE achieves stronger cross-domain generalization than competitive baselines.

Conclusion: HyTBE effectively broadens target-background relation patterns and leverages hyperbolic geometry to model relation cues, yielding improved cross-domain IRST detection performance.

Abstract: Infrared small target detection (IRSTD) has achieved substantial progress under domain-consistent evaluation, yet detector performance often degrades markedly when generalizing to unseen infrared domains. Existing methods primarily improve detection by enhancing target responses and suppressing background interference. However, when trained on only a limited set of source domains, their learned decision rules are inevitably established from a restricted range of source-domain target-background relation patterns. We formulate this cross-domain failure as target-background relation shift: unseen domains may exhibit relation patterns that are not observed during training, thereby weakening the discriminative capability learned from the source domains. To address this problem, we propose HyTBE, a Hyperbolic Target-Background Expert model that expands source-domain relation patterns and adaptively adjusts visual representations using explicit relation cues. The Target-Background Relation Intervention selectively perturbs either targets or backgrounds, broadening the observable relation patterns during training while maintaining valid supervision. Subsequently, the Hyperbolic Relation Modeling maps multi-scale visual cues into a Poincaré ball and characterizes the target-background relation of each feature token according to its relative distances to the target and background anchors. The Hyperbolic-guided MoE Adapter further uses these hyperbolic relation representations to calibrate multi-scale visual features and aggregate expert-specific feature corrections for different relation patterns. Leave-one-domain-out experiments on NUAA-SIRST, NUDT-SIRST, and IRSTD-1K demonstrate that HyTBE achieves stronger cross-domain generalization than competitive baselines.

</details>


### [55] [SR-JEPA: Learning Predictive Latent State in 3D Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05774)
*Zihan Zhou,Qifu Wen,Xi Zeng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SR-JEPA demonstrates a queryable, predictive latent for missing objects in scene-scale 3D point clouds, achieving meaningful semantic and AP metrics without reconstruction supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand what a trained predictive pathway infers when an entire entity is absent from a native 3D scene, and to assess whether the latent representations can be queried to infer context-dependent content.

Method: Introduce SR-JEPA, a point-native JEPA for scene-scale point clouds with a frozen predictive pathway that can be queried at a supplied location. Evaluation removes every point of a target object and replaces it with a 32-point shape-free query at its centroid. Training uses self-contained 3D EMA targets; no reconstruction, semantic labels, language, or lifted 2D features.

Result: On 5,953 held-out ARKitScenes objects, the imputed latent attains 43.13% semantic-identity macro accuracy (22.18 points above the strongest floor). Randomizing the prediction path reduces performance by 9.78 points, while substituting matched donor context reduces by 21.98. On 8,570 Sr3D support pairs, the full latent reaches 41.15 AP; identity decoded from the missing-object latent, combined with anchor identity and geometry, reaches 39.37 AP, leaving a residual 1.78 points. These results indicate a queryable, compositional 3D predictive state that completes context-dependent entity content for downstream use with metric geometry.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that predictive pathways in 3D can encode semantic identity and object content that are context-dependent and composable with geometry, enabling queryable inferences about missing entities without explicit reconstruction or labels; this suggests new directions for 3D scene understanding and object-centric representations.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures learn by predicting latent representations of missing observations, yet many masked JEPAs are evaluated primarily through the encoders they produce. We ask what a trained predictive pathway itself infers when an entire entity is absent from a native 3D scene. We introduce SR-JEPA, a point-native JEPA for scene-scale point clouds whose original frozen predictive pathway can be queried at a supplied location. At evaluation, every point of one object is removed before encoding and replaced by the same shape-free 32-point query at its centroid. Training uses only self-contained 3D EMA targets: no reconstruction, semantic labels, language, or lifted 2D features. On 5,953 held-out ARKitScenes objects, the imputed latent reaches 43.13% semantic-identity macro accuracy, 22.18 points above the strongest floor. Randomizing the prediction path removes 9.78 points, while substituting matched donor context removes 21.98 points. On 8,570 Sr3D support pairs, the full latent reaches 41.15 AP; identity decoded from the missing-object latent, combined with anchor identity and geometry, reaches 39.37 AP, leaving an unresolved 1.78-point residual. These results reveal a queryable, compositional 3D predictive state: the model completes context-dependent entity content, which downstream computation combines with metric geometry.

</details>


### [56] [Vorch-Director: Interactive World Story Model via Noise-Aware Error Rectification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05776)
*Lisai Zhang,Yidi Wu,Qi Liu,Xin Ma,Yang Ding,Gang Yue,Siqian Yang,Jingyuan Chen,Lin Ma,Yaohui Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Noise-level-aware residual correction (Vorch-Director) improves autoregressive long-horizon audio-visual generation by aligning residual errors with their originating noise levels and integrating task embeddings for unified conditioning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive video-audio generation suffers from drift, over-smoothing, and desynchronization when trained with ground-truth histories but inferred with self-generated histories. Residual-based data augmentation helps, but effectiveness hinges on matching noise levels during residual injection. There is a need for noise-aware, long-horizon conditioning and unified task-aware conditioning.

Method: Vorch-Director introduces a noise-level–aware residual correction strategy that tags each residual with its originating noise level and injects residuals from matched noise regimes during training. It builds on the audio-visual LTX-2 diffusion transformer and adds task embeddings to distinguish historical video, reference images, and target video, enabling unified conditioning for long-horizon generation. It uses a clean conditioning sink and mixed-task training to support multi-shot, multi-subject, and reference-guided generation.

Result: On ST-Bench, and with a new long-horizon AV benchmark, Vorch-Director yields improved stability and audio-visual fidelity compared to strong baselines. Metrics assess quality drift and long-range consistency, with extensive experiments showing reduced identity drift, less over-smoothing, and better synchronization.

Conclusion: Noise-level–aware residual correction combined with unified long-horizon conditioning enables robust, multi-shot, reference-guided audio-visual generation. Vorch-Director improves stability and fidelity for long videos with realistic histories, outperforming strong baselines.

Abstract: Autoregressive continuation provides a natural path toward minute-scale audio-visual generation by repeatedly extending a short-window generator conditioned on previously generated video and audio. However, models are trained on clean ground-truth histories, while inference relies on their own generated histories, where accumulated errors cause identity drift, over-smoothing, and audio-visual desynchronization. Recent methods reduce this mismatch by reusing prediction residuals as synthetic corruption, but we observe that the effectiveness of residual correction critically depends on the flow-matching noise level at which residuals are produced. We propose Vorch-Director, a noise-level-aware residual correction strategy that associates each residual with its originating noise level and injects residuals from matched noise regimes during training. By aligning injected errors with the denoising process, Vorch-Director produces more realistic autoregressive histories while retaining efficient teacher-forcing training. Built on the audio-visual LTX-2 diffusion transformer, Vorch-Director further introduces task embeddings to distinguish historical video, reference images, and target video, enabling unified conditioning for long-horizon generation. Together with a clean conditioning sink and mixed-task training, Vorch-Director supports multi-shot, multi-subject, reference-guided audio-visual long-video generation. We evaluate Vorch-Director on ST-Bench and introduce a new long-horizon audio-visual benchmark with metrics for quality drift and long-range consistency. Extensive experiments demonstrate improved stability and audio-visual fidelity over strong baselines.

</details>


### [57] [Evidence-Driven Dynamic Visual Selector for Efficient Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05780)
*Bo Zhang,Wenxin Wang,Feng Chen,Zhihao Zhang,Zixuan Wang,Changsheng Li,Yinjie Lei*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes EviSelect, a dynamic, evidence-conditioned visual sampler for MLLM-based long-form video understanding that aligns token selection with the target model's internal attention, yielding substantial efficiency gains (≈50% tokens, ≈3.9x speedup) while improving accuracy on three benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods rely on external proxy scorers and static heuristics, causing misalignment with MLLM evidence and failing to handle non-uniform spatiotemporal information; a method that directly leverages internal attention signals and adapts sampling density to information density is needed.

Method: Approximate target MLLM attention with compressed visual inputs and sparse attention; compute three complementary attention components from this prior; train a lightweight stochastic policy (selector) to choose timestamps and adjust local sampling rate and spatial resolution; optimize with GRPO under a joint accuracy-efficiency reward, guided by group-relative comparisons.

Result: Outperforms baselines on three long-form video benchmarks; reduces visual tokens by about 50%; achieves 3.9x end-to-end speedup.

Conclusion: EviSelect demonstrates that evidence-conditioned spatiotemporal sampling improves efficiency without sacrificing accuracy, by aligning selection with the MLLM's internal attention; the approach is effective for long-form video understanding and potentially generalizable.

Abstract: Recent advancements in MLLM-based long-form video understanding have mitigated inference-time computational cost and limited context lengths by selecting query-relevant frames. However, existing approaches predominantly rely on external proxy scorers and rigid heuristic rules, inevitably suffering from misalignment with the target MLLM's intrinsic evidence and failing to accommodate the non-uniform spatiotemporal information density. In this paper, we propose a fine-grained dynamic visual selection framework named EviSelect, grounded in the target MLLM internal attention evidence. Our method efficiently probes visual evidence via sparse prefilling as a structured prior to guide distribution-aware dynamic sampling. Specifically, we efficiently approximate attention maps of the target MLLM using highly compressed visual inputs and sparse attention, well-aligned to the full counterpart. Conditioned on three complementary attention components derived from this prior, we design a lightweight selector that not only precisely locates query-relevant timestamps but also adaptively adjusts the local sampling rate and spatial resolution. To enable evidence-conditioned spatiotemporal sampling, we formulate the selector as a stochastic policy and optimize it via GRPO under a joint accuracy--efficiency reward. By rewarding correct predictions under lower visual cost through group-relative comparisons, our method encourages the policy to allocate computation dynamically according to the information density of each video. Across three long video understanding benchmarks, EviSelect achieves superior performance compared to existing methods while reducing selected visual tokens by about 50\% and achieving a 3.9x end-to-end speedup.

</details>


### [58] [VSMP-IMU: Video-Grounded Semantic Motion Programs for Sensor-Aware Synthetic IMU Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05782)
*Lala Shakti Swarup Ray,Vitor Fortes Rey,Mengxi Liu,Paul Lukowicz,Bo Zhou*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VSMP-IMU is a video-grounded, controllable synthetic IMU data generator using a Structured Semantic Motion Program (SMP) to decouple activity semantics from label-preserving variation, improving HAR performance across datasets and resource settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HAR is limited by scarce labeled sensor data, especially in low-resource, class-imbalanced, and subject-generalization scenarios. Video-driven methods are visually grounded but sensitive to pose-estimation errors; text-driven methods are controllable but loosely grounded in actual activity execution.

Method: From input video, extract and augment a Structured Semantic Motion Program (SMP); use SMP to synthesize motion; convert motion into virtual IMU signals; ground these signals to the target wearable domain; evaluate on five public IMU-HAR datasets with leave-one-person-out experiments.

Result: Average Macro-F1 of 78.33%, outperforming real-only training by 9.77% and the strongest prior synthetic baseline by 4.04%. In low-resource settings, improvements are 18.54% over real-only and >6% over priors. In long-tail, tail-class Macro-F1 improves by 19.86% over Real-only and by 4.76% over SOTA.

Conclusion: Structured video-grounded semantics provide a practical foundation for controllable, wearable-relevant synthetic sensor data generation, enabling better HAR performance under data scarcity and imbalance.

Abstract: Wearable human activity recognition (HAR) is often limited by the scarcity of labeled sensor data, especially in low-resource, class-imbalanced, and subject-generalization settings. Synthetic IMU generation can reduce this dependency and enhance HAR machine learning model's performance, but existing approaches face a trade-off without addressing all factors: video-driven methods are visually grounded but sensitive to pose-estimation errors, while text-driven methods are controllable but often weakly grounded in how activities are actually performed. We present VSMP-IMU, a video-grounded framework for controllable synthetic IMU generation based on a structured Semantic Motion Program (SMP), which separates activity-defining semantics from label-preserving variation. Given an input video, VSMP-IMU extracts and augments an SMP, uses it to synthesize motion, converts the motion into virtual IMU signals, and grounds the resulting signals to the target wearable domain. We evaluate VSMP-IMU against state-of-the-art synthetic data generation methods on five public IMU-HAR datasets under leave-one-person-out evaluation. VSMP-IMU achieves an average Macro-F1 of 78.33%, improving over real-only training by 9.77% and over the strongest prior synthetic baseline by 4.04%. In low-resource settings with reduced training data-samples, it improves over real-only training by 18.54% and over the strongest prior synthetic baselines by more than 6% on average. Under long-tail evaluation in imbalanced datasets, it improves tail-class Macro-F1 by 19.86% over Real-only training and by 4.76% over SOTA. These results show that structured video-grounded semantics provide a practical foundation for controllable, wearable-relevant synthetic sensor data generation.

</details>


### [59] [KVAE: Family of Tokenizers for Multimodal Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05798)
*Andrey Shutkin,Denis Parkhomenko,Ivan Kirillov,Kirill Chernyshev,Kirill Malakhov,Ilia Vasiliev,Ilia Trushkin,Valeriya Kobenko,David Chikovani,Alexander Ivanov,Azat Saginbaev,Egor Silvestrov,Ivan Mikheev,Konstantin Zakharov*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: The paper introduces KVAE tokenizers for audio, image, and video to support text-conditioned latent diffusion models. It claims high-quality reconstructions and competitive generation, with dedicated KVAE-Audio (48 kHz, 64 channels at 50 Hz latent), KVAE-3D (two casual 4x16x16 and 4x8x8 video tokenizers), and KVAE-2D (image tokenizer with 8x compression and 32 channels), outperforming or matching frontier tokenizers; and it provides training details and ablations with publicly available code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokenizers in latent diffusion models are pivotal for learning speed, sample quality, and downstream applications. The paper seeks robust, domain-specific tokenizers for audio, image, and video to improve end-to-end generation performance and openness of the research process.

Method: Proposes a set of KVAE tokenizers aimed at different modalities: KVAE-Audio (continuous full-band 48 kHz, 50 Hz latent with 64 channels); KVAE-3D (two causal video tokenizers for 4x16x16 and 4x8x8 compression); KVAE-2D (image tokenizer with 8x compression, 32 channels). They compare reconstruction and generation with objective metrics (Frechet Distance, CLIP, CLAP, etc.) and subjective evaluations against established tokenizers (Wan-2.2, HunyuanVideo-1.5, FLUX.2, MovieGen, StableAudio, MMAudio). They also provide training details, model selection methods, and ablations; code is released.

Result: KVAE tokenizers achieve reconstruction and generation results that match or surpass frontier open-source tokenizers across objective metrics (e.g., Fréchet-based distances, CLIP/CLAP scores) and subjective evaluations. The approach demonstrates versatility across audio, image, and video and provides explicit design choices validated via ablations.

Conclusion: KVAE tokenizers offer competitive, multi-modal tokenization for latent diffusion models, with publicly shared training details and code enabling community adoption. They show promise as effective replacements or complements to existing tokenizers for text-conditioned generation.

Abstract: Latent diffusion modeling (LDM), a prominent paradigm, utilizes tokenizers to map input signal to compressed representation. This dependency positions tokenizer as an integral part of generation process itself, since it affects learning speed, quality of synthesized samples and lay foundation for later applications. This report presents series of KVAE tokenizers for audio, image and video, all designed for subsequent text-conditioned generation: KVAE-Audio, a continuous full-band 48 kHz tokenizer with a 50 Hz latent of 64 channels; KVAE-3D -- two causal video tokenizers for 4x16x16 and 4x8x8 compression; KVAE-2D, an image model, compressing input by factor of 8 with 32 channels. We demonstrate that reconstruction (PSNR, LPIPS, PESQ, etc.) and generation results on objective (Frechet Distance, CLIP score, CLAP score, etc.) and subjective (side-by-side evaluation) metrics matches or surpasses frontier opensource tokenizers, such as VAEs from Wan-2.2, HunyuanVideo-1.5, FLUX.2, MovieGen, StableAudio and MMAudio. Considering difficulty of development, we share with community training details, model selection method and ablation on design choices. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/kandinskylab/kvae and https://github.com/kandinskylab/kvae-audio.

</details>


### [60] [Vorch-Omni: Multi-Task Orchestration of Sight and Sound](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05803)
*Vorch Team,Xiaoyu Chen,Yang Ding,Cong Han,Menglin Han,Yuxin Hong,Jiebo Hou,Zequn Jie,Xiang Li,Jing Liu,Qi Liu,Yulei Lu,Siyuan Luo,Lin Ma,Xin Ma,Yinlong Qian,Peng Shi,Fang Wan,Siqi Wang,Yaohui Wang,Yaole Wang,Yidi Wu,Siqian Yang,Mingyu Yin,Haoran Yu,Gang Yue,Lisai Zhang,Yuting Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A unified diffusion-based framework (Vorch-Omni) for audio-visual generation that supports arbitrary conditioning and outputs in a single model, enabling over 10 tasks without task-specific architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fragmented task-specific models for diverse audio-visual generation hinder scalability and consistency. A general model must distinguish targets, sources, and references across modalities while minimizing interference.

Method: A single flow-matching diffusion transformer trained with an arbitrary-condition-to-arbitrary-output formulation. Uses token-level conditioning masks and task IDs to mark targets, sources, and references; position types separate temporal context from independent conditions. Employs two conditioning streams: a vision-language pathway interpreting frames with text instructions, and a video VAE encoding conditions into latent tokens. A distributed data pipeline curates temporally aligned AV clips and structured metadata. No architectural changes for different tasks.

Result: Supports over 10 tasks, including text-to-video, text-to-audio-video, image- and reference-conditioned generation, temporal extension, audio-driven generation, video transformation, and audio-visual editing, all within a single model.

Conclusion: Provides a scalable, general-purpose foundation for audio-visual generation and manipulation, reducing the need for task-specific models and enabling flexible conditioning and output configurations across modalities.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative video modeling have enabled diverse generation, reference-based synthesis, extension, and editing, but existing approaches often rely on fragmented task-specific models. A general model must distinguish heterogeneous target, source, and reference signals to determine what to generate, preserve, or use as guidance, while reducing interference among tasks. Joint audio-visual generation further increases this challenge by introducing diverse conditioning and output configurations across modalities. We present Vorch-Omni, a unified multi-task framework for audio-visual synthesis based on an arbitrary-condition-to-arbitrary-output formulation. It flexibly treats video and audio signals as either conditioning inputs or generation targets. Token-level conditioning masks and task identifiers distinguish targets, source content, and references, while position types separate temporal context from independent conditions. To capture semantic and structural information, Vorch-Omni employs complementary visual conditioning pathways: a vision-language model interprets sampled frames with text instructions, and a video VAE encodes conditions into latent tokens for direct guidance. We further build a distributed data pipeline to curate diverse temporally aligned audio-visual clips, generate structured captions and metadata, and balance heterogeneous task distributions. Built on a single flow-matching diffusion transformer without task-specific architectural changes, Vorch-Omni supports over 10 tasks, including text-to-video, text-to-audio-video, image- and reference-conditioned generation, temporal extension, audio-driven generation, video transformation, and audio-visual editing. This unified framework provides a scalable foundation for general-purpose audio-visual generation and manipulation.

</details>


### [61] [Ordered Diffusion for 3D Human Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05804)
*Mattia Masiero,Ilya A. Petrov,Daniel Cremers,Gerard Pons-Moll,Riccardo Marin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Treat 3D human registration as a diffusion-based generative process (ODin) instead of a regression task to handle inherent uncertainty and preserve template semantics; achieves state-of-the-art performance with faster registration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Registration under real-world noise, occlusions, and soft-tissue dynamics leads to ambiguity and biased average solutions when using regression; a probabilistic/generative approach can model multiple plausible alignments.

Method: ODin formulates registration as a 3D diffusion process that generates a point cloud aligned with target geometry while preserving template semantics via consistent point ordering. It uses global, local, and positional conditioning to guide each point to its correct location.

Result: Generative formulation outperforms regression-based baselines, sets a new state-of-the-art surpassing engineered methods, and reduces registration time by ~66%. Pre-trained models and code are released.

Conclusion: Modeling registration as a diffusion-based generative process better handles inherent ambiguity in 3D scans, preserves semantic structure, and achieves faster, more accurate alignments compared to regression approaches.

Abstract: 3D human registration has historically been treated as a regression task, assuming a unique ground-truth alignment exists between the template and an input point cloud. In reality, acquisition noise, occlusions, and unknown soft tissue dynamics introduce inherent ambiguity into human scans. Regression-based methods consequently converge to an average prediction, often failing to represent a plausible geometry. In our work, we embrace such uncertainty by modeling the registration as a distribution of alignments. We propose ODin, which formulates registration as a 3D diffusion process that generates a point cloud aligned with the target geometry while preserving template semantics through consistent point ordering. To achieve this, ODin relies on global, local, and positional conditioning, guiding each point to its correct location. Our experiments demonstrate that such a generative formulation not only outperforms its regression-based baseline, but also establishes a new state of the art, surpassing highly engineered methods while reducing the registration time by two-thirds. Pre-trained models and code are available at https://riccardomarin.github.io/odin/.

</details>


### [62] [STAIL: Semantic Text-Anchored Incremental Learning for Medical Imaging via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05808)
*Songpan Gao,Yajie Zhang,Guanxing Chen,Jiayu Qian,Zhenzhen Liu,Shijun Li,Xiaowei Zhu,Yao Hu,Kay Chen Tan,Yu-An Huang,Shiqi Wang,Zhi-An Huang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: STAIL introduces Semantic Text-Anchored Incremental Learning with an asymmetric consolidation buffer that uses a few image anchors plus extensive textual descriptions, governed by an LLM-derived anchoring mechanism, to mitigate catastrophic forgetting in medical-imaging continual learning while reducing storage and privacy concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Catastrophic forgetting in continual learning for medical imaging is exacerbated by pixel-level rehearsal that is storage-heavy, privacy-sensitive, and insufficiently captures data distributions with small exemplars. A semantically grounded, memory-efficient approach is needed.

Method: STAIL deploys an asymmetric semantic consolidation buffer (SCB) combining a minimal set of image anchors with rich textual descriptions. An LLM-derived Semantic Anchoring Mechanism (LSAM) aligns evolving visual features with frozen language-space priors, constraining plasticity and stabilizing representations at macro and micro levels. The framework is designed as a plug-in to existing baselines.

Result: Evaluated on three heterogeneous medical datasets (fundus, ultrasound, X-ray). STAIL yields average improvements of 2.24% in AAA-AUC for sustained performance and 3.55% in BWT-AUC for reduced forgetting across baselines; code released.

Conclusion: STAIL offers a storage-efficient, semantically grounded continual learning solution for medical imaging, delivering consistent performance gains across modalities and serving as a versatile plug-in for existing IL methods.

Abstract: Deep learning models applied to medical image analysis suffer from severe catastrophic forgetting when continually adapting to new clinical tasks in dynamic environments. Mainstream incremental learning methods typically mitigate this by rehearsing raw historical images. However, this pixel-level rehearsal incurs significant storage overhead, raises privacy concerns, and fails to adequately capture the true data distribution with sparse exemplars. Inspired by human cognitive mechanisms, we propose a novel framework termed Semantic Text-Anchored Incremental Learning (STAIL) for sequential clinical tasks. To overcome the rehearsal bottleneck, STAIL introduces an asymmetric semantic consolidation buffer (SCB). By incorporating a minimal set of image anchors and extensive textual descriptions, the SCB enables dense semantic reconstruction of old tasks at a minimal storage cost. Furthermore, we design an LLM-derived Semantic Anchoring Mechanism (LSAM) that leverages the stable semantic space of frozen large language models as developmental priors. This mechanism explicitly anchors evolving visual features to textual representations, guiding and constraining plasticity and stability at both macroscopic and microscopic levels. Extensive experiments across three heterogeneous medical datasets, covering fundus, ultrasound, and X-ray imaging, demonstrate that STAIL acts as a highly effective plug-and-play module. It comprehensively enhances the performance of various existing baselines, achieving average gains of 2.24\% in AAA-AUC for sustained performance and 3.55\% in BWT-AUC for reduced forgetting. Code is available.

</details>


### [63] [Energy-Guided Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05811)
*Haoyang Tong,Yu He,Fang Li,Lichen Ma,Jingling Fu,Dong Chen,Zhen Chen,Junshi Huang,Jie Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces Energy-Guided Flow Matching (EG-FM), a coarse-to-fine pixel-space generative model that uses a heat-kernel-filtered endpoint and energy-guided scheduling to explicitly control frequency content during trajectory learning, achieving strong FID with fewer epochs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard flow matching uses a fixed endpoint and learns spectral evolution implicitly, which can hinder explicit coarse-to-fine control and convergence in high-dimensional pixel space.

Method: Replace fixed endpoint with a heat-kernel-filtered endpoint enabling a low-to-high-frequency generative trajectory; apply image-specific energy-guided scheduling to adjust the fraction of high-frequency content, thus retargeting velocity in flow matching; no backbone or data adaptation required; scales to 256/512 resolution with minimal overhead; code available.

Result: ImageNet 256x256: FID 1.55 at 200 epochs, 1.45 at 600 epochs; 512x512 adaptation yields FID 1.58 after 40 high-resolution epochs; GenEval 0.85 and DPG-Bench 83.9 for text-to-image transfer.

Conclusion: EG-FM enables explicit coarse-to-fine control in pixel-space diffusion/flow models, improves sample quality with fewer training epochs, and transfers to text-to-image generation without backbone adjustments, indicating robust generalization across settings.

Abstract: Pixel-space generative models bypass lossy latent compression, yet necessitate joint learning of global structure and fine-grained details in a high-dimensional space. Standard flow matching interpolates noise toward a fixed clean-image endpoint, leaving the spectral evolution to be learned implicitly. In this paper, we introduce Energy-Guided Flow Matching(EG-FM) that explicitly models a coarse-to-fine generative trajectory by moving endpoint. Specifically, EG-FM replaces the fixed endpoint with a heat-kernel-filtered endpoint that evolves smoothly from low-frequency image to clean image.The fraction of high-frequency signal in moving endpoint is released by an image-specific energy-guided scheduling, leading to the re-targeting of velocity in flow matching.Our framework requires no adaptation of the backbone and training data, bringing negligible cost on the training and inference stages. In our experiment, EG-FM consistently achieves lower FID on the ImageNet class-conditional image generation task at $256 \times 256$ with fewer epochs, reaching an FID of 1.55 at 200 epochs and 1.45 at 600 epochs. We continue training the generation task on the setting of $512 \times 512$ resolution, yielding a FID of 1.58 after only 40 high-resolution adaptation epochs.Furthermore, we transfer EG-FM on text-to-image generation and achieve 0.85 on GenEval score and 83.9 on DPG-Bench. Code is available at https://github.com/ysng123/EG-FM.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [64] [Agentic Nesting: A New Methodology for Existing Enterprise Application Integration and Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05159)
*Xi Wang,Kun Li,Xianyao Ling,Gang Yin,Liang Zhang,Jiang Wu,Wenbo Lei,Jun Xu,Annie Wang,Fu Zhang,Weizhe Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Agentic Nesting: encapsulating enterprise apps as autonomous AI agents in a hierarchical, conversational integration framework to overcome data silos and high coupling in traditional EAI approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise systems are fragmented across heterogeneous applications; traditional integration approaches (ESB, API gateways, RPA) suffer from coupling, high maintenance, and limited intelligence, necessitating a scalable, AI-enabled integration paradigm.

Method: Wrap legacy applications with digital agent proxies enabling natural-language interaction; organize agents into layered stewardship topologies; central orchestrator handles task decomposition and dynamic dispatch; expose a unified conversational interface for cross-application querying and orchestration; introduces Application-as-Agent and Conversation-as-Integration concepts.

Result: Conceptual framework with potential generalization to heterogeneous system coordination and large-scale data applications; no empirical results reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: Presents a novel integration paradigm leveraging AI agents and conversation-driven orchestration to unify and coordinate enterprise applications, highlighting scalability and applicability and outlining avenues for future experimentation.

Abstract: Enterprise operations extensively rely on multiple heterogeneous business systems and information applications, which also result in severe data silos and process fragmentation. Enterprises have invested considerable financial and material resources in building these applications, however, effectively leveraging and orchestrating them remains a formidable challenge. Conventional approaches to enterprise application integration, encompassing middleware architectures such as Enterprise Service Bus (ESB), API gateway infrastructures, and Robotic Process Automation (RPA), suffer from inherent limitations like high architectural coupling, escalating operation and maintenance costs, and limited intelligence capabilities. This paper proposes Agentic Nesting, a multi-agent collaboration framework in which existing enterprise applications are encapsulated as autonomous AI agents within a hierarchically nested structure. Rather than flat interconnection, agents are organized into layered stewardship topologies that mirror the compositional complexity of enterprise ecosystems. The framework extracts a digital agent proxy from each legacy application to enable natural-language interaction and autonomous manipulation, coordinates multiple agents through a central orchestrator for task decomposition and dynamic dispatching, and exposes a unified conversational interface for cross-application querying and process orchestration. The main contributions of this paper are the proposition of the "Application-as-Agent" integration paradigm and the "Conversation-as-Integration" interaction philosophy, together with an exploration of the generalization potential of this methodology in scenarios encompassing heterogeneous system coordination, and large-scale data applications.

</details>


### [65] [The Ignition Index: Measuring Global Workspace Dynamics in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05160)
*Saman Rahbar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Ignition Index (I) is a validated scalar metric that quantifies GWT-like ignition in transformer language models by fitting a four-parameter sigmoid to per-layer probe accuracy across input strengths, extracting a steepness beta-hat. High beta-hat indicates abrupt ignition-like transitions; low indicates graded build-up. Across 11 models in five architectures, the metric shows strong selectivity for genuine linguistic structure over spurious probes (9.6×, p<0.001). Key findings: feedforward transformers exceed SSMs in beta-hat; some models show workspace-like, iteration-axis ignition; a PELT-detected phase transition precedes induction-head formation in Pythia-410M. Hypotheses linking ignition to scale or signal strength were not supported. Provides a validated link between GWT dynamics and mechanistic interpretability; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To operationalize Global Workspace Theory within transformer models by creating a quantitative, comparative measure of ignition-like dynamics. The goal is to bridge high-level dynamical predictions with practical interpretability and architecture-level differences.

Method: Fit a four-parameter sigmoid to per-layer linear probe accuracy as a function of input signal strength, across layers. Extract the steepness parameter (beta-hat) as the Ignition Index. Use shuffled-label controls to assess selectivity for linguistic structure. Compare across 11 models from five architecture families. Analyze iteration versus depth axes in recurrent networks. Use PELT to detect phase transitions during training. Provide code for replication.

Result: 9.6-fold selectivity for genuine linguistic structure over spurious probes (p<0.001). Feedforward transformers show ~89% higher aggregate beta-hat than SSMs (p<1e-13, d=0.52); Mamba exhibits near-linear profiles (absent ignition). Huginn-3.5B has higher ignition along iteration axis than depth axis. Pythia-410M shows PELT-detected phase transition at training step 256 (+67%), preceding induction-head formation. Hypotheses linking ignition to scale or signal strength not confirmed. The Ignition Index provides a validated quantitative bridge between GWT and mechanistic interpretability with strong architecture-level discriminability.

Conclusion: The Ignition Index validates a quantitative link between GWT-inspired ignition dynamics and model interpretability, enabling robust cross-architecture comparisons and revealing architecture-level dissociations in ignition-like behavior. The work yields high selectivity and discriminability not previously characterized in scaling studies, and provides code for replication.

Abstract: We introduce the Ignition Index (I), a validated scalar metric that operationalizes Global Workspace Theory's (GWT) all-or-none ignition prediction in transformer language models. The metric fits a four-parameter sigmoid to per-layer linear probe accuracy as a function of input signal strength, extracting steepness parameter beta-hat: high values indicate abrupt, ignition-like transitions; low values indicate graded build-up. Across 11 models spanning five architecture families, shuffled-label controls demonstrate 9.6-fold selectivity for genuine linguistic structure over spurious probe capacity (p < 0.001, Mann-Whitney U-test). We find: (1) Feedforward transformers exceed SSMs by 89% in aggregate beta-hat (p < 1e-13, Cohen's d = 0.52), with Mamba exhibiting near-linear profiles consistent with absent global broadcast. (2) Huginn-3.5B exhibits 2.12-fold higher ignition along its iteration axis than its depth axis, demonstrating that recurrent architectures manifest workspace-like transitions along the recurrence dimension. (3) Pythia-410M shows a PELT-detected phase transition at training step 256 (+67%), preceding induction-head formation. (4) Hypotheses linking ignition to model scale and signal strength were not confirmed, suggesting transformer architectures may saturate available ignition mechanisms. The Ignition Index provides the first validated quantitative bridge between GWT's dynamical predictions and mechanistic interpretability, with 9.6-fold measurement selectivity and architecture-level discriminability not previously characterized in the scaling literature. Code: https://github.com/saman-rahbar/ignition-index

</details>


### [66] [Woodpecker Distillation: Weak Models Diagnose Reasoning Bugs in Strong Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05168)
*Dayu Wang,Jiaye Yang,Weikang Li,Jiahui Liang,Yang Li,Deguo Xia,Jizhou Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Patch-based corrections steer LLMs' reasoning by shaping future steps rather than copying the patch text; Woodpecker Distillation uses contrastive local interventions to train a strong model via a corrective teacher distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Failures in reasoning often stem from localized intermediate-step bugs. While patches can fix outcomes, direct fine-tuning on patches is ineffective; the key signal is how such interventions alter the model's future reasoning distribution.

Method: At each prefix, compare successful and unsuccessful weak-model patches (contrastive patches). Construct a corrective teacher distribution from the induced future token predictions, then distill this signal into the strong model (Woodpecker Distillation).

Result: On mathematical reasoning benchmarks, Woodpecker Distillation consistently improves strong-model performance and surpasses direct imitation baselines.

Conclusion: Weak-to-strong distillation using contrastive local interventions reshapes future reasoning trajectories and is an effective approach to enhance reasoning in large language models.

Abstract: Large language models often fail on reasoning tasks despite possessing the capability to solve them. We argue that many such failures arise from localized reasoning bugs in intermediate steps rather than from global incompetence. We show that these bugs are frequently repairable: inserting a short patch generated by a weak probe model after the same strong-model reasoning prefix can redirect the trajectory toward a correct solution.
  However, this corrective effect is not reliably internalized by directly fine-tuning on weak patches or repaired trajectories, suggesting that the useful signal lies not in the intervention text itself, but in how it reshapes the model's future reasoning distribution. We therefore propose Woodpecker Distillation, a weak-to-strong training framework that learns from contrastive local interventions. Our method contrasts successful and unsuccessful weak-model patches at the same prefix, constructs a corrective teacher distribution from their induced future token predictions, and distills this signal into the strong model.
  Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that Woodpecker Distillation consistently improves strong-model performance and outperforms direct imitation baselines.

</details>


### [67] [From Continuous Predictors to Clinical Thresholds: Early Evidence on Performance Trade-offs of Guideline-Based Categorisation for Ischaemic Stroke Outcome Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05203)
*Esra Zihni,Katryna Cisek,Hamzah Ziadeh,Hendrik Knoche,Robert Mikulik,John D. Kelleher*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Discretizing continuous predictors into guideline-based categories preserves predictive performance in most cohorts for 90-day stroke outcome, with consistent feature rankings; categorisation is viable but may reduce accuracy in at least one cohort.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To align model explanations with clinicians' reasoning by using clinically meaningful categorical encodings and to test whether guideline-aligned thresholds can replace continuous predictors without sacrificing performance.

Method: Comparison of standard gradient-boosted models (using continuous predictors) with fully categorised gradient-boosted models that apply stroke guideline-aligned, treatment-specific thresholds to predictors across a multi-centre European registry, evaluated across three treatment cohorts.

Result: Fully categorised models are statistically indistinguishable from continuous models in two cohorts; one cohort shows a significant drop in predictive accuracy. Global feature importance rankings remain largely consistent across encodings.

Conclusion: Guideline-based categorisation is a viable design choice for stroke-outcome models, preserving the core prognostic hierarchy and aiding clinician-aligned explanations, though performance may vary by cohort.

Abstract: Machine learning models achieve strong predictive accuracy for 90-day outcome prediction in acute ischaemic stroke, yet clinical adoption is limited by the misalignment of model explanations with clinicians' reasoning. Motivated by a clinician user study calling for clinical guideline-aligned cut-offs, we ask whether continuous predictors can be replaced by clinically informed categorical encodings without sacrificing performance. On a multi-centre European registry stratified into three treatment cohorts, we compare standard and fully categorised gradient-boosted models, the latter using stroke guideline-aligned, treatment-specific thresholds. The fully categorised models are statistically indistinguishable from their continuous counterparts in two of the treatment cohorts, with a significant drop in predictive accuracy in one cohort. Global feature importance rankings remain consistent, suggesting that discretising continuous predictors into guideline-based categories preserves the core hierarchy of prognostic factors across all treatment groups. Guideline-based categorisation is thus a viable design choice for stroke-outcome models.

</details>


### [68] [SkillTrace: Multi-Trace Provenance Auditing for LLM-Agent Skill Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05204)
*Jialuo Chen,Minghe Wang,Lingqi Jiang,Jianan Ma,Xinhao Deng,Xiaohu Du,Ruixiao Lin,Yunhao Feng,Linkang Du,Jingyi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SKILLTRACE is a multi-trace provenance auditing framework for LLM-agent skill reuse that uses Expression, Implementation, and Operational traces, with an operational graph (SOG) to capture activation, procedure, and resource flow. It audits reuse by deterministic trace comparisons against same-function negatives. On SKILLTRACE-BENCH it achieves AUROC 0.938 and F1 0.898; a large wild audit shows actionable reuse review queues beyond repo baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reuse of LLM-agent skills is increasingly distributed across text, code, and operational structure. Existing detectors focus on single modalities or whole-package similarity and can miss reuse when only part of a skill is reused. A structured, multi-trace provenance framework is needed to reliably detect cross-modal reuse.

Method: Extract three provenance traces: Expression, Implementation, and Operational. Represent Operational Trace as a Skill Operational Graph (SOG) capturing activation, procedure, and resource-flow. An LLM assists only during ingestion for trace extraction; at audit time SKILLTRACE compares cached traces deterministically, calibrates each trace against same-function strict negatives, and reports which trace supports a reuse decision.

Result: Empirical evaluation on SKILLTRACE-BENCH with 820 transformed positives and 751 negatives yields AUROC 0.938 and F1 0.898. A 36,446-skill wild audit demonstrates trace-attributed evidence yields actionable reuse review queues beyond repository-level baselines.

Conclusion: Trace-based provenance auditing provides effective, actionable evidence for LLM-agent skill reuse and supports improved governance in skill marketplaces. SKILLTRACE demonstrates high detection performance and practical applicability in large-scale audits.

Abstract: LLM-agent ecosystems are rapidly growing around reusable skills: mixed-modality packages of metadata, natural-language instructions, code, tools, references, and operational workflows. As skills become marketplace artifacts, auditing their reuse is no longer the same problem as ordinary code clone detection. Existing detectors target single-modality source code or whole-package similarity, yet skill reuse evidence is distributed across authored text, implementation fragments, and operational structure. As a result, they can miss reuse that preserves only one part of a skill. We present SKILLTRACE, a multi-trace provenance auditing framework for LLM-agent skill reuse. SKILLTRACE extracts three provenance traces: Expression, Implementation, and Operational. It represents the Operational Trace as a Skill Operational Graph (SOG) that captures activation, procedure, and resource-flow structure. An LLM assists only the Operational-trace extraction, once at ingestion; at audit time SKILLTRACE compares cached traces deterministically, calibrates each trace against same-function strict negatives, and reports which trace supports a reuse decision. On SKILLTRACE-BENCH, with 820 transformed reuse positives over 100 marketplace anchors and 751 negative controls, SKILLTRACE achieves AUROC 0.938 and F1 0.898. A 36,446-skill wild audit further shows that trace-attributed evidence surfaces actionable reuse review queues beyond repository-level baselines.

</details>


### [69] [Abstract Event Causal Rules: Induction and Application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05205)
*Ziwei Zheng,Peiqiong Chen,Bang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Abstract Event Causal Rule (AECR) abstracts concrete cause-effect pairs into generalized relation-level causal logic to boost generalization in event causal reasoning and prediction, via a Concrete-to-Abstract Causal Induction (CACI) system and an Abstract Rule-Guided Causal Attention Encoder (AR-GCAE).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instance-level causal pairs overfit to frequent patterns and struggle with long-tail and unseen event combinations. There is a need for transferable, abstract causal knowledge to support risk early warning, decision making, and narrative comprehension across diverse event scenarios.

Method: Introduce AECR as a relation-level abstraction; design a multi-agent Concrete-to-Abstract Causal Induction (CACI) system with similarity-constrained clustering to distill AECRs from noisy data, yielding two AECR knowledge bases. Develop Abstract Rule-Guided Causal Attention Encoder (AR-GCAE) to inject AECRs into causality Graph Event Prediction (CGEP) via rule-guided attention and gated representation fusion.

Result: AECRs strengthen generalization in event causal reasoning and deliver consistent performance gains in CGEP, with the largest improvements on rare and unseen event samples.

Conclusion: Abstract causal knowledge in the form of AECRs enhances robustness and generalization for event-centric analytics; the CAC I + AR-GCAE framework demonstrates practical utility in improving predictions for challenging, unseen event combinations.

Abstract: Event-centric intelligent analytical systems heavily depend on explicit causal event knowledge for risk early warning, decision-making support and narrative comprehension. Nevertheless, existing instance-level causal pairs suffer severe generalization deficits on low-frequency long-tail and unseen event combinations. To address this limitation, this work proposes Abstract Event Causal Rule (AECR), a novel relation-level causal abstraction paradigm that transforms concrete cause-effect pairs into generalized abstract causal logic while retaining their intrinsic causal relationships. We design a multi-agent Concrete-to-Abstract Causal Induction (CACI) system coupled with similarity-constrained clustering to distill trustworthy AECRs from noisy raw causal data, based on which two complete AECR knowledge bases are built. To validate the practical utility of abstract causal knowledge, we propose an Abstract Rule-Guided Causal Attention Encoder (AR-GCAE), which injects the retrieved AECRs into the causality Graph Event Prediction (CGEP) benchmark task via rule-guided attention layers and gated representation fusion. Quantitative experimental results reveal that applying AECRs substantially strengthens the generalization capacity of event causal reasoning and brings consistent performance improvements to event prediction, with the most prominent gains observed on rare and unseen event samples.

</details>


### [70] [Otter: A Time-Aware, History-Conditioned Human Chess AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05206)
*Tarun Kumar S*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Otter is a time-aware, sequential chess model with 15.3M parameters that predicts human moves by conditioning on recent move history and clock pressure. Trained on 6.1B positions from 117M Lichess rapid games on a single T4 GPU, Otter achieves 55.23% top-1 and 90.95% top-5 move-prediction accuracy, surpassing Maia 2 with fewer parameters and less data; performance peaks at 57.38% in the 1900–1999 Elo bracket. The model suggests that incorporating temporal dynamics and time control improves human-move prediction relative to position-only models and is complemented by publicly released code and training logs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of position-only chess models by capturing temporal dynamics and clock-driven behavior to improve human move-prediction accuracy; leverage large-scale game data to learn time-aware sequential patterns in human play.

Method: Two conditioning signals: (1) move history encoder conditioning on the last 20 moves to capture opening preferences, drift, and intra-game tendencies; (2) a time-control module modulating predictions based on clock pressure. Trained on 6.1B positions from 117M Lichess rapid games over 30 days on a single NVIDIA T4 GPU.

Result: 55.23% top-1 and 90.95% top-5 move-prediction accuracy; Maia 2 was surpassed with fewer parameters and less training data. Across 11 Elo brackets, top-1 accuracy peaks at 57.38% in the 1900–1999 range.

Conclusion: Modeling chess as a time-aware, sequential activity yields more human-accurate move prediction with a smaller model and less data, compared to position-only approaches; extensive data and public release facilitate reproducibility and further research.

Abstract: Otter is a 15.3M-parameter human chess AI that predicts human move selection by modeling play as a time-aware, sequential process rather than treating each position in isolation. It combines two conditioning signals: (1) a move history encoder that conditions predictions on the last 20 moves, capturing opening preferences, positional drift, and intra-game behavioral tendencies; and (2) a time control module that modulates predictions based on clock pressure. Otter is trained on 6.1 billion positions from 117 million Lichess rapid games over 30 days on a single T4 GPU.
  Otter achieves 55.23% top-1 and 90.95% top-5 move-prediction accuracy, surpassing the prior state-of-the-art human chess model, Maia 2, with far fewer parameters and less training data. Across 11 Elo brackets (<1100 to >=2000), accuracy peaks at 57.38% in the 1900-1999 bracket. These results show that modeling chess as a time-aware, sequential activity yields more human-accurate move prediction than position-only approaches, using a smaller model. Code, trained models, and complete training logs are publicly released.

</details>


### [71] [SearchAuditor: Auditing and Attributing Failures in Long-Horizon Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05212)
*Zhixiang Liang,Yifei Liu,Yidan Huang,Haozhe Zhao,Beichen Huang,Jiaqi Wang,Nan Duan,Qiong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SearchAuditBench, a dataset of 1,243 failed deep-search trajectories and SearchAuditor, a multi-perspective auditing framework to localize, attribute, and repair failures. Achieves 32.3% end-to-end pass rate, outperforming baselines (e.g., 26.6% with GPT-5.5).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diagnosing long-horizon web-search failures is hard due to long, noisy traces; human inspection is often infeasible. A standardized benchmark and automated auditor aim to reduce human burden and improve repair of failures.

Method: Curate 1,243 failed trajectories (average 73.1 messages, 65.1K tokens) from eight open-weight models on five deep-search benchmarks; expert annotations for critical error step, root cause, and a reference repair with rubrics. Propose SearchAuditor, a multi-perspective auditing framework using evidence-grounded adjudication to localize, attribute, and repair failures. Evaluate against baselines across frontier models.

Result: Baseline GPT-5.5-like models achieve ~26.6% end-to-end pass on these tasks; SearchAuditor consistently outperforms baselines, reaching 32.3% end-to-end pass and enabling resumption of failed runs via repairs to improve recovery from errors.

Conclusion: The combined benchmark and auditing framework demonstrate feasibility and value of automated failure localization, attribution, and repair in long-horizon search agents, reducing human burdens and improving robustness, though current pass rates remain modest and room for improvement exists.

Abstract: Deep search agents tackle challenging questions through long-horizon web interactions, a process that is both complex and fragile: small reasoning errors may propagate through long, noisy trajectories into fluent but incorrect answers. Diagnosing such failures is difficult, requiring the manual inspection of extremely long execution traces, which could be beyond human capacity. We therefore introduce SearchAuditBench, a benchmark that evaluates whether LLM auditors can localize, attribute, and repair these failures, thereby reducing the human burden. SearchAuditBench comprises 1,243 failed trajectories, averaging 73.1 messages and 65.1K tokens, collected from eight open-weight models on five deep-search benchmarks, each expert-annotated with the critical error step, a search-specific root cause, and a reference repair with grading rubrics. We further propose SearchAuditor, a multi-perspective auditing framework that effectively localizes, attributes, and repairs search-agent failures through evidence-grounded adjudication. Experimental results show that even the strongest baseline, when powered by a frontier model like GPT-5.5, attains only a 26.6% end-to-end pass rate. In contrast, our SearchAuditor consistently outperforms all baselines across different frontier models, achieving an end-to-end pass rate of 32.3%, and resuming failed runs with its repairs enables agents to better recover from errors.

</details>


### [72] [PD-GS: Phoneme-Driven 3DGS for Audio-Driven Talking Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05218)
*Ao Fu,Yi Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PD-GS introduces a phoneme-driven enhancement to 3D Gaussian Splatting for talking-heads by injecting time-aligned phoneme tokens via a Linguistic Fusion Module, trained on monocular video, yielding improved lip geometry and fewer articulation artifacts on HDTF.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) struggles with accurate lip articulation due to reliance on continuous acoustic embeddings; regression biases toward averaged mouth configurations; self-supervised speech encoders lack explicit, frame-aligned linguistic targets for discrete articulatory events.

Method: Obtain time-aligned phoneme tokens from an automatic ASR and forced-alignment pipeline. Use a Linguistic Fusion Module (LFM) that adaptively fuses continuous audio context with discrete phoneme embeddings through a learned gate. Integrate into 3DGS to form PD-GS. Train purely from monocular video using image reconstruction and lip landmark supervision.

Result: On the HDTF dataset, PD-GS achieves the best lip geometry among baselines (LMD 2.66). Qualitatively reduces lip closure violations on challenging phoneme sequences, yielding more linguistically faithful neural avatars.

Conclusion: Phoneme-guided fusion complements continuous audio cues to better capture articulation-critical events in neural avatars, enabling more accurate, linguistically faithful lip motion without requiring multi-view data.

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) enables fast, photorealistic talking-head rendering, yet accurate lip articulation remains elusive: mouth motion is often over-smoothed and may violate hard articulatory constraints such as bilabial closures, producing the notorious ``leaky mouth'' artifact. A key difficulty is that brief, discrete articulatory events are inferred from a continuous acoustic embedding under a regression objective, which biases predictions toward averaged mouth configurations. While modern self-supervised speech encoders provide rich prosodic and phonetic cues, they do not provide an explicit, frame-aligned linguistic target that reliably disambiguates closure-level events. We propose \textbf{Phoneme-Driven Gaussian Splatting (PD-GS)}, which augments a 3DGS talker with time-aligned phoneme tokens obtained from an automatic ASR and forced-alignment pipeline. Our core component, the \textbf{Linguistic Fusion Module (LFM)}, adaptively fuses continuous audio context with discrete phoneme embeddings through a learned gate, allowing the model to preserve smooth audio-driven dynamics while strengthening phoneme guidance on articulation-critical segments. PD-GS is trained purely from monocular video using image reconstruction and lip landmark supervision. On HDTF, PD-GS achieves the best lip geometry among the compared baselines (LMD 2.66) and qualitatively reduces closure violations in challenging phoneme sequences, yielding more linguistically faithful neural avatars.

</details>


### [73] [When Privileged Guidance Misaligns: State-Matched Routing and Contextualized Self-Distillation for Multi-Turn Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05219)
*Junzhuo Liu,Weiwei Li,Jun Ling,Peng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SMRC-SD introduces state-matched routing for on-policy privileged distillation to avoid state-reference mismatch in multi-turn agents, using a state-conditioned teacher context only at matched states, yielding strong improvements over unconditional distillation on ALFWorld and WebShop with large gains for Qwen3-1.7B.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Privileged distillation risks guiding the student with references from states not reachable by the student, causing state-reference mismatch in interactive, multi-turn settings.

Method: At each turn, SMRC-SD checks if the current execution state matches a supported state along the reference trajectory. Distillation is applied only at matched states. For matched states, it builds a state-conditioned teacher context from the successful trajectory to ground supervision in the actually reached state.

Result: SMRC-SD consistently outperforms unconditional successful full-path distillation across ALFWorld and WebShop. With Qwen3-1.7B, task success improves from 0.746 to 0.865 on ALFWorld and from 0.574 to 0.693 on WebShop. Ablations show the importance of controlled routing and state-compatible context.

Conclusion: State-matched routing with contextualized teacher guidance effectively mitigates state-reference mismatch in privileged on-policy distillation and yields notable performance gains across benchmarks.

Abstract: Privileged on-policy distillation provides dense supervision for multi-turn agents by allowing a synchronized teacher to re-score the student's response at every turn with access to training-only references, such as successful trajectories. In interactive environments, however, the student's preceding actions continually change the execution state. As the student takes different actions or completes subgoals in a different order, its rollout may reach states not covered by the reference, making the reference an unreliable source of guidance for the state actually reached. Applying privileged distillation indiscriminately therefore creates state--reference mismatch. This mismatch motivates a central objective: providing privileged reference guidance that remains compatible with the student's current execution state. We introduce State-Matched Routing and Contextualized Self-Distillation (SMRC-SD), which explicitly determines when and how a privileged trajectory should guide an on-policy student. At each turn, SMRC-SD verifies whether the student's current execution state matches a supported state along the reference trajectory. Distillation is applied only at matched states, filtering out turns for which the reference lacks locally compatible guidance. For each matched state, SMRC-SD further constructs state-conditioned teacher context from the successful trajectory, grounding supervision in the state actually reached. Across ALFWorld and WebShop, SMRC-SD consistently outperforms unconditional successful full-path distillation. With Qwen3-1.7B, it improves task success from $0.746$ to $0.865$ on ALFWorld and from $0.574$ to $0.693$ on WebShop. Controlled routing and context ablations support both selecting locally supported turns and constructing state-compatible teacher context as contributors to these gains. Code is available at https://github.com/liujunzhuo/SMRC-SD.

</details>


### [74] [Small Foundation Models of Human Cognition and Behaviour](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05224)
*Nick Oh,Fernand Gobet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: In-distribution, model scale yields little benefit; 0.6B–1B params match a 70B baseline on held-out participants. Out-of-distribution, larger models generalize better to novel task structure. Prompt-channel masking and trial-order permutation show stimuli/feedback content carries most learned information (~75.7% affected when masked); choice history alone cannot explain performance. Small cognitively fine-tuned models may serve as noise-ceiling estimators, but results are bounded by training paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLMs trained on human behavioral data capture genuine task structure or rely on statistical shortcuts, and how scale and diagnostic manipulations affect transfer to new task structure and psychological experiment analogs.

Method: Train fourteen models (135M–14B params) across four architecture families on Psych-101 (10.7M trial-level choices from 160 experiments). Evaluate in-distribution and out-of-distribution performance. Apply four prompt-channel ablations (task instructions, stimuli, outcome feedback, choice history) across 27 experiments and permute trial order to assess information sources and sensitivity to trial sequencing.

Result: In-distribution: scale barely matters; 0.6B–1B parameters match a 70B baseline on held-out participants. Out-of-distribution: larger models show a steeper scaling gradient and superior generalization to novel task structure. Prompt-ablation results: masking stimuli/feedback destroys 75.7% of learned information and drops performance below chance, indicating choice history alone is insufficient. Permutation shows independence in trial-order tasks but sensitivity when prior responses determine sequence.

Conclusion: Cognitively fine-tuned, small-to-medium LLMs can act as noise-ceiling estimators for psychological experiments, but their explanatory power is constrained by training data and paradigms; scaling helps mainly for out-of-distribution generalization, and information extraction relies heavily on stimuli and feedback content rather than mere trial history.

Abstract: Large language models fine-tuned on human behavioural data have emerged as general-purpose cognitive proxies, but the scale this requires, and whether these models process task structure or exploit statistical shortcuts, remain open questions. We train fourteen models from 135M to 14B parameters across four architecture families on Psych-101, a dataset of 10.7 million trial-level choices from 160 experiments. In-distribution, scale barely matters. The models fall within a narrow band, as though against a ceiling, and 0.6B to 1B parameters suffice to match a 70B baseline on held-out participants. Out-of-distribution, that band opens into a markedly steeper scaling gradient, with larger models clearly advantaged in generalisation to novel task structure. To determine what information these models use, we run two diagnostics. We progressively strip four prompt channels -- task instructions, experimental stimuli, outcome feedback, and choice history -- across 27 experiments, and permute trial order.
