Masking the content of stimuli and feedback destroys 75.7% of learned information and pushes models below chance, demonstrating that choice history alone does not account for performance. Permutation reveals invariance on tasks with independent trials but sensitivity where trial order is determined by prior responses. Small cognitively fine-tuned models therefore show promise as noise ceiling estimators for psychological experiments, though their scope remains bounded by the paradigms seen in training.

</details>


### [75] [Project2Task: Graph-Guided Project-Level Planning for Autonomous Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05225)
*Huirui Xu,Runtao Xu,Shuo Ren,Jiajun Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Project2Task is a graph-guided planning layer that decomposes a research project into bounded, dependency-aware tasks with explicit contracts, using a Bernoulli block-model to select portfolio decompositions; coefficients enable executor-agnostic integration and improved downstream task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the shortcomings of single-task AI agents handling long-horizon research projects, which often produce vague task sets and poor coordination. A project-level planner should formulate coherent task portfolios with clear ownership, dependencies, and execution order.

Method: Represent candidate contributions as innovation atoms in a directed lineage graph. Use a lightweight Bernoulli block-model objective to choose among horizontal, vertical, and hybrid portfolio decompositions. Generate bounded tasks with explicit ownership, repair overlaps/missing fields, and emit dependency-aware task contracts detailing objectives, inputs, expected artifacts, evaluation requirements, boundary constraints, dependencies, and execution order. Contracts are designed to be executor-agnostic and integrable with downstream systems.

Result: On a benchmark of ten project briefs yielding ~30 tasks, manuscript-based portfolio evaluation yields an average quality score of 7.15 for Project2Task, compared with 4.58 for the Brief Baseline and 5.31 for the Topic-only Setting. Integrating its contracts with AutoResearchClaw raises average downstream task accuracy from 0.536 to 0.759.

Conclusion: Explicit project-to-task planning improves coherence, reduces redundancy, and produces executable research-task portfolios; contract-based outputs enable smoother integration with downstream research executors.

Abstract: Research agents can increasingly search literature, propose hypotheses, generate code, run experiments, and draft manuscripts from a single topic. However, a research project is not merely a larger task: it is a long-horizon agenda that must be advanced through multiple bounded tasks with distinct but related objectives, parallel alternatives, and dependency-aware sequences. Existing single-task systems often treat the project as one oversized task, produce a flat set of vague or overlapping tasks, or leave task boundaries and execution order to manual coordination. We introduce Project2Task, a graph-guided project-level planning layer for autonomous research. Given a project brief, it represents candidate contributions as innovation atoms and organizes them in a directed lineage graph. A lightweight Bernoulli block-model objective selects among horizontal, vertical, and hybrid portfolio decompositions. Project2Task then generates bounded tasks with explicit contribution ownership, repairs overlaps and missing execution fields, and emits dependency-aware task contracts that specify objectives, inputs, expected artifacts, evaluation requirements, boundary constraints, dependencies, and execution order. The contracts are independent of any particular downstream research executor and support integration of task outputs into a coherent project-level result. On a benchmark of ten project briefs yielding roughly 30 tasks, manuscript-based portfolio evaluation gives Project2Task an average quality score of 7.15, compared with 4.58 for the Brief Baseline and 5.31 for the Topic-only Setting. Integrating its contracts with AutoResearchClaw increases average downstream task accuracy from 0.536 to 0.759. These results demonstrate the value of explicit project-to-task planning for producing coherent, non-redundant, and executable research-task portfolios.

</details>


### [76] [TriQua: Reconciling Granularity and Context in Factuality Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05228)
*Jin Liu,Steffen Thoma,Achim Rettinger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TriQua is a flexible, hierarchical fact decomposition framework for LLM factuality evaluation. It uses simple triples for basic claims and hyperrelational facts with qualifiers for complex claims, coupled with TriQuaScore for factuality quantification and fine-grained, explainable verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The decompose-then-verify paradigm suffers from a trade-off between atomicity and contextual richness. There is a need for a model that preserves necessary context without sacrificing granularity in evaluation.

Method: TriQua decomposes claims into simple standard triples or hyperrelational facts augmented with contextual qualifiers for complex claims. The verification process annotates concrete errors at the level of specific triples and qualifiers, enabling fine-grained explainability. TriQuaScore quantifies factuality for structured fact units.

Result: Empirical results show that TriQuaScore correlates strongly with human factuality scores, TriQua achieves robust decomposition quality, and it outperforms existing decomposition-based frameworks in evidence-based fact verification.

Conclusion: TriQua provides an adaptive, explainable approach to factuality evaluation that preserves essential context, enables precise error localization, and improves verification performance over prior decomposition-based methods.

Abstract: The "decompose-then-verify" paradigm for LLM factuality evaluation faces a fundamental trade-off: atomic facts, i.e., one sentence conveying one unit of information, often omit essential context, while broader statements lack the granularity needed for precise assessment. To address this, we introduce TriQua, a framework that flexibly models facts based on their complexity. Simple claims are extracted as standard triples, while complex claims are represented as hyperrelational facts by attaching auxiliary contextual qualifiers. This adaptive structure preserves the necessary context for accurate retrieval and verification without sacrificing atomicity. Furthermore, TriQua's verification process directly annotates concrete errors within specific triples and qualifiers, providing fine-grained explainability for error detection. Alongside the framework, we propose TriQuaScore to quantify the factuality of these structured fact units. Empirical evaluations show that TriQuaScore strongly aligns with human annotated factuality scores, TriQua achieves robust decomposition quality, and outperforms existing decomposition-based frameworks in evidence-based fact verification.

</details>


### [77] [Coherence-Oriented Dream Scene Visualisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05233)
*Azra Açıl,Simon Colton*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A pipeline that converts dream descriptions into a four-panel, temporally coherent visual sequence via LLM-based segmentation and iterative text-to-image generation, with a regeneration step to ensure fidelity; evaluated on DreamBank using CLIP, DINOv2, and Qwen2-VL for quality, fidelity, and coherence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dream narratives are emotionally intense but hard to communicate; a visualisation tool can render dream content for analysis, memory, and storytelling, bridging NLP and vision for temporal, multi-image scenes.

Method: An LLM is prompted to split a dream description into four chronological parts; a text-to-image model generates images for each part with coherence across the sequence; images not suitably matched to text are regenerated; evaluation on 50 visualisations from DreamBank using objective metrics from CLIP, DINOv2, and Qwen2-VL.

Result: The abstract reports quality, fidelity and coherence results derived from the three vision-language models; specific numeric results are not provided in the abstract.

Conclusion: DSV presents a feasible end-to-end approach to turning dream descriptions into a temporally coherent four-panel visual sequence, with measurable alignment to text and cross-image coherence; the work relies on prompting and model alignment and would benefit from broader evaluation and user studies.

Abstract: Dreams can be emotionally intense but difficult to communicate. We describe the Dream Scene Visualiser (DSV) system which turns written dream descriptions into a temporal sequence of four panel images visualising the dream. This starts with a large language model prompted to split a dream description into four chronological parts. Then a text-to-image model produces images for each part with visual coherence maintained across the sequence, and DSV regenerates any image not suitably matching the text. We evaluate DSV over 50 visualisations from dream descriptions in DreamBank, and report quality, fidelity and coherence results via objective measures employing the CLIP, DINOv2 and Qwen2-VL vision-language models.

</details>


### [78] [Search2Skill: Skill Distillation Beyond Knowledge Boundaries Via Rubric-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05245)
*Muyang Ye,Tian Lan,Feihu Jiang,Yongshi Ye,Wuyunsiqin,Bin Zhu,Qianghuai Jia,Zhao Xu,Weihua Luo,Ye Wang,Jinyang Zhang,Longyue Wang,Lingfeng Bao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Search2Skill identifies capability gaps in an LLM agent, searches external sources to address them, and distills retrieved evidence into reusable, structured skills, achieving consistent gains over baselines and transferring across model scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitation that self-evolving skills learned from an LLM's internal parameters or trajectories are bounded by the model's existing knowledge, by leveraging external sources to capture domain conventions and standard procedures.

Method: A rubric-based reinforcement learning framework that (1) automatically identifies capability gaps, (2) searches external sources to fill those gaps, and (3) distills retrieved evidence into modular, reusable skills. The approach is evaluated across eight expert domains from three benchmarks under streaming and held-out evaluation, and includes analysis showing gains stem from skill abstraction and cross-scale transfer.

Result: Search2Skill consistently outperforms both search-augmented and trajectory-based skill-learning baselines across eight domains and three benchmarks, under both streaming and held-out protocols. Gains are attributed to abstraction of skills rather than raw retrieved evidence, and the learned skills transfer across different model scales.

Conclusion: External-source grounded skill learning enables reusable, transferable skills that extend beyond the model's parametric knowledge, enabling more robust self-evolution of agent capabilities.

Abstract: Reusable skills, which encapsulate the procedural knowledge required to solve real-world professional tasks, offer LLM-based agents a path toward self-evolution in expert domains. Existing self-evolving skill methods construct skills internally from the model's parametric knowledge or trajectories, and are therefore bounded by what the model already knows. However, the domain conventions and standard procedures underlying professional skills often lie beyond this boundary and are hard to elicit from the agent alone. To address this issue, we therefore propose a novel framework, Search2Skill, that automatically identifies the agent's capability gaps, searches external sources to address them, and distills the retrieved evidence into structured, reusable skills. Specifically, Search2Skill is optimized by a rubric-based reinforcement learning scheme that jointly improves when to search, how to search, and how to generate skills. Experiments on eight expert-level domains from three benchmarks show that Search2Skill consistently outperforms both search-augmented and trajectory-based skill-learning baselines under both streaming and held-out evaluation protocols. Further analyses show that the gains arise from skill abstraction rather than raw retrieved evidence, and that the acquired skills transfer across model scales.

</details>


### [79] [LUNAR: Benchmarking Personalized Large Language Models on UNiversal User BehAvioR Logs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05246)
*Jiahao Zhang,Yongzhi Tong,Zelin Fu,Pengde Zhao,Yanmei Jiang,Jiang Feng,Min Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LUNAR is a cross-domain, longitudinal-behavior benchmark for LLM personalization, using a coarse-to-fine synthetic pipeline to simulate app interaction histories across clothing, food, housing, and mobility; it reveals that access to behavioral logs helps but is not sufficient, with evidence selection and privacy as key challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current personalized LLM benchmarks focus on textual personas or isolated signals, failing to evaluate cross-domain personalization grounded in heterogeneous daily-life activities.

Method: Introduce LUNAR, a benchmark that assesses LLMs' ability to personalize responses from longitudinal app interaction histories across daily-life domains. Uses a multi-stage coarse-to-fine synthesis pipeline grounded in real-world behavioral patterns to scale benchmark construction while mitigating data sparsity and privacy concerns.

Result: Fidelity analyses show LUNAR aligns more closely with real behavioral distributions than other synthetic benchmarks. In experiments on 19 mainstream LLMs, access to behavioral logs is necessary but not sufficient for deep personalization; neither more context nor larger models guarantee better performance. Effective personalization requires selecting and integrating relevant evidence across domains; direct retrieval of fine-grained records outperforms compressed memory; stronger personalization may reduce privacy protection.

Conclusion: Evidence selection, cross-domain integration, and privacy control are key challenges for personalized LLMs.

Abstract: Existing personalized LLM benchmarks primarily rely on textual personas or isolated behavioral signals, providing limited evaluation of cross-domain behavioral personalization, where responses must be grounded in heterogeneous daily-life activities. To address this gap, we introduce LUNAR, the first benchmark for evaluating how LLMs personalize responses from longitudinal app interaction histories across universal daily-life domains, including clothing, food, housing, and mobility. To support scalable benchmark construction while mitigating data sparsity and privacy concerns, LUNAR uses a multi-stage coarse-to-fine synthesis pipeline grounded in real-world behavioral patterns. Fidelity analyses show closer alignment with real behavioral distributions than other synthetic benchmarks. Experiments on 19 mainstream LLMs show that access to behavioral logs is necessary but not sufficient for deep personalization: neither more context nor larger models guarantees better performance; effective personalization depends on selecting and integrating relevant evidence across domains. Direct retrieval of fine-grained behavioral records consistently outperforms compressed memory, while stronger personalization can come at the cost of privacy protection. These findings identify evidence selection, cross-domain integration, and privacy control as key challenges for personalized LLMs.

</details>


### [80] [WorldClaw: Agentic 3D Open-World Generation at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05248)
*Chunchao Guo,Jinpeng Li,Yang Li,Zilong Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic, coarse-to-fine 3D world generation (WorldClaw) that ensures global terrain coherence while producing editable, high-quality local content through planning, semantic layouts, reusable assets, and render-based refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-world 3D world generation from text is challenging due to the need to maintain global spatial coherence, rich local content, and assets suitable for downstream editing and reuse.

Method: A planning stage translates text prompts into a structured specification of regions, terrain, assets, materials, and spatial relations. The system builds a globally coherent terrain from semantic layouts, reusable assets, generative/procedural materials, and a region-aware height field. For detail-demanding regions, it generates terrain-conditioned compositions, reconstructs editable textured meshes, and places them on the terrain; render-based agents refine terrain, objects, appearance, and contacts.

Result: WorldClaw produces large-scale scenes with coherent spatial organization, compelling local content, and editable instance-level assets while maintaining a consistent global terrain structure across diverse prompts.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a fully agentic, coarse-to-fine framework for open-world 3D scene generation, achieving global coherence and editable, high-quality content. Further work could address scalability, evaluation, and integration with downstream editing pipelines.

Abstract: Generating large-scale, freely explorable 3D worlds from open-ended text remains challenging because a system must jointly maintain global spatial coherence, rich local content, and explicit assets suitable for downstream editing and reuse. We present WorldClaw, a fully agentic, coarse-to-fine framework for open-world 3D scene generation. Planning agents translate a text prompt into a structured specification of regions, terrain, assets, materials, and spatial relations. WorldClaw then builds a globally coherent terrain foundation from semantic layouts, reusable assets, generative or procedural materials, and a region-aware height field. For detail-demanding regions, it generates terrain-conditioned compositions, reconstructs editable textured meshes, and recovers their placement on the terrain; render-based agents further refine terrain, objects, appearance, and contacts. Across diverse open-world prompts, WorldClaw produces large-scale scenes with coherent spatial organization, visually compelling local content, and editable instance-level assets while preserving a consistent global terrain structure.

</details>


### [81] [Posture and Sustainment Optimization Under Adversarial Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05256)
*Amelie Norris,Alyssa Lee,Natan Vidra,Spurthi Setty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Scenario-weighted adversarially robust posture optimization (CEV and RobustCEV) for PSA using a finite-horizon MDP; shows substantial gains over greedy baselines and robust performance against adaptive threats in Indo-Pacific experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address structural vulnerabilities in current greedy posture allocation which produce geographic under-coverage and high vulnerability to threat targeting; provide a robust, scenario-aware framework for pre-conflict asset placement.

Method: Model PSA as a finite-horizon MDP over assets, theater locations, and time steps. Introduce Composite Expected Value (CEV) optimizer maximizing scenario-weighted expected posture efficiency. Extend to RobustCEV, which iterates against a Bayesian adversary updating its targeting distribution. Validate via three experiments in an Indo-Pacific basing environment (20 assets, 5 locations) with statistical tests (paired t-tests with Bonferroni correction and two-level variance decomposition).

Result: Greedy baseline incurs a 25.1% posture efficiency penalty due to geographic under-coverage and a 57.3% scenario-weighted readiness collapse under a value-correlated threat. CEV recovers up to 19.8% efficiency over greedy when threat has geographic signal; 5–20 scenarios capture most gains. RobustCEV yields up to 158% efficiency relative to a naive optimizer under adaptive, deceptive threat priors. All results are statistically validated, indicating structural benefits rather than sampling artifacts.

Conclusion: Scenario-weighted, adversarially robust posture optimization materially improves PSA performance and resilience to adaptive threats. Demonstrates the value of incorporating scenario diversity and threat dynamics into pre-commitment basing decisions; highlights need for careful scenario design and further exploration of computational trade-offs and real-world applicability.

Abstract: Pre-commitment posture, the assignment of military assets to theater locations before conflict scenarios resolve, is a critical and formally unsolved problem in joint operational planning. Current practice relies on greedy heuristics that maximize value and ignore geographic coverage and are structurally vulnerable to adversaries that target high-strategic value locations. This paper presents a scenario-weighted adversarially robust posture optimization engine for the Posture and sustainability allocation (PSA) problem, modeled as a finite-horizon Markov Decision Process over assets, theater locations, and time steps. We introduce the Composite Expected Value (CEV) optimizer, which places assets by maximizing scenario-weighted expected posture efficiency over a distribution of threat scenarios, and the RobustCEV extension, which iterates against a Bayesian adversary that updates its targeting distribution in response to observed placement. Across three experiments in an Indo-Pacific basing environment with 20 assets and 5 theater locations, we demonstrate that: (1) the greedy baseline incurs a permanent 25.1% posture efficiency penalty due to geographic under-coverage and a 57.3% scenario-weighted readiness collapse under value-correlated adversarial threat; (2) the CEV optimizer recovers up to 19.8% efficiency over greedy when the threat distribution carries a geographic signal, with a curated set of 5 to 20 scenarios sufficient to capture the majority of this gain; and (3) the RobustCEV extension recovers up to 158% efficiency relative to a naive optimizer when an adaptive adversary employs a deceptive threat prior. All findings are validated using paired t-tests with Bonferroni correction and two-level variance decomposition, confirming that the performance gaps reported are structural properties of placement strategies rather than sampling artifacts.

</details>


### [82] [OrchestraBench: Evaluating Multi-Agent Orchestration Failure Modes, Recovery, and Decomposition Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05263)
*Yidian Chen,Yingzi Gu,Natan Vidra,Spurthi Setty,Sharon Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OrchestraBench provides a failure-diagnosis framework for multi-agent pipelines with seed-reproducible failure injection, introducing cascade radius and per-failure-mode recovery; compares routing policies; reveals gaps in safety and containment; across multiple domains, reveals that detection/attribution are key and trusted-state signals influence measured containment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond task-accuracy benchmarks and diagnose failure propagation, routing decisions, and recovery in orchestration pipelines.

Method: Seed-reproducible failure-injection harness over templated enterprise workflows; measure cascade radius and per-failure-mode recovery; compare routing policies with bootstrap CIs and paired tests; 26-case diagnostic; experiments across domains (loan-approval) and tools (Sonnet, Opus, Haiku); use Claude agent on arithmetic dependency chain; MAST modes; blind retry; trusted-state repair ablation.

Result: Adversarial keyword/flag router fails on adversarial cases (0%); intent-reasoning router achieves 100% matching oracle; three failure-handling tiers across five MAST modes: 1.0 tool faults, 0.30 ambiguous delegation, 0.0 latent/semantic; cascade radius grows with depth (0.9 to 4.7 for depths 3-7); blind retry increases detection time; trusted-state drives containment gains; results are controlled-mechanism probes, not domain-workload claims; cross-contexts show shifting absolute rates.

Conclusion: Effective failure diagnosis in multi-agent pipelines requires understanding cascade dynamics, timely detection and attribution, and leveraging trusted-state signals; the proposed framework enables systematic probing independent of domain workloads; future work should emphasize containment strategies and robust routing decisions.

Abstract: Multi-agent orchestration frameworks are moving from demos to production, yet benchmarks typically report task accuracy without diagnosing why a pipeline failed, where a cascade began, or which routing decision caused the breakdown. OrchestraBench evaluates failure, recovery, and decomposition through a controlled, seed-reproducible failure-injection harness over templated enterprise workflows. It introduces cascade radius and per-failure-mode recovery as primary metrics and compares routing policies with bootstrap confidence intervals and paired tests. On a 26-case gold-labelled diagnostic, a keyword/flag router scored 0% on adversarial cases with misleading or missing surface flags, whereas an intent-reasoning model router scored 100%, matching the oracle. Controlled mechanism probes with a real Claude agent over a verifiable arithmetic dependency chain revealed three failure-handling tiers across five MAST modes: tool faults recovered fully (1.0), ambiguous delegation recovered partially (0.30), and three latent or semantic modes never recovered (0.0). This ordering persisted when the computation was reframed as a loan-approval workflow and across Sonnet, Opus, and Haiku, although absolute rates shifted with context. Blind retry reproduced latent faults and increased time to detection, indicating that detection and attribution are necessary for containment. Cascade radius increased with pipeline depth (mean 0.9 to 4.7 across depths 3-7). A trusted-state repair ablation showed that apparent containment gains primarily came from the trusted-state signal rather than autonomous detection. These results are controlled-chain mechanism probes, not domain-workload claims.

</details>


### [83] [Agentic self-driving microscopy benchmarks support qualification but do not necessarily generalize to unseen tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05266)
*Nathan S Johnson,Ian Abshire*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A benchmark and trace-logging framework assesses how architectural choices in agentic microscopy controllers affect performance and reveals that surrogates cannot predict unseen-task performance; benchmarks help for qualification and comparison but not a global configuration model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the nascent field of agentic control of physical infrastructure by systematically evaluating how design choices (LLMs, agent count, graph topology, RAG, and context) influence practical microscopy tasks, and to understand generalization to new tasks.

Method: Developed a benchmark and trace-logging framework; evaluated 1-, 2-, and 3-agent graph topologies across 5 LLMs with RAG and context variations; tested 53 microscopy tasks; analyzed 105 configurations, 1,949 test runs, and 49,109 RAG retrievals; compared latency, token usage, cost, and failure modes; attempted surrogate modeling of performance.

Result: Significant differences in latency, token use, cost, and failure modes across configurations; surrogate models trained on architecture and test results did not reliably predict performance on unseen tasks; benchmarks are useful for qualification, regression testing, diagnosis, and direct comparison but lack a task-independent global configuration model.

Conclusion: Current heterogeneous benchmarks support task-specific evaluation but do not enable a universal configuration model for unseen microscopy tasks. This highlights a need for more standardized, generalizable predictors or frameworks to guide architecture choices in agentic control of physical instrumentation.

Abstract: Large language model agents are increasingly being developed to control a wide range of scientific characterization tools including microscopes and synchrotron beamlines. Research into agentic control of physical infrastructure is nascent and there are few well-established paradigms for how to engineer an agentic system. There are many choices to make when designing a microscopy agent, including the choice of LLM, the number of agents to use, agent responsibilities and delegation rules, retrieval-augmented generation parameters, and more. When designing and optimizing an agentic microscope controller, researchers not only want to ensure that the agent can correctly perform known tasks but also that the agent can generalize to new tasks that it has not encountered before. In this study, we develop a benchmark and trace-logging framework that reveals a) how different choices of agent architecture impact performance at microscopy tasks and b) the limitations of benchmarks for predicting if a particular agent will perform well on unseen microscopy tasks. The framework was used to evaluate one-, two-, and three-agent graph topologies, five LLMs, RAG and context parameters, and operational constraints across 53 microscopy benchmark tests. In total, 105 agent configurations, 1,949 individual test runs, and 49,109 RAG retrievals were recorded. Direct comparisons showed clear differences in latency, token use, cost, and failure mode between configurations. However, surrogate models trained on agent architecture and test results did not reliably predict an agent's performance on new, unseen tasks. These results show that these benchmarks are useful for qualification, regression testing, diagnosis, and direct comparison, but the current heterogeneous test suite does not support a task-independent global configuration model.

</details>


### [84] [CASCADE: An Agentic Regulatory Network Framework for Patient-Data-Validated Downstream Perturbation Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05359)
*Jose A. Bird*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CASCADE predicts downstream transcriptional effects of gene perturbations from ARACNe networks and validates directionality using copy-number amplification as a dosage proxy, across TCGA and METABRIC data. Results show gene-specific, not universal, direction-calling accuracy with strong MYC concordance; LLM-grounding of CASCADE queries achieves moderate exact-match rates but exhibits systematic perturbation-type errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether an agentic framework (CASCADE) can predict not just whether a gene is affected but the direction (up/down) of transcriptional changes due to perturbations, using dosage proxies in real tumor data; evaluate generalizability across multiple genes and datasets; explore integration with LLMs for NL-to-tool grounding.

Method: Leverage precomputed ARACNe regulatory networks exposed via MCP. CASCADE predicts knockdown-like effects; validate directionality using focal-gene copy-number amplifications as a proxy for knockdown, comparing predicted vs actual expression changes in TCGA tumors (BRCA, COAD, STAD) and METABRIC; use Fisher's exact test against MSigDB baselines; extend to 15 additional genes to assess gene-specificity; test an LLM-based agent for exact grounding of NL requests into CASCADE tool calls across 35 queries.

Result: MYC: predicted knockdown targets show strong concordance with amplified-vs-non-amplified tumor expression across BRCA (90.0%), COAD (72.0%), STAD (85.7%), all p<0.0013; replication in METABRIC (87.2%). CASCADE's accuracy does not exceed MSigDB-derived knowledge but surpasses naive uniform guesses; 15 additional genes show gene-specific performance (proliferation regulators largely replicate; lineage-identity TFs and CCND2 fail). LLM grounding: local model 71.4% exact match (85.7% for larger model); some schema/gene-alias issues fixed by scale/server corrections; both models tend to predict the wrong perturbation type on ambiguous queries, a fixed trigger not encountered.

Conclusion: Directionality-based predictions by CASCADE are gene-specific with meaningful concordance for certain oncogenic regulators (not universal), suggesting utility for perturbation-effect forecasting but with limitations across gene classes. LLM-grounding can assist NL-to-tool interaction but current models exhibit systematic perturbation-type errors in ambiguous cases; improvements may require targeted fixes and larger-scale deployment.

Abstract: CASCADE is an agentic framework that predicts downstream transcriptional effects of gene perturbation from precomputed
  ARACNe regulatory networks, exposed via MCP. Prior work validates such tools by checking whether predicted genes are
  known cancer genes (membership); we instead test whether the predicted direction of change matches reality, using
  focal-gene copy-number amplification as a dosage-based proxy for the inverse of knockdown against real TCGA patient
  tumor data.
  For MYC, CASCADE's predicted knockdown targets show strong concordance with real amplified-vs-non-amplified tumor
  expression across three cancer types (BRCA: 90.0%, COAD: 72.0%, STAD: 85.7%; all p<0.0013), well above permutation
  baselines, surviving a PAM50 subtype control and replicating in an independent cohort (METABRIC, 87.2%). Compared
  against curated MSigDB gene-set baselines via Fisher's exact test, CASCADE's accuracy is not shown to exceed existing
  public knowledge of MYC- or E2F-driven biology, though its gene-specific direction-calling clearly outperforms a naive
  uniform guess.
  Extending to fifteen additional genes, validation proves gene-specific rather than universal: proliferation-machinery
  regulators mostly replicate, while lineage-identity transcription factors and one cyclin-D paralog (CCND2)
  consistently fail, a pattern we discuss as a hedged, post-hoc hypothesis.
  We separately benchmark whether an LLM-based agent correctly grounds natural-language requests into CASCADE's real MCP
  tool calls. Across 35 queries, a documented local model reaches 71.4% exact match (85.7% for a larger model); schema
  and gene-alias failures are resolved by scale or server-side correction, but both models confidently default to the
  wrong perturbation type on ambiguous queries, a failure a targeted fix could not resolve because its trigger condition
  never occurs.

</details>


### [85] [Counterfactual Analysis via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05367)
*Zonghao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GPT-3.5 with prompt engineering can enhance counterfactual ROI predictions in online lending and generate coherent counterfactuals, approaching traditional models in predictive accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether large language models (LLMs) can be effectively used for counterfactual analysis to inform lending decisions and interest-rate policy.

Method: Compare GPT-3.5 predictions with advanced ML models; apply prompt engineering to improve GPT performance; measure R-squared; generate counterfactual ROIs under alternative interest rates; assess coherence and causal reasoning in GPT outputs.

Result: Prompt engineering increases GPT's R^2 from 1.97% to 2.84%, nearing gradient-boosted regression at 3.48%; GPT can generate counterfactual ROIs with logical coherence and causal reasoning.

Conclusion: LLMs have potential as valuable tools for counterfactual analysis in online lending and can extend to broader predictive and decision-making tasks.

Abstract: Counterfactual analysis aims to predict potential outcomes under hypothetical scenarios, offering valuable insights for decision-making. This paper investigates the application of large language models (LLMs), specifically the GPT-3.5 model, for counterfactual analysis. We focus on the online lending context, where the counterfactual return on investment (ROI) is crucial for evaluating different interest rate schemes. We begin by assessing the predictive performance of GPT and comparing it with advanced machine learning algorithms. The results show that prompt engineering can significantly enhance GPT's predictions, with the R-squared increasing from 1.97% to 2.84%, closely approaching the 3.48% achieved by gradient-boosted regression. Subsequently, we utilize GPT to generate counterfactual ROIs under a set of alternative interest rates. GPT exhibits logical coherence and causal reasoning in its responses. The findings underscore the potential of LLMs as effective tools for counterfactual analysis in online lending, suggesting broader applications for LLMs in various predictive and decision-making contexts.

</details>


### [86] [DoctorAgents: an agentic framework to iteratively refine AutoML pipeline for small clinical temporal data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05375)
*Ruilin Wang,Bo-Hong Wang,Elizabeth Kourbatski,Jun Bai,Hegang Chen,Ziyang Song,Gilles Boire,Marie Hudson,Yue Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new framework DoctorAgents uses LLM agents (generation, validation, refinement) to incrementally construct and optimize clinical ML pipelines with textual gradient descent, outperforming AutoML baselines and producing interpretable representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AutoML for small clinical datasets faces scarcity, heterogeneity, and temporal complexity; traditional AutoML relies on brute-force search and lacks explicit reasoning/memory, hindering reliable deployment and interpretability.

Method: Architectural design with three specialized LLM agents (generation, validation, refinement) that iteratively build and optimize end-to-end ML pipelines. Natural-language feedback is backpropagated via textual gradient descent to update components without exhaustive search.

Result: DoctorAgents consistently outperforms established AutoML baselines across diverse clinical tasks and yields more interpretable, task-specific representations.

Conclusion: Reasoning-driven, memory-enabled AutoML via agentic LLMs offers a promising path for reliable, interpretable ML pipelines on small clinical data; further work should address limitations of LLM reliability, data shift, and reproducibility.

Abstract: Clinical machine learning (ML) has the potential to support high-stakes medical decision-making, but reliable deployment is often constrained by scarce, heterogeneous, and temporal complexity. Developing effective ML pipelines for such data remains time-consuming and error-prone, while existing automated machine learning (AutoML) systems only partially address this challenge because they largely rely on brute-force search over predefined spaces and lack explicit reasoning and memory. We therefore reformulate AutoML for small clinical data from exhaustive search to reasoning-driven refinement. We propose DoctorAgents, an agentic AI framework that autonomously constructs and optimizes end-to-end ML pipelines through specialized large language model (LLM) agents for generation, validation, and refinement. DoctorAgents backpropagates natural-language feedback through textual gradient descent to perform targeted updates without exhaustive search. Experiments across diverse clinical tasks show that DoctorAgents consistently outperforms established AutoML baselines while producing more interpretable task-specific representations.

</details>


### [87] [C$^3$PO: Evaluating Cross-Modal Composition and Counterfactual Performance in Omnimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05381)
*Swapnanil Mukherjee,Agyeya Negi,Tanuja Ganu,Ponnurangam Kumaraguru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C3PO benchmarks cross-modal reasoning robustness in MLLMs via information composition and counterfactual conflict, revealing modality dominance as a key failure mode and emphasizing sustained cross-modal attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address brittleness in cross-modal reasoning due to modality bias in current MLLMs and establish a diagnostic benchmark to pinpoint when and why cross-modal reasoning fails.

Method: Automatic data generation pipeline using 25 logically grounded templates to create 3,404 samples across video, audio, image, and text with paired information composition (IC) and counterfactual conflict (CC) tasks; four-tier design (likely levels of reasoning/structure); attention probes and mid-layer entropy analysis; evaluation of multiple models including Gemini-3.1-Pro and open-source baselines.

Result: Humans achieve 88.64% accuracy; Gemini-3.1-Pro achieves 73.17%; open-source models collapse under conflict; 86–95% of failures due to modality dominance with 87–95% of attention on text; mid-layer attention entropy predicts correctness; 56-point accuracy gap tied to how modalities function structurally in conflict resolution, not simply their combinations.

Conclusion: Multimodal perception alone does not guarantee robust cross-modal reasoning; architectures must enable sustained cross-modal attention to avoid premature commitment to a single modality and conflicts between modalities.

Abstract: Current Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can process diverse sensory inputs, yet their reasoning remains heavily biased toward a dominant modality, resulting in brittle cross-modal reasoning. We introduce C$^3$PO, a benchmark of 3,404 samples spanning video, audio, image, and text, evaluating two abilities: information composition (fusing dispersed evidence) and counterfactual conflict (resolving deliberate contradictions). C$^3$PO's paired IC/CC structure and four-tier design enable targeted diagnosis of when and why cross-modal reasoning fails. Built through a fully automatic pipeline using 25 logically grounded templates, C$^3$PO reveals that while humans achieve 88.64% accuracy, the best model (Gemini-3.1-Pro) reaches only 73.17%, with open-source models collapsing under conflict. Through attention probes, we find 86-95% of failures stem from modality dominance: models commit to one modality while ignoring contradictory evidence, concentrating 87-95% of attention on text. Mid-layer attention entropy predicts correctness-sustained exploration succeeds, premature collapse fails. The 56-point accuracy gap between equally complex templates reveals that performance depends on modalities' structural roles in conflict resolution, not combinations. These findings show multimodal perception does not guarantee robust reasoning; architectures must enable sustained cross-modal attention to avoid premature

</details>


### [88] [Adaptive Arena-based Contestable Argumentative Network-of-Experts for Open-Ended Care Plan Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05391)
*Truong Thanh Hung Nguyen,Hoang-Loc Cao,Phuc Ho,Phuc Truong Loc Nguyen,René Richard,Hung Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CANOE is a multi-agent neuro-symbolic framework for safe, explainable care-plan generation that uses arena-based argumentation and human-in-the-loop contestation, achieving strong clinical correctness in evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monolithic LLM pipelines struggle to provide transparency, safety, and multi-disciplinary synthesis required for care planning; a modular, contestable approach can yield faithful explanations and human oversight.

Method: CANOE comprises five modules: complexity assessment, adaptive team recruitment, role-based argumentative computation via an Arena-based Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Framework (A-QBAF), human-in-the-loop contestation, and care-plan synthesis. Role-specialized agents generate supporting/attacking arguments; conflicts are resolved through arena-based clash resolution; acceptability scores propagate to the final plan; changes trigger deterministic recomputation.

Result: Evaluation on Discharge Me! and MedicalRAG using ROUGE-L, AlignScore, MEDCON F1, FKGL, and LLM-as-a-judge indicates medically fine-tuned models achieve strongest clinical correctness and safety, while CANOE’s argumentative structure provides faithful explanations and human contestability.

Conclusion: CANOE demonstrates that a contestable, argumentation-driven framework can coordinate heterogeneous clinical information into safe, explainable care plans, with strong performance stemming from clinically fine-tuned models and transparent justification for decisions; it enables user-driven edits and deterministic recomputation of plans.

Abstract: Care plan coordination demands synthesizing heterogeneous clinical, functional, and psychosocial information across multiple professional disciplines, where monolithic LLM pipelines cannot perform in a transparent or safe manner. We present CANOE (Contestable Argumentative Network-of-Experts), a multi-agent neuro-symbolic framework that addresses these limitations through five modules: complexity assessment, adaptive team recruitment, role-based argumentative computation via an Arena-based Quantitative Bipolar Argumentation Framework (A-QBAF), human-in-the-loop contestation, and care-plan synthesis. Role-specialized agents generate supporting and attacking arguments for candidate interventions; conflicts are resolved through arena-based clash resolution before acceptability scores propagate across the argumentation graph. Care planners may accept, reject, edit, or add arguments, and the framework will deterministically recompute the final plan. Evaluation on Discharge Me! and MedicalRAG using ROUGE-L, AlignScore, MEDCON F1, FKGL, and LLM-as-a-judge shows that medically fine-tuned models achieve the strongest clinical correctness and safety, while CANOE's argumentative structure provides faithful explanation and human contestability.

</details>


### [89] [Evaluating and Improving Pedagogical Fit in LLM-Based AI Tutors with the Pedagogical Suitability Index](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05411)
*Benjamin Barlog,Hudson Craig,Zedong Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes the Pedagogical Suitability Index (PSI) to quantify and guide the alignment of LLM tutoring with learner readiness and curricular progression, and demonstrates PSI-driven improvements across four tutors on 240 scenarios, with 62 weak cases showing substantial gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classic correctness-focused evaluation misses pedagogical fit. There's a need for a scalable, theory-informed metric that captures how well tutoring responses align with learners' current knowledge, curriculum sequence, and concept introduction timing, and for a feedback mechanism to improve responses.

Method: Define PSI as a composite of six theory-informed sub-scores that assess instructional alignment (learner readiness, curriculum progression, pacing, accuracy, clarity, and scalability). Evaluate four LLM tutors (ChatGPT, Gemini, Gemma4, Qwen3) on 240 scenario-based evaluations using paired standard and defective prompts. Assess baseline PSI, test resilience to prompt perturbations (Delta PSI ≈ -0.002), and apply a PSI-guided regeneration protocol to 62 weak-performing cases. Conduct focused manual evaluation of PSI-selected cases to validate improvements.

Result: Baseline PSI differences among models were modest (PSI range 0.557–0.638). PSI remained largely stable under perturbations (Delta ≈ -0.002), with sub-score trade-offs. PSI-guided feedback improved 51 of 62 cases (82.3%). Manual evaluation supports the instructional relevance of identified weaknesses and the corresponding improvements from PSI-guided regenerations.

Conclusion: Learner- and curriculum-aware alignment can matter more for effective tutoring than model class alone. PSI is measurable and improvable, offering a structured feedback signal to enhance LLM tutoring in classroom-like settings.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as AI tutors, but a correct answer is not always a pedagogically appropriate one. In classroom learning, effective help depends not only on correctness, but also on whether a response matches the learner's current foundation, the course sequence, and the timing of concept introduction. Existing evaluations focus mainly on answer quality, leaving this instructional fit under-measured. We present the Pedagogical Suitability Index (PSI), a composite metric of six theory-informed sub-scores that evaluates how well LLM-generated tutoring responses align with learner readiness and curricular progression, and we further use PSI as a structured feedback signal for response improvement. We evaluate four LLM tutors (ChatGPT, Gemini, Gemma4, and Qwen3) across 240 scenario-based evaluations using paired standard and defective prompts, then apply a PSI-guided regeneration protocol to 62 weak-performing cases. Baseline differences across the four tested models were modest overall (PSI range: 0.557 to 0.638), and open-weight and closed models did not exhibit a clear separation in pedagogical fit. Under the tested prompt perturbations, overall PSI remained largely stable (Delta = -0.002), though sub-score trade-offs emerged. More importantly, PSI-guided feedback substantially improved weak-performing cases: 51 of 62 cases improved (82.3%). Focused manual evaluation of the 62 PSI-selected weak cases provides initial evidence that the identified weaknesses are instructionally meaningful and that many PSI-guided regenerations correspond to human-judged improvement. These results suggest that learner- and curriculum-aware alignment may matter more for effective tutoring than model category alone, and that such alignment is both measurable and improvable.

</details>


### [90] [Negotiating Risk Boundaries in AI for Policing Through Mixed-Stakeholder Deliberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05418)
*Mackenzie Jorgensen,Jo Reilly,Alex Sutherland,Miri Zilka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A mixed-stakeholder workshop evaluating 13 policing AI use cases finds broad openness to AI, with three cases rejected (notably recidivism risk assessment). Incorporating racial equity did not narrow discussions; instead, core questions about tool effectiveness, genuine benefit, and equitable distribution emerged, aligning with a curb-cut effect for inclusive design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically assess how racial bias concerns and stakeholder inclusivity influence decisions about AI adoption in policing, and to identify whether foregrounding racial equity changes deliberation outcomes.

Method: A mixed-stakeholder deliberation workshop with 30 participants (community representatives, police officers, and academics) evaluating 13 AI use cases in policing, focusing on racial bias risks.

Result: Participants were broadly open to AI, rejecting only three use cases, with recidivism risk assessment singled out for rejection. Objections targeted the premise rather than implementation. Foregrounding racial equity did not narrow deliberation; discussions centered on: does the tool work, will it deliver genuine benefit, and will the benefit reach everyone?

Conclusion: Integrating racial bias considerations into early risk-benefit analyses is beneficial and akin to the curb-cut effect; including racial bias lens supports more inclusive evaluation of AI use cases in policing from the outset.

Abstract: AI tools are being increasingly adopted in policing in the UK and worldwide. Racial bias is a known and well-documented risk, yet representatives of affected communities are rarely included in decisions about AI adoption. We present results from a mixed-stakeholder deliberation workshop bringing together 30 community representatives, police officers, and academics to assess the risks of 13 AI use cases in policing, with an explicit focus on racial bias. We found that participants were broadly open to AI adoption, rejecting only three use cases outright, most notably recidivism risk assessment, where objections targeted the premise rather than the implementation. Our analysis reveals that foregrounding racial equity did not narrow the deliberation. Instead, discussions gravitated toward a fundamental set of questions: does this tool actually work, will it deliver genuine benefit, and will that benefit extend to everyone? This integrated reasoning, reminiscent of the curb-cut effect in inclusive design, highlights the benefit of incorporating the racial bias lens into the risk-benefit analysis of AI use cases from the outset.

</details>


### [91] [SCP-NL2TL: Selective Conformal Prediction with Semantic Verification for Natural Language to Temporal Logic Specifications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05439)
*Yixuan Wang,Licheng Luo,Yu Fu,Kaidi Xu,Yue Dong,Mingyu Cai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Selective NL-to-formal-spec translation with conformal risk control and anomaly detection to abstain when the translation is unreliable; demonstrated across STL, LTL, and SpaTiaL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety-critical applications require trustworthy, verifiable translations from natural language to formal specifications. Existing models translate every input, risking unsafe or incorrect specs when the user's intent is unclear.

Method: Introduce two black-box reliability signals: (1) back-translation fidelity (NL → formal → NL) and (2) dispersion of repeated translations under exact semantic equivalence. Combine signals with conformal risk control to decide accept/abstain, with a distribution-free bound on incorrect-acceptance rate. Add a conformal anomaly detector on instruction embeddings to filter out-of-distribution inputs. Framework is language-agnostic across STL, LTL, and SpaTiaL.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows improved translation reliability, robustness to cross-tier shifts, and effective uncertainty-aware abstention across STL, LTL, and SpaTiaL.

Conclusion: The work lays a foundation for trustworthy NL interfaces by enabling models to recognize when generated specifications may be unreliable and abstain accordingly; the framework’s generality across formal languages suggests wide applicability.

Abstract: Translating natural language instructions into machine-interpretable formal specifications enables robots and autonomous systems to plan, reason, and formally verify their behavior. However, existing translation models typically generate a specification for every input, even when the result is unreliable or fails to capture the user's intent, creating risks in safety-critical applications. Inspired by selective conformal prediction, we propose a selective translation framework that not only generates formal specifications but also determines when they can be trusted. Reliability is scored by two complementary black-box signals, the fidelity of the specification back-translated into natural language and the dispersion of repeated translations under exact semantic equivalence, which fail on different errors and jointly separate incorrect translations more sharply than either alone. Conformal risk control calibrates this score into a decision that accepts a specification or abstains, with a distribution-free bound on the rate at which incorrect specifications are accepted for execution, and a conformal anomaly detector on instruction embeddings screens out-of-distribution inputs before any translation is attempted. The proposed framework is general across formal specification languages, with experiments on Signal Temporal Logic (STL), Linear Temporal Logic (LTL), and geometric Spatio-Temporal Logic (SpaTiaL) demonstrating improved translation reliability, robustness under the evaluated cross-tier shifts, and effective uncertainty-aware abstention. This work establishes a foundation for trustworthy natural language interfaces by enabling AI systems to recognize when generated specifications may not be reliable.

</details>


### [92] [Stochasticity Is Not the Hard Part: Reduction and Complexity in Instructional Sequencing over Prerequisite DAGs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05455)
*Zonglin Han,Yichen Chen,Jiawen Jiang,Tongan Shi,Kristian A. Stevens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sequencing concepts with prerequisite dependencies can be treated as a stochastic shortest-path problem that collapses to a deterministic shortest-path on the lattice of prerequisite order ideals, preserving optimal values and actions; however, the problem remains NP-hard in general, with tractable exceptions under structural conditions; a computable diagnostic (mΔ) bounds sequencing value; empirical data show regimes of little to search and regimes requiring targeted search (A*) for hard instances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine when instructional order matters, quantify the cost of finding the best sequence, and connect stochastic learning dynamics to the combinatorial structure of prerequisites.

Method: Theoretically reduce the stochastic learning process to a deterministic shortest-path problem on the lattice of prerequisite order ideals; prove hardness via reduction from feedback arc set in tournaments; identify tractable special cases where transfer preferences are jointly acyclic with prerequisites and where fixed prerequisite width enables polynomial-time dynamic programming; derive a computable diagnostic mΔ; empirically evaluate on a large introductory CS course dataset and construct hard instances to test search algorithms; compare myopic sequencing with exact A* using a consistent heuristic.

Result: Stochasticity can be eliminated exactly, yielding a deterministic SP without loss of optimal value or actions; optimal sequencing is NP-hard even under strong simplifications (no edges, unit costs, binary nonnegative transfer, success prob ≥ 1/2); if transfer preferences are jointly acyclic with prerequisites, any topological order of the residual graph is optimal; fixed prereq width allows polynomial-time DP; mΔ bounds the achievable value in sequencing; empirical data show a “doubly easy” regime with little optimization value and search space, while crafted hard instances reveal large regret for myopic sequencing but linear-state-expansion for exact A* with a consistent heuristic.

Conclusion: The order of instruction is combinatorially hard in general but admits efficient solutions in natural structural regimes; the mΔ diagnostic provides a practical bound to gauge the potential benefit of sequencing, guiding whether to search or rely on simple topological orders; when hard instances occur, targeted search (A*) is effective.

Abstract: When a student must learn concepts connected by prerequisite dependencies, when does the order of instruction matter, and what does it cost to find the best one? We study instructional sequencing as a stochastic shortest-path problem in which attempting a concept succeeds with a state-dependent probability and failure leaves the learner state unchanged. We first prove that this stochasticity can be eliminated exactly: the problem collapses to a deterministic shortest-path problem on the lattice of prerequisite order ideals, preserving optimal values and actions. The collapse removes stochastic complexity but not combinatorial complexity: optimal sequencing remains NP-hard -- via reduction from feedback arc set in tournaments -- even with no prerequisite edges, unit costs, uniform binary nonnegative transfer, and success probabilities at least $1/2$. Hardness is not uniform: when realizable transfer preferences remain jointly acyclic with the prerequisites, any topological order of the residual joint graph is optimal, and fixed prerequisite width yields polynomial-time exact dynamic programming. A computable diagnostic, $mΔ$, bounds the value of sequencing before optimization. On 70,893 interactions from an introductory CS course, the diagnostic certifies a doubly easy regime -- little value to optimize and little space to search -- while constructed transfer instances realize the challenging regime, where myopic sequencing suffers large regret yet exact A* with a consistent heuristic expands only linearly many states on that family.

</details>


### [93] [Recursive Synthesis for Long-Horizon Terminal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05466)
*Zhongzhi Li,Yucheng Shi,Zongxia Li,Ruhan Wang,Anhao Li,Zixun Huang,Junyao Yang,Lei Ke,Ninghao Liu,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RST is a recursive verified synthesis framework that auto-generates long-horizon terminal tasks from seed tasks, enabling scalable task generation and effective model training; achieves ~37.5k tasks at $0.05/task across 15 rounds, improving fine-tuning and PPO performance on Qwen3.5 models, and showing no ceiling after 15 rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-quality long-horizon data is expensive and hard to scale; existing human authoring or LLM generation fails to maintain consistency across tasks; need scalable, verifiable synthesis.

Method: Recursive process: starting from verified seeds, extend reference solutions, realign verifier and instruction to new workflow, validate in fresh sandbox, reuse accepted tasks as seeds; iterate for 15 rounds; measure difficulty metrics; collect rejection-sampled Qwen3.5 trajectories for supervised fine-tuning; train with fine-tuning and PPO.

Result: Generated 37,484 tasks; median reference solution length increases from 67 to 374 lines; median commands from 40 to 244; pass@4 drops from 90% to 2.5%; fine-tuning yields up to +10 points on Terminal benchmarks for Qwen3.5 models; PPO improves to 49.44%, 32.00%, 22.07% on three benchmarks; yields/stability observed across rounds with no ceiling.

Conclusion: RST demonstrates scalable synthesis for long-horizon tasks; synthesis yield and validation rates remain stable as difficulty climbs; process can continue beyond reported scale, enabling scalable data for training terminal agents.

Abstract: High-quality long-horizon training data for terminal agents is expensive to produce, often costing hundreds to thousands of dollars per task, because each task must keep the instruction, environment, reference solution, and verifier mutually consistent. Human authoring does not scale, and direct generation with large language models (LLMs) often breaks these dependencies. We present Recursive Synthetic Terminal Tasks (RST), a recursive verified synthesis framework for constructing long-horizon terminal-agent tasks at scale. Starting from verified seed tasks, RST extends the reference solution, realigns the verifier and instruction to the new workflow, validates the result in a fresh sandbox, and reuses accepted tasks as seeds for subsequent rounds. Across fifteen recursive rounds, RST produces 37,484 synthesized terminal-agent tasks at roughly \$0.05 per task. Task difficulty increases substantially over rounds: the median reference solution grows from 67 to 374 lines, the median number of executed commands grows from 40 to 244, and DeepSeek-V4-Pro pass@4 drops from 90\% at $R_1$ to 2.5\% at $R_{15}$. To demonstrate training utility, we collect rejection-sampled Qwen3.5 trajectories on the synthesized tasks and use them for supervised fine-tuning. Fine-tuning on these trajectories improves Qwen3.5-27B and Qwen3.5-122B-A10B by up to 10 points on Terminal-Bench~2, Terminal-Bench Hard, and Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, while agentic PPO lifts Qwen3.5-27B to 49.44\%, 32.00\%, and 22.07\% on the three benchmarks, corresponding to relative gains of 20.0\%, 41.2\%, and 21.9\% over the base model. Moreover, after 15 rounds, the recursion shows no ceiling: synthesis yield and validation rates remain stable as difficulty keeps climbing, indicating that the process can continue well beyond the scale reported here.

</details>


### [94] [Innovation-Residual Auditing of Autonomous Analysis Agents: Localization, Detection Limits, Error Control, and Identifiability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05490)
*Ahmed Hassoon,Mark Dredze*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework for auditing autonomous data-analysis pipelines by scoring operations against sound analyses to localize errors; it analyzes how score design affects error localization, provides false-positive control under exchangeability, and identifies fundamental limits tied to representation dimensionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As autonomous analyses become common, it is crucial to understand when and how audits can identify the operation that introduced an error, and how choices in scoring and comparison windows affect reliability and guarantees.

Method: Proposes scoring schemes that compare each operation to preceding steps or to reconstructed versions of the intended analysis; derives probabilistic guarantees for falsely flagging correct operations under exchangeable, sound analyses; analyzes how error propagation and the length of reconstruction affect localization; derives limits on detectability; evaluates robustness to imperfect models and selection bias.

Result: Characterizes how the chosen scoring horizon spreads a single error across operations; provides procedures to bound the false-positive rate under exchangeability; quantifies the effect of model misspecification and content-based selection on guarantees; establishes a fundamental limit on detectable error magnitude determined by representation dimensionality rather than data volume.

Conclusion: Auditing automated analyses is feasible with controlled false positives, but there is a fundamental bound on what can be attributed to errors: very small mistakes may be indistinguishable from normal variation, and the power of audits is constrained by the representation size. Larger representation spaces improve detectability more than simply increasing data.

Abstract: Autonomous agents now carry out entire data analyses, selecting cohorts, joining tables, and fitting models with little step-by-step supervision. When such an analysis turns out to be wrong, someone must determine which operation caused it. A recent approach does this without any labelled mistakes, learning instead from analyses known to be sound and flagging operations that depart from what that model predicts; how reliable such audits are has not been studied. This paper supplies that analysis. The choice of score determines whether an error can be localized at all. If each operation is scored by how surprising it is given the operation immediately preceding it, then operations that merely inherit an earlier error are indistinguishable from correct ones, so one mistake produces one flag; scores computed against a longer reconstruction of the intended analysis instead spread a single mistake across many operations. We quantify how far they spread, and how to choose the comparison length when an error accumulates gradually rather than at once. We then give procedures that control the proportion of falsely flagged operations within a single audited analysis, requiring only that sound analyses be exchangeable rather than that the fitted model be correct, and we quantify how much the guarantees weaken when the model is imperfect or when the analysis was selected for review in a way that depends on its content. Finally we establish a limit on what any such audit can report: errors below a certain magnitude cannot be attributed at all, being indistinguishable from ordinary variation among sound analyses. This limit falls so slowly as more sound analyses are collected that at the representation sizes now in use a hundredfold increase reduces it by under two percent, so the dimension of the representation rather than the volume of training data is the binding constraint.

</details>


### [95] [EcoAgent-Bench: Evaluating Economic Decision-Making in Budget-Constrained LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05519)
*Jie Wu,Ming Gong,Feixiang Cheng,Qinqin Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EcoAgent-Bench introduces budgeted action choices for tasks, with priced actions and explicit budgets, to evaluate when to escalate, upgrade tools, or stop. 304 real-derived tasks across five families test decision-making under cost constraints; results show budget-aware completion is distinct from mere success, with low micro success for tool-API agents and limited economic consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployment environments require agents to make economical decisions about tool use, model tier, and escalation, not just task completion; existing benchmarks ignore cost and resource usage, hindering evaluation of economical behavior.

Method: Create EcoAgent-Bench with 304 real-derived tasks across five families adapted from GAIA, HotpotQA, MuSiQue. Each task specifies priced actions and an explicit budget. Evaluate seven LLM agents in tool-API and workspace-CLI settings, plus four oracle scripts. Metrics include micro-averaged accuracy and an economic-consistency score. Perform a threshold-budget sweep to study escalation behavior.

Result: Tool-API agents achieve 3.9-24.0% micro strict success (max 7.3% economic consistency). Always-escalate controls raise micro success on some tasks but harm save-oriented tasks. Budget sweep reduces GPT-5.4 escalation from 0% to ~3%, showing budget and economical action selection are distinct.

Conclusion: Budget-aware task completion is a separate objective from generic task success. The dataset and evaluation pipeline are released to study economical decision-making in agentBench scenarios.

Abstract: Agent benchmarks usually measure task completion and treat resource use as an auxiliary statistic. In deployment, however, the choice among a local lookup, broad search, composite research tool, stronger model, or human escalation is part of the task itself. We introduce EcoAgent-Bench, in which every task specifies priced actions and an explicit budget. Its 304 real-derived tasks span five families adapted from GAIA, HotpotQA, and MuSiQue, and test four decisions: avoiding unnecessary escalation, escalating when local evidence is insufficient, selecting a model tier, and stopping on unsupported premises. We evaluate seven LLM agents in tool-API and workspace-CLI settings, together with four oracle scripted controls. Micro-averaged accuracy rewards one-sided policies: always-escalate controls achieve high micro success while failing save-oriented tasks. We therefore also report an economic-consistency score (the worse of accuracy on upgrade-oriented and save-oriented family groups) which exposes this failure. Tool-API agents attain only 3.9-24.0% micro strict success (at most 7.3% economic consistency), often either stopping before warranted escalation or overspending on cheap tasks. A threshold-crossing budget sweep changes GPT-5.4's escalation rate from 0% to only 3%. These results show that completion under a budget and economical action selection are distinct properties. We release the task bundle, transformation pipeline, frozen evaluation environments, and integrity-bound result artifacts needed to study both.

</details>


### [96] [Hyper-ES: Effective Evolution Strategies for LLM Reasoning via Descent Direction Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05541)
*Yu Gu,Zhi Zheng,Yunpeng Ba,Xialiang Tong,Mingxuan Yuan,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hyper-ES combines a small gradient-based pre-search to define a low-dimensional adaptation subspace, then uses CMA-ES to optimize layer-wise combination of those directions. It aims to address ES inefficiency in full-parameter LLM fine-tuning, achieving better performance than GRPO-LoRA with fewer gradient updates on math reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ES struggles in billion-parameter spaces due to perturbations being mostly orthogonal to useful directions; need data-efficient, high-performance fine-tuning for resource-constrained LLM reasoning.

Method: Compute a few gradient-based descent directions; form a subspace spanned by these directions; apply CMA-ES to optimize coefficients merging layer-wise DARE-TIES directions within this subspace; evaluate on Qwen2.5-Instruct and DeepSeek-R1-Distill across six math datasets.

Result: Hyper-ES consistently outperforms GRPO-LoRA by ~1% with ~10% fewer gradient updates; code released at GitHub.

Conclusion: Subspace-constrained ES leverages ES strengths for low-dimensional optimization, providing a practical, efficient alternative to full-parameter ES for LLM reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Evolution Strategy (ES) is a promising alternative to gradient-based fine-tuning for resource-constrained Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning. However, directly applying ES to billion-parameter LLMs is highly ineffective. In such high-dimensional parameter spaces, most random perturbations are nearly orthogonal to useful update directions, leading to unstable optimization. We propose Hyper-ES, a subspace-based ES framework that avoids the weakness of ES in full-parameter search while exploiting its strength in low-dimensional optimization. Instead of asking ES to discover useful directions from random perturbations in the LLM parameter space, Hyper-ES first performs a small number of inexpensive gradient-based fine-tuning runs to obtain descent directions. Although each direction may provide only a limited improvement on its own, their span forms a compact adaptation subspace that captures useful reasoning updates. Hyper-ES then applies CMA-ES to optimize layer-wise DARE-TIES merging coefficients within this subspace, allowing ES to search over combinations of meaningful descent directions rather than over arbitrary full-model perturbations. We evaluate Hyper-ES on three Qwen2.5-Instruct and DeepSeek-R1-Distill backbones across six mathematical reasoning datasets. Results show that Hyper-ES consistently outperforms GRPO-LoRA by 1% while requiring 10% fewer space-consuming gradient updates. Code at https://github.com/kuangrepi/Hyper-ES.

</details>


### [97] [SkillTV-Bench: Benchmarking How Well Judges Perform on Skill-Augmented Agentic Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05573)
*Zhi Han,Chenxi Zeng,Liuhaichen Yang,Zihan Guo,Ming Zhou,Yang Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SkillTV-Bench and SkillTV-Evolve to enable skill-aware trajectory verification for long-horizon LLM-driven tasks, externalizing verification knowledge as JudgeSkill; shows substantial gains in judge accuracy and rollout success through automated skill evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the gap where evaluating long-horizon agent executions requires not only final outputs but verification of procedural task-time skills and inspectable artifacts; existing benchmarks expose only final results or static traces.

Method: Construct SkillTV-Bench (681 trajectories, 50 tasks, 11 domains) to evaluate skill-aware trajectory verification for LLM- and agent-as-judge setups. Propose SkillTV-Evolve to externalize verification knowledge as JudgeSkill, guiding inspectors and verdicts. Use an automated evolution loop on a disjoint pool to refine JudgeSkill from misjudged cases. Evaluate on SkillTV-Bench with accuracy gains and offline rollout-pool selection.

Result: Refined JudgeSkill improves the same agent judge's accuracy by 14.8 percentage points. In offline rollout-pool selection, trajectory success rises from 22.9% (one rollout) to 45.5% (ten rollouts).

Conclusion: Skill-aware verification and externalized JudgeSkill substantially enhance evaluation and planning for long-horizon, tool-using agents; code and data are publicly available for replication.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly execute long-horizon tasks through tool use and environment interaction, shifting evaluation from final-response scoring to verification of complete executions. For skill-augmented agents, verification additionally requires the procedural knowledge encoded in task-time skills, because this knowledge indicates what evidence to inspect and which failures are task-critical. However, existing judge benchmarks often expose final responses or static trajectories, and rarely combine task-time skills with directly inspectable artifacts and environments. We therefore introduce SkillTV-Bench, a 681-case benchmark of real agent trajectories from 50 tasks across eleven domains, designed to evaluate skill-aware trajectory verification for both LLM-as-a-Judge and Agent-as-a-Judge methods. Additionally, we propose SkillTV-Evolve, which externalizes verification knowledge as a reusable JudgeSkill that guides an agent judge to plan targeted inspections and issue evidence-grounded verdicts. On a disjoint development pool, an automated evolution loop further refines the JudgeSkill using misjudged cases. On SkillTV-Bench, the refined skill increases the same agent judge's accuracy by 14.8 percentage points. In offline rollout-pool selection, it increases selected-trajectory success from 22.9% with one rollout to 45.5% with ten rollouts. The code and data are available at https://github.com/HanZhi306/SkillTV-Bench

</details>


### [98] [StepReflect: Structured UI Transition Reflection for Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05587)
*Linqiang Guo,Wei Liu,Li Gu,Yang Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StepReflect is an 8B model for per-step GUI reflection using supervised structured prediction conditioned on explicit GUI transition specs and visual evidence; it achieves 82.16% transition-level accuracy on AndroidWorld, surpasses zero-shot GPT-5.2 by 11.83 points, shows competitive online task success across four agents, and reduces API costs, positioning it as a practical, deployable alternative to frontier-model reflection for long-horizon mobile GUI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous mobile GUI agents need accurate per-step reflection to enable reliable long-horizon execution. Existing open-ended multimodal reasoning after each action is costly and poorly aligned with the structured, discrete GUI state transitions; a per-step, structured reflection mechanism can be more efficient and accurate.

Method: A staged training pipeline: supervised fine-tuning, teacher–student distillation, and preference- and reward-based refinement. The model learns per-step reflection as supervised structured prediction conditioned on explicit transition specifications and paired visual evidence, trained offline as an 8B parameter model.

Result: Offline, the 8B StepReflect achieves 82.16% transition-level accuracy on AndroidWorld, beating zero-shot GPT-5.2 by 11.83 percentage points. Online, across M3A, Agent-SAMA, MAI-UI-8B, and Seed-2.0-Pro, it attains higher task success in 3 of 4 agent configurations and is within one successful task of the GPT-5.2 Reflection Agent in the fourth; it also reduces paid API charges relative to GPT-based reflection in all four configurations.

Conclusion: StepReflect constitutes a practical, locally deployable alternative to repeated frontier-model reflection for long-horizon mobile GUI agents.

Abstract: Autonomous mobile GUI agents require accurate action reflection for reliable long-horizon execution. Existing approaches rely on open-ended multimodal reasoning after each action, which is costly and poorly matched to the structured nature of GUI state transitions. We propose StepReflect, which formulates per-step GUI reflection as supervised structured prediction conditioned on explicit transition specifications and paired visual evidence. StepReflect is trained through a staged pipeline combining supervised fine-tuning, teacher-student distillation, and preference- and reward-based refinement.
