Offline, the resulting 8B model achieves 82.16% transition-level accuracy on AndroidWorld, exceeding zero-shot GPT-5.2 by 11.83 percentage points under the same structured input. Online, across M3A, Agent-SAMA, MAI-UI-8B, and Seed-2.0-Pro, StepReflect achieves higher task success in three of four agent configurations and remains within one successful task of the GPT-5.2 Reflection Agent in the fourth. It also reduces paid API charges relative to GPT-based reflection in all four configurations. These results establish StepReflect as a practical, locally deployable alternative to repeated frontier-model reflection for long-horizon mobile GUI agents.

</details>


### [99] [GAUGE: A Measurement-Grounded Benchmark for Physical Fidelity in Simulation Engines and Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05948)
*Shuai Wang,Yaxin Feng,Xuekun Jiang,Shihan Tian,Ningyu Yan,Xing Shen,Chaoyang Lyu,Hui Wang,Yunsong Zhou,Hanqing Wang,Jiangmiao Pang,Yang Xiang,Xing Gao,Chunhua Shen,Weinan Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GAUGE is a real-world-grounded benchmark for jointly evaluating physical fidelity of numerical simulators and generative video world models. It uses 22 task families (rigid bodies, cables, textiles, volumetric deformables) with real trajectories, calibrated physical metadata, uncertainty, and task-specific observables. Evaluations on 14 task families for simulators and 5 rigid-body tasks for video models reveal no uniformly faithful physics engine; major gaps occur in impulsive contact, rapid textile motion, and volumetric deformation; video models can reproduce equation forms but misestimate accelerations and timing. Provides a foundation for more faithful simulators and world models in embodied AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of physical fidelity are often isolated and perceptual; there is a need for real-world-grounded diagnostics that identify which physical principles or parameters are violated when using simulators or video-based world models.

Method: Construct 22 task families covering rigid bodies, cables, textiles, and volumetric deformable objects. Tasks are grounded in real-world trajectories with calibrated physical metadata, uncertainty annotations, and task-specific observables. Evaluate 14 task families with generalized trajectory errors on Isaac Sim, Genesis, and Newton; and evaluate 6 image-to-video models on 5 rigid-body tasks by testing physical-law consistency and temporal stability of inferred parameters.

Result: No single physics engine is uniformly faithful. Largest discrepancies are in impulsive contact, rapid textile motion, and volumetric deformation. Video world models can produce trajectories that match the equation form but recover incorrect accelerations, momentum transfer, and oscillation timing.

Conclusion: GAUGE provides a framework for diagnosing and improving physically faithful simulators and video-world models, enabling more reliable embodied intelligence in real-world settings.

Abstract: Physics engines facilitate large-scale training and evaluation for embodied intelligence, while generative video world models are emerging as implicit simulators of future states and interactions. However, existing evaluations of physical fidelity are often conducted in isolation and rely heavily on perceptual similarity or human judgments, providing limited insight into which physical principles or parameters are violated. We introduce GAUGE, a real-world-grounded diagnostic benchmark for jointly evaluating how numerical simulators and generative video world models reproduce or deviate from real-world physics. It comprises 22 controlled task families covering rigid bodies, flexible cables, textiles, and volumetric deformable objects. Grounded in real-world trajectories and paired with calibrated physical metadata, uncertainty annotations, and task-specific observables, these tasks cover fundamental physical processes including collision, friction, momentum transfer, oscillation, self-contact, and deformation across diverse materials and conditions. We benchmark Isaac Sim, Genesis, and Newton on 14 task families using generalized trajectory errors, and evaluate 6 image-to-video models on 5 rigid-body tasks by testing physical-law consistency and the temporal stability of inferred parameters. Our results reveal no uniformly faithful physics engine, with the largest discrepancies arising in impulsive contact, rapid textile motion, and volumetric deformation. We further find that video world models can produce trajectories with the expected equation form while recovering incorrect accelerations, momentum transfer, and oscillation timing. GAUGE lays the groundwork for developing more physically faithful simulators and world models for embodied intelligence.

</details>


### [100] [Epistemic Trustworthiness in Generative AI: A Normative Framework for Warranted Reliance in High-Stakes Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05602)
*Nimisha Karnatak,Max Van Kleek,Nigel Shadbolt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A normative, three-pronged framework for epistemically warranted reliance on GenAI, arguing that trustworthiness—not just accuracy or fairness—determines when users may treat AI outputs as inputs to their reasoning; applies to legal, medical, and hiring contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To specify when reliance on AI outputs is epistemically warranted, distinct from behavioral dependence, by outlining conditions of epistemic humility, access, and resistance to epistemic injustice; addresses gaps in existing metrics.

Method: Theoretical framework grounded in philosophical trustworthiness (competence, audience-orientation); defines three constitutive conditions as jointly necessary and non-fungible; analyzes real-world cases (legal, medical, hiring) to illustrate harms from failures; offers design/evaluation implications.

Result: Three constitutive conditions identified: epistemic humility, epistemic access, and resistance to epistemic injustice; failures in these areas explain harms not captured by accuracy, fairness, or usability metrics; framework guides epistemically warranted reliance design.

Conclusion: Advocate redesign of GenAI design and evaluation toward epistemically warranted reliance, shaping system features and evaluation criteria to ensure humility, access, and justice; extends beyond traditional correctness-centered metrics.

Abstract: Generative AI systems are increasingly deployed in high-stakes professional contexts, where their outputs shape what users believe, how they reason, and what they treat as settled. This raises a central question for responsible AI: under what conditions is reliance on generative AI outputs epistemically warranted rather than behaviourally induced? Existing frameworks largely ask whether AI outputs are accurate, fair, explainable, safe, or trusted by users. These questions remain necessary, and each can contribute to warranted reliance. However, they do not directly specify warranted reliance as a distinct evaluative target: the conditions under which users are justified in treating AI outputs as inputs into their own reasoning. We argue that this requires an account of epistemic trustworthiness: what makes a system epistemically worthy of reliance. Drawing on philosophical accounts of trustworthiness as competence and audience-orientation, we develop a constitutive normative framework comprising three jointly necessary and non-fungible conditions. First, epistemic humility requires systems to represent and communicate the limits of their competence. Second, epistemic access requires systems to enable users to inspect, question, and contest outputs in context. Third, resistance to epistemic injustice requires systems to recognise users as legitimate epistemic agents and avoid marginalising their knowledge and experience. Through real-world case analyses in legal reasoning, medical reasoning, and hiring, we show how failures of epistemic humility, epistemic access, and resistance to epistemic injustice can produce consequential harms that standard measures of accuracy, fairness, and usability do not address on their own. We conclude by outlining design and evaluation implications for GenAI systems organised around epistemically warranted reliance rather than output correctness alone.

</details>


### [101] [Measuring and Detecting Harmful AI Sycophancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05624)
*Bohan Jiang,Dawei Li,Yasin Silva,Huan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces PSRS as a harmful sycophancy in LLMs, presents CAP to collect labeled PSRS data and detect PSRS from a single response, analyzes cross-model generalization across 17 LLMs with 290,460 labeled responses; finds PSRS prevalence, detectable signals, and generalization challenges, and releases data/code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PSRS can reverse stance to fit user preferences; current measures of sycophancy are insufficient; there is a need for automatic, scalable detection from a single response, and robustness to unseen models.

Method: Develops CAP (Contrastive Anchor Probing) to label PSRS data; applies to 17 open- and closed-source LLMs; collects 290,460 responses across 12 everyday-advice domains; evaluates PSRS frequency, detection accuracy on response text, and cross-model generalization; discusses approaches to improve generalization; will release dataset and code.

Result: PSRS rates range from 5% to 56% across models; more capable models are less sycophantic; detection from text is feasible but detectors must learn subtle PSRS patterns; cross-model generalization performance drops for unseen models; proposes initial method to address generalization.

Conclusion: CAP and dataset enable future research; detectors can enable safer deployment by identifying PSRS; emphasizes need for robust cross-model detectors and plans to release resources to the research community.

Abstract: Sycophantic responses are becoming pervasive in large language models (LLMs), and prior work has pointed out that some of them could be harmful. This paper focuses on one harmful sycophancy: preference-induced stance reversal sycophancy (PSRS), where a model reverses an initial stance merely to align with a user's stated preference. While existing research mainly measures how sycophantic a model is, we go further and ask whether PSRS can also be detected automatically from a single response. To investigate this at scale, we introduce CAP (Contrastive Anchor Probing), a framework for collecting labeled PSRS data. Applying CAP to 17 open- and closed-source LLMs, we collect 290,460 labeled responses across 12 everyday-advice domains. We organize our study around three research questions. (1) How often does PSRS occur? (2) How well can it be detected? (3) How does detection generalize to unseen models? We first reveal that PSRS rates range from 5% to 56% across LLMs, with more capable models being less sycophantic. Next, we show that detecting PSRS is feasible from the response text alone, and detectors need to learn subtle PSRS patterns from the training data. Because new LLMs appear rapidly, detectors inevitably encounter unseen models, making cross-model generalization an important framework goal. We demonstrate that detection performance drops on unseen models and propose an initial approach to address this challenge. We will release our dataset and code to support future research.

</details>


### [102] [SkillHEX: Improving Agent Skills via Hypothesis-Driven Autonomous Exploration and Exploitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05628)
*Yuru Feng,Yaoqi Chen,Beidi Zhao,Qianxi Zhang,Xinjiang Wang,Jianan Lu,Zhirui Wang,Shusen Xu,Zengzhong Li,Qi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillHEX offers a closed-loop approach for on-demand, test-time skill revision in LLMs, using falsifiable hypotheses and evidence-guided tree search to derive dense feedback and balance exploitation versus exploration under tight budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world LLM skill maintenance is costly, unscalable, and misaligned. Deployments require autonomous, on-demand skill evolution under sparse rewards, with limited interaction budgets and no training/validation data, risking exploitation traps.

Method: SkillHEX couples hypothesis-driven self-verification with evidence-guided tree search. It converts falsifiable failure hypotheses into executable tests, yields dense reward from diagnostic evidence (without extra environment trials), and guides a search over persistent skill-revision branches to balance exploiting supported edits and exploring plausible alternatives. Evaluated under a five-iteration budget on 87 SkillsBench tasks using GPT-5.3-Codex and Claude Opus 4.7.

Result: On 87 SkillsBench tasks, SkillHEX achieves average pass rates of 55.9% (GPT-5.3-Codex) and 57.9% (Claude Opus 4.7) under five iterations, outperforming existing self-evolving baselines.

Conclusion: SkillHEX demonstrates effective autonomous, budget-constrained skill evolution at test time, leveraging falsifiable diagnostics to provide dense feedback and a principled exploration–exploitation tradeoff across revision branches.

Abstract: Although agent skills equip LLMs with reusable procedural knowledge, manual maintenance suffers from high costs, unscalability, and misalignment. Real-world deployments thus require autonomous, on-demand skill evolution at test time, constrained by limited interaction budgets and a lack of training or validation sets. This setting introduces a severe sparse reward challenge, where outcomes conflate multiple latent failure causes. Under such ambiguity, existing methods that greedily refine a single incumbent skill are particularly vulnerable to an exploitation trap, allowing early misdiagnoses to exhaust limited trials along unproductive trajectories. To address this, we introduce SkillHEX, a closed-loop framework coupling hypothesis-driven self-verification with evidence-guided tree search. SkillHEX translates falsifiable failure hypotheses into executable tests, producing diagnostic evidence as dense reward without additional environment attempts. This evidence guides a search over persistent skill-revision branches, dynamically balancing the exploitation of supported edits with the exploration of plausible alternatives. Evaluated on 87 tasks from SkillsBench, SkillHEX outperforms existing self-evolving methods and achieves an average pass rate of 55.9% and 57.9% using GPT-5.3-Codex and Claude Opus 4.7 under a five-iteration budget, respectively.

</details>


### [103] [Bayesian Expected Uncertainty Reduction (B-EUR) Model: A Computational Account of What Makes Design Options Worth Trying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05642)
*Shimon Honda,Takuma Miyaguchi,Koji Koizumi,Takanori Sano,Tristan Briard,Hideyoshi Yanagisawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Bayesian Expected Uncertainty Reduction (B-EUR) model quantifies the expected epistemic uncertainty reduction of candidate actions to guide design exploration; validated by simulations and human experiments, revealing inverted-U effects of generalizability on epistemic value and that outcome discriminability enhances it, with analogous patterns in subjective measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formalize how changes in uncertainty perception influence action selection within Uncertainty Driven Action (UDA) and address how generalizability and outcome discriminability shape the value of trying candidate actions.

Method: Simulation and human experiments using a graph-shape guessing task to learn action–outcome relations under a limited trial budget.

Result: Epistemic value shows an inverted-U relationship with generalizability and increases with outcome discriminability in simulations; in humans, the subjective value of trying and enjoyment also show inverted-U with generalizability, while choice behavior reflects both properties.

Conclusion: B-EUR provides a computational account of candidate-action evaluation in uncertainty-driven design and has implications for constructing prototype sets, framing design problems, and organizing feedback to support informative exploration.

Abstract: This paper proposes the Bayesian Expected Uncertainty Reduction (B-EUR) model, which formalizes the value of trying a candidate design action as its expected reduction of epistemic uncertainty about action--outcome relations. The model addresses one part of the Uncertainty Driven Action (UDA) model's open question concerning how changes in uncertainty perception determine action selection. We examine two environmental properties: generalizability, or how far knowledge from one trial extends to neighboring candidates, and outcome discriminability, or how clearly differences among outcomes can be distinguished.
  We tested the model through simulations and human experiments using a graph-shape guessing task that isolates learning about action--outcome relations under a limited trial budget. Epistemic value followed an inverted-U-shaped relationship with generalizability and increased with outcome discriminability in the simulations. In the human experiments, the subjective value of trying and enjoyment followed inverted-U-shaped relationships with generalizability, while choice behavior reflected both properties.
  The B-EUR model provides a computational account of candidate-action evaluation within uncertainty-driven design activity and offers implications for constructing prototype sets, framing design problems, and organizing feedback to support informative exploration.

</details>


### [104] [Refining Over Resampling: Test-Time Self-Correction for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05643)
*Ahsan Bilal,Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin,Muhammad Umer,Lena Trigg,Ali Subhan,Muhammad Ali,Dean F. Hougen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A verifier-free breadth-depth refinement strategy at test time refines multiple sampled reasoning trajectories via self-critique and self-correction, then aggregates by majority vote, yielding gains over strong baselines on math benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome diminishing returns from wider sampling and reliance on external verifier models by using test-time compute for both exploration and refinement of reasoning trajectories.

Method: Sample multiple independent reasoning rollouts (breadth). Each rollout undergoes iterative self-critique and self-correction (depth). Aggregate refined answers by majority voting. No external reward model needed, leveraging compute for refinement.

Result: Consistent improvements over greedy decoding, majority voting, verifier-based Best-of-N, beam search, and lookahead decoding across AIME24, AIME25, AMC, OlympiadBench, and MATH500. Notable gains include Qwen2.5-1.5B: MATH500 accuracy up from baseline to 58.0%; AMC from 25.0% to 32.5%.

Conclusion: Test-time compute is more effective when used to refine sampled trajectories than merely sampling more or relying on verifier-guided selection, enabling stronger reasoning performance on math benchmarks.

Abstract: Test-time scaling improves LLM reasoning by using additional inference compute, but wider sampling alone can suffer from diminishing returns: new rollouts often repeat existing answer patterns instead of adding useful reasoning diversity. Verifier-based selection offers an alternative, but its performance depends on the calibration of an external reward model. We propose a verifier-free breadth--depth refinement framework that uses test-time compute to both explore and improve candidate solutions. The method samples multiple independent reasoning rollouts, refines each rollout through iterative self-critique and self-correction, and aggregates the refined answers by majority voting. Breadth preserves diverse initial attempts, while depth repairs local reasoning errors before aggregation. Across AIME24, AIME25, AMC, OlympiadBench, and MATH500, our method consistently improves over greedy decoding, majority voting, verifier-based best-of-$N$, beam search, and lookahead decoding across multiple open-weight models. For instance, with Qwen2.5-1.5B, accuracy increases from the strongest verifier-based baseline to $58.0\%$ on MATH500, and from $25.0\%$ to $32.5\%$ on AMC. These results show that test-time compute can be more effective when used to refine sampled trajectories rather than only to sample more candidates or rely on verifier-guided selection.

</details>


### [105] [A Unified Framework for Trajectory Prediction with Explicit Planning and Reaction Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05673)
*Jiaheng Chen,Jiaxing Li,Tinghe Zhang,Chaopeng Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: INTraJ introduces a planning-driven two-stage framework for trajectory prediction that separates social influence into a planning stage (using future social information to construct reference trajectories) and a reaction stage (adjusting via residuals from full-context predictions). It supports multi-target and single-target settings, showing consistent improvements on multiple benchmarks and achieving state-of-the-art results in several settings; code is released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current social-influence modeling often conflates planning and reactive adjustments, treating social cues as a single influence. This can blur the distinction between anticipatory planning and local adaptation, harming stability, especially at long horizons.

Method: Two-stage framework: planning stage builds reference trajectories using anticipated future social information; reaction stage recovers local adjustments by modeling the residual between full-context prediction and the planning reference. The approach is applicable to both multi-target and single-target paradigms. Evaluated on Argoverse 2, Argoverse 2-ped, ETH/UCY, and SDD; code available.

Result: Consistent improvements in final displacement error (FDE) and long-horizon consistency; achieves state-of-the-art performance in several settings across four benchmarks.

Conclusion: Framing trajectory prediction as a planning-driven two-stage process validates that staged social modeling is crucial for stable, accurate predictions; the work broadens the design space for social-aware predictors and demonstrates robust benefits across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Trajectory prediction has shifted toward structured formulations with explicit social modeling. However, existing methods inadequately distinguish the functional roles of social influence in trajectory planning. Observing that agents typically form motion plans by anticipating others' future behaviors before making local reactive adjustments, we identify social interactions as playing staged roles, namely planning precedes reaction. We propose INTraJ, a unified framework that decomposes social influence into two stages: a planning stage constructs reference trajectories using future social information, and a reaction stage recovers local adjustments from the residual between full-context prediction and the reference. INTraJ supports both multi-target and single-target paradigms. Extensive experiments on four standard benchmarks, including Argoverse 2, Argoverse 2-ped, ETH/UCY, and SDD, demonstrate consistent improvements, particularly in FDE and long-horizon consistency, with state-of-the-art performance achieved in several settings. INTraJ reframes trajectory prediction as a planning-driven two-stage process, validating that staged social modeling is critical for stable predictions. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/11isnotavailable/INTraJ.

</details>


### [106] [Grounded Well-Condition Anomaly Detection on the Volve Field: Constructed Labels, a Baseline, and a Dual-Head Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05685)
*Gospel Bassey,Vincent Fakiyesi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Physically grounded anomaly labeling on Volve field data with unsupervised and dual-head supervised evaluation; dataset and code released for reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real production data often lacks fault logs; need labels anchored in engineering knowledge to evaluate anomaly detection meaningfully and ensure learnability.

Method: Construct anomaly labels tied to field engineering documents with transparent reasoning; evaluate using an unsupervised detector and a compact dual-head model (event presence + event type) trained across wells not seen during training; release dataset, provenance, baselines, models, and code under CC-BY-NC-SA 4.0.

Result: Unsupervised detector, even without labels, identifies regions aligned with the labeled events, indicating labels reflect real structure rather than arbitrary patterns. The compact supervised model generalizes to unseen wells, recovering event presence and type, though temporal localization is approximate.

Conclusion: The work emphasizes transparency and reproducibility, reporting both successes and failures. It provides a grounded dataset with provenance and code, enabling further validation and extension by the community.

Abstract: Most public benchmarks for machine-condition monitoring come from test rigs, where faults are induced on purpose and every event is known. Real production fields rarely offer that. They give you sensor histories with no fault log attached, which is exactly the situation where an anomaly-detection method has to invent its own labels, and where quiet assumptions can slip in unnoticed. We work with the open Volve field data released by Equinor and take two things seriously that such datasets usually skip. First, we build anomaly labels that are not just patterns in the numbers but are checked against what the field's own engineering documents say can physically go wrong, and we release the reasoning behind every label. Second, we test whether those constructed labels are learnable at all, using both an unsupervised baseline and a small dual-head model that marks when an event happens and what kind it is, an idea we carry over from earlier work on defect detection in metal parts. The results are honest. An unsupervised detector that never sees the labels still lands on the same regions our rules flagged, which tells us the labels are not arbitrary. A compact supervised model recovers event presence and event type well across wells it has never seen, and locates events in time only roughly. We report what worked, what did not, and every assumption in between. The dataset, grounded labels, per-label provenance, baseline scores, trained model, and code are released publicly under CC-BY-NC-SA 4.0.

</details>


### [107] [DreamGuard: Efficient Runtime Guardrail for LLM Agents via Risk-Aware World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05695)
*Wenhao Lin,Chenyu Yu,Xingwei Lin,Sicong Cao,Xiang Chen,Lei Xue,Le Yu,Letian Sha,Chunming Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DreamGuard is a proactive, risk-aware guardrail for LLM agents that maintains a compact recurrent latent state to predict未来 latent trajectories, derives immediate-hazard and prefix-risk signals, and fuses them to decide interventions before execution, achieving the best safety–utility trade-off with ~25 ms latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reactive guardrails miss long-horizon risk accumulation; there is a need for an explicit model of how risk evolves along a trajectory to prevent drifts toward hazardous states when LLM agents interact with external tools and real-world systems.

Method: Introduce a risk-aware world model that preserves a compact recurrent latent state over the action trajectory. This model predicts future latent states, from which immediate-hazard and prefix-risk evidence are derived. These signals are fused into multi-horizon risk assessments to inform intervention decisions prior to action execution.

Result: DreamGuard outperforms generic, reactive, and proactive guardrail baselines across four benchmarks and an online guardrail evaluation. It achieves the best safety–utility trade-off among evaluated guardrails and maintains an average end-to-end latency of 25 ms per call.

Conclusion: A proactive, world-model-based guardrail can substantially improve safety for LLM agents without sacrificing real-time performance, effectively mitigating long-horizon risks by anticipating trajectory-wide danger signals.

Abstract: As large language model (LLM) agents increasingly invoke external tools and interact with real-world systems, unsafe actions may cause irreversible consequences on external states, user data, and downstream services. Recent runtime guardrails mitigate such risks by checking proposed actions before execution, but many remain reactive: they primarily assess the apparent safety of the current action, lacking an explicit model of how risk evolves across the trajectory. This limitation creates a critical blind spot for long-horizon risks, where individually benign-looking actions can gradually drift the agent toward hazardous states. In response, we propose DreamGuard, a proactive guardrail for LLM agents built around a risk-aware world model. The world model maintains a compact recurrent latent state over the trajectory and predicts future latent states from which DreamGuard derives immediate-hazard and prefix-risk evidence. It then fuses these multi-horizon signals into intervention decisions before execution. Experiments across four benchmarks and an online guardrail evaluation show that DreamGuard outperforms generic, reactive, and proactive guardrail baselines, achieves the best safety-utility trade-off among evaluated guardrails, and maintains an average end-to-end latency of 25 ms per call.

</details>


### [108] [Shaping Human-AI Interactions to Provide Improvement Pathways and Balance Competing Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05710)
*Keziah Naggita*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A thesis on human-AI interaction loops, aiming to shape beliefs, discourage gaming, and preserve objective alignment; uses theory, modeling, and experiments, contributing to human-centered ML.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individuals form beliefs and strategies about AI, leading to an interaction loop; there is a need to design interactions that improve users' understanding, deter gaming, and maintain the AI system's objectives.

Method: Three-part thesis; integrates theoretical analysis, data-driven modeling, human-subject experiments, and empirical evaluations on real-world and semi-synthetic datasets; analyzes interactions from both evaluated individuals and AI systems.

Result: Provides principles and methods for designing AI systems aligned with human needs, values, and capabilities; advances the field of human-centered ML.

Conclusion: Designing human-AI interactions can help individuals form accurate beliefs, reduce gaming, and sustain AI objectives; the work offers principled guidance for human-centered machine learning.

Abstract: When an AI system is deployed, the individuals who use and or are evaluated by it form beliefs about how the system operates and use those beliefs to strategically present their preferences, behaviors, or attributes. The system then responds with feedback or a decision outcome, thereby creating a human-AI interaction loop. This thesis studies how to design and shape such interactions to achieve three goals: (1) help individuals develop accurate beliefs about the AI systems so they can improve and or secure favorable outcomes at minimal cost, (2) encourage improvement and or discourage gaming behaviors, and (3) ensure that the AI system continues to achieve its intended objectives, such as maximizing accuracy. To address these goals, the thesis is organized into three complementary parts that examine and study human-AI interactions from the perspectives of both evaluated individuals and AI systems. Together, the work presented in this thesis advances human-centered machine learning by providing principles and methods for designing AI systems that align with human needs, values, and capabilities. Methodologically, this thesis integrates theoretical analysis, data-driven modeling, human-subject experiments, and empirical evaluations on real-world and semi-synthetic datasets.

</details>


### [109] [RA-CAD: Learning Post-Execution Critique for State-Aware Text-to-CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05714)
*Shuhao Yan,Changhao He,Xi Peng,Peng Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: State-aware RA-CAD agent for text-to-CAD with a Generate--Execute--Critique--Rewrite loop, bootstrapped with supervised CCB and FAO, achieving state-of-the-art execution validity and geometric quality on CADFusion/Text2CAD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing feedback mechanisms in text-to-CAD often fail to optimize how feedback is interpreted and translated into corrective actions, leading to suboptimal generation quality and utilization of failures.

Method: RA-CAD executes code, observes outcomes, and generates post-execution critique to guide next rewrites in a Generate--Execute--Critique--Rewrite loop. CAD Code Bootstrapping (CCB) uses supervised fine-tuning; Feedback-Driven Agent Optimization (FAO) applies trajectory-level Group Relative Policy Optimization with terminal F1 and Chamfer Distance rewards to full interaction trajectories.

Result: RA-CAD achieves state-of-the-art execution validity and geometric quality on CADFusion and Text2CAD, outperforming existing methods and strong proprietary language models.

Conclusion: A state-aware, critique-informed policy can effectively regulate feedback into constructive revisions, bridging the feedback-utilization gap in text-to-CAD generation.

Abstract: Text-to-CAD generation translates natural-language design intent into editable and executable parametric computer-aided design (CAD) codes, reducing the expertise and effort required for manual modeling. Existing methods incorporate fixed, externally supplied, prompt-induced, or separately optimized critique mechanisms to optimize the generation process, but they do not necessarily optimize how feedback is interpreted and translated into effective corrective actions throughout the generation process. To bridge this feedback-utilization gap, we present RA-CAD (ReAct Agent for CAD), a state-aware agent that interacts with the CAD environment through a Generate--Execute--Critique--Rewrite loop. At each iteration, RA-CAD executes the current code and observes its outcome. Conditioned on the design instruction, current code, and execution feedback, the agent then generates an explicit post-execution critique as an intermediate policy action. This critique either validates the current result for termination or provides revision-oriented guidance that conditions the next rewrite. CAD Code Bootstrapping (CCB) first establishes fundamental parametric CAD coding capabilities through supervised fine-tuning. Feedback-Driven Agent Optimization (FAO) subsequently applies trajectory-level Group Relative Policy Optimization to both policy-generated code and critique sequences, assigning terminal F1 and Chamfer Distance rewards to the complete interaction trajectory. This formulation makes critique an outcome-aligned, learnable policy decision rather than an unoptimized auxiliary output. Experiments on CADFusion and Text2CAD show that RA-CAD achieves state-of-the-art execution validity and geometric quality compared with existing methods and strong proprietary language models, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed state-aware text-to-CAD agent.

</details>


### [110] [BlockPython: A Process-Aware Agent-Supported Platform for the Transition from Block-Based to Python Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05716)
*Jesse Yusuf Chan,Haoming Wang,Mingwei Xu,Xianlong Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Bidirectional block-Python translation with a four-stage pedagogy (Task Decomposition, Block-Based Practice, Code Challenge, Extended Interaction) and process-aware support to bridge block-based and text-based programming, using a rule-based engine and an evidence-driven learning assistant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the cognitive gap learners face when translating visible program structures from block-based representations to abstract Python syntax, enabling better understanding of concepts, runtime behavior, and textual expression.

Method: Design and implementation of BlockPython featuring bidirectional translation between blocks and Python, a four-stage learning workflow, continuous collection of process evidence (block artifacts, code versions, run outcomes, support usage, dialogue), deterministic diagnosis, program visualization, a learning assistant, and a rule-based system for execution, evaluation, and stage control; the assistant provides explanations and guiding prompts based on verified evidence.

Result: Describes the design rationale, learning workflow, and process-aware support mechanisms; presents BlockPython as a system-design reference for facilitating the block-to-text transition and for analyzing learners’ processes; no empirical results are reported in the abstract.

Conclusion: BlockPython offers a structured, evidence-driven platform that integrates bidirectional translation, staged learning, and process-aware support to facilitate the transition from block-based to text-based programming, and to support learning-process analysis.

Abstract: The transition from block-based to text-based programming requires learners to convert visible program structures into abstract textual expressions, which may create a cognitive gap between understanding computational concepts and expressing them in Python syntax. To support this transition, we designed and implemented BlockPython. The platform centers on bidirectional translation between blocks and Python and guides learners through four stages: Task Decomposition, Block-Based Practice, Code Challenge, and Extended Interaction. Across these stages, learners progressively establish connections among program structure, runtime behavior, and textual code. During learning, the platform continuously collects process evidence, including block artifacts, code versions, run outcomes, use of support, and dialogue. Deterministic diagnosis, program visualization, and the learning assistant use this evidence to identify different difficulties in computational understanding and Python expression. The rule-based system is responsible for program execution, objective evaluation, and stage control, while the learning assistant uses verified evidence to provide explanations, prompts, and guiding questions. This report describes the design rationale, learning workflow, and process-aware support mechanisms of BlockPython and provides a system-design reference for supporting the transition from block-based to text-based programming and for analyzing learning processes.

</details>


### [111] [Unified Agent: Managing Interactions across Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05729)
*Xinshuang Liu,Runfa Blark Li,Shaoxiu Wei,Xin Lin,Truong Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A stateful cross-device, cross-time agent (Unified Agent) carries interaction evidence and standing requests as compact state to guide action, outperforming baselines across multiple MLLM settings; code and data to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI capabilities grow, agents must operate across devices over time. Existing designs—single-device tool-based agents or multi-agent systems—lack compact, persistent state that organizes evidence, facts, and standing requests for decision-making across observations and moments.

Method: Propose and instantiate Unified Agent, a stateful agent that preserves interaction evidence across devices and moments and uses it with the current observation to act. Build a benchmark of user–agent interaction across devices and time. Compare Unified Agent to adaptations of four published designs. Evaluate robustness across multiple multimodal LLM families, capabilities, and reasoning effort. Release code/data.

Result: In the default setting, Unified Agent significantly outperforms adaptations of four published designs. Its advantage persists across different MLLM families, capabilities, and reasoning effort, indicating a robust state-design advantage.

Conclusion: State-design is advantageous for cross-device, cross-time agents and is robust across MLLMs; the work supports releasing code and data on GitHub for replication.

Abstract: As capabilities rapidly increase, AI agents can move from running inside one app to acting across a user's devices over time. Yet existing agent systems still fall short in this scenario. This is because observations are scattered across devices and moments, but mainstream systems are not designed around this fact: a single agent that treats devices as tools lacks effective state management for all devices across time, and multi-agent systems coordinate across agents but do not maintain the compact carried state a cross-device, cross-time request needs. We argue that the agent should maintain an effectively designed state that organizes engagement evidence, stated facts, and the standing request in a compact, action-ready form for deciding its action given the current observation. To compare state designs, we construct a benchmark of user-agent interaction across devices and time. We instantiate this principle in Unified Agent, a stateful agent that carries interaction evidence across devices and moments and uses it with the current observation to act. In the default setting, it significantly outperforms our adaptations of four published designs. Across changes in multimodal large language model (MLLM) family, capability, and reasoning effort, it remains ahead of all compared systems, demonstrating that the state-design advantage is robust across MLLM settings. Our code and data will be publicly available on GitHub.

</details>


### [112] [Subliminal Learning is Non-Semantic Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05734)
*Ethan Hadley,Eren Gultepe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The abstract investigates subliminal learning (SL) in language models, showing that non-semantic weight perturbations and steering vectors can enable biases transfer from teacher to student via synthetic data; the intervention type leaves traceable signals in activations and gradients, with implications for auditing and safe AI training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing reliance on synthetic data in frontier training raises the risk of hidden subliminal signals that can bias models. Understanding enabling mechanisms and developing auditing tools are essential for predictability and safety.

Method: Experiments include applying Gaussian noise to teacher and student weights to modulate SL; using steering vectors to craft subliminal data via the teacher; comparing steered vs. prompted data; analyzing student activations to infer the nature of interventions; examining gradients to assess auditability and correlation with steering vectors; reporting transfer magnitudes (e.g., 1.9x in Gemma, 1.3x in Llama).

Result: Subliminal transfer magnitude increases with weight noise; steering vectors enable subliminal data that cause steered students to imitate the intervention, while prompted data do not; activations reflect the type of intervention; gradients show a linear correlation with steering vectors, offering a potential auditing signal.

Conclusion: Non-semantic weight structures and externally applied steering vectors play key roles in SL. The ability to detect latent signals via gradients points to possible auditing strategies as synthetic data become central to training pipelines; there is a need for broader validation, defense mechanisms, and tooling to mitigate hidden biases.

Abstract: Subliminal Learning (SL) is a surprising type of generalization displayed by modern language models. It allows the transfer of a bias or behavior from a teacher model to a student by distilling from seemingly unrelated or random synthetic data from the teacher. This presents challenges in ensuring AI systems remain predictable and are trained safely, as standard auditing of the input data would not catch the hidden subliminal signal. Here, we investigate several open questions as to the enabling mechanisms and drivers of SL. First is the nature of the process by which biases are encoded in the data. We find that by adding Gaussian noise to the weights of the teacher and student models, the magnitude of subliminal transfer is increased by a factor of 1.9 in Gemma and 1.3 in Llama, suggesting that non-semantic weight structures play a crucial role. We show that steering vectors can be applied to the teacher to produce subliminal data, in addition to prompting and finetuning as used in previous studies. Analysis of the activations of the student models that have been trained on steered and prompted data demonstrates that students inherit not just the semantic meaning of the teacher's bias, but also the type of intervention that was used to apply it: steered students imitate steering vectors, prompted students do not. Additionally, the gradients of steered subliminal data show a linear correlation with the teacher's steering vectors, showing promise for data auditing. More broadly, as synthetic data becomes central to frontier training pipelines, being able to see the latent signals hidden in training data becomes paramount.

</details>


### [113] [When Do Prompt-Side Agent Playbooks Transfer? Accuracy, Cost, and Runtime Shift in Agent Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05778)
*Weihong Lin,Lin Sun,Xiangzheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frozen transfer of prompt-side playbooks offers conditional benefits across domains and cost-sensitive trade-offs, not a universal default over target-side redistillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether prompt-side playbooks transferability across domains is practical and beneficial under a distill–validate–transfer protocol.

Method: Cross-domain evaluation using ALFWorld, TAU2-Bench, and XBench-DeepSearch to compare frozen (distilled) playbooks with fixed demonstrations under controlled decoding; apply Holm correction; analyze route-level effects, cost, and termination behavior.

Result: ALFWorld: transfer beneficial under controlled greedy decoding; one near-budget-matched case shows distilled guidance beating five fixed demonstrations. TAU2-Bench: modest average matched-domain advantage; Holm correction yields only 1 of 135 route-level effects significant, indicating compatibility-sensitive heterogeneity. XBench-DeepSearch: artifact-runtime pairing preserves first-try heuristics but increases repeated queries and costs after a context-runtime shift. Across benchmarks, both transferred and target-derived playbooks require target-side validation for success, termination, compatibility, and cost.

Conclusion: Frozen transfer is a conditional cold-start option, not a default reuse or universally preferable alternative to target-side redistillation; practitioners should validate target-domain compatibility and cost before deployment.

Abstract: Prompt-side playbooks can improve tool-using language agents without retraining, but their portability beyond the source setting is unclear. We study frozen playbook transfer under a shared distill--validate--transfer protocol. On ALFWorld, transfer is beneficial under controlled greedy decoding and, in one near-budget-matched comparison, distilled guidance outperforms five fixed demonstrations. On TAU2-Bench, a prespecified aggregate contrast supports a modest average matched-domain advantage, but global Holm correction retains only one of 135 route-level effects; the remaining grid provides descriptive evidence of compatibility-sensitive heterogeneity. On XBench-DeepSearch, one artifact--runtime pairing preserves useful first-try heuristics while producing repeated queries, delayed stopping, and substantial cost inflation after a context-runtime shift. Across benchmarks, transferred and target-derived playbooks both require target-side validation of success, termination, protocol compatibility, and cost. Frozen transfer is therefore a conditional cold-start option, not a reuse-by-default strategy or a universally preferable alternative to target-side redistillation.

</details>


### [114] [Activity Frames: Deterministic Screen-Activity Compilation for Agent Memory and Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05784)
*Nossa Iyamu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A deterministic, byte-identical compiler converts passive screen-capture streams into compact, query-ready memory blocks for agents, enabling high-accuracy day-level inferences with minimal model tokens and introducing cost metrics for routine reuse.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce agent re-derivation of user routines by precomputing memory from raw captures; quantify cost and performance of routine-oriented execution; enable auditable, zero-model-token replay.

Method: Segment local capture streams into typed frames; bounded episodes with app/site/time/input/evidence pointers; no learning model in the loop; deterministic pipeline; evaluate on a single-user corpus; compare to LLM-based summaries; derive Routine Overhead Ratio R and recurrence h; open-source artifacts.

Result: Output is 86x smaller prompt-ready context block (68 ms) from 128,756 frames over 51 days; agent accuracy 98.4% vs 66-80% with LLM summaries; R upper bound 60-343x; recurrence 9.0% in-sample, 7.7% out-of-sample; token ceiling near 8%; zero-model-token replay realized; open schema/compiler/eval harness.

Conclusion: Compiled routines provide deterministic, model-free replay and potential cost savings; open sources enable reproducibility and auditing; suggests frontier improvements for agent memory and cost modeling.

Abstract: Computer-use agents pay full frontier inference to re-derive routines their user has already performed, because an agent's memory today records what the user said, not what the user did. We compile passively captured screen activity into agent memory with a deterministic, zero-model pipeline: it segments a local capture stream into typed activity frames, bounded episodes carrying application, site, timing, input volume, and evidence pointers back to the raw rows, with no model in the loop, so the output is byte-identical, cacheable, and mechanically auditable. On one professional's single-user corpus of 128,756 frames over 51 active days, the compiler reduces a day of raw capture to a prompt-ready context block 86x smaller in 68 ms, and an agent reading that block answers questions about the day at 98.4% accuracy (Wilson 95% CI 91.7-99.7%) against an independent oracle, versus 66-80% for an LLM summary of the same capture, a mid-tier model reading the block matching a frontier one.
  The same compiler doubles as a demand-side cost instrument. Read off passive, pre-delegation human activity rather than agent rollouts, it supplies two parameters that agent-cost models assume but, to our knowledge, have not measured: the Routine Overhead Ratio R and the routine recurrence h. We report first values of R, a modeled upper bound, at 60-343x, and a delegable recurrence of 9.0% in-sample and 7.7% out-of-sample, for a realistic all-fleet token ceiling near 8%; a compiled routine replays deterministically with the model out of the loop, demonstrated live at zero model tokens on a guard-matched hit. Schema, compiler, and evaluation harness are open.

</details>


### [115] [ChainClaw: A Layered Agent Framework for Reliable On-Chain Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05790)
*Jiacheng Wei,Zhaoxin Fan,Xin Wen,Yuqin Lan,Dongrun Li,Wenjun Wu,Faguo Wu,Xiao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChainClaw is a blockchain-native agent framework that closes three key gaps in on-chain automation (Reactivity, Irreversibility, Observability) via a layered architecture—event-driven orchestration, simulation-based safety, and on-chain monitoring—with a cross-layer memory, achieving safer and more effective task completion on a 7-task benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General-purpose LLM agents excel in tool-based tasks but falter in blockchain environments due to statefulness, adversarial dynamics, and economically irreversible actions, creating reactivity, irreversibility, and observability gaps that hinder safe, reliable on-chain automation.

Method: Introduce ChainClaw, a three-layer architecture built atop OpenClaw: (1) event-driven orchestration layer for event ingestion and feedback; (2) simulation-based safety intelligence layer for pre-execution checks; (3) on-chain monitoring runtime layer with a read adapter and transaction monitor; a cross-layer memory subsystem unifies data across layers. Safety pipeline includes transaction simulations and action guards before on-chain execution. Evaluation uses a purpose-built benchmark spanning seven tasks, four categories, and five dimensions.

Result: ChainClaw consistently outperforms representative baselines in both safety and task completion across the benchmark, demonstrating effective mitigation of the reactivity, irreversibility, and observability gaps on-chain.

Conclusion: A blockchain-native agent framework with a layered, memory-augmented architecture can effectively address the core operational gaps of on-chain agents, enabling safer and more capable tool-augmented automation in blockchain environments.

Abstract: General-purpose large language model agents have achieved strong performance on tool-augmented tasks, yet they rely on assumptions break down in blockchain environments. On-chain execution is stateful, adversarial, and economically irreversible, exposing three fundamental gaps: Reactivity, Irreversibility, and Observability. We propose ChainClaw, a blockchain-native agent framework built on OpenClaw, that addresses all three gaps through a layered architecture comprising an event-driven orchestration layer, a simulation-based safety intelligence layer, and an on-chain monitoring runtime layer, unified by a cross-layer memory subsystem. ChainClaw closes the Reactivity gap via event ingestion and simulation feedback, the Irreversibility gap via a pre-execution safety pipeline with transaction simulation and action guard, and the Observability gap via an on-chain read adapter and transaction monitor. We evaluate ChainClaw on a purpose-built benchmark covering seven tasks across four categories and five dimensions. ChainClaw consistently outperforms representative baselines on both safety and task completion.

</details>


### [116] [When Agentic AI Meets Integrated Sensing and Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05792)
*Kai Li,Conggai Li,Sarah Ali Siddiqui,Syed Sohail Ahmed,Xin Yuan,Shenghong Li,Wei Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unifying AISAC framework that casts ISAC as a six-stage closed-loop agentic system and maps maturity into five levels. It audits literature across sensing, learning, RIS, edge, multi-agent coordination, and AI-native management, revealing limited agentic coverage and a set of open challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To consolidate disparate agentic AI advances into Integrated Sensing and Communication (ISAC) under a unified, goal-driven, closed-loop paradigm, and to establish a maturity framework and evaluation criteria

Method: Systematic survey synthesizing work across multimodal intelligence, LLMs, reinforcement learning, federated learning, RIS-controlled networks, UAV/vehicular networks, and AI-native network management; defines a six-stage perception–reasoning–action loop with feedback, plus a five-level agentic maturity scale; conducts an audit against nine agentic-specific criteria

Result: Finds that no studied system meets more than one or two agentic criteria; highlights that privacy, security, resilience, and sustainability cut across the loop; outlines advances in multimodal AI, LLMs, RL, FL, RIS, and autonomous networking; identifies gaps between claimed and demonstrated agentic maturity

Conclusion: Open challenges include physical-to-semantic grounding, predictive world models, real-time agent–PHY interaction, safe tool use, heterogeneous multi-agent collaboration, benchmarking, and resource-efficient autonomy; calls for standardized benchmarks and integrated evaluation to advance true agentic ISAC

Abstract: Agentic artificial intelligence (AI) is transforming Integrated Sensing and Communication (ISAC) from a function-oriented physical-layer technology into a goal-driven, closed-loop intelligent system, a paradigm we term AISAC. Existing work on learning-based sensing, resource allocation, reconfigurable intelligent surfaces (RIS), edge intelligence, multi-agent coordination, and resilient networking has developed largely in isolation. This survey unifies the literature within a six-stage closed-loop framework comprising observation, contextualization, reasoning and prediction, planning and orchestration, execution and collaboration, and feedback and resilience. It also introduces five levels of agentic maturity, ranging from physical-layer primitives to fully closed-loop agentic ISAC. We use this framework to review advances in multimodal intelligence, large language models, reinforcement learning, federated learning, RIS-assisted control, Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) and vehicular networks, and AI-native network management, and analyze privacy, security, resilience, and sustainability as cross-cutting requirements of the full perception-reasoning-action loop. An audit of representative studies against nine agentic-specific evaluation criteria shows that no system reports more than one or two of them, exposing a gap between claimed and demonstrated agentic maturity. We identify open challenges in physical-to-semantic grounding, predictive world models, real-time agent-PHY interaction, safe tool use, heterogeneous multi-agent collaboration, benchmarking, and resource-efficient autonomy.

</details>


### [117] [When Self-Evolution Backfires: Pre-Commit Gating against Skill Contamination in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05810)
*Linfang Shang,Ming Xu,Yiding Sun,Tianle Xia,Lingxiang Hu,Lan Xu,Ning Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-evolving agents suffer nonmonotonic capability growth due to cross-round contamination; a Verifier-as-Gatekeeper (VaG) with three critics and marginal-gain subset selection mitigates this, yielding improved, transferable skill pools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify why increasing the skill pool beyond a point degrades performance in self-evolving agents and propose a principled pre-commit screening mechanism to prevent contamination.

Method: Introduce Verifier-as-Gatekeeper (VaG) architecture with three heterogeneous critics—structural validity, behavioral harmlessness, semantic consistency—and a marginal-gain subset selection to prune harmful skills at the top tier before runtime. Formalize capability-contamination phase transition, demonstrate irreversibility of contamination, and evaluate on Terminal-Bench 2. Assess transferability to four backbones and a second benchmark; conduct ablations showing critics are complementary.

Result: VaG yields consistent improvements across rounds, achieving 72% pass@1 with a pool roughly 5x smaller. Unconditional accumulation peaks and then degrades; post-hoc removal recovers little of the loss, illustrating irreversibility. Frozen skill pools transfer positively to four other backbones and a second benchmark without re-evolution. Ablations show the three critics are complementary and non-substitutable, each catching distinct classes of harmful skills.

Conclusion: Skill admission should be treated as a pre-commitment step rather than post-hoc cleanup. VaG provides a practical, transferable gating mechanism to prevent contamination, with empirical evidence of irreversibility and broad transferability across backbones and benchmarks.

Abstract: Self-evolving agents accumulate capability by distilling reusable skills from their execution trajectories, but we find this process is not monotonic: past a critical pool size, newly added skills degrade performance instead of improving it. We formalize this capability-contamination phase transition and trace it to a structural cause: once a defective skill enters the decision context, it becomes reference material for distilling later skills, forming cross-round contamination chains. We further show the contamination is structurally irreversible: removing a source skill after the fact cannot erase the flawed reasoning its descendants have already inherited, so post-hoc rollback recovers only a small fraction of the lost performance. This makes skill admission a pre-commit necessity rather than a post-hoc fix, and motivates Verifier-as-Gatekeeper (VaG): a progressive trust hierarchy whose three heterogeneous critics - structural validity, behavioral harmlessness, and semantic consistency - filter each skill individually, coupled with a marginal-gain subset selection that removes combinatorial contamination at the top tier before skills reach the runtime context. On Terminal-Bench 2, unconditional accumulation rises to a peak and then degrades, giving back most of its gains as the pool keeps growing, and post-hoc removal of the culprit skills recovers only a small part of the drop - the empirical signature of irreversibility. In contrast, VaG improves every round, reaching 72% pass@1 with a pool roughly 5x smaller, and its frozen skill pool transfers positively to four other backbones and a second benchmark without re-evolution. Ablations confirm the three critics are complementary and mutually non-substitutable, each intercepting a largely disjoint class of harmful skills.

</details>


### [118] [Cautious Context Steering for Language Model Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05813)
*Gihoon Kim,Jeyoung Lee,Suhan Woo,Sekwon Oh,Minsu Jeon,Hyounsoo Han,Euntai Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CCS adds a lightweight adapter to a frozen language model to modulate user-context influence at each generation step, trained from an oracle context-conditioned LM. It enables per-token personalization without per-user fine-tuning, with lower inference cost and strong generalization across unseen users and domains; shows improvements in-domain and on four OOD personalization benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-user adapters and reward-model approaches suffer from data sparsity and poor generalization to unseen users. While ICL and Context Steering leverage pretrained capabilities, they either do not adapt influence across decoding steps or require extra passes. CCS aims to enable adaptive, per-token personalization efficiently within a frozen backbone.

Method: Attach a lightweight adapter to a frozen backbone LM. Train the adapter to decide, at each token, whether and how strongly user context should affect generation, using an oracle context-conditioned LM as the teacher. The adapter preserves the base LM when context is not helpful. A single CCS adapter trained on one dataset improves both in-domain and out-of-distribution personalization. CCS avoids per-user fine-tuning and the extra context-conditioned forward pass required by CoS, reducing inference cost.

Result: Generation quality improves on in-domain data and across four out-of-distribution personalization benchmarks. CCS demonstrates robust generalization to new users and domains. Inference cost is substantially reduced compared with Context Steering, since CCS requires no per-user fine-tuning and no additional per-token forward pass beyond the backbone LM.

Conclusion: CCS provides efficient, generalizable personalization by adaptively modulating user-context influence through a small adapter on a frozen LM. It delivers performance gains with minimal data, good cross-domain transfer, and lower inference cost compared with existing adaptive methods like CoS.

Abstract: Personalizing language models (LMs) to individual user preferences is essential for aligning responses with diverse goals and backgrounds. Existing methods typically train a separate adapter for each user or learn a reward model whose scores depend on the user. Despite explicitly optimizing for each user, these methods must learn from limited observations and therefore suffer from data sparsity and poor generalization to unseen users and domains. In-context learning (ICL) and Context Steering (CoS) can instead provide more effective personalization by conditioning the base LM directly on user context and leveraging its pretrained capabilities without per-user training. Yet neither adapts the influence of that context across decoding steps: ICL leaves it uncontrolled, whereas CoS applies a fixed steering coefficient and requires two LM forward passes per step. We propose Cautious Context Steering (CCS), which adds a lightweight adapter to a frozen backbone LM to decide at each token whether and how strongly user context should affect generation. The adapter learns this behavior from an oracle context-conditioned LM and preserves the base LM when the context is not helpful. A single CCS adapter trained on only one dataset improves generation quality both in-domain and across four out-of-distribution personalization benchmarks, demonstrating robust generalization to new users and domains. CCS also avoids per-user fine-tuning and the additional context-conditioned forward pass required by CoS, substantially reducing inference cost.

</details>


### [119] [ViSR-KGC: Visual Subgraph Reasoning with Vision-Language Models for Multimodal Knowledge Graph Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05833)
*Jiafan Li,Mengxue Yang,Jiaqi Zhu,Liang Chang,Ying Li,Hongan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ViSR-KGC is a visual subgraph reasoning framework for multimodal knowledge graph completion that extracts a query-aware subgraph, renders it as a layouted image, and prompts a vision-language model with the subgraph image, entity visuals, and textual descriptions to infer the missing entity, thereby integrating topology, multimodal cues, and commonsense knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard embedding-based KGC struggles when relation-specific evidence is scarce; LLMs on linearized prompts lose graph topology and visual information; VLMs excel multimodal reasoning but cannot natively handle structured graphs. A unified approach that preserves topology, leverages multimodal cues, and injects commonsense is needed for MMKGC.

Method: 1) Learn multimodal embeddings to extract a compact, query-aware subgraph from the MMKG. 2) Transform the subgraph into a visually interpretable image using a layout strategy selected via empirical comparison. 3) Fuse the visualized subgraph, entity images, textual descriptions, and candidate answers into a single prompt for a vision-language model to infer the missing entity. 4) Emphasize three complementary capabilities: global topology dependencies via representation learning, local multimodal evidence via VLMs, and commonsense knowledge from pre-trained models.

Result: The paper outlines a full pipeline (ViSR-KGC) that bridges structured MMKG data with VLM reasoning by generating a visual, query-aware subgraph and using it in a unified prompt to a VLM. The abstract reports the approach and the empirical layout selection but does not present quantitative results.

Conclusion: ViSR-KGC offers a principled integration of topology-aware representation learning, multimodal evidence via VLMs, and pre-trained commonsense knowledge to enhance MMKGC. Further work likely includes empirical evaluation, ablations, and potential extensions to additional modalities.

Abstract: Knowledge graph completion (KGC) aims to infer missing entities or relations from incomplete graph structures, and has evolved into multimodal knowledge graph completion (MMKGC), where entities are associated with multiple modalities such as text and images. Traditional representation learning approaches follow the embedding-based paradigm and may struggle when relation-specific evidence is limited. Meanwhile, LLM-based reasoning methods typically linearize graph structures into textual prompts, which obscures structural topology and neglects vital visual information. While vision-language models (VLMs) excel at multimodal reasoning, they cannot natively interpret structured graph topology, particularly when it comes to knowledge graphs where nodes and edges carry complex semantics. To bridge this gap, we propose ViSR-KGC, a visual subgraph reasoning approach for KGC. It integrates three complementary capabilities to capture semantic correlations: identifying global topology dependencies via representation learning, analyzing local multimodal evidence using VLMs, and providing necessary commonsense knowledge inherent in pre-trained models. Based on learned multimodal embeddings, our framework first extracts a compact and query-aware subgraph from the MMKG. Then, this subgraph is transformed into a visually interpretable image using a layout strategy selected through empirical comparison.Finally, the visualized subgraph, entity images, textual descriptions, and candidate answers are combined into a unified prompt, enabling the VLM to infer the missing entity.

</details>


### [120] [Runtime Observability for Heterogeneous Attention Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05863)
*Fanzhe Wei,Li Liu,Ziyang Wang,Chenyu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A runtime observability contract is proposed to cover diverse memory architectures in large models, with a risk ledger that aggregates per-stage bounds across configurations and a machine-adjudicated, tiered certification for claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ensure reliability, verifiability, and reproducibility of memory systems in modern LLMs under compression and concurrency by unifying disparate memory forms under a formal contract and executable risk ledger.

Method: Define a runtime observability contract consisting of three operators that span four memory classes (plain KV cache, latent caches, learned sparse selectors, recurrent states). Instantiate across six model configurations and five architectures; compose per-stage bounds into a request-level risk ledger; manage metric compatibility via formal bridges; run large-scale experiments (12.4M reads, eight-way concurrency); deploy a fused always-on probe under CUDA graphs; apply to a DeepSeek-V4 stack with a packed compressed-KV prototype; release artifacts with reproducible numbers.

Result: An executable risk ledger that quantifies safe trade-offs and maintains a zero-violation risk budget under observed workloads; precise localization of a silent corruption to a structural boundary in eviction-free, identity-isolated regime; machine-adjudicated discrimination adjudges the inferences, correcting some misinterpretations; comprehensive artifacts allow regeneration of all reported numbers.

Conclusion: Contracts provide a tiered, machine-adjudicated credibility framework (fully/partially empirical) with weakest-tier inheritance across composition; the framework enables precise fault localization in memory subsystems of deployed models and supports reproducibility via shipped tooling.

Abstract: Modern models no longer keep a plain KV cache: latent caches, learned sparse selectors and recurrent states each carry the model's memory in a different form, and each fails differently under compression. We give a runtime observability contract that covers all four memory classes with three operators, instantiate it on six model configurations across five architecture families, and compose the per-stage bounds into an executable request-level risk ledger. Contracts carry their error metric as a type -- composition is only defined when metrics match, and this check rejected our own first composed chain; the repaired chain crosses metrics through two proved bridges, and whatever no formal system can certify is measured instead, dropping the composed tier to empirical automatically: every claim is certified, partially certified, or empirical, composition inherits the weakest tier, and the tier is decided by the machine. Replayed over $12.4$M entry reads and run under eight-way concurrency with per-request budgets and fail-closed identity attribution, the ledger quantifies the honest trade-off on today's witness and holds its risk budget with zero violations. A fused always-on probe observes a declared one-layer subset under CUDA graphs inside the serving noise floor. Applied to a served DeepSeek-V4 stack with a packed compressed-KV prototype, the same machinery localizes a silent corruption to a precise structural boundary -- exact in the eviction-free, identity-isolated regime, with every observed failure in an eviction or slot-reuse regime -- through a machine-adjudicated discrimination campaign whose calculus rejected two of our own confounded inferences along the way. All artifacts, guards, and the Lean development are released at https://github.com/metask-ai/witprobe-attention-memory; every number in this paper regenerates from the shipped artifacts by one command.

</details>


### [121] [Seeing Is Not Deciding: Can Multimodal LLMs Act as Effective CEOs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05864)
*Yuyang Dai,Xueqing Peng,Yuxia Wang,Preslav Nakov,Zhuohan Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multimodal benchmarking shows visual data improves reasoning but degrades constrained action; proposes selective grounding


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether LLMs can use visual business evidence in executive decisions; address gap in text-only benchmarks; quantify impact across tasks and models

Method: Introduce C-SUITEBENCH: five decision tasks, 50 scenarios, paired text-only and multimodal conditions; nine frontier models acting as CEO; evaluate decision quality; ablation analyses on signal crowding and decoding constraints

Result: Modal inputs improve evidence-centric reasoning; largest gains in risk forecasting and board justification; paradox: visual data degrades constrained resource allocation; ablations show signal crowding issue; individual channels help; visual grounding improves but combination disrupts constraint satisfaction

Conclusion: Visual perception and constrained action are separable bottlenecks; indiscriminate visual augmentation can harm high-stakes decisions; call for selective grounding strategies

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly applied as autonomous decision-making agents. However, in executive business decisions, existing benchmarks are limited to textonly settings. This makes it unclear whether models can perceive visual business evidence and effectively integrate it to improve decision quality. We introduce C-SUITEBENCH, a controlled multimodal benchmark that includes five decision tasks under paired text-only and multimodal conditions across 50 scenarios. We place nine frontier models in the role of a chief executive officer and evaluate their decision-making ability. Multimodal inputs consistently improve evidence-centric reasoning, with the largest and most reliable gains appearing in risk forecasting and board-facing justification. However, we uncover a multimodal integration paradox: adding visual business information degrades constrained resource allocation for all nine models, even as visual grounding itself improves. Ablation experiments reveal that this failure emerges from signal crowding, although each visual channel helps individually, their combination disrupts constraint satisfaction during decoding. These findings demonstrate that visual perception and constrained action are separable bottlenecks in multimodal agents, and that indiscriminate visual augmentation can harm high-stakes decision making, motivating selective grounding strategies for future executive AI systems.

</details>


### [122] [Improving Interoperability among Defence and National Security Ontologies: Analysis and Evaluation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05867)
*Jonathon Dilworth,Pedro Giesteira Cotovio,David Herron,Paul Cripps,Nigel Dewdney,Catia Pesquita,Ernesto Jiménez-Ruiz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Surveys 60+ defense/national security ontologies, highlights interoperability challenges, and introduces a new OAEI track with eight tasks, consensus alignments, and a silver-standard of manually-curated mappings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interoperability across heterogeneous defense and national security ontologies is essential for effective information sharing and situational awareness, yet is hampered by domain breadth and specialization.

Method: Systematically analyze and document over 60 publicly available ontologies; introduce a new OAEI track comprising eight ontology-matching tasks; generate consensus alignments by aggregating outputs from multiple state-of-the-art ontology alignment systems; create a silver-standard by manual validation of the consensus alignment plus a subset of mappings proposed by individual systems.

Result: Provision of a new evaluation track within OAEI with eight tasks, along with consensus alignments and a silver-standard mapping set derived from manual validation and selective expert curation; a comprehensive catalog of defense-related ontologies.

Conclusion: Interoperability in defense and national security ontologies is challenging but tractable through standardized evaluation resources; the paper contributes an actionable track, tasks, and silver-standard mappings to advance ontology alignment research in this domain.

Abstract: The use of ontologies and knowledge graphs is becoming increasingly widespread in the defence and national security domain. Numerous ontologies have been developed through initiatives led by academia, industry, and government. Achieving interoperability across diverse defence and national security ontologies remains a major challenge due to the domain's breadth and specialisation. In this work, we analyse and document over 60 publicly available ontologies and introduce a new track for the Ontology Alignment Evaluation Initiative (OAEI). This track comprises eight matching tasks, consensus alignments and manually-curated (silver-standard) mappings. The consensus alignments are derived by aggregating the outputs of several state-of-the-art ontology alignment systems. The silver-standard is obtained from the manual validation of the consensus alignment together with a subset of the unique mappings (i.e., mappings suggested by only one system).

</details>


### [123] [Personalized Deep Research Query Refinement with Graph-Scaffolded Evidence Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05876)
*Soojin Yoon,Dongha Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: G-STEER refines user requests into personalized research specifications for deep research agents using an Intent Elicitation Graph and a learned clarification policy to balance evidence costs and target coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable deep research agents to tailor evidence gathering to user goals, constraints, preferences, and evaluation criteria by refining the input request and selecting relevant framing factors and evidence strategies.

Method: Introduce an Intent Elicitation Graph that encodes dependencies among framing factors.
