Train a clarification policy from graph-scaffolded trajectories to decide which factors to elicit, whether to retrieve memory, ask the user, or refine the query. The policy optimizes refined queries for target coverage while accounting for evidence acquisition costs.

Result: G-STEER achieves the strongest overall weighted target coverage and highest downstream report personalization across two evaluated deep research agents, while asking roughly one third as many user questions as a strong clarification baseline.

Conclusion: G-STEER provides an effective mechanism for personalizing deep research by structured elicitation of user framing factors, enabling efficient, cost-aware clarification and improved downstream personalization.

Abstract: User requests serve as research specifications for deep research agents, shaping what evidence to seek and how to synthesize it. In personalized deep research, these specifications must additionally reflect user goals, constraints, preferences, and evaluation criteria. User context can be incorporated either within the deep research pipeline or into the research specification provided as its input. We focus on the latter, refining the user request into a personalized research specification before passing it to an unchanged deep research agent. This requires resolving three coupled decisions: which framing factors are relevant, whether the available user context sufficiently supports them, and whether to retrieve user memory, ask the user, or stop and refine the query. For training, G-STEER organizes framing factors as elicitation targets in an Intent Elicitation Graph that captures their dependencies. It learns a clarification policy from graph-scaffolded trajectories spanning diverse factor dependencies and evidence conditions. The policy produces a refined query while balancing target coverage against the costs of evidence acquisition. Experiments show that G-STEER achieves the strongest overall weighted target coverage and the highest downstream report personalization across both evaluated DRAs, while asking roughly one third as many user questions as a strong clarification baseline.

</details>


### [124] [AppDeltaWorld: Transition-Grounded Delta Code World Model for Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05891)
*Weikai Xu,Yunren Feng,Haoxiang Lei,Kun Huang,Yuxuan Liu,Kang Zhao,Xiaolin Hu,Shuo Shang,Bo An*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A transition-grounded delta code world model (AppDeltaWorld) predicts GUI changes as executable code updates, enabling high-fidelity world modeling and improved policy learning for mobile GUI agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real trajectories are difficult to obtain due to privacy and sensitive apps; existing simulators are costly to scale; GUI world models suffer unstable generation, limited modality coverage, and inconsistent action-transition logic.

Method: AppDeltaWorld retrieves app-specific Level-1 HTML references under an action-transition constraint, generates Level-2 executable HTML conditioned on the current screen, action, predicted next-screen text, and retrieved structure, and inserts generated visual assets into image slots before browser rendering. It uses a training regime with filtered closed-loop SFT data and supports test-time RL via a world-model.

Result: Achieves the highest fidelity on CMGUIBench-500 under Code2World evaluation, with clear gains in structural layout and UI element reconstruction over image-only and code-only baselines. Attains state-of-the-art performance on AndroidLens and consistent gains on MobileGym and MobileWorld; world-model-based test-time RL further improves policy adaptation.

Conclusion: Grounding GUI world modeling in delta-code transitions yields scalable, high-fidelity models that improve data efficiency and downstream policy learning, including test-time reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents can operate apps through pixel perception and touch actions, making them a promising interface for collecting and improving long-horizon mobile interaction policies. However, real trajectories are difficult to obtain for sensitive apps and privacy-critical operations. At the same time, existing simulated environments are costly to scale up, and GUI world models still suffer from unstable generation, limited modality coverage, and inconsistent action-transition logic. To address these limitations, we propose AppDeltaWorld, a transition-grounded delta code world model that predicts the next GUI as a reachable code update rather than as an unconstrained image or text description. AppDeltaWorld retrieves app-specific Level-1 HTML references under an action-transition constraint, generates Level-2 executable HTML conditioned on the current screen, action, predicted next-screen text, and retrieved structure, and inserts generated visual assets into image slots before browser rendering. As a world model, AppDeltaWorld achieves the highest fidelity on CMGUIBench-500 under Code2World evaluation, with clear gains in structural layout and UI element reconstruction over image-only and code-only baselines. As a training environment, AppDeltaWorld supports filtered closed-loop SFT data construction that, when combined with public supervision, enables AppDeltaAgent to achieve state-of-the-art performance on AndroidLens and consistent gains on MobileGym and MobileWorld. Moreover, world-model-based test-time reinforcement learning enables policy adaptation and shows further improvements without additional interaction with real apps.

</details>


### [125] [ECG-LENS: Lead-Aware Clinical Context Enriched ECG Report Generation and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05893)
*Akanta Das,Tasinul Islam Ahon,Ahmed Mahir Sultan Rumi,Md Mahbubur Rahman,Tausif Amim Shadly,Tanzima Hashem*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ECG-LENS is an end-to-end ECG report-generation framework that fuses multi-lead ECG representations with clinically grounded prompts to guide a GPT-2 decoder, plus an ECG-specific metric F1-ECGBERT, achieving improved METEOR, ROUGE-L, and F1-ECGBERT scores on PTB-XL and MIMIC-IV-ECG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transforming multi-lead ECG signals into reliable, clinically meaningful reports is challenging due to the need to analyze subtle temporal morphologies and produce dense clinical language; existing methods emphasize classification or produce unusable reports.

Method: Lead-wise encoders preserve local waveform morphology and a global encoder captures inter-lead dependencies. Signals are fused with clinically enriched textual prompts to condition a GPT-2 decoder. An ECG-specific report-preprocessing strategy focuses on clinically meaningful findings. A new ECG-specific metric, F1-ECGBERT, measures agreement between diagnostic labels from generated and reference reports.

Result: ECG-LENS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with absolute gains of 4.0% (METEOR), 6.3% (ROUGE-L), and 11.5% (F1-ECGBERT) on PTB-XL and cross-domain MIMIC-IV-ECG evaluation.

Conclusion: The framework advances automated ECG report generation by integrating signal modeling with clinically grounded text generation and a domain-specific evaluation metric, demonstrating improved cross-domain performance.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG) is one of the most widely used non-invasive tools for diagnosing cardiovascular disease, but transforming multi-lead ECG recordings into reliable clinical reports remains challenging. Automating ECG report generation could reduce clinicians' interpretive workload, improve diagnostic efficiency, and expand access to cardiac assessment in underserved communities. Unlike image-based report-generation tasks, ECG interpretation requires the analysis of subtle temporal morphologies, followed by coherent diagnostic reasoning expressed in dense clinical terminology. Existing systems predominantly focus on classification, while current report-generation methods often produce outputs that remain inadequate for practical clinical use. To address these challenges, we propose ECG-LENS, an end-to-end ECG report-generation framework that jointly integrates multi-lead signal modeling, diagnosis-aware representations, and clinically grounded text generation. ECG-LENS combines lead-wise encoders that preserve localized waveform morphology with a global encoder that captures inter-lead dependencies. To guide report generation, we fuse signal representations with clinically enriched textual prompts that condition a GPT-2 decoder. We further introduce an ECG-specific report-preprocessing strategy that helps the model focus on clinically meaningful findings. Finally, because lexical metrics may under- or overestimate report quality, we propose F1-ECGBERT, a BERT-based, ECG-specific metric that measures agreement between diagnostic labels extracted from generated and reference reports. In-domain experiments on PTB-XL and cross-domain evaluation on MIMIC-IV-ECG show that ECG-LENS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods, with absolute gains of 4.0%, 6.3%, and 11.5% in METEOR, ROUGE-L, and F1-ECGBERT, respectively, over the strongest baselines.

</details>


### [126] [GSBF: Gaussian Splatting for Environment-Aware Beamforming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05896)
*Yijie Bian,Wei Guo,Zixin Wang,Shenghui Song,Jun Zhang,Khaled B. Letaief*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Environment-aware MIMO beamforming via 3D Gaussian splatting (GSBF) renders an angular propagator map from AP pose/user position, enabling direct constant-modulus beamforming without instantaneous CSI, and outperforms exhaustive beam alignment with lower latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce pilot overhead and computational complexity of conventional beamforming that relies on accurate instantaneous CSI by leveraging geometry and multi-modal environmental information.

Method: Model the environment with a persistent 3D Gaussian representation using reciprocity-preserving bidirectional spherical Gaussian (Bi-SG) kernels. Use two-sided electromagnetic rasterization to render an angular propagator map, then aggregate with an over-complete array-manifold dictionary and project to constant-modulus beamformers, synthesizing beams from AP pose and user position without online CSI.

Result: Simulations show GSBF consistently outperforms baselines such as exhaustive beam alignment (EBA) with lower latency.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the viability of geometry- and environment-driven beamforming (GSBF) that eliminates the need for online instantaneous CSI and reduces latency, at the cost of relying on accurate environment representation.

Abstract: Beamforming plays a key role in multiple-input-multiple-output (MIMO) communication systems. However, conventional beamforming design normally requires accurate instantaneous channel state information (CSI) and iterative optimization, which incur substantial pilot overhead and computational complexity. Recognizing that radio propagation is intrinsically governed by the physical geometry, we develop a 3D Gaussian splatting for environment-aware beamforming (GSBF) pipeline based on multi-modal data, which characterizes the environment through a persistent 3D Gaussian representation. Specifically, GSBF models the environmental scattering response with reciprocity-preserving bidirectional spherical Gaussian (Bi-SG) kernels and performs two-sided electromagnetic rasterization to render an angular propagator map. The rendered map is then aggregated through an over-complete array-manifold dictionary and projected to the constant-modulus beamformers, thereby synthesizing beams directly from the access point (AP) pose and user position without online instantaneous CSI. Simulations demonstrate that GSBF consistently outperforms baselines such as exhaustive beam alignment (EBA) with lower latency.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [127] [MS-MLB: An Open Machine Learning Benchmark for Blood-Based MS Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05196)
*Adam Simson,Ankush Dutta,Quang Bui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MS-MLB is an open, reproducible benchmark for MS vs healthy control classification from GSE17048 whole-blood RNA, using nested CV and a documented submission pathway; gradient boosting achieved top MS Research Score 93.83 with high AUC-ROC 0.989, but clinical validation is lacking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a transparent, reusable framework to evaluate ML methods on a publicly available MS blood transcriptomic dataset, enabling fair comparisons and leakage control; address reproducibility concerns in prior work.

Method: Convert GSE17048 to an MS vs HC task; implement leakage-controlled pipeline; nested cross-validation; untouched holdout set; bootstrap CIs; ROC/PR analysis; calibration; MS Research Score; external model submission pathway.

Result: Gradient Boosting ranked first on holdout with MS Research Score 93.83; AUC-ROC 0.989; sensitivity 0.950; specificity 0.778; F1 0.927; Brier 0.050; prior studies exist; not clinically validated; benchmark is open.

Conclusion: MS-MLB provides a reproducible benchmark with a documented model submission pathway; useful for research comparisons but must be clinically validated before translation.

Abstract: Multiple sclerosis (MS) is diagnosed through clinical assessment, magnetic resonance imaging, laboratory evidence when appropriate, and exclusion of better explanations. Blood RNA expression data may contain disease associated immune signal, but a blood RNA classifier cannot be treated as a replacement for clinical diagnosis. This paper presents MS-MLB (Multiple Sclerosis Machine Learning Benchmark), a reproducible open benchmark for machine learning based MS research classification from whole blood RNA expression data. MS-MLB uses the public GSE17048 cohort, converts it into an MS versus healthy control task, and evaluates multiple algorithms under a shared, leakage controlled pipeline that a researcher can rerun without reconfiguring the evaluation. The evaluation includes nested cross-validation, an untouched stratified holdout set, bootstrap confidence intervals, ROC and precision recall analysis, calibration measurement, and an exploratory MS Research Score. In the final benchmark summary, Gradient Boosting ranked first by MS Research Score on the holdout set, with an MS Research Score of 93.83, AUC-ROC of 0.989, sensitivity of 0.950, specificity of 0.778, $F_{1}$ score of 0.927, and Brier score of 0.050. Prior studies have applied machine learning to MS blood transcriptomic data, including PBMC stage classification and whole blood diagnostic signature modeling. The contribution here is different and narrower. To our knowledge, MS-MLB is the first open benchmark focused on MS versus healthy control classification from GSE17048 whole blood RNA expression data with a documented external model submission pathway built into the framework. The score is intended for research comparison only and has not been clinically validated. The benchmark is accessible here: https://github.com/duckyquang/MS-MLB.

</details>


### [128] [When Do Corrective Features Help? An Agent for Corrective Feature Discovery on Black-Box Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05207)
*Fangxin Wang,Ziyi Zhang,Diyi Zhuang,Langzhou He,Shiyu Wang,Baichuan Mo,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CRAFTER: a post-hoc corrective framework that preserves a frozen backbone while mining its residual with two generators—compositional input-channel search and an LLM-based feature proposer—plus a validation gate, to outperform dedicated feature-engineering methods across multiple datasets and backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frozen forecasters exhibit structured, recurring failures and are costly to repair via fine-tuning; there is a need for lightweight, interpretable corrective features that target the model-failure process rather than the data-generating process.

Method: CRAFTER employs two complementary generators to mine corrective residual features: (1) a compositional search over raw input channels, and (2) an LLM that proposes named feature combinations, binary flags, and short executable code. A validation-grounded gate evaluates each candidate from either source, and a post-hoc corrector applies accepted features while leaving the backbone unchanged. The pipeline is source-agnostic, enabling fair comparison with prior feature-engineering systems.

Result: Across six public datasets and six frozen backbones, CRAFTER surpasses every dedicated feature-engineering system at every feature budget, roughly doubling the improvement achieved by the corrector alone and reducing the error of the weakest backbones by up to 27%. Gains are robust across different LLM backends and persist when applied on top of fine-tuned backbones.

Conclusion: CRAFTER provides a robust, source-agnostic post-hoc correction mechanism that leverages corrective residual features to substantially improve frozen forecasters without altering their parameters; its dual-generation strategy and validation gate enable strong performance across diverse settings.

Abstract: Frozen pretrained forecasters often fail in structured, recurring ways that are costly to repair through fine-tuning. We study corrective feature discovery: mining interpretable features of a frozen forecaster's residual to drive a lightweight post-hoc corrector. Prior automated feature engineering models the data-generating process; corrective features instead model the model-failure process. We present CRAFTER (Corrective Residual Agent with Feature-based Temporal Exploration and Reasoning), which keeps the backbone frozen and mines its residual with two complementary generators: a compositional search over the raw input channels, and a large language model (LLM) that proposes named feature combinations, binary flags, and short executable code. A single validation-grounded gate accepts or rejects every candidate regardless of its origin, and a validation-selected corrector applies the accepted features or leaves the forecast unchanged. This source-agnostic pipeline also allows prior feature-engineering systems to be evaluated under identical conditions, making CRAFTER an instrument for attributing forecast improvements to the feature source alone. Across six public datasets and six frozen backbones, CRAFTER surpasses every dedicated feature-engineering system at every feature budget, roughly doubling the improvement achieved by the corrector alone and reducing the error of the weakest backbones by up to 27%. These gains are robust across different LLM backends and persist even when applied on top of fine-tuned backbones.

</details>


### [129] [PPDL: LLM-Based Flows as Probabilistic Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05234)
*Louis Mandel,Guillaume Baudart,Mandana Vaziri,Martin Hirzel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a probabilistic language to quantify and propagate uncertainty in LLM-driven flows, enabling uncertainty-aware decision making and scaling experiments without extra coding; validated via experiments and a Rocq theorem prover case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs lack calibrated confidence and multi-step flows accumulate uncertainty; need a formal framework to track, propagate, and compare uncertainty sources in LLM-based applications.

Method: Introduce a probabilistic language for LLM-based flows; supports uncertainty propagation and scalable inference techniques; allows experimentation without altering the flow logic; demonstrates with experiments and a Rocq theorem prover agent.

Result: Experimental study demonstrates capability to quantify/propagate uncertainty; case study shows building a theorem proving agent for Rocq; results indicate practical viability of uncertainty-aware flows.

Conclusion: This framework enables reliable, tunable uncertainty management in LLM-enabled pipelines and facilitates comparison of inference strategies, without additional coding, advancing trustworthy AI applications.

Abstract: Building reliable applications that leverage large language models (LLMs) remains a significant challenge. While LLMs offer impressive capabilities across diverse tasks, their outputs often lack accuracy and provide no clear measure of confidence. This uncertainty compounds in flows of multiple calls to LLMs and other tools, making it difficult for developers and end-users to trust the results. This paper introduces a probabilistic language for programming LLM-based flows. It enables developers to quantify and propagate uncertainty throughout the application's flow, and experiment with different inference scaling techniques without adding a single line of code beyond the flow's logic. We present an experimental study to demonstrate this capability, and a case study building a theorem proving agent for the Rocq theorem prover.

</details>


### [130] [Decoupling Perception from Description: Computation-Grounded Representation Alignment between Multivariate Time Series and Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05238)
*Xinran Feng,Yi Xie,Chao Zhang,Ruikun Li,Wanyun Ling,Ziyue Li,Chenxi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computation-grounded time-series–language model (CGTime) decouples perception from description to overcome self-supervision limits in multivariate time-series understanding, yielding superior multivariate factuality and captions with a 4B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the self-supervision trap in multimodal time-series learning, where labels are bounded by the perceptual ability of the labeler and cross-variable patterns require multivariate data, leading to a reliability-realism-scalability trilemma.

Method: Decouple perception from description by deterministically computing a set of statistics from real, open-source multivariate series, then having an LLM verbalize those precomputed facts. Perception is handled by computation; description by the LLM.

Result: CGTime (4B) outperforms far larger general-purpose models on multivariate understanding tasks, achieving the best multivariate fact score of 0.283 on a held-out benchmark, versus 0.173 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.203 for GPT-5.4-nano, with Holm-corrected paired significance tests against every baseline. It also produces captions with verifiable numerical facts and covers a broader range of statistical properties.

Conclusion: Decoupling perception from description enables a computation-grounded model to achieve strong multivariate understanding with a smaller, tractable model, addressing the trilemma of reliability, realism, and scalability.

Abstract: Training multimodal models to align time series with language runs into a self-supervision trap. The usual recipe asks an LLM to read a series and write a description, so label quality is capped by the perceptual skill the model is supposed to learn. The data can never teach more than the labeler already knows. A second gap makes this worse: most datasets use a single variable, but the patterns that matter (cross-channel correlation, lead-lag structure, co-occurring anomalies) appear only with several variables, right where the labeling LLM's limits are most exposed. These two problems create a trilemma: existing methods are reliable, realistic, or scalable, but none achieves all three. We resolve this by decoupling perception from description. Deterministic code computes a set of statistics from real, open-source multivariate series; the LLM verbalizes those precomputed facts. Perception, which LLMs do poorly, is handled by computation, while the LLM handles expression. This produces CGTime, our 4B-parameter computation-grounded time-series-language model. CGTime outperforms far larger general-purpose models on multivariate understanding tasks: it attains the best multivariate fact score on our held-out benchmark (0.283 vs. 0.173 for GPT-4o-mini and 0.203 for GPT-5.4-nano), a gap that survives Holm-corrected paired significance tests against every baseline. It also states verifiable numerical facts in generated captions more accurately and covers a broader range of statistical properties.

</details>


### [131] [Disentangling 3D Modeling from Spatial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05242)
*Haoze Sun,Jiequan Cui,Qingshan Xu,Richang Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DiSR disentangles 3D perception from reasoning by constructing explicit 3D evidence from perception models and fine-tuning an LLM with LoRA to reason over that evidence, yielding competitive spatial reasoning with better interpretability and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Leverage the complementary strengths of perception models (accurate 3D geometry) and LLMs (compositional, symbolic reasoning) while avoiding large-scale end-to-end 3D VQA training and complex tool-use policies.

Method: Builds structured 3D evidence from off-the-shelf perception models that estimate geometry; fine-tunes an LLM using LoRA to perform reasoning solely over the explicit geometric evidence; does not require end-to-end 3D VQA training.

Result: Achieves competitive performance on standard spatial-reasoning benchmarks; offers improved interpretability, modularity, and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Explicitly separating perception and reasoning is a scalable and effective alternative to end-to-end modeling for spatial intelligence, with benefits in interpretability and modularity.

Abstract: In this work, we explore an alternative paradigm for spatial reasoning by explicitly disentangling 3D perception from reasoning, rather than jointly acquiring implicit 3D perception and reasoning through large-scale training. Our key observation is that modern perception models excel at estimating continuous 3D geometry, whereas large language models (LLMs) are particularly effective at compositional and symbolic reasoning. Motivated by these complementary strengths, we propose the Disentangled Spatial Reasoner (DiSR), a simple yet effective framework that reconstructs the physical world into structured 3D evidence using off-the-shelf expert perception models and fine-tunes an LLM with LoRA to perform reasoning solely over this explicit geometric evidence. Without large-scale 3D VQA training or complex tool-use policies, DiSR achieves competitive performance on popular spatial reasoning benchmarks. Beyond its strong performance, DiSR offers improved interpretability, modularity, and computational efficiency, demonstrating that explicit separation of perception and reasoning is a scalable and effective alternative paradigm to end-to-end modeling for spatial intelligence.

</details>


### [132] [Marginal Matching Does Not License Factorized Sampling: Auditing Conditional Style Leakage in Factorized Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05243)
*Duong Bach,Hai Nguyen Hong,Cuong Do*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Marginally matching a latent style z_s to a Gaussian prior does not guarantee independence from class labels; the style can remain highly predictive of the class despite Gaussian-looking marginals, revealing leakage not captured by marginal statistics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenge the common claim in factorized generative modeling that enforcing a Gaussian prior on z_s enforces independence from class information; show that marginal constraints do not constrain class-conditional distributions and that factorization requires additional conditions.

Method: Theoretical decomposition identifying four conditions required for true factorized sampling; empirical evaluation with a case-study model and four latent baselines across perturbations; analysis of marginal MMD vs. class-label leakage using linear probes; exploration of mitigation strategies and post-hoc priors; two-dataset experiments (MNIST, CIFAR-10) with multiple baselines including an empirical style bank.

Result: Near-zero global MMD can coexist with strong class-information leakage (74–100% accuracy from a linear probe; 99.15% clustering accuracy); externally evaluated class-conditional generation is poor (16%); leakage persists under multiple perturbations; several mitigations reduce probe accuracy to 21–46% but do not fully erase within-class dependence; post-hoc conditional priors improve MNIST but not CIFAR-10; style bank achieves 0.88 on CIFAR-10.

Conclusion: Marginal-distribution metrics alone cannot certify independence between style latent and class labels; eliminating the identified mismatch is necessary but not sufficient for factorization; robust verification requires conditioning on class information and addressing conditional distributions, not just marginal statistics.

Abstract: Factorized generative models commonly regularize a latent style variable z_s by matching its marginal distribution to a fixed Gaussian prior and interpret this as evidence that the style representation is independent of class information. We show that this interpretation is incorrect. Matching only the marginal distribution places no constraint on the class-conditional distributions, allowing the latent style to remain highly predictive of the label despite appearing perfectly Gaussian in aggregate. We derive an exact decomposition showing that this mismatch is one of four conditions required for factorized sampling, and demonstrate that eliminating it is necessary but not sufficient to obtain the intended factorization.
  Empirically, our case-study model and four representative latent baselines achieve near-zero global MMD while still allowing a linear probe to recover class labels with 74%--100% accuracy (10% chance level). Our model reaches 99.15% clustering accuracy, whereas externally evaluated class-conditional generation succeeds only 16% of the time. This leakage remains under six independent perturbations involving model capacity, curriculum, prior geometry, and supervision across two datasets. Four mitigation strategies reduce probe accuracy to 21%--46%, although they leave within-class dependence largely unchanged. A post-hoc conditional prior improves externally evaluated class generation to 0.97 on MNIST without retraining but reaches only 0.41 on CIFAR-10, while an empirical style bank achieves 0.88 on CIFAR-10. These results demonstrate that no divergence computed solely on the marginal distribution of the style latent can certify independence from class labels, and that reporting marginal statistics alone does not verify the property commonly claimed in factorized generative models.

</details>


### [133] [PRISM: Priority-aware Rubric Internalization via Structured Multimodal Data Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05249)
*Xiaomin He,Dongling Xiao,Jiahao Xie,Ruiqi Lu,Qianle Wang,Zhongbin Guo,Wanxuan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reframes multimodal instruction following as rubric-based verification (executor) and presents PRISM for data synthesis and PRISM-Eval for deterministic evaluation; shows substantial Strict accuracy gains with 10k synthetic samples while preserving general performance and transferring across models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap that real-world multimodal instructions bundle multiple, unevenly important requirements; existing data reduces to a single-question paradigm, hindering multi-rule, priority-aware instruction following.

Method: PRISM: four-stage data synthesis — (1) persona-task pairs, (2) prefix-guided rule sets, (3) quality-filtered rubrics, (4) structured verification traces; PRISM-Eval with Loose and Strict metrics using fixed labels to avoid inference-time judges.

Result: On Qwen3-VL-4B, Strict accuracy improves from 9.5% to 30.1% on PRISM-Eval; 10k samples; average benchmark performance preserved; gains transfer to four other open-source MLLMs across dense and Mixture-of-Experts architectures.

Conclusion: Structured rubric supervision is a scalable path toward multi-rule, priority-aware multimodal instruction following.

Abstract: Real-world multimodal instructions often bundle multiple requirements with unequal importance, yet most multimodal training data still reduce instruction following to answering one self-contained question. We study this gap through \textbf{rubric comprehension}, which casts the model not as a generator measured against rubrics but as an \textbf{executor} that follows them: given an image and a typed, prioritized rubric, the model must verify each rule before producing an overall judgment. To support this setting, we propose \textbf{PRISM}, a four-stage data synthesis framework that produces persona--task pairs, prefix-guided rule sets, quality-filtered rubrics, and structured verification traces. We further introduce \textbf{PRISM-Eval}, whose Loose and Strict metrics use deterministic matching against fixed labels and therefore require no inference-time judge model. With only 10K synthesized samples, PRISM lifts Qwen3-VL-4B from 9.5\% to 30.1\% Strict accuracy on PRISM-Eval while preserving average performance on general benchmarks, and the gains transfer to four additional open-source MLLMs across dense and MoE architectures, suggesting that structured rubric supervision is a scalable path toward multi-rule, priority-aware multimodal instruction following.

</details>


### [134] [Beyond Full-Model Rollback: AuroSFT for Adapter-State Multi-Task Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05250)
*Yue Han,Ziniu Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AuroSFT is a parameter-efficient, mergeable adapter-based solution for overfitting-aware multi-task SFT that outperforms msft while keeping the backbone frozen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: msft treats heterogeneous multitask data as a single optimization, causing suboptimal generalization timing for different tasks; full-model checkpoints for stage changes are costly.

Method: Freeze pretrained backbone; train injected adapters with an AuroRA-inspired adaptive nonlinear layer on a low-rank factor; rollback adapter checkpoints at task peaks; adapters are linear in input, rank-bounded, and mergeable into the frozen projection.

Result: 61.36% average accuracy vs 59.85% for msft across five backbones; higher accuracy on all backbones; code available.

Conclusion: AuroSFT delivers a compact, mergeable, backbone-freezing framework for multitask SFT that improves performance and reduces deployment/storage overhead compared with msft.

Abstract: Multi-task supervised fine-tuning (SFT) often casts a heterogeneous data mixture as a single optimization problem, even though different tasks may reach their best generalization at different times. msft exposes this mismatch through task-wise roll-out, exclusion, and rollback, but its original formulation materializes the scheduler state as full-model checkpoints, making stage transitions costly to store, restore, and deploy. This paper introduces AuroSFT, a parameter-efficient framework that recasts the carried state of overfitting-aware multi-task SFT as a compact, mergeable adapter state. AuroSFT freezes the pretrained backbone, trains only injected adapters, rolls back adapter checkpoints at task-wise peaks, and continues on the remaining active mixture. At the layer level, each adapter applies an AuroRA-inspired adaptive nonlinear layer to a low-rank weight factor rather than to the sample representation. The resulting update remains linear in the input, rank-bounded, and exactly mergeable into the frozen projection. Under the retained-backbone comparison protocol, AuroSFT achieves 61.36% average accuracy, compared with 59.85% for the corresponding msft reference row, and obtains higher accuracy on all five backbones. Our code is available at the anonymous repository: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/AuroSFT-80D1.

</details>


### [135] [Beyond Rotations: AuroOFT for Expressive Quantized Orthogonal Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05253)
*Yue Han,Dianlin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AuroOFT augments quantized orthogonal fine-tuning (qoft) with a zero-initialized, gated, low-rank nonlinear residual per adapted layer, enabling input-dependent nonlinear corrections while preserving quantization stability. It yields consistent Macro-6 gains over qoft (1.3–2.7%) and large gains over QLoRA (6.5–10.6%), with substantial trainable-parameter savings (32–45%), and maintains a quantization-compatible initialization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Qoft is restricted to linear orthogonal updates, which limits input-dependent nonlinear corrections. AuroOFT seeks to introduce expressive nonlinear residuals without breaking the stability and quantization compatibility of qoft.

Method: Attach a zero-start gated low-rank nonlinear residual to each adapted linear layer in qoft. Map activations to an RMS-normalized compact latent space and use adaptive nonlinear bases with bounded or token-dependent gating. The zero initialization keeps AuroOFT identical to qoft at start; orthogonality is a branch-level property, not of the full nonlinear layer.

Result: With matched data/optimization/decoding/parser protocols, AuroOFT improves Macro-6 over matched qoft by 1.30–2.70% on 1.5B/3B Qwen2.5; exceeds QLoRA by 6.52–10.62%; saves 32.3–44.7% trainable parameters vs QLoRA in representative scales. The small MC math set is used as a protocol-sensitivity diagnostic.

Conclusion: AuroOFT provides improved accuracy and parameter efficiency while preserving quantization compatibility and stability; code available at the anonymous repository.

Abstract: Quantized orthogonal fine-tuning (qoft) enables parameter-efficient adaptation of low-bit language models by learning structured activation rotations before frozen quantized weights. However, its task-specific updates remain constrained to linear orthogonal transformations, limiting input-dependent nonlinear corrections. We introduce AuroOFT, which keeps qoft as a stable quantization-compatible branch while attaching a zero-start gated low-rank nonlinear residual to each adapted linear layer. AuroOFT maps activations into an RMS-normalized compact latent space and uses adaptive nonlinear bases with bounded or token-dependent gating. The zero-initialized up projection makes AuroOFT functionally identical to qoft at initialization, while orthogonality remains a branch-level stability property rather than a property of the combined nonlinear layer. Under matched data, optimization, decoding, and parser protocols, AuroOFT improves Macro-6 over matched qoft by 1.30-2.70% on the 1.5B/3B Qwen2.5 settings, exceeds QLoRA by 6.52-10.62%, and saves 32.3-44.7% trainable parameters relative to QLoRA in representative scales. The small exam-style multiple-choice math set is treated only as a protocol-sensitivity diagnostic. Our code is available at the anonymous repository: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/AuroOFT-F3FD.

</details>


### [136] [An Emerging Retail Portfolio Management Application: Personalized, Tax-Aware Reinforcement Learning with Natural Language Goals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05255)
*Ramin Pishehvar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Integrated, RL-based retail portfolio advisor with live-broker integration and MoE/LORA personalization; pre-deployment validation with backtests and end-to-end testing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address lack of personalized, tax-aware investment management for retail investors and gap between static robo-advisors and institutional systems.

Method: Build an end-to-end FastAPI backend plus web dashboard; route user goals to six mandates; three-phase RL stack: self-supervised cross-asset encoder; Mixture-of-Experts allocation with learned intent router; LoRA adapter for user-specific personalization; live Alpaca paper trading; multi-user auth; trust-first confirm flow; daily emails; auditable integrity chain; 14-day walk-forward backtests.

Result: Functional, integration-tested system; not yet released to real users; validated with 14-day walk-forward backtests with bootstrapped CIs; documented engineering lessons and a concrete deployment path.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of a deployed, RL-driven, personalized portfolio system for retail investors and highlights practical considerations for applied RL with live data.

Abstract: Retail investors lack access to the kind of personalized, tax-aware portfolio management that institutional clients take for granted -- existing robo-advisors use static, rule-based allocation, and institutional-grade systems require account minimums and technology stacks unavailable to individual investors. We present a fully built, integration-tested application that closes this gap: a FastAPI backend and web dashboard that let a user describe an investment goal in plain language (e.g. "I want steady growth but need to sell some shares next month for a down payment"), routes that goal to one of six investment mandates, and produces a live, broker-integrated portfolio recommendation from athree-phase reinforcement learning system -- a self-supervised cross-asset encoder, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) allocation policy with a learned intent router, and a lightweight LoRA adapter that personalizes recommendations from an individual's revealed brokerage behavior without retraining the shared model. The system is functionally complete and integration-tested end-to-end against a live brokerage API (Alpaca, paper-trading mode), including multi-user authentication, a trust first preview-before-apply confirmation flow, daily email digests, and an auditable action-integrity chain, but has not yet been opened to real end-users; we report this honestly as an emerging, pre-deployment application with a concrete path to full deployment, alongside 14-day walk-forward backtests (bootstrapped confidence intervals included) as preliminary, pre-deployment validation rather than production performance. We also report several practical engineering lessons -- silently-inactive integration paths, hanging third-party API calls, and the value of end-to-end empirical verification over trusting checkpoint metadata -- that we believe generalize to other applied RL systems built on external, live data sources.

</details>


### [137] [Evaluating Machine Learning Models for Post-Wildfire Debris-Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05265)
*Quinn Ledingham,Zhengsen Xu,Yimin Zhu,Zack Dewis,Mabel Heffring,Saeid Taleghanidoozdoozan,Motasem Alkayid,Megan Greenwood,Lincoln Linlin Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic benchmarking of 15 machine learning models, including TabPFN, on post-wildfire debris-flow prediction with limited data. The study uses SHAP for interpretability and synthetic data augmentation to address data scarcity. TabPFN achieves the highest unaugmented threat score (~0.637), while short-duration rainfall and storm accumulation are the primary drivers; burn severity and terrain contribute less. Synthetic augmentation boosts performance for most models except CNN, with the largest gain among deep learning models (+0.041).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address data scarcity and feature overlap in post-wildfire debris-flow prediction by evaluating a broad set of ML models for predictive performance and interpretability, and by testing synthetic data augmentation.

Method: Benchmark 15 ML models (including TabPFN) on basin-scale post-wildfire debris-flow data from the western US using repeated stratified cross-validation. Evaluate via threat score. Use SHAP to identify feature importance. Test synthetic augmentation by generating TabPFN-based samples and re-evaluating models, comparing performance with and without augmentation.

Result: TabPFN yields the highest unaugmented threat score of 0.637, narrowly outperforming best tree-based models. SHAP identifies short-duration rainfall intensity and storm accumulation as the most influential features; burn severity and terrain features contribute less. Synthetic data augmentation improves all models except CNN, with the largest deep-learning boost of +0.041. The study presents a framework combining benchmarking, interpretable feature analysis, and augmentation to improve post-wildfire debris-flow prediction.

Conclusion: A comprehensive framework is established for improving post-wildfire debris-flow prediction by integrating rigorous model benchmarking, interpretable feature attribution, and synthetic data augmentation. Findings highlight key predictors (rainfall metrics) and demonstrate the potential of TabPFN and synthetic augmentation for data-scarce contexts.

Abstract: Prediction of post-wildfire debris flows is critical for mitigating hazards to communities, infrastructure, and resources during intense rainfall in recently burned areas. However, identifying reliable machine learning models is complicated by overlapping debris-flow and non-debris-flow events in feature space, the need for model interpretability, and limited training data. This paper addresses these challenges through a systematic evaluation of machine learning models in terms of predictive performance, feature importance, and synthetic data augmentation. Using basin-scale observations of post-wildfire debris-flow events across the western United States, we compare 15 models, including the Tabular Prior-Data Fitted Network (TabPFN). Repeated stratified cross-validation shows that TabPFN achieves the highest unaugmented performance with a threat score of 0.637, closely followed by the best tree-based models. SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) are used to identify the features driving predictions, revealing that short-duration rainfall intensity and storm accumulation consistently rank highest, while burn severity and terrain features contribute less. We further evaluate synthetic data augmentation using TabPFN-generated samples to address the scarcity of debris-flow observations. Synthetic augmentation improves the performance of all models except CNN, with the largest mean threat score increase of +0.041 among the deep learning models. By combining rigorous model benchmarking, interpretable feature analysis, and synthetic data augmentation, this work provides a comprehensive framework for improving post-wildfire debris-flow prediction.

</details>


### [138] [Rectifying Geometric Misalignment: Online Source-Free Adaptation for Class-Imbalanced EEG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05315)
*Shiwen Chu,Shanglin Li,Motoaki Kawanabe,Reinmar Kobler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces OSPDIM, a source-free online UDA method for EEG BCIs on the SPD (Riemannian) manifold to address label shift. It learns a tangent-space bias constrained by the manifold, optimized via information maximization, enabling on-the-fly correction without batch statistics and outperforming offline Riemannian baselines under severe class imbalance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Riemannian alignment methods (e.g., RCT) assume balanced class priors; in online BCI settings label distributions drift, causing geometric misalignment and degraded cross-subject/session transfer.

Method: Proposes OSPDIM: incorporate a manifold-constrained bias in tangent-space mapping and optimize it with information maximization to counteract skew due to label shift. Operates online, estimates and corrects geometric bias without relying on global batch statistics (source-free). Uses simulations on 2D SPD matrices and extensive motor-imagery datasets for evaluation.

Result: On 2D SPD simulations, OSPDIM rectifies misalignment where centering fails. In motor imagery datasets, it significantly outperforms standard Riemannian baselines, especially in online adaptation with severe class imbalance.

Conclusion: OSP DIM provides a robust, plug-and-play solution for online BCI adaptation under label shift, bridging the gap between theoretical Riemannian alignment and practical, dynamically imbalanced data streams.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) based Brain-Computer Interfaces (BCIs) often require unsupervised domain adaptation (UDA) to generalize across subjects and sessions. While Riemannian alignment methods like the Riemannian Centering Transformation (RCT) are effective for handling covariate shifts, they implicitly assume balanced class priors. However, in realistic online BCI scenarios, the label distributions vary dynamically (label shift), causing standard alignment techniques to geometrically misalign the target data distributions. In this work, we propose OSPDIM (Online SPD manifold information maximization), a source-free online UDA framework designed to address label shifts on the Riemannian manifold. OSPDIM introduces a manifold-constrained bias parameter into the tangent space mapping, which is optimized via information maximization to correct the geometric skew caused by imbalanced data streams. Unlike offline methods relying on global batch statistics, OSPDIM estimates and corrects geometric bias on-the-fly. Simulations on 2D SPD matrices visually demonstrate that OSPDIM successfully rectifies the misalignment where standard centering fails. Extensive experiments on multiple motor imagery datasets show that OSPDIM significantly outperforms standard Riemannian baselines, particularly in challenging online adaptation scenarios with severe class imbalance, offering a robust solution for practical, plug-and-play BCI systems.

</details>


### [139] [QEvict: Recoverable Quantized KV Eviction for Attention-Drift-Robust Long-Context Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05326)
*Ayushman Garg,Akshita Gupta,Shaswata Bhattacharya,Abhishek Gupta,Sandeep Kumar,Manoj Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: QEvict introduces a recoverable three-tier KV-cache eviction strategy for autoregressive LLM inference to reduce missed attention under fixed memory, by retaining high-confidence windows in full precision, quantizing intermediate recoverable windows, and deleting only the lowest-confidence windows; windows can be dequantized and promoted when they regain importance, improving context retention on long-context tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autoregressive LLM inference is memory-constrained by KV-cache size. Traditional eviction policies perform irreversible removals, risking loss of potentially useful states as decoding evolves. Token/window importance drifts during generation, making early evictions brittle and reducing long-context performance.

Method: Propose QEvict: a three-tier KV-cache with (1) full-precision high-confidence windows, (2) quantized recoverable intermediate windows, and (3) deleted-lowest-confidence windows. During decoding, cumulative attention updates window importance; when a quantized window becomes important again, it is dequantized and promoted to full precision. Under a fixed memory budget, this preserves broader historical context while keeping exact precision for the most important regions.

Result: Empirical evaluations across long-context understanding, retrieval, and reasoning benchmarks show QEvict consistently outperforms representative eviction and quantization baselines, reducing missed attention and improving information retention.

Conclusion: QEvict enables recoverable eviction under fixed memory budgets, balancing memory efficiency with preservation of historical context and exact precision for critical regions, leading to improved long-context performance across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Autoregressive large language model inference is increasingly constrained by the memory footprint of the Key-Value (KV) cache. A dominant line of work reduces this footprint by evicting tokens that appear unimportant under attention-derived scores. However, such policies make an implicit irreversible decision: once a token is evicted, it cannot become useful again. We show that this assumption is brittle during decoding. Token and window importance drift as generated queries evolve, causing standard eviction policies to permanently discard states that later receive substantial attention under the full-cache model. To characterize this behaviour, we introduce Future Missed Mass and Global LIR, two diagnostics that measure future attention assigned to discarded states and the reactivation of historically inactive regions. We propose QEvict, a three-tier KV-cache management scheme that replaces binary retain-or-delete eviction with recoverable eviction. QEvict maintains high-confidence windows in full precision, stores intermediate windows in a quantized recoverable tier, and deletes only the lowest-confidence windows. During decoding, cumulative attention scores update window importance and when a quantized window becomes important again, it is dequantized and promoted to the full-precision. Under a fixed memory budget, this design preserves broader historical context while retaining exact full precision for the most important regions. Across long-context understanding, retrieval, and reasoning benchmarks, QEvict consistently improves over representative eviction and quantization baselines, reducing missed attention and improving information retention

</details>


### [140] [DG-FedReuse: Proxy-Gradient-Gated Cached-Update Reuse with Matched Sparse Uplink Accounting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05358)
*Rahil Aftab,Vineet Kumar Rakesh,Soumya Mazumdar,Tapas Samanta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DG-FedReuse introduces a simulator-level client-update reuse mechanism based on age-decayed cached updates governed by a stochastic discrepancy proxy, with cache-age controls and adaptive Top-K representations, achieving substantial uplink savings but with limited evidence on generalization, runtime, or end-to-end benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce federated-learning communication by reusing stale updates to save uplink traffic while respecting freshness constraints and client heterogeneity.

Method: Propose DG-FedReuse: reuse cached updates when a stochastic head-gradient discrepancy proxy is below a round-dependent threshold; enforce a hard cache-age limit and minimum fresh-client quota; represent fresh updates with an adaptive per-tensor Top-K numerical-field scheme; evaluate on six image-classification datasets, 50 virtual clients, Dirichlet label heterogeneity (α=0.5), three seeds; compare at 90 rounds against matched Top-K FedAvg; explore checkpointing for archival evidence; assess downlink sensitivity.

Result: Uplink data-field savings of 83.36-85.42% vs 76.88% for Top-K FedAvg; seed-aligned accuracy differences range -5.29 to -0.14 percentage points. Best test accuracies under test-controlled checkpointing show -2.38 to +0.45 percentage points relative to matched FedAvg. Symmetric dense-model-downlink effects reduce savings to ~41.68-42.71% and increase over Top-K FedAvg to 3.24-4.27 percentage points, highlighting dependence on accounting boundary. The study characterizes the reuse rule within the simulator and does not claim unbiased generalization, end-to-end bandwidth/energy savings, faster convergence, or superiority over stale-update or lazy-aggregation methods.

Conclusion: The work documents a simulator-based reuse rule for updates in federated learning, showing potential uplink savings under constrained reuse, but it does not establish unbiased generalization, end-to-end performance gains, or practical superiority over existing stale-update approaches.

Abstract: Federated learning repeatedly incurs local optimization and model-update transmission. We study DG-FedReuse, a simulator-level mechanism that allows selected clients to contribute age-decayed cached updates when a stochastic head-gradient discrepancy proxy remains below a round-dependent threshold. A hard cache-age limit and minimum fresh-client quota constrain reuse, while fresh updates use an adaptive per-tensor Top-K numerical-field representation. Experiments cover six image-classification datasets, 50 virtual clients, Dirichlet label heterogeneity (α=0.5), and three seeds. At a common 90-round budget, DG-FedReuse yields 83.36-85.42% modeled update-data-field uplink saving, compared with 76.88% for matched Top-K FedAvg; the seed-aligned accuracy differences range from -5.29 to -0.14 percentage points. Best-observed test accuracies obtained under test-controlled checkpointing are retained only as exploratory archival evidence and range from -2.38 to +0.45 percentage points relative to matched FedAvg. A symmetric dense-model-downlink sensitivity reduces the headline saving to 41.68-F42.71% and the incremental gain over Top-K FedAvg to 3.24-4.27 percentage points, demonstrating the dependence of communication conclusions on the accounting boundary. The study characterizes the proposed reuse rule in the implemented simulator; it does not establish unbiased generalization, end-to-end bandwidth reduction, runtime or energy savings, faster convergence, or superiority over existing stale-update and lazy-aggregation methods.

</details>


### [141] [Quantum-Structured World Models (QSWMs) for Predictive Latent Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05371)
*Hailong Jiang,Emran Hossain,Feng Yu,Jianfeng Zhu,Guilin Zhang,Wulan Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantum-structured world models (QSWMs) explore complex-valued and density-matrix-like latent representations for predictive world modeling. The paper establishes three foundational properties and reports mixed empirical results: promising local prediction for complex-valued QSWMs but limited long-horizon rollout for density-matrix variants, along with a framework for structured latent states and measurement-inspired decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether quantum-inspired mathematical structures can provide useful inductive biases for world models, potentially enabling more compact, expressive latent representations and improved prediction/planning across time.

Method: Propose the QSWM framework with structured latent states, latent transition operators, and measurement-inspired decoding maps. Instantiate two variants—complex-valued and density-matrix-like latents—and evaluate them on elementary cellular automata against strong classical baselines.

Result: Complex-valued QSWMs show promising local predictive potential. Density-matrix-like variants exhibit limitations in long-horizon rollout.

Conclusion: We establish three foundational properties for QSWMs: classical inclusion, predictive sufficiency, and structured compactness. Empirical results suggest local predictive gains from complex-valued representations, but highlight challenges for density-matrix variants in long-horizon tasks; further work is needed to realize robust, scalable quantum-inspired world models.

Abstract: World models learn latent states that summarize interaction histories, evolve over time, and support prediction, simulation, or planning. Most existing world models represent these states using classical vectors, probability distributions, recurrent hidden states, or transformer activations. In this paper, we introduce Quantum-Structured World Models (QSWMs), a quantum-inspired framework for predictive world modeling with structured latent states, latent transition operators, and measurement-inspired decoding maps. We study whether mathematical structures inspired by quantum theory, such as complex-valued representations and density-matrix-like latents, provide useful inductive biases for world modeling. We establish three foundational properties: classical inclusion, predictive sufficiency, and structured compactness. We then instantiate complex-valued and density-matrix-like QSWM variants and evaluate them on elementary cellular automata against strong classical baselines. Results show promising local predictive potential for complex-valued QSWMs, while also revealing limitations in long-horizon rollout, density-matrix variants

</details>


### [142] [Spectral Distillation: From Nonlinear Dynamics to Linear State-Space Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05416)
*Liane Galanti,Devan Shah,Shlomo Fortgang,Elad Hazan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-stage, provable pipeline converts nonlinear dynamics into a compact linear state-space. First learn an implicit spectral predictor via Observation Spectral Filtering (OSF) in a convex setup, then distill it into an explicit recurrent linear dynamical system (LDS). The prediction error splits into a vanishing distillation term and an OSF-dependent term, with a dimension-free bound based on observer complexity. Empirically, train-then-distill yields compact LDS predictors that match or beat baselines on LDS benchmarks and MuJoCo behavior cloning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether nonlinear dynamical systems can be faithfully represented by compact linear state-space models without solving non-convex system-identification problems, and to provide provable guarantees that the resulting linear model is competitive or superior.

Method: 1) Learn an implicit spectral predictor using Observation Spectral Filtering (OSF), a convex method that competes with the best linear observer. 2) Apply spectral-to-LDS distillation to convert this predictor into an explicit recurrent linear dynamical system (LDS).

Result: A main theorem shows the average prediction error of the distilled LDS decomposes into an exponentially-small distillation term plus the OSF learning term governed by the Luenberger complexity of the best observer. The guarantee is dimension-free, depending on observer complexity rather than latent nonlinear dimension. Experiments on linear LDS benchmarks and MuJoCo behavior cloning demonstrate that the train-then-distill pipeline yields compact LDS predictors that match or outperform directly trained baselines.

Conclusion: The paper presents the first end-to-end provable method for extracting a best-in-hindsight LDS representation of nonlinear dynamics via convex learning followed by provable distillation, achieving dimension-free guarantees and competitive empirical performance.

Abstract: Can nonlinear dynamical systems be learned through a compact linear state-space representation, without directly solving a non-convex system-identification problem? We give a provable pipeline for doing so. Starting from observations of an unknown nonlinear dynamical system, we first learn an implicit spectral predictor using Observation Spectral Filtering (OSF), a convex method that competes with the best linear observer for the system. We then apply spectral-to-LDS distillation to convert this predictor into an explicit recurrent linear dynamical system. Our main theorem shows that the average prediction error of the distilled LDS decomposes into an exponentially-small distillation term and the OSF learning term governed by the Luenberger complexity of the best observer. The guarantee is dimension-free: it depends on observer complexity rather than on the latent dimension needed to represent the nonlinear system. To our knowledge, this yields the first end-to-end provable method for extracting a best-in-hindsight LDS representation of nonlinear dynamics through convex learning followed by provable distillation. Experiments on linear LDS benchmarks and MuJoCo behavior cloning show that the train-then-distill pipeline produces compact LDS predictors that match or outperform directly trained baselines.

</details>


### [143] [Perturbation Sensitivity at Convergence: A Simple Signal for Identifying Spuriously Correlated Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05419)
*Nilesh Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A converged model contains samples that rely on spurious correlations; a fixed input perturbation flips predictions on distribution-specific samples, enabling a two-pass, group-free detector that reweights training to improve worst-group accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate reliance on spurious correlations in ERM without group annotations or early-stopping cues; improve worst-group performance on datasets like Waterbirds.

Method: After convergence, apply a fixed perturbation to inputs; compare predictions pre/post perturbation to classify samples into spurious-consistent vs input-specific; requires two forward passes per sample; no group annotations or early stopping; use detected samples to rebalance training.

Result: Worst-group accuracy on Waterbirds improved from 57.3% to 80.8%; performance approaches group-labeled supervision (85.8%).

Conclusion: The perturbation-based detection provides an effective, annotation-free mechanism to identify vulnerable samples and boost robustness against spurious correlations, reducing reliance on early-stopping cues and ground-truth groups.

Abstract: Models trained by empirical risk minimization on data containing spurious correlations achieve high average accuracy while failing on subpopulations where the correlation does not hold. Existing methods for identifying the affected samples without group annotations rely on signals from early training, which requires locating the epoch at which to intervene, a hyperparameter typically selected using group-labeled validation data. We show that a usable signal is available after convergence, when loss no longer distinguishes the two populations. Samples consistent with the spurious correlation are classified by a shared rule, while the remaining samples are fit through configurations specific to individual inputs and are correspondingly more fragile. Applying a fixed perturbation to a converged model's inputs flips the predictions of the latter far more often than the former. The resulting procedure requires two forward passes per training sample, no group annotations at any stage, and no early-stopping epoch. Using the detected samples to rebalance training raises worst-group accuracy on Waterbirds from 57.3% to 80.8%, against 85.8% with ground-truth group labels.

</details>


### [144] [IFlowNets: Extending Generative Samplers to Learn Strategies in Incomplete Information Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05422)
*Conor M. Artman,Nicholas Di,Scott Perkins*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extends Adversarial Flow Nets (AFlowNets) to incomplete information games via Information Flow Networks (IFNs) and introduces IFlowNets, which fix inadmissible constraints for valid strategy densities and training objectives; preliminary results show competitive speed and performance versus OS-MCCFR and RL baselines in three standard games.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge generative sampling (GFlowNets) with game-theoretic incomplete-information settings to produce valid density-based strategies and efficient training, addressing limitations of AFlowNets when applied to CIGs.

Method: Generalize AFlowNets to incomplete information games by formulating Information Flow Networks (IFNs) and a further generalization, IFlowNets, to ensure admissible densities and training objectives; provide theoretical proofs showing inadmissibility of complete-information GFlowNet constraints in CIGs and demonstrate that IFlowNets strictly generalize AFlowNets; empirical evaluation in three standard game environments comparing to OS-MCCFR and RL baselines.

Result: IFlowNets resolve the inadmissibility issue, yield valid strategy densities, and achieve comparable or superior performance and speed relative to OSMCCFR and RL-based methods in the three benchmark games.

Conclusion: IFlowNets offer a principled generalization enabling valid density-based sampling of strategies in incomplete-information games, with promising preliminary results; future work should explore scalability, stronger theoretical guarantees, and broader empirical benchmarking.

Abstract: While many algorithms blend reinforcement learning (RL) with counterfactual regret (CFR) methods to leverage tradeoffs in computational speed and performance, there are fewer investigations into generative sampling frameworks in game theoretic applications in incomplete information games. We extend a generative flow network framework, Adversarial Flow Networks (AFlowNets), to incomplete information games, called Information Flow Networks (IFNs). We prove that previously established constraints for generative flow networks in complete information games are inadmissible for obtaining valid densities (corresponding to player strategies) and a valid training objective. We show that our proposed generalization, IFlowNets, alleviates this issue and strictly generalizes AFlowNets. In preliminary results for three standard game environments, IFlowNets perform comparably to or better than Outcome Sampling Monte Carlo Counterfactual Regret (OSMCCFR) and standard RL-based methods in performance and speed.

</details>


### [145] [Why the Third Axis Is Freedom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05423)
*Michael Timothy Bennett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Freedom—not merely exploration—drives XM's generalization. Increasing K and selecting for freedom improves matching probability and performance, outperforming MDL-based selection, especially under distribution shift.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reframe Explora­tive Modeling's (XM) 'third axis' as a property of the function (freedom) rather than a model form or exploration behavior, and test whether optimizing for freedom yields better generalization than traditional MDL-based criteria.

Method: Theoretically analyze XM loss as a function of miss probability, showing miss probability scales with K as a power; prove that with K>1, match probability grows with freedom. Empirically, perform Forward XM to vary K and measure freedom; train XM candidate pools; compare validation selection against a freedom selector using unlabelled parent contexts; assess performance across context-dependent targets and after distribution shift (n=30 cases).

Result: Theoretical results: average XM loss increases as the chance of missing an acceptable region increases, with exploration elevating miss probability to the power K; for K>1, higher freedom yields higher match probability. Empirically, XM increases measured freedom with larger K; in 29 of 30 cases, the freedom-based selector outperformed a standard validation selector using unlabelled contexts. Freedom as a concept outperforms generative expressivity as a proxy; XM improves under distribution shift by optimizing for freedom.

Conclusion: Freedom is the actionable objective behind XM; generative expressivity is a byproduct proxy. Selecting for freedom yields stronger generalization and better XM performance under distribution shift, suggesting the true third axis is freedom, not exploration per se.

Abstract: In generative training, a model produces an output and is penalised for its difference from an example. With one output per comparison, a model that produces one common answer can outperform a model retaining a broader repertoire. Explorative Modeling (XM) produces $K$ outputs per comparison and updates on the closest, claiming exploration as a "third pretraining axis" associated with generative expressivity. Here I show the third axis is actually freedom, meaning the weakness of the constraint implied by a model's behaviour. Previous work showed freedom is a property of function rather than form. Parameters, architecture, minimum-description-length (MDL), and data can vary while the behavioural constraint remains unchanged. It was formally proved that weakest models are likeliest to generalise, and freedom selection beat MDL by 110-500\% in induction experiments. I prove average XM loss depends on the chance a candidate misses an acceptable region, with exploration raising miss probability to power $K$. For $K>1$, match probability rises with freedom. I then demonstrate empirically that XM optimises for freedom. In a Forward XM experiment, larger $K$ increased or saturated measured freedom, and increased freedom at every tested value under context-dependent targets. I trained XM candidate pools and compared validation selection with a freedom selector that read unlabelled parent contexts. Freedom won in 29 of 30 cases. Generative expressivity is a mode-count proxy for freedom, that discards the extension structure that gives freedom its generalisation significance. XM is a means, freedom an end, and selecting for freedom improved XM under distribution shift.

</details>


### [146] [EvoHarness-RL: Learning Self-Evolving Runtime Harness for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05446)
*Xuying Ning,Dongqi Fu,Tianxin Wei,Hanqing Zeng,Yuanchen Bei,Bingxuan Li,Zihao Li,Qifan Wang,Xiang Shen,Yifan Wu,Jiayi Liu,Hong Li,Yinglong Xia,Xiangjun Fan,Hanghang Tong,Jingrui He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EvoHarness-RL introduces offline-trained harness policies that manage an external workspace for long-horizon LLM agents by exposing Belief, Progress, and Experience (BPE). It combines supervised harness fine-tuning with cost-aware GRPO to coordinate when to read/update/consolidate external state, enabling more effective use of external tools than generic prompts or larger memories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon LLM agents rely on external execution support to maintain state and progress, but current approaches rely on manually engineered workflows or ad-hoc prompts. There is a need for learnable harness policies that can form and control external state during runtime.

Method: Define BPE as policy-facing harness state. Train base agent with supervised harness fine-tuning to learn the harness action space and how to construct external state. Use cost-aware GRPO (a policy optimization method) to learn coordination strategies for selectively reading, updating, and consolidating external state during long-horizon tasks. Evaluate on ALFWorld using Qwen3-8B as the backbone.

Result: EvoHarness-RL achieves 96.9% success on ALFWorld. The study identifies two dynamics: (1) harness annealing — training internalizes recurring harness-use patterns, reducing frequent external-state access; (2) harness evolution — progress updates and experience consolidation yield a compact, task-adaptive harness state.

Conclusion: Training policies to construct and coordinate with external harness workspaces is beneficial for long-horizon agents, beyond simply adding tools or increasing memory. Harness-structured state and learned coordination can improve performance and efficiency.

Abstract: Long-horizon LLM agents increasingly rely on external execution support to maintain state, track progress, invoke tools, verify outcomes, and reuse experience across interactions. However, effective harness use raises two coupled challenges: state formation from noisy interaction traces and runtime control over external-state access. Existing agents usually handle both through prompts, heuristics, or domain-specific conventions, leaving the external workspace and its usage policy manually engineered. To address this, we study the problem of harness policy learning, where agents learn harness policies offline and deploy them to construct and update external harness state online during runtime task execution. We introduce EvoHarness-RL, which exposes Belief, Progress, and Experience (BPE) as policy-facing harness state. Supervised harness fine-tuning teaches the base agent the harness action space and how to construct useful external state, while cost-aware GRPO explores coordination policies to selectively read, update, and consolidate that state during long-horizon interaction. Instantiated on ALFWorld with a Qwen3-8B LLM, EvoHarness-RL reaches 96.9% success and reveals two key dynamics: harness annealing, where training internalizes recurring harness-use patterns into the model policy and shifts the agent from frequent harness calls toward selective external-state access, and harness evolution, where progress updates and experience consolidation refine the harness into a compact, task-adaptive state substrate. These results suggest that long-horizon agents benefit from trainable policies for constructing and coordinating with external harness workspaces, beyond simply adding stronger tools or larger memories.

</details>


### [147] [Hybrid Probabilistic Zonotopes for Identifiable and Refinable Predictive Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05454)
*Zhen Zhang,Amr Alanwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Hybrid Probabilistic Zonotope (HProbZ) as a unified head for modeling three sources of uncertainty—discrete mode choice, bounded systematic drift, and stochastic noise—with a closed-form likelihood via convolution, shared bounded generator across steps for joint refinement, and identifiability up to permutation; shown to be representationally distinct from finite Gaussian mixtures and capable of per-mode risk estimation and distribution-free conformal sets, with empirical gains over encoder-based mixtures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard probabilistic heads (Gaussian mixtures or conformal regions) fail to disentangle and jointly model distinct sources of uncertainty in real tasks. A single-head approach that separates discrete, bounded, and stochastic components and ties predictions across time can improve both predictive accuracy and calibration while providing tractable inference and conformal guarantees.

Method: Propose HProbZ: a zonotopic output head with three generators representing the discrete mode, bounded drift, and stochastic noise. The three generators form a zonotope whose convolution yields a closed-form likelihood. The bounded generator is shared across prediction steps, coupling multi-step forecasts so observing one step refines the entire predictive distribution in a single forward pass. Prove identifiability of the three generators up to permutation and show the HProbZ density is distinguishable from any finite Gaussian mixture. Provide analytic per-mode risk measures and distribution-free multi-modal conformal sets at inference. Compare to same-encoder mixture baselines on representative benchmarks.

Result: The approach yields a closed-form likelihood via convolution, enabling efficient multi-step conditioning; the shared bounded generator creates algebraic coupling across steps; identifiability up to permutation is established and the density is functionally distinct from finite Gaussian mixtures; supports per-mode risk computation and distribution-free conformal sets; empirical results indicate improvements over baseline mixture models without sacrificing interpretability or conformal guarantees.

Conclusion: HProbZ offers a principled, tractable framework that jointly models multiple uncertain sources with structural advantages unattainable by standard mixtures or convex conformal predictors. It enables exact likelihood computation, cross-step refinement, and interpretable, mode-aware risk and conformal sets, demonstrating advantages on predictive benchmarks.

Abstract: Probabilistic prediction heads in neural networks typically output either a Gaussian mixture or a single conformal region. Neither separates the distinct sources of uncertainty often present in real prediction tasks: a discrete choice among modes, bounded systematic drift within the chosen mode, and irreducible stochastic noise. We introduce the Hybrid Probabilistic Zonotope (HProbZ), an output head that represents these three sources as binary, bounded, and stochastic generators of a zonotope, and admits a closed-form likelihood by convolution.
