Sharing the bounded generator across prediction steps couples future predictions algebraically, so observing one step refines the predictive distribution at every remaining step in a single forward pass. We establish that the three generators are identifiable from the likelihood up to permutation, and that an HProbZ density is representationally distinct from any finite Gaussian mixture. The same shared structure provides analytic per-mode risk and distribution-free multi-modal conformal sets at inference time. Empirical analysis on representative prediction benchmarks supports the effectiveness of the design relative to same-encoder mixture baselines, while offering structural properties that mixture or convex-conformal predictors do not jointly provide.

</details>


### [148] [Matrix Zonotopic Attention: A Context-Adaptive Value Projection for Set Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05472)
*Zhen Zhang,Amr Alanwar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Transformation Degrees of Freedom (TDOF) to quantify input-dependent directions needed to represent permutation-invariant set targets; shows depth requirements for context-rigid attention vs a single layer with context-adaptive values; proposes Matrix Zonotopic Attention (MZAttn) with input-dependent gates and generator matrices; preserves permutation equivariance; experimental results support TDOF-based predictions and show selective architectural advantages for high-rank, sparse targets over aggregate-statistic tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the expressivity limits of standard multi-head attention for permutation-invariant set tasks and to develop a more expressive attention mechanism that adapts the value projection to the input context.

Method: Define TDOF as a complexity measure of the target operator's input-dependence. Perform a depth-separation analysis showing that context-rigid attention needs depth proportional to TDOF, while a context-adaptive value family can match with a shallow network. Introduce Matrix Zonotopic Attention (MZAttn): a center matrix plus a sum of generator matrices weighted by input-dependent gates, forming a matrix-zonotope family. Prove initialization equivalence to standard multi-head attention and permutation equivariance, with a data-driven reachability interpretation. Empirically evaluate on set-prediction tasks against diverse targets.

Result: The depth-separation analysis indicates that targets with higher TDOF require deeper context-rigid attention, whereas a single layer with a context-adaptive value family (MZAttn) can represent the same targets. MZAttn reduces to standard multi-head attention at initialization, preserves permutation equivariance, and offers a data-driven reachability view. Experiments show alignment with predictions: MZAttn's advantage appears mainly for targets that depend on the input set in a high-rank, sparsely combinatorial manner, with limited gains on aggregate-statistic targets where standard attention performs well.

Conclusion: MZAttn provides a principled enhancement to attention for permutation-invariant set tasks by introducing adaptive value projections via a matrix-zonotope framework. The benefit is target-structure dependent (high TDOF, sparse combinatorial targets), while for aggregate statistics standard attention suffices. The framework offers a clear interpretation through TDOF and reachability, warranting further exploration of TDOF-driven design and efficiency.

Abstract: Multi-head attention combines an input-dependent softmax routing with an input-independent linear value projection, so the per-sample operator mapping aggregated values to outputs is the same for every input set. We study the consequences of this asymmetry for permutation-invariant set targets. We introduce the Transformation Degrees of Freedom (TDOF) of a target operator, a complexity measure counting the input-dependent directions an exact representation requires, and present a depth-separation analysis showing that context-rigid attention needs depth proportional to the target's TDOF, whereas a single layer with a context-adaptive value family can represent the same target. Building on this analysis, we propose Matrix Zonotopic Attention (MZAttn), which replaces the fixed value projection with a context-adaptive matrix-zonotope family: a centre matrix plus a sum of generator matrices weighted by input-dependent gates. The construction reduces to standard multi-head attention at initialisation, preserves permutation equivariance, and admits a data-driven reachability interpretation. Experiments on a range of set-prediction tasks are consistent with the TDOF prediction that the architectural advantage is selective: it appears on targets that depend on the input set in a high-rank, sparsely combinatorial way, and is small on aggregate-statistic targets where parameter-matched standard attention is already competitive.

</details>


### [149] [KV-Skill: Forging Expertise in the Model's Native Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05475)
*Zhaowei Han,Xiang Zhang,Bing Han,Kai Liu,Danqi Hu,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: KV-Skill introduces a design space of external factorized operators that reads through a lightweight interface to a frozen language model, enabling two routes—text-derived registration and reward-based latent operators—to encode task knowledge without changing the backbone or prompting. This yields modular, shareable, and loadable task knowledge that often outperforms text-skills and baseline adapters across multiple benchmarks and backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the fragility and loadability of task knowledge stored as prompt text or as full model weights. The goal is to externalize, compress, and share task knowledge as independent operators that can be loaded into a frozen LM without increasing prompt length or modifying the backbone.

Method: Introduce KV-Skill with two complementary paths. Registration converts an authored text skill into a text-derived operator and trains a shared per-backbone interface. Reward learning derives a compact latent operator directly from task outcomes, with or without an authored skill. Neither path adds positions to the prompt. Evaluate across ten benchmarks and four backbones from three model families, comparing against SkillOpt and SoftSkill, and analyze directionality and forgetting. Demonstrate that a single shared interface can host multiple KV-Skills with negligible forgetting.

Result: Registration achieves strong accuracy gains (e.g., 77.2 on Qwen3.5-4B LiveMath vs 23.4 for source text skill; 52.0 SkillOpt; 64.5 SoftSkill). Under matched budgets, KV-Skill is the best in 7 of 8 settings compared to soft prefixes, prefix tuning, and LoRA. Post-hoc analysis shows text-derived operators largely retain their benefits with a task-aligned direction per injection layer; three independently loadable KV-Skills can coexist on one interface without measurable forgetting.

Conclusion: Task knowledge can be efficiently acquired from text or experience and compressed into an external operator that decouples from the backbone, enabling modular, shareable, and reusable task knowledge. The approach offers strong empirical gains across diverse models and tasks, with code available for replication.

Abstract: Task knowledge is commonly stored either as text in the prompt or as an update to model weights. Text is modular but must be interpreted on every use, while weight adaptation makes the resulting capability difficult to load, remove, or share independently. We introduce KV-Skill, a design space of external factorized operators that a frozen language model reads through a lightweight interface. KV-Skill supports two complementary paths. Registration converts an authored text skill into a text-derived operator and trains a shared per-backbone interface. Reward learning develops a compact latent operator directly from task outcomes, with or without an authored skill. Neither path adds positions to the prompt. Across ten benchmarks and four backbones from three model families, converting text to a KV-Skill consistently makes the same procedural knowledge more effective. On Qwen3.5-4B LiveMath, registration reaches 77.2 accuracy, compared with 23.4 for the source text skill, 52.0 for SkillOpt, and 64.5 for SoftSkill. Under matched reward training and parameter budgets, KV-Skill gives the best result in seven of eight matched settings against soft prefixes, prefix tuning, and LoRA. A post-hoc rank analysis further shows that text-derived operators retain nearly all of their benefit with one task-aligned direction per injection layer, while matched random directions fail. Finally, one shared interface retains three independently loadable KV-Skills without measurable forgetting. These results show that task knowledge can be acquired from text or experience, compressed into an external operator, and deployed separately from the backbone. Code is available at: https://github.com/shawnzhg/KV-Skill

</details>


### [150] [Align-RAG: Alignment Is All You Need for TSFM In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05571)
*Mohammad Asadi,Soheil Hor,Bardiya Akhbari,Jack W. O'Sullivan,Tahoura Nedaee,Layne C. Price,Raviteja Anantha,Euan Ashley,Ehsan Adeli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A training-free alignment method (Align-RAG) applies per-pair amplitude rescaling and integer-lag phase shifts to retrieved past–future windows, enabling dynamic in-context use in frozen Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) without fusion adapters; it outperforms trained adapters on seven benchmarks (avg MSE −3.75%) and yields zero-shot gains (2.5%–13.7%) across four backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retrieval-augmented forecasting aims to adapt frozen TSFMs to new domains without fine-tuning. Prior work relies on learned fusion adapters, assuming frozen backbones cannot dynamically incorporate retrieved context. The paper argues this assumption is unnecessary and seeks a training-free solution.

Method: Apply a closed-form per-pair amplitude rescaling and integer-lag phase shift to retrieved past–future windows before feeding them into a frozen backbone. This requires no learned parameters and avoids training any fusion module.

Result: On a frozen Chronos-Bolt backbone across seven datasets, Align-RAG outperforms the state-of-the-art trained retrieval adapter with an average MSE improvement of 3.75%. It also achieves 2.5%–13.7% zero-shot MSE improvements across four additional frozen TSFMs without per-backbone tuning.

Conclusion: Frozen TSFMs can dynamically utilize retrieved context via simple, closed-form alignment. Align-RAG should be the default baseline for retrieval-augmented forecasting before training any fusion modules, as it matches or exceeds learned adapters and demonstrates robust zero-shot gains.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented forecasting promises to adapt frozen Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) to new domains without fine-tuning, but recent methods typically rely on learned fusion modules, i.e., trained adapters that merge retrieved examples into the backbone's forecast, based on the assumption that frozen backbones cannot dynamically incorporate retrieved context on their own. We show this assumption is unnecessary. We introduce Align-RAG, a training-free method that applies a closed-form per-pair amplitude rescaling and integer-lag phase shift to retrieved past-future windows before they enter a frozen backbone's context. With no learned parameters, Align-RAG outperforms the state-of-the-art trained retrieval adapter on a frozen Chronos-Bolt on all seven datasets of the standard benchmark (avg -3.75% MSE), showing that the gains previously attributed to learned fusion are recoverable without any training. Align-RAG further improves zero-shot MSE on four additional frozen TSFMs with various architectures by 2.5% to 13.7% per backbone with no per-backbone tuning. To probe why alignment helps, we compare the frozen backbone's prediction shift under aligned demonstrations to the closed-form ridge prediction shift on the same pairs. We find that aligned demonstrations induce prediction shifts that track a closed-form ridge predictor on the same pairs, with a future-shuffle control ruling out a futures-averaging account. Together, these results indicate that frozen TSFMs already support dynamic in-context use of retrievals, and that closed-form alignment should be the default baseline for retrieval-augmented forecasting before any fusion module is trained. Code available at: https://github.com/masadi-99/align-rag

</details>


### [151] [LC-GRPO: Bridging Train-Inference Gap for Flow-Based GRPO with Langevin Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05600)
*Yingqing Guo,Hui Yuan,Zijian He,Mengdi Wang,Zheng Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LC-GRPO introduces Langevin correction to align stochastic training rollouts with deterministic test-time ODE inference in flow-based GRPO, improving optimization and reducing distribution mismatch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the mismatch between stochastic rollouts used in training (SDE) and deterministic ODE inference at test time in flow-based generative policy optimization, which can degrade sample quality and learning efficiency.

Method: First perform an inference-aligned Euler step for each rollout, then apply a Langevin correction that targets the marginal distribution at the updated timestep. The score is obtained directly from the flow velocity without extra score models; the transition remains an isotropic Gaussian with tractable likelihood. Theoretical result: a single Langevin correction step reduces Wasserstein error under suitable conditions. At matched randomness, this transition can exceed the accuracy of Euler–Maruyama discretization of the reverse SDE.

Result: Empirical results on SD3.5-Medium, FLUX.1-Dev, and HunyuanVideo show LC-GRPO improves reward optimization across text-to-image and text-to-video tasks, preserves generation quality, and narrows the gap between stochastic training rollouts and deterministic test-time ODE inference.

Conclusion: LC-GRPO provides a simple, theoretically justified correction to reconcile training-time stochasticity with test-time inference in flow-based GRPO, yielding improved optimization performance and more accurate sampling without extra score models.

Abstract: Flow-based generative models are typically sampled by solving a deterministic ordinary differential equation (ODE), whereas online reinforcement learning requires stochastic rollouts for policy exploration and optimization. Existing GRPO methods for flow models therefore replace the inference-time ODE with a stochastic differential equation (SDE) during training. Although the ODE and SDE share the same marginal distributions in continuous time, their finite-step discretizations can differ substantially. In particular, SDE rollouts often become blurry as the exploration noise increases, creating a mismatch between the samples used for reinforcement learning and those generated by the test-time ODE sampler. We introduce LC-GRPO, a flow-based GRPO framework with Langevin correction. Each rollout transition first takes an inference-aligned ODE Euler step and then applies a stochastic Langevin correction targeting the marginal distribution at the resulting timestep. The required score is recovered directly from the flow velocity, requiring no additional score model, while the resulting transition remains an isotropic Gaussian with a tractable likelihood for policy optimization. We theoretically show that, under suitable conditions, one Langevin correction step reduces the Wasserstein error of an imperfect ODE Euler step. At a matched randomness level, we further show that the proposed transition can be more accurate than the standard Euler--Maruyama discretization of the reverse SDE. Experiments on SD3.5-Medium, FLUX.1-Dev, and HunyuanVideo demonstrate that LC-GRPO consistently improves reward optimization across text-to-image and text-to-video tasks, preserves generation quality, and substantially narrows the gap between stochastic training rollouts and deterministic test-time ODE inference.

</details>


### [152] [GAUGE: Granularity-Adaptive Counterfactual Gating of Evidence for Incomplete Multimodal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05608)
*Yunping Shi,En Yu,Kairui Guo,Jie Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight, fine-grained counterfactual gating method (GAUGE) for incomplete multimodal classification that uses a single forward-backward pass to compute Taylor-based evidence scores, applying additive biases to unit-level evidence, with empirical gains across six benchmarks and a Taylor remainder analysis for justification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multimodal learning, missing or incomplete inputs are common. Existing imputation/fusion operates at coarse modality level and risks discarding informative components or retaining misleading ones within the same modality, hurting reliability. A need exists for fine-grained, evidence-level control that works within a fixed backbone.

Method: Impute missing modalities with a frozen imputer and encode both observed and recovered data as fine-grained evidence units. Compute the counterfactual effect of replacing each unit with a reference via prediction-aware Taylor evidence scores, all in a single forward-backward pass. Map scores to continuous gates and convert them into additive attention-logit biases to modulate unit-level evidence without changing the backbone.

Result: GAUGE outperforms strong baselines across six benchmarks under various incomplete-input settings. A Taylor remainder analysis characterizes the first-order approximation error relative to the exact counterfactual effect, supporting principled, scalable fine-grained evidence control under modality incompleteness.

Conclusion: GAUGE provides a principled, scalable framework for fine-grained evidence control in incomplete multimodal classification, enabling reliable suppression of misleading components and retention of informative ones without altering the backbone.

Abstract: Multimodal classification typically assumes all modalities are available, yet real-world inputs are often incomplete. Imputation and dynamic fusion can mitigate such incompleteness, but existing methods operate at a coarse modality level and thus cannot retain reliable components while suppressing misleading ones within the same recovered modality, compromising prediction reliability. To address this issue, we propose GAUGE, a lightweight counterfactual gating framework for incomplete multimodal classification. GAUGE first imputes missing modalities with a frozen imputer and encodes observed and recovered inputs uniformly as fine-grained evidence units. Rather than intervening on each unit explicitly, GAUGE scores the counterfactual effect of replacing every unit with a reference representation through prediction-aware Taylor evidence scores, all obtained in a single forward-backward pass. These scores are mapped to continuous gates, which are converted into additive attention-logit biases for unit-wise evidence modulation without altering the backbone architecture. Experiments across six benchmarks demonstrate that GAUGE outperforms strong baselines across diverse incomplete-input settings. Furthermore, a Taylor remainder theoretical analysis characterizes the error of the first-order approximation relative to the exact counterfactual effect, establishing GAUGE as a principled and scalable framework for fine-grained evidence control under modality incompleteness.

</details>


### [153] [Reasoning Errors Have a Region and a Direction in the Residual-Stream Trajectory of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05660)
*Hamed Damirchi,Ignacio Meza De la Jara,Damith Ranasinghe,Yuhang Liu,Javen Shi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Three-stream detector uses motion plus restricted location views to detect reasoning validity, improving performance on unseen benchmarks and revealing complementary signals; also reads factual completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reliably distinguish sound vs flawed reasoning in language models by leveraging signal from state-conditioned motion while avoiding full-state probing, addressing limitations of displacement-only and single-layer probing methods.

Method: Proposes a three-stream detector: a motion stream based on layerwise residual-stream displacements; a coarse region reader using vector quantization; and a fine direction reader over normalized multi-layer states. The two restricted views provide enough state context to interpret motion without full-state probing.

Result: On reasoning benchmarks unseen during training, achieves up to 12% higher selection accuracy over the displacement-only state of the art and up to 21% over single-layer probing baselines. Also reads factual completion and fact verification ahead of other detectors. Ablations show motion, region, and direction provide complementary signals.

Conclusion: Reasoning validity is better read from state-conditioned motion that is augmented by constrained location views, rather than from static states or decontextualized trajectories alone.

Abstract: As language models are increasingly used for tasks that require verifiable reasoning, reliably distinguishing sound reasoning from flawed reasoning has become an important practical problem. Recent trajectory-based methods seek this signal in layerwise residual-stream displacements, which capture how representations change while attenuating some stable, token-specific information. However, displacement omits the state from which an update originates, whereas restoring the full state risks reintroducing shortcut-prone information. We identify this trade-off and propose a three-stream detector that combines motion with two restricted views of location. A coarse region reader based on vector quantization and a fine direction reader over normalized multi-layer states. This design restores enough state context to interpret the motion without returning to full-state probing. On reasoning benchmarks unseen during training, our method improves selection accuracy by up to 12% over the displacement-only state of the art and 21% over single-layer probing baselines. Although trained only on reasoning benchmarks, it also reads factual completion and fact verification, ahead of every detector we compare against, which places the signal on correctness rather than on a kind of reasoning. Ablations further show that motion, region, and direction provide complementary signals. These results suggest that reasoning validity is better read from state-conditioned motion than from either static states or decontextualized trajectories alone.

</details>


### [154] [Potential Matching Optimal Transport: Continuous Normalizing Flows for Exact $p$-Wasserstein Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05666)
*Lishuo Zhang,Ruizhi Huang,Yang Yu,Lei Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PMOT introduces a potential-flow framework for p-cost OT, achieving zero-loss exactness under regularity, and demonstrating competitive density modeling and flexible terminal matching across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify optimal transport with generalized p-costs via a Benamou–Brenier–style potential flow; enable exact optimality and flexible terminal constraint handling in high-dimensional settings.

Method: Parameterize the CNF velocity field by a scalar potential within generalized Benamou–Brenier formulation for exponent p. Train the gradient of the potential using a self-induced matching loss on bridges linking model endpoints, while allowing flexible terminal distribution matching.

Result: Zero-loss exactness: under stated regularity, exact terminal matching, and uniqueness, any zero-loss solution satisfies the OT optimality system and recovers the p-optimal map and dynamics. Empirically, PMOT recovers p-specific OT maps on synthetic data, performs competitively as a likelihood-based density model on high-dimensional tabular data, and enables flexible sample-based terminal matching demonstrated via an MMD-based color transformation.

Conclusion: PMOT provides a principled potential-flow approach to p-cost OT with exactness guarantees, accurate p-specific mappings, and flexible terminal matching capabilities, suitable for density estimation and data-to-data transport tasks.

Abstract: We introduce Potential Matching Optimal Transport (PMOT), a potential-flow framework for general $p$-cost optimal transport with $c_p(x,y)=\|x-y\|^p$. PMOT parameterizes the CNF velocity field with a scalar potential in the generalized Benamou--Brenier form for the chosen exponent $p$. It trains the potential gradient with a self-induced matching loss along straight bridges determined by the model's own endpoints, while allowing flexible terminal distribution matching. Our main result establishes zero-loss exactness: under the stated regularity, exact terminal matching, and uniqueness assumptions, any zero-loss solution satisfies the generalized Benamou--Brenier optimality system and recovers the corresponding $p$-optimal transport map and dynamics. On synthetic benchmarks, PMOT learns $p$-specific maps that agree with the corresponding $p$-matched OT references. It also remains competitive as a likelihood-based density model on high-dimensional tabular data, and an MMD-based color transformation experiment demonstrates flexible sample-based terminal matching.

</details>


### [155] [When Does Consensus Mean Correctness? Measuring the Agreement-Accuracy Coupling with Semantics-Preserving Re-Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05670)
*Rasul Khanbayov,Hasan Kurban*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RENDEQ generates render-equivalence image sets from data-driven rendering pipelines to study how agreement across perturbed renders relates to model correctness in vision-language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural perturbations often preserve meaning only by assumption; figures enable semantically equivalent re-renders with exact programmatic ground truth, allowing label-free reliability signals.

Method: Construct RENDEQ to produce render-equivalence sets; evaluate three open-weight VLMs across three independent instantiations; compare re-rendering to resampling; measure agreement as a reliability signal and compare to an evidence-carrying baseline (mean token log-probability); analyze dispersion over style factors (e.g., plotting library); examine cross-render self-training by fine-tuning on cross-render consensus.

Result: Re-rendering improves both accuracy and reliability over resampling; agreement outperforms baseline on two models and ties on the third; a replication bug traced to a rendering-pipeline failure; dispersion of errors concentrates mainly in the plotting library style factor; cross-render fine-tuning reverses the effect, reducing accuracy across five runs, contrary to natural-image results; agreement certifies correctness only above a threshold set by error diffuseness; optimizing for agreement can destroy the desired diffuseness of errors.

Conclusion: Agreement as a reliability signal is conditional and depends on how diffusely a model errs; objectives that optimize agreement may undermine the intended behavior by reducing error diffuseness and misaligning with true correctness.

Abstract: A model's agreement across perturbed inputs is used both as a label-free reliability signal and as a self-training target, on the premise that agreement tracks correctness. That coupling is rarely measured directly: natural-image perturbations preserve meaning only by assumption, and no exact answer key localizes errors. Scientific figures remove both obstacles, a figure is drawn from data by a program, so redrawing it yields images that are semantically equivalent by construction and share a programmatically exact answer. We build RENDEQ, a generator of such render-equivalence sets, and measure the coupling on three open-weight VLMs, checking every finding across three independent instantiations. Re-rendering beats resampling on both accuracy and reliability. Agreement beats an evidence-carrying baseline, mean token log-probability, on two of three models and ties on the third, reversing an intermediate, buggy replication traced to a rendering-pipeline failure. The dispersion behind this is concentrated in one style factor, the plotting library, more than double the next-largest factor and an order of magnitude above the noise floor. Fine-tuning on the model's own cross-render consensus inverts: accuracy falls in every one of five replication runs, the opposite sign to published results on natural images. Agreement certifies correctness only above a threshold set by how diffuse a model's errors are, and an objective that rewards agreement destroys exactly that diffuseness.

</details>


### [156] [Consistency Has a Computable Blind Spot: A Commutation Theory of Label-Free Reliability for Vision-Language Figure Reading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05675)
*Rasul Khanbayov,Hasan Kurban*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes a computable blind spot in label-free reliability for vision-language reading by studying when input edits commute, formalizing invariance and equivariance, and introducing tools to detect and measure these properties across models and data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand and quantify blind spots in label-free verification of reading in vision-language models, and to provide a computable, model-agnostic detector.

Method: The authors build a theoretical framework using commutativity of edits to identify invisible errors (centralizer) and a complementary focus on equivariance via matched edits. They prove completeness results for affine reading errors with two edits, show gaps for label permutations, and use cyclic relabeling to close much of that gap. They instantiate these ideas as the Equivariance-Consistency Score (a label-free detector) and release REND-EQUIV, a dataset pairing matched invariance and equivariance sets on identical data, with empirical validation across three models and hand-labeled data.

Result: They demonstrate a computable blind spot (invisible errors under perturbations), characterize when and how it arises (commutativity and centralizers), and validate the proposed detector and dataset. The predicted ordering of detectability holds; cyclic relabeling improves detection for real data; the work reframes metamorphic-testing results by showing detectability depends on the relation and fault class, not the relation alone.

Conclusion: A unified theory for label-free reliability via invariance and equivariance yields computable blind spots, practical detectors (Equivariance-Consistency Score, REND-EQUIV), and clearer interpretation of metamorphic testing results, with broad applicability to reading in vision-language systems.

Abstract: Label-free reliability for vision-language models rests on invariance: perturb the input and a faithful reader's answer should not change. This has a known blind spot, a systematic misreading survives the perturbation and gets certified wrong, which we show is computable, not just real: an error is invisible to an edit exactly when the two commute, so the errors a suite cannot reach form its joint centralizer, a set that shrinks as edits are added and can be written down rather than guessed at. We act on the complementary relation, equivariance: edit a figure's data and the correct answer must change by a computable amount. Two matched edits are provably complete for affine reading errors; no suite of swap edits is complete for label permutations, and cyclic relabeling closes most of that gap. We instantiate the theory as the Equivariance-Consistency Score, a label-free, training-free detector, and release REND-EQUIV, pairing matched invariance and equivariance sets over identical data. The predicted ordering holds across three models and a hand-labeled population immune to the one circularity in how it is selected; a second invariance-family method confirms the blind spot belongs to the relation, not to any implementation; and cyclic relabeling delivers its predicted gain on a matched real sample. The same characterization explains a reported inversion of this ordering in the classifier metamorphic-testing literature: detectability is a joint property of the relation and the fault class, never of the relation alone.

</details>


### [157] [SEAM: Global consistency beyond local accuracy in scientific machine learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05702)
*Gnankan Landry Regis N'guessan,Bum Jun Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SEAM offers a generator-agnostic, global-consistency test for local explanations by building an explanation-sheaf over regions and turning overlaps into channel-resolved obstructions, enabling exact repairs or residual attributions; demonstrated on 19 experiments with PDEs and FNO monitoring, enabling a global audit of local explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local explanation checks (per subdomain or prediction) cannot confirm that those explanations assemble into a globally coherent scientific account. A framework is needed to assess cross-regional consistency, handle distribution shift, and distinguish inconsistency from non-identifiability.

Method: Construct the finite explanation-sheaf SEAM-Ω, where each region is represented by a structured explanation with state, closure, and observation channels plus contract metadata. Compare neighboring explanations on overlaps; convert disagreements into channel-resolved obstructions. Solve exact feasibility for repairs; when exact repair is unavailable, use residual-aware regularized records for empirical attribution. Separate inconsistency from non-identifiability, monitor learned generators under distribution shift, and prove theorems on minimum-cost intervention and conservation-contract detectability, with companion results for identifiability and closure recoverability.

Result: Empirically evaluated across 19 experiments with synthetic PDE systems and out-of-distribution Fourier neural operator (FNO) monitoring. SEAM detects incompatible explanations even when local predictions are accurate and attributes failures to specific channels and overlaps."

Conclusion: SEAM adds a global explanation-consistency audit to existing solvers and learning models, enabling testing of whether their local explanations coherently form a single scientific account across regions, sensors, and regimes.

Abstract: Scientific machine learning commonly validates models at the level of a subdomain, a benchmark split, or an explanation for one prediction. Yet such local checks cannot establish whether the resulting explanations can be assembled into one globally admissible explanation. We introduce Scientific Explanation-Admissibility Machines (SEAM), a generator-agnostic framework that makes this local-to-global consistency question computable across regions, sensors, regimes, and model components. The finite explanation-sheaf instantiation SEAM-$Ω$ represents each region by a structured explanation with state, closure, and observation channels together with optional contract metadata; compares neighboring explanations on their overlaps; and converts disagreement into a channel-resolved obstruction. This obstruction locates inconsistency and tests competing declared accounts by restricting each repair to the revisions that one account permits. Exact feasibility refutes or retains an account; when exact repair is unavailable, residual-aware regularized records provide a separately labeled empirical attribution. The framework also separates inconsistency from non-identifiability and monitors learned generators under distribution shift. We establish theorems for minimum-cost intervention and conservation-contract detectability, together with companion results for identifiability and closure recoverability. Across nineteen experiments involving synthetic partial differential equation systems and out-of-distribution Fourier neural operator (FNO) monitoring, SEAM detects incompatible explanations even when local predictions are accurate, and attributes failures to specific channels and overlaps. SEAM adds a global explanation-consistency audit to existing solvers and learning models, testing whether their local explanations form a coherent scientific account.

</details>


### [158] [Spectral Aliasing Pretext: A novel task for Self-Supervised fault diagnosis in rotating machinery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05705)
*Victor Gialis,Maxime Metz,David Esteve,Abdenour Soualhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAP uses spectral aliasing as a self-supervised pretext task for fault diagnosis. A Transformer reconstructs the unfolded spectrum from a folded one, trained on unlabeled vibration data. Linear probing with the pretrained model yields high accuracy with limited labeled data, often outperforming full supervised fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Labeled fault data in industrial settings is scarce. Unlabeled vibration data is abundant. A pretext task that leverages spectral properties can learn robust, frequency-domain fault features without destructive data augmentations.

Method: Deliberately undersample signals to create folded (aliased) spectra. Train a Transformer to reconstruct the original unfolded spectrum from the folded input in a self-supervised pretraining phase. Evaluate representations via linear probing and compare to full fine-tuning on fault diagnosis tasks using the CWRU dataset.

Result: SAP produces stable, discriminative representations. In linear probing, it achieves high classification performance with only a small fraction of labeled data and low variance. Full fine-tuning does not yield more stable or better results in this setting.

Conclusion: SAP, combined with linear probing, can be more effective and reliable than fully supervised training for fault diagnosis when labeled data are limited.

Abstract: Deep learning is a new way for machinery fault diagnosis but requires extensive labeled data, a scarce resource in industrial settings. We propose Spectral Aliasing Pretext (SAP), a self-supervised learning method that pretrains models on unlabeled vibration data by exploiting spectral aliasing. We deliberately undersample signals to create folded spectrum, then train a Transformer to reconstruct the original unfolded spectrum. This pretext task forces the model to learn frequency-domain invariants characteristic of mechanical faults, without potentially destructive augmentations. Experiments on the CWRU dataset show that SAP learns stable and highly discriminative representations. In a linear probing setting, SAP quickly achieves very high classification performance with only a small fraction of labeled data and low variance. In contrast, full fine-tuning, including fully supervised training, does not lead to more stable or better results. Overall, these findings suggest that SAP combined with linear probing can be more effective and reliable than fully supervised training for fault diagnosis with limited labeled data.

</details>


### [159] [CircuitSteer: Geometrically Aligned Multi-Layer Steering via Sparse Autoencoder Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05732)
*Mehrshad Saadatinia,Parsa Razmara,Ardalan Aryashad,Ali Abbasi,Seyedarmin Azizi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CircuitSteer introduces multi-layer circuit steering in LLMs using Sparse Autoencoders to identify and manipulate distributed semantic circuits across layers, enabling dense, multi-point interventions that preserve fluency while controlling behavior. It outperforms fixed single-layer methods (e.g., CAA) across toxicity, emotion-intensity, sycophancy, and refusal tasks on two model families, suggesting geometry-aligned multi-layer features yield more robust control. Code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current steering methods rely on fixed single-layer interventions derived from aggregate activation differences, which can degrade semantic coherence and fail to produce consistent behavioral changes across layers. There is a need for interventions that operate on distributed, multi-layer circuits to achieve robust, fluent control over LLM behavior.

Method: Identify coherent semantic circuits distributed across multiple layers using Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs). Build a feature flow circuit based on feature co-activation and the geometric alignment of decoder directions. Isolate multi-layer subcircuits responsible for a target behavior. Synthesize dense steering vectors from sparse features and apply multi-point interventions to guide internal semantic trajectories.

Result: Empirical evaluation on toxicity, emotion-intensity, sycophancy, and refusal tasks across two model families shows CircuitSteer consistently preserves fluency, unlike competing methods that degrade text quality or lack coverage. CircuitSteer achieves more robust and effective behavioral control, especially on complex behaviors like sycophancy and refusal.

Conclusion: Multi-layer circuit steering, enforced through geometric alignment among selected features, provides strictly more robust and effective behavioral control than static single-point interventions; demonstrates the viability of distributed, geometry-aware interventions for LLM alignment; code released.

Abstract: Controlling the behavior of large language models (LLMs) remains a critical challenge for AI alignment. Existing steering methods, such as Contrastive Activation Addition (CAA), typically rely on fixed single-layer interventions derived from aggregate activation differences. These methods impose a single intervention across semantically diverse inputs and often fail to sustain consistent behavioral changes across layers, limiting the effectiveness of the steering. In this work, we introduce CircuitSteer, a novel framework that leverages Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to identify and manipulate coherent semantic circuits distributed across multiple layers. By constructing a feature flow circuit based on feature co-activation and the geometric alignment of decoder directions, we isolate the specific multi-layer subcircuits responsible for a target behavior. We then synthesize dense steering vectors from these sparse features and apply multi-point interventions to guide the model's internal semantic trajectory. We evaluate CircuitSteer using contrastive examples across a diverse set of tasks, including toxicity, emotion-intensity, sycophancy, and refusal, spanning two model families. Across all models and datasets, CircuitSteer is the only method to consistently produce fluency-preserving interventions; competing methods either sacrifice text quality or lack coverage, failing entirely on complex behaviors like sycophancy and refusal. These results demonstrate that multi-layer circuit steering, enabled by enforcing geometric alignment among selected features, yields strictly more robust and effective behavioral control than static single-point interventions. Code is available at https://github.com/mehrshad-sdtn/CircuitSteer.

</details>


### [160] [Multivariate Time Series Forecasting needs Cross Variable Loss](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05742)
*Kuiye Ding,Yifan Hu,Hanchen Wang,Hao Xue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CvLoss is a cross-variable graph-based residual regularizer for multivariate time-series forecasting that enforces consistency of cross-variable and lagged residuals in multi-step forecasts, addressing the Direct Forecasting objective gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct Forecasting (DF) objectives often ignore cross-variable and lagged dependencies in future values, leading to an objective gap when variables co-evolve under shared dynamics. A regularizer that enforces cross-variable residual consistency can bridge this gap without altering forecasting backbones.

Method: Introduce CvLoss as a plug-in structural regularizer that builds a cross-variable graph of variable interactions and penalizes inconsistent edge-wise residual differences across forecast patches, enforcing synchronization and asynchrony consistency in residuals for multi-step forecasts.

Result: Empirical evaluations show CvLoss improves competitive forecasting models, beats representative learning objectives, and remains compatible with diverse forecasting backbones.

Conclusion: CvLoss closes the objective gap in DF for multivariate forecasting by enforcing cross-variable residual consistency, offering a flexible, backbone-agnostic regularizer for various multivariate forecasting tasks.

Abstract: Multivariate time series forecasting presents unique challenges because future variables often co-evolve under shared system dynamics. While existing studies mainly focus on cross-variable dependencies in historical observations, dependencies among future values are much less explored. Specifically, modern forecasting models largely follow the Direct Forecasting (DF) paradigm, generating multi-step forecasts with point-wise objectives that do not explicitly constrain cross-variable structure. In this work, we show that the DF objective is mismatched in the presence of cross-variable and lagged dependencies, revealing an objective gap. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{C}ross-\textbf{V}ariable \textbf{Loss} (CvLoss), a plug-in structural regularizer that constrains forecast residuals on a cross-variable graph. CvLoss penalizes inconsistent edge-wise residual differences over forecast patches, encouraging consistency across both synchronous and asynchronous interactions. Our experiments show that CvLoss consistently improves competitive forecasting models, outperforms representative learning objectives, and is compatible with a variety of forecasting backbones.

</details>


### [161] [Equipment-centric workpiece localization in near real-time using deep learning-based vision and event-driven finite state machines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05744)
*Dohyeon Kong,Jaebong Cho,Hyunbo Cho*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A vision-based, equipment-centric approach for continuous workpiece localization in hot forging using multi-camera 3D localization, event-driven validation, and a keypoint-guided attention 3D CNN to improve activity recognition, achieving precise event detection and localization with interpretable reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust, continuous traceability and process coordination in hot forging under harsh conditions where direct tracking is unreliable due to extreme temperatures, surface degradation, and irregular routing.

Method: Combine multiple static 2D cameras to estimate 3D equipment coordinates and detect grasp/release actions. Use event-driven finite state machines to validate activities as discrete handling events, updating workpiece states in real time. Integrate a keypoint-guided attention mechanism within a 3D convolutional neural network to focus on functionally relevant equipment regions for improved activity recognition.

Result: In an operational hot forging factory, achieved 100% event detection accuracy within a 33-second tolerance window, mean localization error of 317.8 mm, and mean system latency of 21 seconds. Provides interpretable event-driven reasoning and visualization of workpiece transfers and equipment operations.

Conclusion: The framework successfully links perception with interpretable reasoning to support reliable, continuous traceability and process coordination in harsh manufacturing environments, enabling visualization and quantitative analysis of equipment interactions.

Abstract: Continuous workpiece localization is essential for traceability and process coordination in hot forging, but direct tracking is unreliable because of extreme temperatures, surface degradation, and irregular routing. This study presents an equipment-centric framework that infers workpiece locations from handling equipment observed by multiple static 2D cameras. The framework estimates floorplan-space 3D equipment coordinates and recognizes grasp and release activities. Event-driven finite state machines validate these activities as discrete handling events and continuously update workpiece states and locations. A keypoint-guided attention mechanism integrated into a 3D convolutional neural network improves activity recognition by focusing on functionally relevant equipment regions. Evaluation in an operational hot forging factory achieved 100\% event detection accuracy within a 33-second tolerance window, a mean localization error of 317.8 mm, and a mean system latency of 21 seconds. The framework connects vision-based perception with interpretable event-driven reasoning and supports visualization of workpiece transfers and quantitative analysis of equipment operations.

</details>


### [162] [Accelerating nanodrug development in continuous flow systems using informed prediction models based on low-cost surrogate nanoparticles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05761)
*Kai Dahms,Eilien Heinrich,Jochen Schmid,Michael Bortz,Iryna Savych,Regina Bleul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Shape-constrained predictive modeling for nanoparticle fabrication; achieves accurate size and PDI predictions across process conditions with minimal empirical data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nanoparticle properties are highly sensitive to process parameters; predictive frameworks are lacking, leading to costly trial-and-error experimentation.

Method: Develops a shape-constrained model trained on microfluidic liposome/LNP data across lipid concentrations, flow rates, and aqueous/organic ratios; integrates experimental data with expert knowledge to constrain plausible relationships; validates the model for pharmaceutical use with limited data.

Result: Accurate prediction of nanoparticle size and dispersity under varied conditions; reduces need for extensive experimental screening; demonstrates utility for rational process development.

Conclusion: Shape-constrained modeling is a practical, generalizable tool to streamline nanomedicine manufacturing, enabling efficient exploration of processing parameter space.

Abstract: The development of nanotherapeutics often involves extensive empirical optimization due to the sensitivity of nanoparticle properties, such as size and polydispersity index (PDI), to minor changes in process parameters. Factors like formulation concentration, flow rates, and mixing ratios can significantly influence clinical efficacy and therapeutic outcomes. The absence of predictive mathematical frameworks has made iterative experimental screening necessary, increasing both costs and development time.
  This study introduces and validates a predictive modeling approach based on shape constraints, aiming to enhance the estimation of nanoparticle characteristics across various process conditions. Using controlled microfluidic methods, liposomes and lipid nanoparticles were systematically prepared under varying lipid concentrations, flow rates, and aqueous-to-organic mixing ratios.
  The shape-constrained model, informed by both experimental data and expert knowledge, was subsequently validated for a pharmaceutical application using minimal empirical data. Results reveal that shape-constrained modeling facilitates accurate prediction of nanoparticle size and dispersity, reducing the need for extensive experimental workflows. This framework supports rational and efficient process development for manufacturing nanomedicine systems.

</details>


### [163] [Observation-Grounded Self-Predictive Reinforcement Learning for Visual Continuous Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05989)
*Xinwei Liu,Junyuan Liang,Jianting Zhang,Wuhui Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OG-SPR combines latent temporal self-prediction with next-observation prediction using lightweight adapters, enabling a shared representation to benefit from temporal dynamics without over-constraining it. It yields improved sample efficiency on 28 DM Control tasks, with notable gains in hard domains like dog and humanoid.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve sample-efficient model-free visual RL by leveraging both latent-temporal predictive signals and observation-level dynamics, addressing the limitation that either objective alone may underperform.

Method: A model-free visual RL approach that learns a shared representation with two auxiliary objectives: (i) multi-step latent self-prediction with adapters to avoid over-constraint, and (ii) next-observation prediction; evaluation on 28 tasks from the DeepMind Control Suite.

Result: OG-SPR outperforms state-of-the-art self-predictive and observation-predictive methods on aggregate metrics, with pronounced gains in challenging domains (e.g., dog and humanoid).

Conclusion: Jointly grounding representations in observation dynamics while maintaining temporal predictability in latent space via adapters yields superior sample efficiency; the approach mitigates over-constraint issues and offers robust improvements across diverse visual control tasks.

Abstract: Sample-efficient policy learning from pixels is a long-standing challenge in reinforcement learning (RL). Recent dynamics-based representation learning methods have significantly improved the sample efficiency of model-free visual RL by learning dynamics-aware representations through auxiliary prediction performed either in latent space (self-prediction) or observation space (observation prediction). However, state-of-the-art methods from both categories still struggle on challenging visual control tasks when training data is limited. We posit that relying on either predictive objective alone may be insufficient. In contrast, observation prediction grounds learned representations in observation-level dynamics, but does not directly regularize the temporal predictability of latent representations over extended horizons. In this paper, we propose Observation-Grounded Self-Predictive Representations (OG-SPR), a model-free visual RL algorithm for continuous control that learns representations that are both temporally predictive in latent space and grounded in observation-level dynamics. OG-SPR incorporates two core auxiliary objectives: multi-step latent self-prediction and next-observation prediction. We empirically show that directly imposing latent self-prediction on the shared representation may over-constrain it and does not necessarily improve performance. To address this issue, OG-SPR introduces two lightweight adapters for latent self-prediction, allowing the shared representation to benefit from temporally predictive signals without being forced to directly satisfy the self-prediction objective. Experiments on 28 visual control tasks from the DeepMind Control Suite show that OG-SPR improves aggregate performance over state-of-the-art self-predictive and observation-predictive RL methods, with particularly pronounced gains in challenging domains such as dog and humanoid.

</details>


### [164] [Neuro-Symbolic Closed-Loop Control of Laser Powder Bed Fusion with an In-Loop Ontology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05773)
*Gisuk Hong,Jaebong Cho,Hyunbo Cho*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A geometry-conditioned, neuro-symbolic, closed-loop control architecture for laser powder bed fusion uses a standards-aligned ontology within the control loop to map geometry and process objectives/constraints to observable signals, via a description-logic reasoner, and refines targets with a Gaussian-process-calibrated depth-to-width ratio. It demonstrates dross reduction and robust performance on IN625 using an Eagar–Tsai surrogate, with ontology-driven retargeting and minimal code changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To integrate high-level geometric/process constraints with low-level control in LPBF, enabling enforcement of quality-related bounds that are not directly measurable during build and to enable retargetability to different materials without reworking controller algorithms.

Method: A neuro-symbolic, ontology-driven control loop where a description-logic reasoner translates objectives/constraints into per-scan references and bounds; a geometry- and power-dependent depth-to-width ratio (supplied with calibrated uncertainty by a Gaussian process) maps a hidden quality metric (melt pool depth) to an observable width bound; a monotone guard, lack-of-fusion floor, and energy-density cap define constraints; per-scan control reduces to a small quadratic program.

Result: In an Eagar–Tsai surrogate calibrated to the NIST AM-Bench IN625 benchmark, the architecture eliminates geometry-blind dross, achieves zero dross with a small residual lack-of-fusion under dual scoring, degrades gracefully under plant mismatch, and retargets to new alloys/constraints by editing ontology data rather than code.

Conclusion: The architecture is feasible and an experimental calibration of the key ratio is the principal next step, with demonstrated potential for robust, ontology-driven constraint satisfaction and reconfigurability without code changes.

Abstract: A geometry-conditioned, neuro-symbolic closed-loop architecture is proposed for laser powder bed fusion, in which a standards-aligned ontology operates inside the control loop and couples symbolic reasoning with statistical learning to set the targets of a constraint-aware predictive controller. The ontology links the process objectives and constraints to the signals a controller can observe, and a description-logic reasoner converts them into the references and bounds enforced on each scan. The demonstrated case is overhang dross, a quality limit on the melt pool depth, which governs quality yet cannot be measured during the build, is mapped through a geometry- and power-dependent depth-to-width ratio onto a bound on the observable width, with the ratio and its calibrated uncertainty supplied by a Gaussian process. The reasoner classifies each upcoming feature and selects the active constraints-adding a lack-of-fusion floor at overhangs, a monotone guard beyond the calibrated range, and an energy-density cap where a process window is declared while running only on changes of geometric context and otherwise leaving a single small quadratic program on the per-scan path. In an Eagar-Tsai surrogate calibrated to the NIST AM-Bench benchmark for IN625, the architecture eliminates the dross produced by a geometry-blind controller, holds dross at zero with only a small residual lack-of-fusion under dual scoring, degrades gracefully under deliberate plant mismatch, and retargets to new alloys and constraints by editing ontology data rather than code. The results establish architectural feasibility, experimental calibration of the ratio is the principal next step.

</details>


### [165] [GROM: Gradient-Free Rapid One-Shot Machine Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05783)
*Paweł Batorski,Przemysław Spurek,Paul Swoboda*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GROM introduces a one-shot, exact analytical unlearning method for LLMs using a ridge-regularized least-squares update to targeted weights, enabling fast, gradient-free removal of specific knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current unlearning relies on costly iterative fine-tuning; gradient-based or quantization can fail to fully remove information, risking recovery; need a fast, provable removal method.

Method: Formulates unlearning as a ridge-regularized least-squares problem; derives a closed-form additive weight update for selected layers; relies on gradient-free forward passes; no backpropagation or convergence; preserves behavior on retained data while suppressing unwanted content.

Result: Updates run in seconds and are orders of magnitude faster than fine-tuning; achieves state-of-the-art forgetting-utility trade-offs on TOFU-5%, TOFU-10%, MUSE-Books, MUSE-News, WMDP; better robustness to low-bit quantization; code released.

Conclusion: Analytical, one-shot unlearning provides efficient, robust removal of targeted knowledge without iterative optimization, offering practical advantages over traditional fine-tuning approaches.

Abstract: Machine unlearning has become a critical capability for safely removing specific, sensitive knowledge from large language models (LLMs). Current state-of-the-art approaches primarily rely on iterative, training-time unlearning via fine-tuning. However, even when utilizing parameter-efficient dimensionality reduction techniques like LoRA, gradient-based optimization remains computationally expensive and lacks explicit analytical formulations. It can also leave the targeted knowledge merely hidden rather than removed, to the point that simply quantizing the unlearned model restores much of what it was supposed to have erased. To resolve this, we propose a novel one-shot unlearning approach, abandoning iterative optimization in favor of a direct, exact analytical solution. We frame the unlearning process as a ridge-regularized least-squares optimization problem, deriving a closed-form additive update for targeted weight matrices. This update forces the selected layer to suppress unwanted content while strictly preserving its behavior on retained data. Computed from gradient-free forward passes alone, with no backpropagation and no iteration to convergence, GROM applies the weight edit in mere seconds, which makes it orders of magnitude faster than traditional fine-tuning. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that GROM achieves state-of-the-art forgetting-utility trade-offs on TOFU-5%, TOFU-10%, MUSE-Books, MUSE-News and WMDP, significantly reducing computational overhead without sacrificing overall model performance. Because the update removes the targeted content from the weights instead of masking it, GROM also withstands the low-bit quantization attack that recovers much of the content a gradient-based baseline had appeared to forget. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/Batorskq/GROM.

</details>


### [166] [Predicting Task Difficulty Without Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05797)
*Stefan Krsteski,Charlotte Meyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Ex ante difficulty prediction across 17 agentic benchmarks; AUC can be misleading; token-level entropy is a useful signal; residuals between expected and observed difficulty reveal environment flaws like contamination and infeasibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecast task difficulty from task descriptions before costly simulations to enable benchmark calibration and progressive curricula, especially for long-horizon domains; counteract limitations of prior static-only or narrow-feature approaches.

Method: Empirical study over 17 agentic benchmarks spanning coding, mathematics, ML, web navigation, function calling, and more; analyze ex ante difficulty via predictive signals (including AUC and token-level entropy) and residuals between predicted and actual difficulty.

Result: AUC alone can mask poor estimates; token-level entropy provides predictive value; residuals help identify hidden environment issues such as contamination and infeasibility; demonstrates feasibility of ex ante difficulty prediction across diverse tasks.

Conclusion: Ex ante difficulty prediction is feasible and informative across diverse domains; it enables better benchmark design and early detection of environment flaws, supporting more efficient evaluation and training curricula.

Abstract: Task difficulty dictates an agent's likelihood of success, and estimating it without rollouts means forecasting this directly from a task description before executing costly simulations in stateful environments. Reliable estimates would therefore allow environment designers to calibrate evaluation benchmarks and construct progressive training curricula. This becomes increasingly important as agents move into long-horizon domains, where empirical trial-and-error is a severe computational bottleneck. Prior work on early prediction is limited to static tasks or isolated coding environments, often relying on narrow features and inaccurate evaluation metrics. We study \textit{ex ante} difficulty prediction across 17 agentic benchmarks spanning coding, mathematics, machine learning, web navigation, function calling, and other domains. We show that AUC can mask poor difficulty estimates, identify token-level entropy as a useful predictive signal, and show how residuals between expected and observed difficulty can expose hidden environment flaws such as contamination and infeasibility.

</details>


### [167] [Learning to Rank Tensor Network Contraction Plans for GPU-Accelerated Quantum Circuit Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05819)
*Alfred M. Pastor,Maribel Castillo,Jose M. Badia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learning-to-rank improves contraction-plan selection for tensor-network simulations; a listwise gradient-boosted model yields the best decisions, with robustness across GPU backends but partial backend dependence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Contraction cost in tensor-network simulations varies greatly with plan choice; automatic, effective plan ranking can substantially speed up GPU-based quantum circuit simulations.

Method: Represent each contraction plan by structural features derived from its pairwise contractions; train gradient-boosted rankers (listwise and pairwise) from GPU measurements using both listwise and pairwise objectives; evaluate on diverse circuit families with in-distribution and distribution-shift test sets; compare against random and MinFill baselines; assess cross-GPU transfer by training on one GPU and testing on another.

Result: Rankers generally identify better contraction plans; the listwise model provides the strongest overall decision quality; rankings are substantially stable across GPUs (though not perfectly); source-trained models retain useful decision quality when transferred to a second device without retraining.

Conclusion: Learning-to-Rank is a practical approach to reduce contraction-plan search overhead in tensor-network simulations, with performance that is partly backend dependent but generally robust across GPU architectures.

Abstract: Classical simulation remains essential for developing and validating quantum algorithms, but its cost grows rapidly with circuit size. Tensor-network contraction can reduce this cost by exploiting circuit structure, although its efficiency depends strongly on the chosen contraction plan. On GPUs, plans with similar theoretical complexity may perform very differently because execution also depends on parallelism, reduction structure, memory traffic, and contraction geometry. We present a learning-to-rank framework for selecting efficient contraction plans before executing them. Each plan is represented by structural features derived directly from its sequence of pairwise contractions, and gradient-boosted rankers are trained from GPU measurements using listwise and pairwise objectives. We evaluate the resulting models on diverse circuit families, using separate in-distribution and circuit-family-shift test sets, and compare them with random and MinFill-based baselines. The learned rankers generally identify better plans, with the listwise model providing the strongest overall decision quality. We also study backend shift by comparing empirical plan orderings on two GPU architectures and evaluating the source-trained models on the second device without retraining. The rankings remain substantially, though not perfectly, stable across GPUs, and the models retain useful decision quality. These results support Learning to Rank as a practical way to reduce contraction-plan search, while showing that performance remains partly backend dependent.

</details>


### [168] [Evidential Rule Learning for Interpretable Classification with Abstention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05859)
*Javier Fumanal-Idocin,Javier Andreu-Perez*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FERL is an interpretable fuzzy-rule classifier that outputs evidential beliefs and abstention directly in a single deterministic pass, achieving strong empirical gains and Lipschitz stability without extra heads or hold-outs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for transparent evidence behind decisions and reliable abstention, avoiding post-hoc calibration and multi-step inferences in rule-based learners.

Method: Fast Evidential Rule Learning (FERL) learns fuzzy rules whose memberships yield belief, plausibility, and abstention in one pass. It requires no auxiliary head, no held-out set, and no repeated inference, and it is proven to be Lipschitz stable.

Result: FERL outperforms state-of-the-art rule learners on 30 tabular datasets (average accuracy +2.6% over the second best). It achieves best utility-discounted accuracy among credal classifiers (u65/u80 ≈ 0.80/0.83) with high coverage (0.92). It matches dedicated OOD detectors on tabular near-OOD AUROC (≈77.7). In detector-class-disjoint concept-bottleneck tasks, it is within 2.3 AUROC points of the strongest detector on CUB and AwA2 and attains top AwA2 AUPR-Out and novel-class rejection, with the ability to name anomalous attributes.

Conclusion: FERL provides interpretable, accurate, and abstention-capable rules with evidence-based outputs, combining strong empirical performance with theoretical stability, and broad applicability to tabular and vision-related tasks requiring robust uncertainty handling.

Abstract: Interpretable classification often requires more than accurate predictions for real-life deployment: models should be transparent about the evidence behind their decisions and abstain when they cannot decide reliably. We introduce Fast Evidential Rule Learning (FERL), a method that learns interpretable, accurate fuzzy rule models whose outputs are evidential. Unlike post-hoc calibration, FERL's belief, plausibility, and abstention capabilities arise directly from the fuzzy memberships in a single deterministic pass, with no auxiliary head, held-out set, or repeated inference. Our theoretical analysis further shows that FERL is Lipschitz stable, which means that its evidential outputs vary smoothly with the input. Against state-of-the-art rule learners, FERL is statistically significantly more accurate across a 30 tabular-dataset benchmark ($+2.6\%$ average accuracy over the second best). Its native set predictions attain the best utility-discounted accuracy among credal classifiers ($u_{65}/u_{80}=0.80/0.83$ vs.\ $0.79/0.80$ for the naive credal classifier), at higher set coverage ($0.92$ vs.\ $\le0.82$). FERL also matches dedicated out-of-distribution detectors on tabular near-OOD detection ($77.7$ vs.\ $77.4$ AUROC for the strongest baseline). Under detector-class-disjoint concept-bottleneck evaluation, its it is within $2.3$ AUROC points of the strongest dedicated detector on both CUB and AwA2, while attaining the best AwA2 AUPR-Out ($68.3$) and novel-class rejection ($57.2$), while being able to name which attributes are anomalous.

</details>


### [169] [Beyond Feature Importance: A Comparative Analysis of Pattern Detection Methods in Cluster Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05880)
*Benjamin Connor,Anna Jurek-Loughrey,Lu Bai,Muhammad Fahim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-hoc explainability methods fail to reliably detect pattern-level cluster patterns; RF with permutation feature importance, LIME, and PCA each recover some features but miss patterns, indicating a need for dedicated pattern-detection methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpretation of clustering is difficult; existing explainability tools focus on feature importance or local explanations rather than structured patterns within clusters; synthetic controlled evaluation demonstrates gap and motivates new methods.

Method: Develop synthetic datasets with predefined injected patterns; apply three post-hoc analysis techniques (Random Forest surrogate with permutation feature importance, LIME, PCA); assess their ability to recover injected patterns.

Result: Each method can recover relevant features to some extent, but none consistently detects all injected pattern types; performance varies by pattern type.

Conclusion: There is a gap between current explainability tools and the needs of pattern-level cluster interpretation; results motivate developing dedicated pattern-detection methodologies for clustering.

Abstract: Interpreting clustering outcomes remains a fundamental challenge in data analysis, particularly in domains such as healthcare where meaningful patterns must be extracted from high-dimensional data. While numerous explainability techniques exist, they are primarily designed to assess feature importance or provide local instance-level explanations rather than to identify structured patterns present within clusters. This work presents a comparative evaluation of commonly used post-hoc analysis methods for pattern detection in clustering results. To enable controlled evaluation, we introduce a suite of synthetic datasets in which predefined patterns are systematically injected. Three widely used techniques are evaluated: a Random Forest surrogate model with permutation feature importance, LIME (Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations), and principal component analysis. Results demonstrate that although each method can successfully recover relevant features, none consistently detects all injected pattern types. These findings high- light a critical gap between existing explainability tools and the requirements of pattern-level cluster interpretation, motivating the development of dedicated pattern detection methodologies.

</details>


### [170] [Alternating Levenberg-Marquardt Training of Physics-Informed Neural Networks with Fourier-Enhanced Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05892)
*Yulun Wu,Matthieu Barreau,Miguel Aguiar,Karl H. Johansson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decouples representation learning from coefficient fitting in PINNs via a Fourier-enhanced alternating Levenberg–Marquardt framework (FALM-PINN). An upper-level learns a Fourier-enriched basis to capture high-frequency components; a lower-level solves for projection coefficients with Levenberg–Marquardt. Demonstrates global convergence and substantial accuracy gains (up to 100x reductions in relative L2 error) on challenging high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: PINNs struggle with high-frequency/multiscale PDEs and strongly nonlinear problems due to spectral bias and representation-coefficient coupling. A decoupled, Fourier-enriched approach could better capture fine-scale features while avoiding entangled optimization.

Method: A bilevel optimization framework: (upper level) learn a Fourier-enhanced basis to enrich the latent space with high-frequency content; (lower level) solve for projection coefficients on this basis via nonlinear least-squares using the Levenberg–Marquardt algorithm. The alternating training decouples representation learning from coefficient fitting; linear PDEs reduce to a single-step convex problem; global convergence is established for both linear and nonlinear/coupled PDEs.

Result: Numerical experiments on multiple challenging high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs show that FALM-PINN achieves relative L2 errors up to two orders of magnitude lower than state-of-the-art baselines; global convergence of the alternating scheme is proven.

Conclusion: FALM-PINN effectively mitigates spectral bias and representation-coefficient coupling, yielding substantial accuracy gains across nonlinear and coupled PDEs. The framework is broadly applicable, with a theoretical convergence guarantee and improved efficiency for linear cases.

Abstract: Physics-informed neural networks (PINNs) often fail to accurately resolve partial differential equations (PDEs) with high-frequency or multi-scale solutions, as well as strongly nonlinear problems. Two factors underlie this difficulty: spectral bias, the tendency of neural networks to underfit high-frequency features; and representation-coefficient coupling, the entanglement of representation learning and coefficient fitting within a single nonconvex optimization objective. In this work, we propose the Fourier-enhanced alternating Levenberg--Marquardt PINN (FALM-PINN), an optimization framework that decouples representation learning from coefficient fitting. The upper-level problem learns a Fourier-enhanced basis that enriches the latent space with high-frequency components, while the lower-level problem resolves the coupling by fitting the projection coefficients on this basis, solving a nonlinear least-squares problem with the Levenberg--Marquardt algorithm. The framework applies to general nonlinear and coupled PDE systems, and reduces to a single-step convex optimization problem for linear PDEs. We prove global convergence of the alternating training scheme in both cases. Numerical examples on multiple challenging high-frequency and nonlinear PDEs show that FALM-PINN achieves relative $L^2$ errors up to two orders of magnitude lower than state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [171] [CohortHijack: Robustness of Single Cell Annotation to Companion Cell Removal](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05900)
*Arash Vashagh,Yasmin Vashagh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CohortHijack reveals that altering non-target cells in the query cohort can preserve the target's expression and the model but still change refined labels, exposing a vulnerability in single-cell annotation pipelines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether refinement-based single-cell annotation is robust to deliberate manipulation of the query cohort, identifying potential attack surfaces and vulnerabilities.

Method: Introduce CohortHijack robustness audit: remove selected non-target cells from the query cohort (keeping target unchanged) using random vs structured removal, explored with greedy, multi-start, and beam search on PBMC3K and Paul15. Classifiers: logistic regression and calibrated linear SVM. Also assess CellTypist majority voting; ablations disable neighborhood refinement to isolate effects.

Result: Structured removal outperformed random removal on Paul15. Multi-start search altered 24.33% of linear-SVM targets and 19.67% of logistic-regression targets while removing a small cohort fraction and keeping mean collateral changes <0.4%. Effects vanish when neighborhood refinement is disabled. In CellTypist voting, independent predictions stayed same, but refined labels changed with small companion-cell removals.

Conclusion: Query cohort composition is a target-preserving attack surface in single-cell annotation; refinement-based labeling can be destabilized without altering the target profile or model, highlighting robustness concerns and the need for defenses that mitigate cohort-driven label changes.

Abstract: Many single-cell annotation tools refine an initial cell label using nearby cells or cluster-level voting. We study whether this refinement can be manipulated without changing the target cell. We introduce CohortHijack, a robustness audit that removes selected non-target cells from the query cohort while preserving the target expression profile, base prediction, and trained model.
