We evaluate random and structured removal methods, together with greedy, multi-start, and beam search, on PBMC3K and Paul15 using logistic regression and calibrated linear SVM classifiers. Structured removal was consistently stronger than random removal on Paul15. Multi-start search changed 24.33% of linear-SVM targets and 19.67% of logistic-regression targets while removing a small fraction of the cohort and keeping mean collateral changes below 0.4%. Ablations confirmed that the effect disappeared when neighborhood refinement was disabled. We also evaluated CellTypist majority voting, where independent predictions remained unchanged across all evaluations, but refined labels changed after small companion-cell removals. These findings identify query cohort composition as a target-preserving attack surface in single-cell annotation.

</details>


### [172] [BioM-JEPA: joint-embedding prediction of graph-connected gene blocks in single cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05928)
*Yuhao Wang,Zelin Zang,Yuxuan Liu,Zhen Lei,Stan Z. Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioM-JEPA introduces a block-level joint-embedding predictive architecture for single-cell data, predicting graph-connected gene blocks instead of individual genes using a student–teacher framework with EMA updates and linear attention. It yields higher effective rank embeddings, robust representations, and substantially higher throughput vs baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address sparsity and limited predictive power of gene-level self-supervision in scRNA-seq by leveraging biologically informed gene blocks and scalable attention to improve representation quality and efficiency.

Method: Define gene blocks from protein-association networks and corpus-derived coexpression evidence. A student network predicts each target-block representation from the remaining genes in a cell; a slowly updated teacher supplies the corresponding block target from the full observed gene set. Use linear attention to avoid quadratic gene-by-gene interactions. Evaluate on CellBench tasks and hPancreas with a one-epoch run, comparing against token-prediction and reconstruction controls.

Result: Block-level predictions yield embeddings with higher effective rank and weaker dependence on gene depth. Frozen BioM-JEPA embeddings preserve expression, pathway, and neighborhood information and achieve the lowest aggregate perturbation–response error among evaluated models. Diagnostics align with canonical pancreatic programs and perturbation relationships. In one-epoch hPancreas, linear-attention BioM-JEPA attains 5.75× higher fine-tuning throughput and 3.76× higher embedding throughput than scFoundation.

Conclusion: Graph-connected gene blocks are effective prediction units for JEPA-style self-supervised representation learning in single-cell biology, offering improved representation quality and computational efficiency.

Abstract: Single-cell transcriptomes are sparse observations of coordinated biological programmes, yet most self-supervised models learn by reconstructing individual genes. Here we present BioM-JEPA, a joint-embedding predictive architecture that instead predicts aggregate representations of graph-connected gene blocks defined by protein-association and corpus-derived coexpression evidence. A student network infers each target-block representation from the remaining genes in a cell, while a slowly updated teacher supplies the corresponding target from the full observed gene set. Under the reported extraction procedure, block-level prediction produced embeddings with higher effective rank and weaker association with detected-gene depth in the tested diagnostics than token-prediction, random-block and reconstruction controls. Across CellBench tasks, frozen BioM-JEPA embeddings retained expression, pathway and neighbourhood information and achieved the lowest aggregate perturbation-response error among the evaluated models. Representation diagnostics were also consistent with canonical pancreatic programmes and compositional relationships between genetic perturbations. Linear attention avoids constructing a quadratic gene-by-gene attention matrix; in a matched one-epoch hPancreas experiment at batch size 8, BioM-JEPA provided 5.75-fold higher fine-tuning throughput and 3.76-fold higher held-out embedding throughput than scFoundation. Together, these results support graph-connected gene blocks as useful prediction units for JEPA-style representation learning in single-cell biology.

</details>


### [173] [How Far Do Simple Transformations Translate Across Text Embedding Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05980)
*Sid Ali Hamideche,Louis Adrien Dufrene,Quentin Lampin,Guillaume Larue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Simple linear mappings can align some heterogeneous text embeddings, enabling transfer for certain model pairs, but not universally across all nine models; compatibility depends on architecture, training objective, pooling, and data distribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether latent universality exists across independently trained text embedding models and whether simple translators can enable AI-to-AI latent communication without human decoding.

Method: Evaluate nine embedding models with varying architectures, pooling strategies, and training objectives. Train lightweight linear translators and assess cross-model compatibility using CKA, downstream transfer tasks, fidelity, and retrieval performance.

Result: Translators recover meaningful shared structure for some compatible model pairs and can support transfer, but fail for others. Compatibility is jointly determined by architecture, training objective, pooling, and data distribution. Overall, heterogeneous embedding spaces are not universally related by simple mappings.

Conclusion: Latent universality among heterogeneous embedding spaces is not guaranteed. Simple linear mappings are not universally sufficient to align different embedding models; alignment strategies may need to account for architectural and training differences and data distribution.

Abstract: We investigate whether simple transformations can translate representations across heterogeneous text embedding models. Understanding how independently trained models organize semantic information is an enabler for AI-to-AI latent communication without decoding into human-readable text. Focusing on lightweight translators such as linear mappings, we test the literature hypothesis of latent universality in a realistic text setting beyond simplified benchmarks. Across nine embedding models differing in architecture, pooling strategy, and training objective, we evaluate compatibility using CKA, downstream transfer, fidelity, and retrieval. Simple translators recover meaningful shared structure and support transfer for some compatible pairs, but fail sharply for others. Compatibility depends jointly on architecture, training objective, pooling, and data distribution. Overall, the results show that heterogeneous embedding spaces are not universally related by simple mappings as often suggested in some literature.

</details>


### [174] [THBKG: A Temporal Biomedical Knowledge Graph for Decision-Aligned Clinical Advancement Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05982)
*Pui Chung Siu,Claudia Cabrera,Mani Mudaliar,Arkaitz Zubiaga*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Temporal Heterogeneous Biomedical Knowledge Graph (THBKG) with time-stamped evidence for target-disease links, enabling decision-time retrospective validation and improved prediction over direct evidence; large-scale graph with explainable, path-based insights and released dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inadequate target-disease linkage accounts for 40-50% of Phase II efficacy failures; existing biomedical knowledge graphs cannot reconstruct past evidence profiles at the decision date, hindering retrospective validation and anticipatory hypothesis testing.

Method: Construct THBKG with 110,396 entities and 11.1M edges across 19 relation types, each edge annotated with the year its supporting evidence changed. Define a decision-aligned benchmark: predict, for a target-disease pair entering Phase II, whether it advances to Phase III using evidence datable before the decision. Use graph propagation over the THBKG and a path-based explainer to decompose predictions into the underlying evidence landscape.

Result: Graph propagation over THBKG outperforms direct-evidence baselines under the same decision-aligned protocol, achieving a relative success of 4.3–4.5 for the top ten pairs per therapeutic area. Notably, 72.8% of pairs have no direct target–disease evidence at decision time, yet the encoders rank them above chance by propagating through intervening biology. A path-based explainer attributes predictions to the evidence landscape behind the hypothesis. The THBKG is released as a continually updated substrate for retrospective validation.

Conclusion: THBKG provides a temporally aware substrate to study therapeutic target hypotheses, enabling retrospective validation and explainable predictions, with improved early-phase decision support beyond what direct-edge evidence can offer.

Abstract: Inadequate target--disease linkage accounts for 40--50\% of Phase~II efficacy failures, so anticipating which programmes will advance would let sponsors back the hypotheses most likely to reach patients. What a programme can be judged on is the evidence that supported its linkage \emph{when it entered the clinic}. No existing biomedical knowledge graph allows that evidence profile to be assembled as of a past date. We present the Temporal Heterogeneous Biomedical Knowledge Graph (THBKG), which describes and predicts therapeutic target--disease links through time: 110,396 entities and 11.1M edges across nineteen relation types, each edge carrying the year its evidence changed, so a pair's profile can be recovered as it stood when its own decision fell due. On this graph we define a decision-aligned benchmark that predicts, for a target--disease pair entering Phase~II, whether it advances to Phase~III on evidence datable before that decision. Graph propagation over the THBKG outranks every direct-evidence reference scored under the same decision-aligned protocol, reaching a relative success of 4.3--4.5 at the top ten pairs per therapeutic area. The gain concentrates on the 72.8\% of pairs with no direct target--disease evidence at their decision point, where a direct-edge model has nothing to read: the encoders still rank five- to sixfold above chance, recovering the signal by propagating over the intervening biology. Adapting a path-based explainer to the decision-time subgraph decomposes each prediction into the evidence landscape behind the hypothesis for explainable prediction. We release the THBKG as a continually updated substrate for studying therapeutic target hypotheses by retrospective validation.

</details>


### [175] [A Unified Risk View of Uncertainty: Posterior Risk for Disentanglement and Evaluation Beyond Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05995)
*Frieder Wizgall,Georg Tirpitz,Moritz Seiler,Kerstin Ritter,Bálint Mucsányi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theoretical and benchmark proposal to unify uncertainty as pointwise posterior risk, enabling direct (oracle) epistemic and aleatoric uncertainty targets via semi-synthetic data, with a benchmark that avoids proxy tasks like OOD detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In safety-critical settings, it is essential to decompose predictive uncertainty into epistemic and aleatoric components. Current literature lacks consistent definitions and reliable ground-truth uncertainty targets, hindering fair evaluation.

Method: Define uncertainty as the expected loss under the distribution of plausible ground-truth functions given data (pointwise posterior risk). Combine Bayesian function uncertainty with estimator deviations from posterior mean. Use semi-synthetic datasets with real covariates and known generative processes to compute oracle uncertainties, enabling direct, non-proxy evaluation.

Result: A theory-backed benchmark that permits direct computation of oracle epistemic and aleatoric uncertainty. Empirically, accurate predictions do not guarantee good uncertainty disentanglement, and the benchmark reveals method differences and sensitivity to datasets and modeling choices.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled, ground-truth-aligned way to evaluate uncertainty estimates; it highlights that good predictive accuracy does not imply well-calibrated or properly disentangled uncertainty, and it supports nuanced comparison of uncertainty estimation methods.

Abstract: Reliable uncertainty estimates are critical in safety-sensitive applications, where understanding the sources of predictive uncertainty is essential. This often requires disentangling epistemic uncertainty from aleatoric uncertainty, yet these uncertainty types are not defined consistently across the literature, making it difficult to assess whether a method produces accurate uncertainty estimates. Evaluation is further complicated by the fact that ground-truth epistemic uncertainty is typically unavailable. Existing benchmarks therefore mostly rely on proxy tasks such as out-of-distribution detection, which do not provide complete ground-truth uncertainty targets and offer limited insight into the structure and quality of uncertainty estimates. We propose a unified definition of uncertainty as pointwise posterior risk, the expected loss of a predictor under the distribution of plausible ground-truth functions given the data. This view combines Bayesian uncertainty over functions with estimator-dependent deviations from the posterior mean, capturing effects such as misspecification and optimization error. This formulation constitutes the foundation of a theory-backed benchmark that enables direct computation of oracle epistemic and aleatoric uncertainty using semi-synthetic datasets with real covariates and known generative processes. By avoiding proxy evaluations, the benchmark enables fine-grained analysis of uncertainty estimates. Empirically, we find that accurate prediction does not guarantee reliable uncertainty disentanglement. The benchmark reveals practically useful differences between methods, identifying approaches with meaningful alignment to oracle uncertainty targets while exposing sensitivity to datasets and modeling choices.

</details>


### [176] [Do Tabular Foundation Models Agree with Themselves?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06004)
*Christian Klötergens,Vijaya Krishna Yalavarthi,Lars Schmidt-Thieme,Tom Hanika*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TFMs fail to satisfy joint-distribution consistency: marginalization and factorization consistency do not hold; authors propose two criteria and report violations across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Since the ground-truth joint distribution over tabular data is unknown, evaluating TFMs against the true posterior is impractical. The paper reframes evaluation to assess whether a model's predictions could arise from any joint distribution by enforcing distribution-consistency criteria.

Method: Introduce two requirements: marginalization consistency (marginalized conditionals equal directly predicted marginals) and factorization consistency (different factorization orders yield the same joint). Evaluate TFMs on classification and regression via autoregressive sampling and show violations of both criteria across all datasets.

Result: Every TFM evaluated violates both marginalization and factorization consistency for both classification and regression datasets.

Conclusion: TFMs' faithfulness to an underlying joint distribution is questionable. The proposed consistency criteria reveal fundamental limitations of current TFMs, suggesting need for models that satisfy these properties or alternative evaluation frameworks when the ground-truth distribution is unavailable.

Abstract: Tabular Foundation Models (TFMs) are currently the best approach to tabular prediction problems. They are constructed as transformers that approximate the Bayesian posterior predictive distribution based on a pre-training prior. These univariate predictors can be converted into multivariate ones autoregressively by sampling one target and adding it to the features.
  However, the faithfulness of the resulting joint has not been investigated. Furthermore, TFMs cannot be evaluated against the posterior itself, at least not on real-world datasets, because the ground-truth distribution is unknown. We therefore propose asking a different question: could a model's predictions result from any joint distribution? To answer this question, we pose two requirements that any such model must satisfy. The first is marginalization consistency, which demands that marginalized conditionals are equal to directly predicted marginals. The second is factorization consistency, which demands that different factorization orders result in equal joint distributions. Every TFM that we evaluate violates both of these requirements for both classification and regression across all datasets.

</details>


### [177] [ProDVI: Programmatic Dynamics Priors for Value Network Initialization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06015)
*Xinwei Liu,Junyuan Liang,Jianting Zhang,Wuhui Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProDVI uses LLM-generated executable code to create synthetic dynamics priors for pretraining a value-network encoder, improving sample efficiency without relying on datasets, simulators, or meta-learning resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL is sample-inefficient, and relying on pre-collected data, simulators, or related-task meta-learning is often impractical. ProDVI provides a resource-light initialization by leveraging world knowledge encoded in language models.

Method: Prompt a code-generating language model to produce executable Python functions that encode coarse environment dynamics. Use these functions to generate synthetic transitions and train an auxiliary dynamics-prediction objective to pretrain the state-action encoder of the value network within an actor-critic framework. The priors are used only for representation pretraining; online RL will adjust from real transitions.

Result: Experiments on OpenAI Gym and DeepMind Control Suite show improved sample efficiency for model-free RL algorithms.

Conclusion: Programmatic Dynamics Priors offer a practical, data-light initialization that introduces dynamics-aware inductive biases; generated programs do not need to faithfully simulate the target environment and can be corrected through online learning.

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) is notoriously sample inefficient. One contributing factor is that RL agents are typically initialized from scratch, forcing them to acquire task-relevant knowledge through online interaction. Existing approaches obtain informative initializations through pre-collected datasets, high-fidelity simulators, or meta-learning over related tasks, but these prerequisites may be difficult to access or even unavailable. In this paper, we propose Programmatic Dynamics Priors for Value Network Initialization (ProDVI), a framework that leverages the commonsense and domain knowledge encoded in large language models to initialize RL agents without relying on these resources. Specifically, ProDVI prompts a code-generating language model to produce executable Python functions that encode coarse hypotheses about environment dynamics. These functions are then used to generate synthetic transitions. Based on these transitions, we construct an auxiliary dynamics prediction objective to pretrain the state-action encoder of the value network in an actor-critic framework. The learned representation provides dynamics-aware inductive biases before online RL begins. Importantly, the generated programs are used only for representation pretraining and are not required to faithfully simulate the target environment. While the generated programs may be inaccurate, their induced initialization can be corrected through online learning from real transitions and rewards. Experiments on OpenAI Gym and DeepMind Control Suite tasks show that ProDVI can effectively improve the sample efficiency of model-free RL algorithms.

</details>


### [178] [BioKD: Selective Physiology-to-Video Knowledge Distillation via Reliability Gate for Emotion Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06023)
*Bojing Hou,Ruohao Li,Yitong Zhu,Hongjun Liu,Luwen Yu,Yuyang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BioKD is a reliability-aware knowledge distillation framework that uses physiological signals as privileged information during training to guide a video-based emotion recognition model, with gating and progressive distillation to handle unreliable supervision, achieving better performance on DEAP/AMIGOS without extra inference cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video-based emotion recognition struggles with ambiguous and masked cues and physiology-based signals are hard to deploy; there is a need for robust cross-modal distillation that accounts for noisy teacher supervision and subject variability.

Method: Introduce BioKD: a physiology-to-video distillation framework where physiology serves as privileged information during training; includes a sample-wise reliability-aware gating mechanism and a progressive distillation strategy to adaptively regulate knowledge transfer; results in a video-only inference pipeline with no additional inference-time overhead.

Result: Empirical evaluation on DEAP and AMIGOS shows BioKD improves valence and arousal recognition under both trial-wise and subject-wise settings; e.g., 68.01% (trial-wise arousal) on DEAP and 65.29% (subject-wise), and better handling of overconfident teacher errors compared to entropy-only weighting.

Conclusion: Reliability-aware privileged-information distillation enables stable cross-modal learning for affective recognition with non-intrusive inference, removing the need for physiological sensing at test time while maintaining or improving performance.

Abstract: To address the limitations of video-based emotion recognition under ambiguous or socially masked behavioral cues, as well as the poor deployability of physiological signals, this paper proposes a reliability-aware physiology-to-video knowledge distillation framework, termed BioKD. The proposed framework leverages physiological signals as privileged information during training to guide a video-based student model in learning deep affective representations, while relying solely on non-intrusive video inputs at inference time. To cope with the high noise and instability of physiological teacher supervision caused by inter-subject variability, signal artifacts, and temporal inconsistency, BioKD incorporates a sample-wise reliability-aware gating mechanism together with a progressive distillation strategy. By adaptively regulating the strength of knowledge transfer, the framework suppresses negative transfer induced by unreliable physiological supervision and enables more stable cross-modal distillation. Experiments on DEAP and AMIGOS show that BioKD consistently outperforms representative baselines under both trial-wise and subject-wise evaluation protocols for valence and arousal recognition. For example, BioKD achieves 68.01\% on DEAP (trial-wise arousal) and 65.29\% under the more challenging subject-wise setting, demonstrating improved performance under a subject-independent evaluation setting. Further analyses show that BioKD effectively mitigates overconfident teacher errors and outperforms an entropy-only weighting strategy, confirming the importance of explicitly modeling supervision reliability. In addition, BioKD introduces no additional inference-time overhead relative to the same video student architecture and removes the need for physiological sensing and multimodal synchronization.

</details>


### [179] [Hybrid-Adaptive Thread Tuning to Mitigate Simulation Execution Bottlenecks in High-Performance Reinforcement Learning Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06025)
*Jiming Su,Hantao Hua,Lujia Yin,Yiping Yao,Feng Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AutoThread is an adaptive thread-tuning approach for RL simulation inference that uses a Physics-Informed Neural Operator (PINO) to predict thread counts and a finite-source M/M/1 queue to constrain predictions, plus load-aware online fine-tuning. It achieves notable gains over static strategies and prior baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulation-in-the-loop RL often suffers from dynamic, workload-sensitive execution overhead due to suboptimal thread resource allocation. Existing multithreaded strategies cannot align resources quickly enough, causing contention and reduced throughput.

Method: A hybrid adaptive thread-tuning framework: (1) PINO-based predictor to estimate optimal thread count; (2) finite-source M/M/1 queue model to constrain/prompt predictions under dynamic workloads; (3) load-aware online fine-tuning to adapt to prediction errors and refine allocations.

Result: Empirical results show 18.4% average speedup over static strategies, throughput of 1.7x and 1.8x that of XGBoost and Reinforcer, and up to 83.8% reduction in execution time compared with state-of-the-art methods; code and data are publicly available.

Conclusion: AutoThread demonstrates that combining physics-informed neural prediction with queueing theory can robustly adapt thread resources in dynamic RL inference workloads, delivering substantial performance gains and practical reproducibility; future work could explore other queueing models and broader RL workloads.

Abstract: In simulation-in-the-loop decision-making systems, reinforcement learning (RL) inference is often constrained by simulator-side execution overhead, where workloads are highly dynamic and sensitive to runtime thread configurations. Existing multithreaded strategies struggle to match thread resources before or during execution, causing resource contention, scheduling overhead, and reduced throughput. Through empirical analysis, we identify the ratio of task execution time to scheduling time as the key factor determining the optimal thread count. Building on this insight, we propose AutoThread, a hybrid adaptive thread-tuning method for mitigating simulation bottlenecks in RL inference. AutoThread employs a Physics-Informed Neural Operator (PINO) as a thread-count predictor and incorporates a finite-source M/M/1 queueing model to constrain and guide prediction, enabling fast and accurate estimation under dynamic workloads. It further performs load-aware online fine-tuning to compensate for prediction errors and refine resource allocation. Experiments show that AutoThread improves average speedup by 18.4\% over static strategies, achieves average throughput of 1.7x and 1.8x that of XGBoost and Reinforcer, respectively, and reduces execution time by up to 83.8\% compared with state-of-the-art methods. Our code and dataset are publicly available at https://github.com/suchenjm/AutoThread.

</details>


### [180] [Dynamic Graph Prompting via Topology-Routed Mixed-Curvature Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06031)
*Quanxin Wang,Xuanting Xie,Bingheng Li,Xingtong Yu,Shuo Wang,Ruiyi Fang,Zhao Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CurvPrompt introduces a topology-routed, geometry-aware prompting framework for dynamic graphs. It uses a bank of curvature-diverse Riemannian experts with learnable prompts and a topology-aware gate to create personalized, mixed-curvature representations, switching from soft routing during pre-training to hard Top-K routing for downstream adaptation. Result: significant gains in few-shot link prediction and strong node classification, demonstrating geometry adaptation is beneficial.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temporal shifts in local graph topology (clustering and degree heterogeneity) alter the optimal geometric representation, causing a mismatch between a fixed embedding space and evolving data—termed geometry under-adaptation. This drives the need for geometry-aware prompting that can adapt the representation geometry over time.

Method: Maintain a bank of curvature-diverse Riemannian experts, each with a learnable prompt. A topology-aware gate routes each node–time instance to a sparse subset of experts, yielding a personalized mixed-curvature representation. Employ soft routing during pre-training to learn a continuous topology–geometry mapping; switch to hard Top-K routing with uniform weights during downstream adaptation to ensure efficiency and stability.

Result: Experiments on four benchmark datasets show that CurvPrompt markedly improves few-shot link prediction and yields strong, consistent performance on node classification, validating the advantage of geometry-adaptive prompting in dynamic graphs.

Conclusion: Geometry-adaptive prompting is necessary for dynamic graphs; CurvPrompt provides an effective framework that leverages multiple curved geometries and topology-aware routing to adapt representation geometry over time, especially under label scarcity.

Abstract: Dynamic graph prompting freezes a pre-trained temporal backbone and adapts it to label-scarce downstream tasks using lightweight prompts. However, existing methods operate within a single, fixed embedding space. In this work, we reveal that temporal shifts in local clustering and degree heterogeneity actively reorganize the edge curvature spectrum---indicating that the optimal representation geometry dynamically evolves with local topology over time. We formalize this unaddressed mismatch as geometry under-adaptation. To overcome this limitation, we propose CurvPrompt, a topology-routed geometry prompting framework for dynamic graphs. Instead of relying on a single space, CurvPrompt maintains a bank of curvature-diverse Riemannian experts, each paired with a learnable prompt. A topology-aware gate dynamically routes each node--time instance to a sparse subset of experts, constructing a personalized mixed-curvature representation. To ensure parameter efficiency and training stability under extreme label scarcity, CurvPrompt employs soft routing during pre-training to build a continuous topology--geometry mapping, and transitions to hard Top-K routing with uniform weights during downstream adaptation. Extensive experiments across four benchmark datasets show that CurvPrompt significantly advances few-shot link prediction while delivering strong, consistent performance on node classification tasks, validating the necessity of geometry-adaptive prompting.

</details>


### [181] [Does Latent Context Help? A Controlled Evaluation of Inverse Reinforcement Learning in Arctic Shipping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06105)
*Vaishnav Vaidheeswaran,Dilith Jayakody,Biruk Ambaw,Jaswanth Kumar,Md Mahbub Alam,Gabriel Spadon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nonlinear shared reward improves held-out likelihood over a linear baseline; adding per-vessel latent context hurts performance. Observable route and environmental features largely explain apparent vessel-level variation; no single metric suffices to evaluate learned rewards. Recommend checking observed features before introducing latent context to improve trustworthy AI in safety-critical routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the need for interpretable and robust reward models in AI-assisted Arctic navigation. IRL recovers reward structures from trajectories; latent-context meta-IRL aims to capture behavioral heterogeneity, but it is unclear whether latent factors reflect genuine hidden preferences or information already present in observed states.

Method: Compare three reward models using the same nonlinear architecture: (i) linear shared reward, (ii) nonlinear shared reward, (iii) latent-context nonlinear reward. Dataset: 3,186 AIS voyages from 202 vessels across nine Arctic shipping seasons. Evaluate using held-out likelihood and additional analyses: behavioral analysis, context probes, and a pre-registered feature-hiding ablation. Examine predictive accuracy, route fidelity, and reward transfer to assess model rankings.

Result: Nonlinear reward improves held-out likelihood by 50.9% over the linear baseline. Adding vessel-specific latent context reduces performance by 16.5%. Behavior analysis and ablations indicate most vessel-level variation is explained by observable route and environmental conditions rather than hidden vessel-specific factors. Predictive accuracy, route fidelity, and reward transfer yield different model rankings; no single metric suffices to evaluate rewards.

Conclusion: Before adding per-vessel latent context, test whether observed route, environmental, and vessel features already explain behavioral variation. This approach supports more trustworthy AI deployment in safety-critical domains and informs how to evaluate learned rewards using multiple metrics.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI)-assisted navigation can help Arctic shipping adapt to rapidly changing sea-ice conditions, but reliable deployment requires reward models that are interpretable and robust to changing environments. Inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) provides a framework for recovering such rewards from vessel trajectories, while recent meta-IRL methods introduce latent context variables to capture behavioral heterogeneity. However, it remains unclear whether these latent representations recover genuinely hidden preferences or simply re-encode information already available in the observed state. We conduct a controlled evaluation on 3,186 AIS-derived voyages from 202 vessels across nine Arctic shipping seasons, comparing a linear shared reward, a nonlinear shared reward, and a latent-context model built on the same nonlinear architecture. The nonlinear reward improves held-out likelihood by 50.9% over the linear baseline, whereas adding vessel-specific latent context reduces performance by 16.5%. Behavioral analysis, context probes, and a pre-registered feature-hiding ablation show that apparent vessel-level variation is largely explained by observable route and environmental conditions rather than hidden vessel-specific factors. Moreover, predictive accuracy, route fidelity, and reward transfer yield different model rankings, demonstrating that no single metric is sufficient to evaluate learned rewards. These findings motivate testing whether the observed route, environmental, and vessel features already explain behavioral variation before adding per-vessel latent context. This supports more trustworthy AI deployment in safety-critical domains.

</details>


### [182] [Kastor: An efficient fine-tuning strategy for generative emulation of PDE simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06107)
*Guillaume Couairon,Alexis Jacq,Yu-Han Wu,Renu Singh,Yana Hasson,Quentin Berthet,Romuald Elie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kastor combines a large-stride causal AR model with a non-causal temporal super-resolution network and mean prediction regularization (MPR) to create fast, stable generative surrogates for deterministic physics simulations; enhanced by spatial gradient matching, yielding strong accuracy and efficiency gains over Walrus on The Well datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auto-regressive ML emulators for PDE-like systems suffer from error accumulation and difficulty capturing stochasticity; a scalable, accurate generative surrogate is needed for fast, faithful physics simulations.

Method: Two-stage inference: a large-stride causal autoregressive model paired with a non-causal temporal super-resolution network; Mean Prediction Regularization (MPR) to constrain predictions to the deterministic distribution mean under null noise; spatial gradient matching; evaluation on The Well benchmark; applicable to Functional Generative Networks (FGN) and diffusion-based emulators.

Result: Significant improvements in forecasting accuracy, spectral fidelity (power spectrum density), and computational efficiency; 42.9% average reduction in forecasting error compared to Walrus finetuning baseline; outperforms Walrus on VRMSE for 8 of 10 datasets.

Conclusion: The Kastor framework delivers robust, efficient, and high-fidelity generative surrogates for physics simulations, combining two-stage inference, MPR, and gradient matching to surpass existing baselines across diverse datasets and models.

Abstract: Machine learning offers a promising avenue to accelerate physical simulations by replacing computationally expensive traditional Partial Differential Equation (PDE) solvers with fast, differentiable surrogate models. However, standard auto-regressive ML emulators often suffer from error accumulation over long horizons and struggle to capture the stochasticity of complex physical systems. In this paper, we propose Kastor, a comprehensive methodology to adapt a deterministic physics foundation model into a highly efficient and accurate generative surrogate. First, we introduce a two-stage inference scheme that combines a large-stride causal auto-regressive model with a non-causal temporal super-resolution network, significantly reducing error accumulation while minimizing computational cost. Second, we present Mean prediction regularization (MPR), a novel training objective that constrains the generative model to predict the deterministic distribution mean under null noise conditioning. This regularization dramatically improves the performance and stability of both Functional Generative Networks (FGN) and diffusion-based emulators. Finally, we demonstrate that incorporating spatial gradient matching improves the accuracy and physical fidelity of the simulations as measured by power spectrum density. Extensive evaluations on diverse simulation datasets of the benchmark The Well show that with these components, our model outperforms competing methods in forecasting accuracy, spectral consistency, and computational efficiency. Our model achieves a 42.9% average reduction in forecasting compared to our reference based on the Walrus finetuning methodology, and outperforms Walrus for 8 out of 10 datasets on variance-normalized RMSE (VRMSE).

</details>


### [183] [Is Self-Pretraining really useful to improve diagnosis in medical Time Series?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06122)
*Omar Coser,Antonio Orvieto,Paolo Soda,Loredana Zollo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Self-pretraining (SPT) on transformer-based medical time-series improves classification accuracy by 0-6 percentage points across three tasks (rehabilitation robotics, stress detection, Parkinson's gait), with larger gains for deeper models, and benefits extend to both multivariate and simple univariate inputs without task-specific architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether the benefits of Self-PreTraining observed for long-context transformers extend to medical time-series data, including multivariate, univariate, and multimodal settings, especially under limited data.

Method: Evaluate transformer architectures on three medical time-series tasks: Camargo rehabilitation robotics, Non-EEG Stress, and Gait Parkinson's Disease. Train from scratch or with SPT using four masking-based objectives aimed at promoting temporal and cross-modal representation learning. Systematically vary model depth to study capacity interaction with pre-training. Compare across datasets, masking strategies, and input modalities.

Result: SPT consistently improves classification accuracy by 0-6 percentage points depending on masking strategy, dataset, and architecture. Gains appear in multivariate contexts and even with simple univariate inputs. Improvement amplifies with deeper models that better leverage enriched temporal representations learned during pre-training.

Conclusion: SPT is a simple, general pre-training strategy that enhances transformer performance on medical time-series tasks without task-specific architectural changes, suggesting improved robustness and accuracy in data-limited clinical settings.

Abstract: Inspired by recent evidence that transformer architectures benefit from Self-PreTraining (SPT) on long-context benchmarks, we investigate whether similar gains extend to multimodal, multivariate, and even simple univariate medical time series. Our objective is to assess the impact of SPT on the performance and scalability of transformer-based models across diverse medical applications, particularly under limited data conditions. We evaluate transformer architectures on three representative medical time-series tasks: rehabilitation robotics (Camargo dataset), stress detection (Non-EEG Stress), and Parkinson's disease detection (Gait Parkinson's Disease). Models are trained either from scratch or through SPT using four masking-based objectives designed to promote temporal and cross-modal representation learning, and we systematically vary model depth to examine how capacity interacts with pre-training benefits. Across datasets and configurations, SPT consistently improves classification accuracy by 0-6 percentage points depending on masking strategy, dataset and architecture, with gains observed not only in multivariate settings but also when models are restricted to simple univariate inputs. The improvements increase for deeper models that can better exploit the enriched temporal representations learned during pre-training. These findings indicate that SPT is a simple and general strategy that enhances transformer performance on medical time-series tasks without requiring task-specific architectural changes, supporting its potential to improve robustness and accuracy in data-limited clinical settings.

</details>


### [184] [LLM Inference Under Bursty Workload Distribution: Modifying the WAIT Algorithm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06135)
*Anjali Gangadhar Katageria,Shobha Rani,Raghu Nandan Sengupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extend the WAIT scheduling algorithm to adapt to time-varying, non-Poisson arrival rates via online estimation of request intensity from interarrival times, evaluated on MMPP-based synthetic workloads. Shows higher throughput than Sarathi-Serve, ORCA, and vLLM in low arrival-rate shift scenarios with comparable latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world traffic is bursty and dynamic, violating Poisson assumptions in existing WAIT-based scheduling. There is a need for an online, lightweight method to adapt to changing arrival rates without requiring prior traffic knowledge.

Method: A lightweight extension to WAIT performing online estimation of request intensity using observed interarrival times. Evaluation uses Markov Modulated Poisson Process (MMPP)-based synthetic workloads across diverse request types, comparing against Sarathi-Serve, ORCA, and vLLM.

Result: The proposed method achieves higher throughput than the baselines in the evaluated scenarios with low arrival-rate shifts, while maintaining comparable latency.

Conclusion: Time-varying arrival-rate adaptation via online intensity estimation enhances throughput for LLM serving queues with minimal latency impact and without prior traffic knowledge.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) such as ChatGPT and Claude are widely used for information retrieval and problem-solving. Recent work has focused on improving scheduling algorithms to boost throughput while maintaining low latency. However, these approaches often assume Poisson request arrivals with constant rates - an assumption that fails to reflect the inherently bursty and dynamic nature of real-world traffic. We propose a lightweight extension to the state-of-the-art WAIT algorithm [1], which adapts to time-varying arrival rates without prior traffic knowledge. The proposed algorithm performs online estimation of request intensity based on observed interarrival times. Using Markov Modulated Poisson Process (MMPP)-based synthetic workloads with diverse request types, we conduct a simulation-based evaluation demonstrating that the proposed method achieves higher throughput than Sarathi-Serve [2], ORCA [3], and vLLM [4] in the evaluated low arrival-rate shift scenarios while maintaining comparable latency.

</details>


### [185] [SkillTFM: Gated Skill Evolution for Training-Free Adaptation of Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06137)
*Yi He,Zhengkang Guan,Anpeng Wu,Peng Cui,Fei Wu,Kun Kuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SkillTFM introduces a training-free framework that adapts tabular foundation models via a gated pool of reusable skills guided by boundary evidence, improving AUC under distribution shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address distribution shifts, heterogeneous feature semantics, and task-specific patterns in tabular data without costly fine-tuning or extra labeled data.

Method: Develop a verifiable, extensible skill bank that pairs boundary evidence identification with gated skill evolution; retrieve and extend reusable skills with explicit validation; evaluate across simulated boundary settings and real-world electricity-price forecasting; demonstrate compatibility across TFMs backbones.

Result: AUC improvements in the range of 0.128–0.142; nonlinear-boundary AUC rises from 0.699 to 0.898; effectiveness and generality shown across different TFM backbones.

Conclusion: SkillTFM enables training-free adaptation of tabular foundation models to new distributions and tasks via skill evolution, indicating practical benefits and broad applicability, though dependent on the quality of the skill bank and boundary signals.

Abstract: Tabular data are ubiquitous in real-world applications and are crucial for data-driven prediction and decision-making across science, industry, finance, healthcare, and public services. Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose tabular learning, offering reusable predictors across diverse datasets and substantially reducing the need for task-specific training, tuning, and model development. However, their practical deployment remains constrained by distribution shifts, heterogeneous feature semantics, and task-specific patterns that are difficult to capture without costly fine-tuning or additional labeled data.
  To this end, we propose SkillTFM, a training-free system that shifts TFM adaptation from parameter updates to the gated evolution of agentic skills. The core of SkillTFM is a verifiable and extensible skill bank that couples boundary evidence identification with gated skill evolution: the former characterizes task structure and base-model failure patterns, whereas the latter retrieves and extends reusable skills subject to explicit validation. Across simulated boundary settings and real-world electricity-price forecasting, SkillTFM improves AUC by 0.128--0.142, raises nonlinear-boundary AUC from 0.699 to 0.898. Furthermore, experiments across TFM backbones demonstrate the effectiveness and generality of SkillTFM.

</details>


### [186] [Threshold-Based Early Stopping of Accumulations in Neural Networks with Binary Activation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06177)
*Quentin Luquet de Saint-Germain,Massil Ait Abdeslam,Jean Pierre David*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-training early-stopping for binary networks reduces arithmetic by predicting final sign early; minimal retraining, achieves substantial term pruning with modest accuracy loss on VGG11/CIFAR-10.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce compute and energy in binary neural networks by avoiding unnecessary accumulations since the final sign can be predicted early in running sums.

Method: Characterize running accumulations on training data; use that to predict final sign early; assume an idealized ordering of weights; no parameter retraining; count operations saved; apply to VGG11 on CIFAR-10; report two scenarios (one block vs three deepest convolutions).

Result: Up to 86.6% of deepest-convolution accumulation terms removed with 0.37-point accuracy drop; 25% full-network arithmetic reduction with 1.36-point drop when applying to three deepest convolutions together.

Conclusion: Post-training, non-retraining early-stopping can substantially reduce arithmetic in binary networks with modest accuracy cost; applicability depends on weight ordering and architecture; potential for deployment but needs evaluation on broader datasets and robustness.

Abstract: Binary neural networks are very attractive for constrained deployment, enabling small footprint and low-power inference. For binary activations, the dot products become sign-controlled additions or subtractions, but the number of operations is unchanged. Indeed, every neuron or output channel still accumulates all of its input, even though only the sign will be retained, which is often wasteful. As the accumulation progresses, the running partial sum frequently drifts so far from zero that its final sign becomes highly predictable long before the last term is reached; every contribution evaluated after that point changes the value of the sum but not the final output activation. This paper turns this observation into a post-training early-stopping mechanism. We characterize the behavior of the running accumulations on the training dataset and use this information to predict the final sign as soon as possible. No model parameter is retrained. We count the number of operations under an idealized ordering of weights. On VGG11 applied to the CIFAR-10 dataset, the method removes $86.6\%$ of the accumulation terms of the deepest convolution for a $0.37$-point accuracy drop, and $25\%$ of the full-network arithmetic when used on the three deepest convolutions simultaneously, for a $1.36$-point drop.

</details>


### [187] [SAGA: Score-Weighted Adaptive Generation Alignment for Low-Resource Nordic Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06179)
*Hoda Fakharzadehjahromy,Emil Wiman,Andreas Bueff,Hafsteinn Einarsson,Fredrik Heintz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A parser-guided preference optimization approach (SAGA) replaces human labels with dependency-parser supervision to improve grammatical quality in low-resource languages, using delta-DPO with a composite reward and reward-gap filtering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable effective preference-based fine-tuning for language models in morphologically rich, low-resource languages where human preference annotations are scarce, by leveraging available high-quality dependency parsers.

Method: SAGA converts parser judgments into preference pairs for delta-DPO, combines parser quality with lexical diversity in a composite reward, filters low-information pairs via a reward-gap criterion, and monitors for reward hacking to preserve reliable supervision.

Result: Across Danish, Icelandic, and Norwegian Bokmål with GPT-SW3-1.3B, SAGA improves grammatical quality without human labels: Danish parse success 69.0%→93.8%; Icelandic +4.5 pp on Stanza eval (three-run mean +3.3 pp) with 80% native-speaker preference in pairwise comparisons; Norwegian Bokmål +28 pp.

Conclusion: Parser-derived supervision is a practical alternative to human preference annotation for grammatical alignment in low-resource languages where high-quality dependency parsers are available.

Abstract: Preference optimisation has proven effective for improving large language models but typically relies on costly human preference annotations. Extending these methods to morphologically rich, low-resource languages remains challenging because such annotations are scarce. We present SAGA (Score-weighted Adaptive Generation Alignment), a parser-guided preference optimisation framework that replaces human labels with dependency-parser supervision. SAGA converts parser judgements into preference pairs for delta-DPO, combines parser quality with lexical diversity in a composite reward, filters low-information pairs using a reward-gap criterion, and monitors reward hacking to maintain reliable supervision. Across Danish, Icelandic, and Norwegian Bokmål using GPT-SW3-1.3B, SAGA consistently improves grammatical quality without requiring human preference labels. Danish parse success increases from 69.0% to 93.8%, Icelandic achieves a +4.5 percentage-point improvement on an independent Stanza evaluation (three-run mean +3.3 percentage points) while native speakers prefer SAGA outputs in 80% of pairwise comparisons, and Norwegian Bokmål improves by +28 percentage points. These results demonstrate that parser-derived supervision is a practical alternative to human preference annotation for grammatical alignment in low-resource languages where high-quality dependency parsers are available.

</details>


### [188] [Continual Learning in Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06216)
*Zhiyan Hou,Dan Zhang,Tao Feng,Liyuan Wang,Wei Li,Xiangzhao Hao,Hongyan An,Junfeng Fang,Haokai Ma,Zhaohui Xu,Haiyun Guo,Jinqiao Wang,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a tri-axial framework (When, How, Where) to analyze continual learning, shifting from parameter-centric designs to system-level adaptation; offers systematic survey across axes and discusses challenges and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fill the gap between traditional parameter-centric CL and emerging system-level approaches (on-policy, test-time training, external memory/structures) and provide a unified perspective to understand the ongoing evolution.

Method: Proposes a tri-axial analysis—How (off-policy, on-policy, beyond-gradient), When (pre-training, post-training, inference-time), Where (internal parameters vs external structures)—and conducts a systematic survey of representative methods classified along these axes to trace the transition in continual learning.

Result: Clarifies the landscape by mapping methods into the tri-axial framework, identifies the ongoing transition toward system-level adaptation, and highlights key challenges, broader implications, and future directions.

Conclusion: There is a paradigm shift in continual learning from purely parameter-space updates to system-level adaptation, and the tri-axial framework provides a structured lens to organize current work and guide future research.

Abstract: Classical continual learning (CL) has primarily focused on enabling models to update and retain knowledge through parameter-centric mechanisms, e.g., training strategies, architectural designs, and weight adaptation. However, emerging paradigms are reshaping the scope of CL beyond this traditional model adaptation view. For instance, on-policy learning broadens the space of update mechanisms; test-time training extends CL from the training phase to inference; and external harness components such as memory, skill libraries, and interaction protocols extend the evolutionary boundaries of model capabilities far beyond the static parameter space. Collectively, these developments indicate a transition from parameter-centric learning toward system-level adaptation. To characterize this transition, we examine the evolution of continual learning through three dimensions: When, How, and Where learning occurs. The How dimension encompasses off-policy, on-policy, and beyond-gradient optimization mechanics. The When dimension captures evolution across pre-training, post-training, and inference-time stages. The Where dimension delineates updates occurring within internal parameters versus external structural constraints. Anchored by this tri-axial framework, we systematically survey representative methods, trace the ongoing transition of continual learning, and discuss the key challenges, broader implications, and future directions arising from this paradigm shift.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [189] [VLAff: Vision-Language-Affordance Model for Unified Actionable Affordances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05215)
*Jihoon Oh,Kento Kawaharazuka,Kei Okada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes EgoAffordance and VLAff to learn embodiment-agnostic object-centric affordances from egocentric videos, enabling zero-shot and affordance-guided robot manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodiment mismatch between humans and robots makes direct imitation hard; need scalable, embodiment-agnostic representations of how to interact with objects.

Method: Construct EgoAffordance dataset from egocentric videos using 3D SfM and hand mesh to extract visual, grasp, and trajectory affordances; develop VLAff, a vision-language foundation model that maps visual and textual instructions to affordance heatmaps, grasps, and trajectories; convert to executable actions using 3D scene info.

Result: VLAff achieves state-of-the-art visual affordance prediction and demonstrates zero-shot manipulation and affordance-guided robot learning in real robots.

Conclusion: A scalable, cross-modal framework for learning actionable affordances can bridge human demonstrations and robot execution, with a large dataset and foundation model enabling generalization across tasks and embodiments.

Abstract: Learning manipulation skills from human videos is promising for scalable robot learning. However, the embodiment mismatch between humans and robots makes this challenging. One promising solution is to learn object-centric actionable affordances that are embodiment-agnostic. In this work, we propose a framework that leverages egocentric human videos with state-of-the-art 3D Structure-from-Motion and hand mesh reconstruction to extract actionable affordances such as visual, grasp, and trajectory affordances that explicitly encode where to interact, how to grasp, and how to move. We construct EgoAffordance, a large-scale dataset comprising 204K episodes with 5.6M visual affordances and 11.6M grasp and trajectory affordances. Building on this, we introduce VLAff, a large vision-language model-based unified foundation model that learns cross-modal correlations across all actionable affordances. Given a visual observation and instruction, VLAff generates visual affordance heatmaps, grasp poses, and trajectories, which are then converted into directly executable actions by utilizing 3D scene information. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that VLAff not only achieves state-of-the-art performance on visual affordance prediction, but can also be effectively applied to real robot applications such as zero-shot manipulation and affordance-guided robot learning.

</details>


### [190] [A System for Train Condition Monitoring and Structural Health Assessment of Rail Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05221)
*Maximilian Posner,Martin Dazer,Daniela Lauer,Robert Winkler-Höhn,Mathilde Laporte,Tobias Herrmann,Martin Köppel*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Real-time vehicle condition monitoring for rail vehicles by fusing structural sensors with AI to detect impacts, drive-overs, and damage for condition-based maintenance and design optimization, enabling safer operation and progression toward GoA4 on mainline rail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GoA4 deployment on mainline rail is limited due to stringent safety requirements and the difficulty of reliably detecting impacts and driving-over events with camera/radar/lidar perception alone. Continuous monitoring and long-term analytics are needed to enable predictive maintenance and inform vehicle design for safer, more automated operation.

Method: Integrates structural sensor data with AI-based analysis to (1) automatically detect impacts, damage, and driving-over events, (2) enable condition-based maintenance via continuous monitoring, and (3) perform long-term data analytics to support design optimization and transition toward automated operation.

Result: Demonstrates feasibility of the proposed multimodal framework and indicates potential improvements in operational safety, predictive maintenance, and support for GoA4 transition in mainline rail.

Conclusion: The approach offers a path to safer, more reliable mainline rail systems by combining structural sensing with AI analytics, enabling proactive maintenance and data-driven design improvements.

Abstract: The ongoing digitalization of rail systems and the increasing use of artificial intelligence (AI) are fundamentally transforming the design, operation, and maintenance of rail vehicles. While fully automated operation at Grade of Automation 4 (GoA4) is well established in metro systems, its deployment in mainline rail remains limited. This is primarily due to stringent safety requirements and the complexity of open operational environments. Current perception systems based on cameras, radar, and lidar are effective in detecting objects but provide limited capability for reliably identifying impacts, collisions, and driving-over events. This paper presents a novel approach for real-time vehicle condition monitoring and impact detection that integrates structural sensor technologies with AI-based data analysis. The proposed framework addresses three key applications: (1) automated detection of impacts, structural damage, and driving-over events, (2) condition-based maintenance enabled by continuous monitoring, and (3) long-term data analytics to support vehicle design optimization. The results demonstrate the feasibility of the proposed approach and highlight its potential to enhance operational safety, enable predictive maintenance strategies, and support the transition toward fully automated operation in mainline rail systems

</details>


### [191] [Failing Gracefully: Mitigating Impact of Inevitable Robot Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05313)
*Duc M. Nguyen,Saad A. Ghani,Andrew Marshall,Allison Andreyev,Gregory J. Stein,Xuesu Xiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a probabilistic safety formulation for failure impact in household service robots and a MuJoCo-based simulator (FailBench) to study failure modes, enabling safer and more robust planning and policies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Failures in service robots are inevitable in household environments; mitigating their consequences is essential for safe and reliable deployment, requiring quantitative risk assessment across entities and outcomes.

Method: Develop a safety formulation that jointly evaluates the probability and severity of failure-induced interactions, and introduce FailBench (MuJoCo-based) to simulate robot–environment interactions under diverse failure modes (e.g., sensing issues, actuator malfunctions) for evaluation and policy testing.

Result: Introduces a risk-aware safety framework and the FailBench simulation platform, providing a foundation for evaluating and improving motion plans and learned policies under realistic failure conditions.

Conclusion: The approach enables informed planning that balances safety with task efficiency and offers a systematic tool for developing safer, more robust policies in real-world household settings.

Abstract: Service robots operate in household environments shared with humans, pets, and everyday objects, where they are highly susceptible to failures such as software crashes, hardware degradation, or unpredictable interactions. While roboticists strive to minimize failures, some remain inevitable, making it critical to mitigate their potential consequences for safe and reliable deployment. This paper introduces a novel safety formulation that evaluates both the probability of impactful interactions between robots and surrounding entities during failures, and the severity of their outcomes. By quantifying the impact of failures on different entities, our approach enables robots to make informed planning decisions that balance safety with task efficiency. To support systematic evaluation, we also present FailBench, a MuJoCo-based simulation framework for studying robot-environment interactions under diverse failure modes, including sensing issues and actuator malfunctions. Together, our safety formulation and FailBench provide a foundation for developing safer and more robust motion plans and learned policies in real-world household environments.

</details>


### [192] [Unified Planning-Learning Framework for Robust UUV Navigation Under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05365)
*Md Ether Deowan,Eleni Kelasidi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A unified autonomy pipeline for UUVs that merges onboard-vision-based occupancy mapping, clearance-aware global planning, and a risk-aware RL-based local controller, augmented with latent-state learning and BT-based distillation for safety under partial observability; validated in high-fidelity Isaac Sim with multi-seed reproducibility and outperforming BT-only and standard RL baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust autonomous navigation for UUVs in dynamic, partially observable underwater environments where traditional planners struggle with uncertainty, dynamic obstacles, and limited sensing.

Method: 1) Build occupancy maps from onboard sonar and depth images. 2) Use a clearance-constrained global planner for long-horizon structure. 3) Integrate an RL policy for short-range tracking and reactive avoidance. 4) Learn a compact latent state representation from sensors to capture environment, obstacle dynamics, and uncertainty. 5) Apply behavior-tree distillation with staged supervision to enhance safety and training stability. 6) Use an uncertainty-calibrated distillation to reweight teacher guidance by online latent-model uncertainty, emphasizing uncertain regimes; keep TTC and clearance cues explicit in planning and local policy features. 7) Evaluate in high-fidelity GPU simulation (NVIDIA Isaac Sim) with a reproducible multi-seed protocol; compare against BT-only and standard RL baselines.

Result: The framework yields improved robustness and safety under dynamic conditions, demonstrating the feasibility of a unified planning-learning architecture for UUV autonomy under partial observability and providing a reproducible methodology for robust performance.

Conclusion: A general, reproducible pipeline that combines hybrid planning and learning to enhance safety and robustness of UUV operation in dynamic underwater environments; latent-state encoding and calibrated distillation contribute to stability and performance under partial observability.

Abstract: This paper presents an observation-only autonomy framework for Unmanned Underwater Vehicles (UUVs) navigation in dynamic underwater environments that integrates persistent occupancy mapping, global clearance-aware planning, and risk-aware local control. The proposed pipeline constructs occupancy maps solely from onboard sonar and depth image observations, adapts a clearance-constrained global planner (GP) to provide long-horizon structure, and integrates a reinforcement learning (RL) policy to handle short-range tracking and reactive avoidance. To further support decision-making under partial observability, the system learns a compact latent state representation from onboard sensor data, encoding environmental structure, obstacle dynamics, and uncertainty. Behavior tree (BT) distillation with staged supervision is introduced to improve safety and training stability, while an uncertainty-calibrated distillation mechanism reweights teacher guidance using online latent-model uncertainty, emphasizing uncertain regimes during learning, with time-to-collision (TTC) and clearance cues remaining explicit in planning and local policy features. To demonstrate the efficacy of the framework, a reproducible multi-seed evaluation protocol is established in high-fidelity GPU-accelerated simulation using NVIDIA Isaac Sim, and performance is benchmarked against BT-only and standard RL baselines. The results obtained demonstrate improved robustness and safety under dynamic conditions, thus providing a general pipeline with a unified hybrid planning learning architecture and a reproducible methodology for robust UUV autonomy under partial observability.

</details>


### [193] [World-to-Wrist: Task-Conditioned Future Wrist Modeling for Fine-Grained Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05369)
*Yuhao Pan,Haosong Peng,Zhengshen Zhang,Zhengyang Yan,Yalun Dai,Fushuo Huo,Chujie Wang,Tianyu Qi,Xiucheng Wang,Nan Cheng,Wenchao Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel World-to-Wrist VLA (W2-VLA) model couples main-view and wrist-view data with task-conditioned future wrist modeling, using a latent interface to predict future wrist latents and improve fine-grained manipulation; it is complemented by W2-CoT structured annotations for auxiliary supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitation of treating main-view and wrist-view as parallel inputs in VLA models and enable task-conditioned anticipation of wrist-local dynamics for fine-grained manipulation.

Method: Introduce W2-VLA that uses latent modeling tokens as an interface between a vision-language model and a wrist predictor. The predictor, conditioned on the interface and wrist history, forecasts future wrist latents which inform action prediction. Propose W2-CoT to synthesize structured annotations describing manipulation progress, transition cues, and wrist-local evidence to supervise the interface.

Result: Empirical evaluation on LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world tasks shows improved fine-grained and contact-sensitive manipulation in single-arm and bimanual setups, with action-generation rates above 80 Hz.

Conclusion: Task-conditioned future wrist modeling via a compact latent interface and auxiliary W2-CoT supervision enhances fine-grained, contact-sensitive manipulation and sustains high-speed action generation in VLA systems.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models often treat main-view and wrist-view observations as parallel visual inputs, overlooking their distinct roles in robot manipulation. Fine-grained manipulation, however, benefits from anticipating how wrist-local interactions may evolve under the global task context. To address this limitation, we present World-to-Wrist VLA (W2-VLA), a VLA model for fine-grained robot manipulation with task-conditioned future wrist modeling. Given current multi-view observations and a task instruction, W2-VLA contextualizes a set of latent modeling tokens as a compact interface between the vision-language model and the wrist predictor. Conditioned on this interface and the observed wrist history, the predictor forecasts future wrist latents, which are transformed into future-aware context for action prediction. In addition, we introduce W2-CoT, a synthesis pipeline that produces structured annotations describing manipulation progress, physical transition cues, and wrist-local evidence. These annotations provide auxiliary supervision that shapes the task-conditioned latent interface. Experiments on LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate improved fine-grained and contact-sensitive manipulation across both single-arm and bimanual settings, while maintaining action-generation rates above 80 Hz.

</details>


### [194] [Sliding Sensors: Configurable Confidence in State Estimation for Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05410)
*Ella Walsh,Spencer Teetaert,Eric Diller,Timothy D. Barfoot,Jessica Burgner-Kahrs*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Mechanically reconfigurable sensing (slidable sensor) to shape estimation uncertainty in continuum robots; sliding sensor along the robot improves task-relevant confidence and reduces full-body shape estimation errors versus a fixed-tip sensor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uncertain environments yield spatially non-uniform estimation uncertainty; global accuracy is not always necessary; need adequate confidence at task-relevant locations along the continuum robot.

Method: Introduce concept hardware enabling longitudinal translation of a sensor inside the continuum robot; experimental demonstration of uncertainty shaping by varying sensor location; compare sliding sensor vs fixed tip sensor.

Result: State estimation confidence can be reconfigured by sensor location; sliding the sensor over time reduces full-body shape estimation errors compared to a single fixed tip sensor.

Conclusion: Uncertainty shaping via mechanical sensor reconfiguration is feasible for continuum robots and can improve state estimation performance at task-relevant locations; potential to tailor sensing to application needs.

Abstract: Continuum robots often operate in uncertain environments, where accurate state estimation is essential for safe interactions. Estimate uncertainty is inherently spatially non-uniform: confidence varies depending on where measurements are available. Global estimation accuracy is not always the top priority, but rather achieving sufficient confidence at task-relevant locations along the robot. This extended abstract introduces mechanically reconfigurable sensing enabling uncertainty-shaping in state estimation for continuum robots. We present a concept hardware design demonstrating the feasibility of longitudinal translation of a sensor within a continuum robot. We demonstrate that state estimation confidence can be reconfigured by varying the sensor location, and show a reduction of full-body shape estimation errors when sliding the sensor back and forth over time, compared to a single fixed tip sensor.

</details>


### [195] [ARGUS: Aligning Robot Scene Geometry Under Shifting Views with Large 3D Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05579)
*Rishik Sathua,Haonan Chen,Katherine Driggs-Campbell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ARGUS is an observation pre-processing pipeline that aligns image observations from arbitrary viewpoints to a canonical viewpoint using large-scale 3D vision models, improving visuomotor learning, especially with viewpoint-diverse data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To remove the coupling between scene geometry and camera viewpoint that hampers learning from diverse viewpoints, thereby improving data efficiency and generalization of visuomotor policies.

Method: ARGUS pre-processes observations by leveraging large-scale 3D vision models to transform images into a canonical viewpoint before feeding them to the downstream visuomotor policy, enabling consistent performance across varied camera configurations.

Result: ARGUS consistently outperforms prior methods in both limited-view and view-diverse training regimes, and achieves 4–6× faster convergence on view-diverse data by simplifying the observation space and enabling efficient learning from diverse datasets.

Conclusion: Using large-scale 3D vision models for observation alignment reduces the learning burden on visuomotor policies and enables more efficient learning from large-scale, viewpoint-diverse robot datasets.

Abstract: Large-scale visuomotor policies have demonstrated impressive performance across a wide range of robot manipulation tasks. However, despite this success, manipulation polices often entangle scene geometry with the corresponding viewpoint, learning where objects lie in an image rather than where it lies in the task space. This entanglement inherently limits the corresponding policy's ability to learn from viewpoint-diverse datasets (ex. DROID, BridgeV2) and generalize beyond the viewpoints captured in their training data. In this work, we present ARGUS, an observation pre-processing pipeline that uses large-scale 3D vision models to align image observations from arbitrary camera viewpoints into a canonical viewpoint before passing it to downstream visuomotor policies. Experiments across training datasets with varying levels of viewpoint diversity, from fixed multi-view camera configurations to highly varied camera placements, show that our method consistently outperforms prior approaches across both limited-view and view-diverse training regimes. In efficiency comparisons, ARGUS demonstrates an ability to learn from view-diverse data, converging to high success rates 4-6x faster than previous methods by leveraging a simplified observation space. Overall, our findings show that leveraging large-scale 3D vision models reduces the learning burden on visuomotor policies, enabling more efficient learning from large-scale, viewpoint-diverse robot datasets.

</details>


### [196] [PathCover: A Fast Convex Decomposition along a Path via Randomized Iterative Space Partitioning (RISP) on Point Clouds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05586)
*Kunal S. Narkhede,Abhijeet M. Kulkarni,Guoquan Huang,Ioannis Poulakakis*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PathCover is a real-time corridor generator for autonomous navigation that builds sequences of convex polytopes directly from raw LiDAR data using RISP, a randomized linear-time algorithm with probabilistic elimination. It provides obstacle-free regions for MPC/trajectory optimization, with finite termination guarantees and continuous progress along reference paths; benchmarks show order-of-magnitude speedups with similar corridor volumes, validated in simulation and real-world deployments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time obstacle-free region generation from sensor data is a bottleneck for trajectory planning. Existing corridor generators struggle to meet sensor-rate constraints, limiting safe and efficient navigation.

Method: RISP is a randomized algorithm that constructs convex polytopes directly from point-cloud data. It outputs overlapping obstacle-free polytopes that constrain downstream MPC and trajectory optimization. The algorithm has a mild probabilistic elimination condition, runs in expected linear time, guarantees finite termination, and ensures progress along any obstacle-free reference path.

Result: The approach achieves an order-of-magnitude speedup over state-of-the-art methods while maintaining comparable corridor volumes. It is validated on synthetic and real LiDAR datasets, demonstrated in high-fidelity quadrotor simulations, and deployed on a quadrupedal robot navigating constrained environments with live LiDAR perception.

Conclusion: PathCover delivers real-time, reliable corridor generation for planning pipelines, enabling efficient MPC and trajectory optimization in cluttered environments. The method shows robust performance across simulations and real-world experiments and is suitable for perception-to-action loops in robotic navigation.

Abstract: Autonomous robot navigation requires the rapid generation of obstacle-free regions for trajectory planning. However, existing corridor generators struggle to meet real-time, sensor-rate computational constraints. To resolve this bottleneck, we introduce PathCover, a framework driven by RISP; a novel randomized algorithm that constructs convex polytopes directly from raw point cloud data in expected linear time under a mild probabilistic elimination condition. PathCover generates sequences of overlapping, obstacle-free polytopes that safely constrain downstream MPC and trajectory optimization. We mathematically guarantee that the algorithm terminates in finite steps while ensuring continuous progress along any obstacle-free reference path. Extensive benchmarks on synthetic and real-world LiDAR datasets demonstrate an order-of-magnitude speedup over state-of-the-art methods while maintaining comparable corridor volumes.
