The complete pipeline is validated via high-fidelity quadrotor simulations and physical deployment on a quadrupedal robot navigating constrained environments using live LiDAR perception.

</details>


### [197] [Search-Aided Joint Agent-Environment Reinforcement Learning for Robust Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding with Rotations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05588)
*He Jiang,Jingtian Yan,Yulun Zhang,Yimin Tang,Tanishq Duhan,Rishi Veerapaneni,Guillaume Sartoretti,Jiaoyang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SJRL combines a single-step search-based collision resolver (Causal PIBT) with a unified reinforcement learning framework where an environment policy learns graph edge costs to guide agents via backward Dijkstra, improving LMAPF performance under real-world constraints and high density.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world LMAPF must handle robust safety constraints and in-place rotation, which are often oversimplified in learning-based planners. The LMAPF-R2 model increases coordination difficulty in constrained spaces, necessitating methods that integrate local collision resolution with global path guidance and adapt to real-world hardware.

Method: 1) Augment neural policies with Causal PIBT to resolve collisions and propagate agents’ intentions in a single step. 2) Propose a unified RL formulation optimizing both agent and environment policies, where the environment policy learns edge costs to provide global guidance, implemented via backward Dijkstra search. 3) Evaluate on high-density maps and in a mixed-reality warehouse with 8 physical and 248 virtual robots.

Result: SJRL significantly outperforms the strong search-based planner Causal-PIBT across multiple high-density maps and demonstrates feasibility in a mixed-reality warehouse with real robots.

Conclusion: Integrating a search-based collision resolver with a learnable environment-guided cost model yields robust coordination for LMAPF-R2, improving performance in dense, constraint-heavy settings and validating applicability to real-world multi-robot systems.

Abstract: Lifelong Multi-Agent Path Finding (LMAPF) requires repeatedly planning collision-free paths for agents that continuously receive new goals upon reaching their current ones. While many learning-based planners have been proposed for LMAPF, most rely on oversimplified kinematic assumptions that may overlook motion constraints critical to real-world performance. In this work, we study a more realistic LMAPF model derived from many real-world automated warehouse systems, termed LMAPF-R2, which incorporates robust safety constraints and in-place rotation constraints. These constraints substantially increase coordination difficulty, particularly in highly constrained spaces. To address these challenges, we propose Search-Aided Joint Reinforcement Learning (SJRL). We first augment neural policies with Causal PIBT, a single-step search-based planner that resolves agents' collisions and propagates their intentions. We then introduce a unified RL formulation that jointly optimizes agent and environment policies, where the environment policy learns graph edge costs to provide global movement guidance via backward Dijkstra search. Experiments demonstrate that SJRL achieves significant improvements over the strong search-based planner, Causal-PIBT, across multiple high-density maps. We further validate SJRL in a challenging mixed-reality warehouse environment with 8 physical robots and 248 virtual robots.

</details>


### [198] [KILVO: Kinematic-Inertial-LiDAR-Visual Odometry with Robust Multimodal Adaptation for Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05647)
*Jixin Gao,Fucheng Liu,Teng Zhang,Fusheng Zha*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KILVO is a kinematic-inertial-LiDAR-visual odometry framework for humanoid robots using an asynchronous-sequential hybrid ESKF, integrating joint encoders, IMU, LiDAR, and camera. It uses inertial prediction, high-rate leg kinematics, LiDAR-based priors, and photometric vision updates, with multimodal adaptation and a compact contact estimation module. It demonstrates robustness and competitive accuracy across multiple humanoid platforms; code released on GitHub.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots require reliable, real-time odometry that leverages multimodal sensors and proprioception, while handling sensor dropouts and asynchronous data streams. Existing fusion methods may underutilize leg kinematics and fail gracefully under sensor degradation.

Method: Asynchronous-sequential hybrid error-state iterated Kalman filter (ESIKF) where inertial data predict state; leg kinematics provide proprioceptive constraints at high rate; LiDAR points provide geometric priors for exteroception; visual update via photometric errors; multimodal adaptation for resilience; compact contact estimation module sharing information with state estimation; no extra sensors beyond common humanoid suite.

Result: On public datasets and real-world experiments across multiple humanoid robots and gait patterns, KILVO achieves competitive accuracy, efficiency, and output rate, and shows robust performance under sensor degradation/failures; claims superiority over state-of-the-art fusion methods; code and data released on GitHub.

Conclusion: KILVO offers a robust, efficient, and flexible fusion framework for humanoid odometry by tightly integrating proprioceptive, exteroceptive, and visual information in an asynchronous ESKF with explicit contact estimation; suitable for real-world humanoid robotics and resilient to sensor faults.

Abstract: This article presents a kinematic-inertial-LiDAR-visual odometry for humanoid robots, called KILVO. Tailored to the platform features, requirements, and real-world complexity, it fully utilizes the sensors commonly equipped on humanoid robots, including joint encoders, IMU, LiDAR, and camera, within an asynchronous-sequential hybrid error-state iterated Kalman filter (ESIKF). Specifically, inertial data are used for prediction, leg kinematics are processed asynchronously at a high rate and provide proprioceptive constraints, while exteroception is updated sequentially, first by registering LiDAR points for geometric priors and then by updating the visual component via photometric errors. Moreover, the framework is elaborately designed with multimodal adaptation for resilience to sensor failures. A compact contact estimation module is also developed, sharing information with state estimation without additional sensors. Extensive experiments on public datasets and in the real world across multiple humanoid robots, gait patterns, and scenarios demonstrate that KILVO achieves highly competitive accuracy, efficiency, and output rates, with strong robustness against sensor degradation and failures, making it more suitable for humanoid robots than state-of-the-art fusion methods. Our code and datasets are released on GitHub.

</details>


### [199] [JoyAI-RA 0.5: Scaling Robot Manipulation Learning via Dual Action Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05674)
*JoyAI-RA Team*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A generalist manipulation framework (JoyAI-RA 0.5) learns from heterogeneous data via dual action alignment and world-dynamics priors, enabling scalable transfer from human egocentric video, simulation, and robots; strong AgiBot results with increasing human data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robot manipulation data is scarce and comes from heterogeneous sources with different supervision and embodiment, causing negative transfer if naively pooled. A unified action representation and dynamics priors are needed to align these sources and scale learning.

Method: A Vision-Language-World-Action (VLWA) framework with dual action alignment: implicit latent-action inference from visual transitions and explicit groundings in a canonical action space (camera-frame, end-effector actions). Uses world-dynamics priors, an inner-outer-loop reinforcement learning stage for efficient task adaptation and foundation-policy improvement, enabling learning across human, simulation, and robot data.

Result: On real-world AgiBot, JoyAI-RA shows strong performance on seen tasks and unseen variations; task scores improve with the volume of human egocentric pretraining data and show no plateau at the largest scale.

Conclusion: Abundant but weakly labeled human video can be transformed into a transferable training signal, making human experience a primary axis for scaling manipulation capabilities across heterogeneous data sources.

Abstract: Robot data is scarce, so generalist policies need to learn from heterogeneous sources, including human egocentric video, simulation, and real robots, which differ in supervision and embodiment, with action labels missing or mutually incompatible. Human egocentric data scale best but sit farthest from robot data, and naive pooling causes negative transfer rather than knowledge sharing. We propose JoyAI-RA 0.5, a generalist Vision-Language-World-Action (VLWA) framework that couples physical world-dynamics priors with visual semantics and scales manipulation learning across such data via dual action alignment. Implicit action alignment infers latent actions from visual transitions, enabling action-free human, simulation, and robot data to guide a latent-action-conditioned world model in learning physical dynamics. Explicit alignment grounds reliable human and robot trajectories in a unified physical action space through a canonical action representation and camera-frame chunk-relative end-effector actions. An inner-outer-loop reinforcement stage then pairs efficient task adaptation with foundation-policy improvement. On a real-world AgiBot benchmark, JoyAI-RA performs strongly on both seen tasks and unseen variations. The task score improves consistently as the volume of human egocentric pretraining data increases and shows no sign of plateauing at our largest scale. This suggests that abundant but weakly labeled human experience can be converted into a transferable training signal, making human video not merely a weak auxiliary source but a primary axis along which manipulation capability can be scaled. Project page can be found at https://joyai-ra-05.github.io/.

</details>


### [200] [Nonvisual Classification of Ground-Condition by Artificial Proprioception in an Amoeba-Inspired Autonomous Walking Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05684)
*Hyoto Yamaguchi,Zenji Yatabe,Seiya Kasai*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Nonvisual ground-condition classification for an amoeba-inspired quadruped using multimodal proprioception (accelerometer, foot-pressure sensors) and reservoir computing to distinguish flat vs rough terrain and enable adaptive gait.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve reliable terrain awareness without image sensing, enabling robust locomotion on diverse grounds by exploiting proprioceptive signals and RC-based pattern classification.

Method: Integrates a 3-axis accelerometer, eight foot pressure sensors, and reservoir computing. Handles large sensor fluctuations due to motion, performs terrain classification (flat vs rough), enables on-site gait switching, and analyzes sensor contributions.

Result: High classification accuracy under dynamic walking; successful on-site gait adaptation; insights into individual sensor contributions to ground-condition discrimination.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effectiveness of multimodal proprioception with RC for nonvisual terrain recognition and adaptive locomotion in legged robots; highlights sensor roles and potential improvements.

Abstract: Nonvisual classification of ground condition based on a multimodal sensing approach was investigated for an amoeba-inspired autonomous walking robot. To classify ground condition without image sensing and processing, we implemented artificial proprioception by integrating a three-axis accelerometer, eight foot pressure sensors, and reservoir computing (RC). Even when large fluctuations in the sensor outputs are caused by dynamic motions of a four-legged robot in walking, our system can classify the ground condition, flat or rough, with high accuracy. We demonstrate on-site switching of walking gait depending on ground condition in the robot. We also discuss the contribution of each sensor to ground condition classification.

</details>


### [201] [Hijacking Robots with a Piece of Paper: A Systematic Study of Physical Prompt Injection in VLM-Controlled Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05715)
*S. M . Bhagya P. Samarakoon,M. A. Viraj J. Muthugala,W. K. R. Sachinthana,Mohan Rajesh Elara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Systematic study of physical prompt injection attacks on vision-language model (VLM) controlled robotic sorting. Introduces a four-category taxonomy (indirect signage, task redefinition, authority impersonation, conflict injection), benchmarks 20 prompts across 3 scene layouts and 3 command formulations. Demonstrates non-trivial attack success across GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5 Flash, and Qwen3-VL-32B; analyzes reasoning traces and defenses. Shows simple mitigations substantially reduce risk with trade-offs to reading-in-scene labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As VLMs increasingly act as planners in robotics, they become vulnerable to environmental adversarial prompts embedded in the visual field. A systematic taxonomy, a realistic physical-benchmark, and practical defenses are needed to understand and mitigate prompt-injection risks.

Method: Develop a four-category taxonomy of adversarial prompts (indirect signage, task redefinition, authority impersonation, conflict injection). Build a benchmark of 20 attack prompts evaluated over three physical scene layouts and three command formulations (varying destination specificity and rule explicitness). Conduct 5,670 trials on three frontier VLMs (GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Qwen3-VL-32B). Analyze success rates, transferability of attacks (notably authority impersonation and negation), and reasoning traces. Evaluate three defenses: prompt-based defense (model-dependent effectiveness 75–100%), two-stage verification (85–100%), and pre-processing text masking (100%).

Result: Attacks succeed at 27.0%, 29.4%, and 5.0% across the three models. Authority-impersonating and negation attacks transfer across all models. Successful compromises are nearly always acknowledged by the model (≈99.9%). Defenses reduce risk: prompt-based defense (75–100% effective, model-dependent), two-stage verification (85–100%), masking (100%). Defenses preserve task capabilities in the benchmark but may hinder tasks requiring reading in-scene labels.

Conclusion: VLM-controlled manipulation via physical signage is a meaningful vulnerability. Simple defenses substantially reduce risk, but defense choice entails trade-offs, particularly for tasks requiring in-scene text reading; overall, defenses maintain general capabilities in the benchmark.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly deployed as planners in robotic systems, where they translate natural-language commands into executable actions grounded in visual scene understanding. This tight coupling between perception and instruction-following introduces a new attack surface: adversarial text placed within the robot's visual field can act as an indirect prompt injection into the VLM's reasoning stack. We present a systematic study of physical prompt injection attacks against VLM-controlled sorting, introducing a four-category taxonomy, indirect signage, task redefinition, authority impersonation, and conflict injection, instantiated as a benchmark of 20 attack prompts evaluated across three physical scene layouts and three command formulations that vary in destination specificity and rule explicitness. Across 5,670 trials on three frontier VLMs (GPT-4o, Gemini 2.5 Flash, Qwen3-VL-32B), attacks succeed at 27.0%, 29.4%, and 5.0% respectively, with authority-impersonating and negation attacks transferring across all three models. Analysis of reasoning traces reveals that successful compromise is nearly always conscious (99.9% acknowledgment rate), and that models defend through structurally different mechanisms, explicit rejection for Gemini, perceptual inattention for GPT-4o. We evaluate three simple mitigations: prompt-based defense (75-100% effective, model-dependent), two-stage verification (85-100%), and pre-processing text masking (100%). Our findings show that VLM-controlled manipulation is meaningfully vulnerable to human-readable physical signage, and that simple defenses substantially reduce risk, though defense choice involves trade-offs. The defenses preserve general task capabilities in our benchmark, but they may impair tasks that require reading in-scene labels.

</details>


### [202] [ATP: Anatomical Torque with Passivity-based Control Framework for Safe Upper-Limb Exoskeleton Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05723)
*Yu Chen,Gong Chen,Xiang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ATP provides safe, passive upper-limb exoskeleton torque assistance using a RL-trained musculoskeletal controller, online torque refinement, and a passivity-preserving energy tank, demonstrated in simulations, real-world tests, and EMG studies with up to 48% muscle activity reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend anatomical torque-based assistance from lower limbs to complex nonperiodic upper-limb movements; ensure safety, generalization across tasks, and provable passivity.

Method: 1) scalable musculoskeletal simulation trains unified reinforcement-learning muscle controller to generate anatomical reference torques; 2) online torque-refinement adapts references, suppresses tendon spikes, adds anomaly score for safety; 3) cable-driven compliant exoskeleton with interaction torque control; energy tank preserves passivity with guarantees on torque tracking.

Result: Accurate torque tracking for long-duration sequences; generalization to real-time movements; resumption after energy-tank replenishment; EMG study (n=5) shows up to 48% reduction in target-muscle activity vs gravity compensation and open-loop.

Conclusion: ATP enables safe, generalizable, torque-based upper-limb exoskeleton assistance with provable passivity; demonstrates practical viability and user-muscle reduction benefits.

Abstract: Providing assistance across diverse movements is a central objective of exoskeletons, and anatomical knowledge can enable responsive support that generalizes across tasks. However, anatomical assistance has mainly been studied for lower-limb exoskeletons, where periodic, weight-bearing motions impose lower demands on torque precision. Extending such assistance to complex, nonperiodic upper-limb movements remains challenging. This paper proposes Anatomical Torque with Passivity-Based Control (ATP) for safe upper-limb exoskeleton assistance. First, a scalable musculoskeletal simulation framework trains a unified reinforcement-learning muscle controller that generalizes across upper-limb movements and generates anatomical reference torques without complex biomechanical computations. Second, an online torque-refinement scheme adapts the reference to diverse movements, suppresses tendon-induced spikes, and incorporates a learned anomaly score for safe and comfortable assistance. Third, an interaction torque controller delivers assistance through a cable-driven compliant exoskeleton without constraining motion to predefined trajectories, while an energy tank preserves passivity with theoretical guarantees on torque tracking and system passivity. Simulations and real-world experiments show accurate tracking of long-duration motion sequences and generalization to real-time human movements. The controller achieves accurate torque tracking while preserving passivity and resumes tracking after energy-tank replenishment. An EMG study with five participants further shows reduced target-muscle activity during static and dynamic tasks compared with gravity compensation and open-loop assistance, with reductions of up to 48% relative to movement without the exoskeleton in a dynamic multi-joint task.

</details>


### [203] [Near-sensor Computing for Rapid Visuotactile Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05725)
*Zhengying Zhu,Ruilin Zhang,Runze Hu,Chenxi Xiao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Near-sensor, streaming spectral Poisson solver enables deterministic, low-latency tactile reconstruction for visuotactile sensors, reducing power and data-transfer delays while maintaining accuracy and enabling fast reflex responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Host-based processing of dense surface-gradient data for tactile sensing incurs higher power, bandwidth, and scheduling latency, limiting reaction speed. A near-sensor pipeline aims to reduce latency and energy while preserving accuracy.

Method: Deploy a fully streaming hardware pipeline implementing a spectral Poisson solver as the core; operate at 166 MHz with ~347 mW power, eliminating data-dependent branching and iterative convergence to achieve deterministic latency. Produce first depth value for 128x128 frame after 35,107 input pixels.

Result: Fixed latency of 0.211 ms to first depth value. Depth reconstruction errors of 0.17% of peak contact depth across 15 contact geometries. Reflex loop closes in 28.3 ± 4.9 ms (near-sensor) vs 169.9 ± 27.8 ms (host-based) using same actuator.

Conclusion: Near-sensor reconstruction provides accurate, energy-efficient, and deterministic tactile geometry with latency suitable for rapid robotic contact responses.

Abstract: Visuotactile sensors reconstruct dense contact geometry from measured surface gradients, but host-based processing increases power consumption and introduces data-transfer delays and variable scheduling latency, limiting the sensing and response speed of robotic systems. To address these limitations, we implement a near-sensor computing framework that includes a spectral Poisson solver as a fully streaming hardware pipeline. The computational core logic has an estimated power consumption of 347 mW and achieves high throughput without data-dependent branching or iterative convergence, thereby providing deterministic latency. Operating at 166 MHz, the pipeline produces the first depth value of each 128x128 frame 35,107 cycles after receiving the first input pixel, corresponding to a fixed latency of 0.211 ms. Across 15 contact geometries, the reconstructed depths differ from a double-precision reference by 0.17 % of the peak contact depth. On-chip decisions based on these reconstructions close a robot protective reflex loop in 28.3 +/- 4.9 ms, compared with 169.9 +/- 27.8 ms for an equivalent host-based loop using the same actuator. These results demonstrate that near-sensor reconstruction can provide accurate, energy-efficient, and deterministic tactile geometry on timescales suitable for rapid robotic contact responses.

</details>


### [204] [In-Context VLA: Endowing Vision-Language-Action Models with Language via In-Context Post-Training and Agentic Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05738)
*Jiarui Yang,Wen Huang,Jiale Zhang,Maowei Hu,Hang Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CoT-based reasoning hurts low-level robot control; proposed \ourmethod{} uses grounded language via in-context post-training and agentic tool-use to achieve SOTA results across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap between language-enabled reasoning and grounded control in Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. Free-form textual CoT is ungrounded, incurs latency, and creates conflicting optimization objectives that favor narration over action.

Method: Propose \ourmethod{} with (i) in-context post-training: inject perceptual evidence as structured context and train only on actions; (ii) an agentic tool-use interface: policy queries open-vocabulary detectors, monocular depth, and a vision–language model to actively acquire task-relevant information. A data engine generates diverse, paraphrased, evidence-conditioned spatial descriptions to train the policy to interpret language beyond verbatim prompts.

Result: Analytical and empirical evidence show that free-form CoT degrades low-level control due to ungrounded reasoning, timing latency, and conflicting objectives. In contrast, \ourmethod{} yields state-of-the-art performance and efficiency compared with CoT-based approaches under matched configurations across RoboCasa-GR1, SimplerEnv, LIBERO, and 8 real-world robot manipulation tasks.

Conclusion: Grounded language consumption, not generation, is essential for effective VLA manipulation. By combining in-context grounding and intentional tool-use, \ourmethod{} enables robust generalization, improved efficiency, and strong empirical results across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have become the dominant recipe for generalist manipulation, yet they are almost universally trained by behavior cloning: a policy imitates expert action chunks conditioned on a static image and a fixed instruction. A natural remedy is to inject explicit reasoning through textual chain-of-thought (CoT). We show, both empirically and analytically, that free-form textual CoT degrades low-level control: the reasoning it produces is ungrounded, its latency breaks closed-loop timing, and, crucially, the reasoning and action tokens are optimized against conflicting objectives so that the policy learns to narrate rather than to act. We argue that what a VLA needs is not the ability to generate language, but the ability to consume grounded language. To this end we introduce \textbf{\ourmethod{}}, a framework that endows a VLA with language competence through (i) in-context post-training, in which perceptual evidence is injected as structured context and the model is supervised only on actions, and (ii) an agentic tool-use interface, in which the policy queries open-vocabulary detectors, monocular depth, and a vision--language model to actively acquire task-relevant information. Rather than emitting a single templated caption, our data engine produces diverse, paraphrased, and evidence-conditioned spatial descriptions, so that the policy learns to interpret language it has never seen verbatim. Across the RoboCasa-GR1, SimplerEnv, and LIBERO simulation benchmarks, together with 8 real-world robot manipulation tasks, our method consistently achieves SOTA results in both performance and efficiency when compared with CoT-based approaches under matched configurations.

</details>


### [205] [Acoustic-driven millimetric helical robot: ultrasonic synergistic manipulation in confined fluidic environment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05746)
*Hanlin Wang,Xin Wang,Xinwei Wei,Jiaxu Liu,Le Wang,Shengze Cai,Chao Xu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A coordinated multi-acoustic-field strategy using acoustic radiation forces and acoustic streaming enables millimeter-scale helical robots to locomote under confinement, validated by simulations and experiments including in vitro vessel tests, showing versatile planar, inclined, and vertical motion with improved maneuverability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend acoustic micromanipulation from microscale to millimeter-scale robots and operate effectively in confined biological environments, addressing propulsion efficiency and controllability gaps.

Method: Employ synergistic acoustic fields combining radiation forces and streaming; perform multiphysics simulations of millimeter-scale helices under combined fields; experimental validation of locomotion (planar, inclined, vertical); semi-autonomous navigation; in vitro tests in porcine venous vessels to assess confinement performance.

Result: Demonstrated locomotion across multiple modes (planar navigation, inclined climbing, vertical motion); ultrasonic synergy substantially improves maneuverability; confirmed unidirectional and reciprocating motion under biologically relevant confinement.

Conclusion: The work provides mechanistic insight into scaling acoustic micromanipulation to the millimeter regime and supports biomedical applications requiring versatile and controllable robotic mobility under confinement.

Abstract: Acoustic field-driven manipulation provides a non-contact and non-invasive strategy for controlling microscale and nanoscale objects, yet its extension to millimeter-scale robots was limited by insufficient propulsion efficiency in confined biological environments. Here, a coordinated multi-acoustic-field approach is introduced, which harnesses the synergistic action of acoustic radiation forces and acoustic streaming flows to enable controlled locomotion of millimeter-scale helical robots and enhance propulsion. Multiphysics simulations captured the dynamics of millimeter-scale helical robots under combined acoustic fields, and experimental validation demonstrated their locomotion capabilities, including planar navigation, inclined climbing, and vertical motion. Semi-autonomous navigation experiments further confirmed that ultrasonic synergy substantially improved maneuverability. In vitro tests in porcine venous vessels demonstrated that coordinated acoustic fields supported both unidirectional and reciprocating motion under biologically relevant confinement. These findings provide mechanistic insight into scaling acoustic micromanipulation to the millimetre regime and support biomedical applications requiring versatile and controllable robotic mobility.

</details>


### [206] [XEWorld: Can Action-Conditioned World Models Generalize to Unseen Robot Embodiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05799)
*Yixiang Chen,Jiabing Yang,Yuan Xu,Qisen Ma,Keji He,Peiyan Li,Kai Wang,Ziheng He,Xiangnan Wu,Jing Liu,Nianfeng Liu,Yan Huang,Liang Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: XEWorld reveals that current action-conditioned world models fail to generalize to unseen robot embodiments, acting mainly as 2D visual pattern matchers. Zero-shot rendering requires heavily grounded cues, and even with adaptation models forget seen embodiments. Architecture must decouple visual appearance from physical dynamics to achieve true cross-embodiment generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether learned simulators truly encode physical dynamics or simply memorize visuals, and to assess cross-embodiment generalization by evaluating held-out robots in identical scenes.

Method: Introduce XEWorld, a controlled cross-embodiment testbed that holds out robots within physically identical scenes to isolate embodiment. Systematically analyze zero-shot and few-shot settings, focusing on translating numeric joint actions into visual trajectories and predicting dynamic changes from static observations, while assessing forgetting after adaptation.

Result: Identified a shared architectural bottleneck: models primarily perform 2D visual pattern matching with generalization governed by visual similarity rather than physical kinematic similarity. They struggle to translate actions into coherent visuals and cannot predict dynamics from static inputs. Zero-shot rendering demands pixel-space actions and precise spatial-temporal alignment; few-shot adaptation triggers catastrophic forgetting. These failures show models cannot transfer learned dynamics to novel appearances.

Conclusion: To achieve true cross-embodiment generalization, architectures must decouple visual appearance from underlying physical dynamics and incorporate mechanisms that reason about kinematics and dynamics independently from rendering, enabling robust generalization to unseen embodiments.

Abstract: Action-conditioned world models are promising learned simulators for robotic manipulation, yet evaluating them exclusively on training robots fails to reveal whether they capture physical dynamics or merely memorize visual patterns. To answer whether a model can faithfully render a robot it has never seen, we introduce XEWorld, a controlled cross-embodiment testbed for world models that isolates embodiments by evaluating held-out robots within physically identical scenes. Our systematic analysis uncovers a shared architectural bottleneck: current models act primarily as 2D visual pattern matchers whose generalization is governed by visual similarity rather than physical kinematic similarity. Driven by this limitation, they struggle to translate abstract numeric joint actions into coherent visual trajectories, and fail to predict dynamic visual changes from static initial observations. Consequently, successfully rendering an unseen embodiment zero-shot strictly requires heavily grounded cues, specifically pixel-space actions and explicit spatial-temporal alignment. Even when bypassing this zero-shot barrier via few-shot adaptation, the forced appearance recovery triggers catastrophic forgetting of seen embodiments. Together, these failures expose a critical inability to apply learned physical dynamics to novel visual appearances, highlighting that achieving true cross-embodiment generalization requires architectural innovations that decouple visual appearance from underlying physical dynamics.

</details>


### [207] [Coordinated Multi-Robot Disassembly for Makespan Optimization of Large-Scale Assemblies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05830)
*Niklas Hargus,Andreas Orthey,Marc Toussaint*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CoMuDi is a coordinated planning framework for multi-robot disassembly in confined workspaces, integrating space-time RRT* (ST-RRT*) to minimize makespan and improve success, evaluated on up to 49 pieces with 9 robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disassembly in constrained environments requires efficient, collision-free, time-optimized plans that coordinate multiple robots under task dependencies; existing approaches struggle with tight spaces and synchronization.

Method: Formulate compound tasks for pick/place/exit from robots, assemblies, and a dependency graph; propagate temporal constraints to maximize parallelism while avoiding collisions; integrate ST-RRT* into CoMuDi to minimize arrival times of tasks and overall makespan; compare ST-RRT* with RRT* under varying time bounds; evaluate on six assemblies with up to 49 pieces and 9 robots.

Result: CoMuDi with ST-RRT* yields higher success rates and shorter makespans than RRT*-based variants; demonstrates low idle times and scalability to large assemblies; validates effectiveness across multiple assemblies and robot counts.

Conclusion: CoMuDi effectively coordinates multi-robot disassembly in constrained spaces, leveraging ST-RRT* to enhance planning efficiency, reduce idle time, and scale to large mission sizes.

Abstract: Multi-robot task and motion planning for disassembly tasks requires robots to operate in confined workspaces while coordinating their motions with other robots. To tackle this problem, we propose a planning method called coordinated multi-robot disassembly (CoMuDi). CoMuDi coordinates a team of robots for disassembly tasks. The input is a team of robots, an assembly of objects, and a dependency graph. Based on this information, we create compound tasks for pick, place, and exit motions. By propagating temporal constraints, we ensure that each robot can start and end their tasks as early as possible while avoiding collisions with nearby robots. By integrating the space-time RRT* planner (ST-RRT*) into CoMuDi, we ensure that individual tasks minimize arrival time and thereby help us minimize overall makespan. We compare the performance of CoMuDi using both ST-RRT* and RRT* planners with varying time bounds, demonstrating that the combination of CoMuDi and ST-RRT* leads to a higher success rate while minimizing makespan. Finally, we evaluate CoMuDi on six assemblies with up to 49 pieces and up to 9 robots. In those scenarios, we show that CoMuDi returns robot paths that exhibit low idle times, thereby demonstrating that CoMuDi can reliably solve large-scale assemblies.

</details>


### [208] [SkillMemo: Expert-guided Skill Memory Framework for Compositional Embodied Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05970)
*Changyuan Wang,Chubin Zhang,Zhenyu Wu,Runhao Li,Angyuan Ma,Ke Chao,Yinan Liang,Xiuwei Xu,Ziwei Wang,Yansong Tang,Jiwen Lu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SkillMemo introduces an expert-guided, Mixture-of-Experts–based trajectory segmentation to decompose long-horizon demonstrations into latent skills and stores them in a dynamic episodic memory for retrieval and fusion, improving DP and Vision-Language-Action backbones on manipulation tasks with strong OOD compositional generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome data scarcity in embodied trajectory datasets and enhance compositional generalization by explicitly modeling reusable skill structures through latent primitives and a memory bank.

Method: 1) Expert-guided trajectory segmentation via a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) module that partitions trajectories into skill primitives encoded as gating coefficients. 2) A skill-level episodic memory that stores compact key-value representations of primitives. 3) During inference, retrieve relevant skills and fuse them with the model’s gating distribution to provide contextual priors for action prediction.

Result: Empirical results on simulation and real-world manipulation tasks show SkillMemo consistently improves Diffusion Policy (DP) and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) backbones, achieves state-of-the-art performance, outperforms the π_{0.5} baseline, and demonstrates strong compositional generalization to unseen task configurations.

Conclusion: SkillMemo effectively leverages latent skill decomposition and a memory-augmented retrieval mechanism to enhance long-horizon robotic manipulation, enabling better performance and generalization for DP and VLA systems.

Abstract: Embodied visuomotor models, including Diffusion Policy (DP) and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, have demonstrated promising performance on robotic manipulation benchmarks. However, their potential remains fundamentally constrained by the scarcity of large-scale embodied trajectory datasets, leading to insufficient compositional generalization in out-of-distribution (OOD) scenarios with limited capability to capture reusable skill structures. To address this limitation, we propose Skill-Based Memory (SkillMemo) framework that implicitly decomposes long-horizon demonstrations into latent atomic skills and integrates skill-level features into a dynamic episodic memory bank for solving compositional tasks. Specifically, we first introduce an expert-guided trajectory segmentation module built upon a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture, which implicitly partitions trajectories into distinct skill primitives represented by learned gating coefficients. We further design a skill-level episodic memory architecture that stores compact skill representations as retrievable key-value pairs. During inference, the memory bank retrieves the most relevant skill primitives which are subsequently fused with the model's current gating distribution, providing a robust contextual prior to refine action predictions. Extensive experiments on the simulation benchmark and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that SkillMemo consistently enhances both DP and VLA backbones, achieving state-of-the-art performance and outperforming $π_{0.5}$, while exhibiting strong compositional generalization to unseen task configurations.

</details>


### [209] [TRACE: Learned Proprioceptive Odometry for Legged Robots under Unreliable Contact Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05975)
*Taehyeon Kong,Woojin Kim,Jemin Hwangbo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TRACE is an end-to-end proprioceptive odometry estimator for legged robots that uses a history of onboard IMU and joint data with a foot-aware cross-attention mechanism to robustly fuse information under unreliable contact; trained with physics-inspired losses and policy-randomized sim-to-real training, achieving reduced position drift across terrains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust odometry for legged robots under unreliable contact conditions and significant sim-to-real transfer challenges, where standard filtering, hybrid, or purely learning-based methods underperform.

Method: An end-to-end estimator that predicts relative displacement, relative rotation, and body-frame velocity from recent IMU and joint histories. A foot-aware cross-attention module adaptively weights IMU and leg-like tokens without manual contact or slip thresholds. Two physics-inspired auxiliary losses enforce kinematic consistency and effective use of leg information. Training includes policy randomization to improve sim-to-real transfer, followed by partial real-world fine-tuning of the temporal encoder and prediction head.

Result: Experiments on indoor and outdoor terrains show consistent reductions in position drift relative to classical filtering-based, hybrid, and purely learning-based baselines. Ablation studies validate the contributions of the training objectives, policy randomization, and real-world fine-tuning, particularly under unreliable contacts and sim-to-real mismatch.

Conclusion: TRACE provides robust proprioceptive odometry for legged robots in unreliable contact scenarios, achieving better drift performance and improved sim-to-real transfer through adaptive attention weighting and physics-inspired training objectives.

Abstract: In this paper, we present TRACE (Tokenized Robust Attention for Contact-Aware Estimation), an end-to-end learned proprioceptive odometry estimator for legged robots under unreliable contact conditions. The proposed estimator directly predicts relative displacement, relative rotation, and body-frame velocity from a recent history of onboard inertial and joint measurements. To improve robustness under unreliable contact conditions, we introduce a foot-aware cross-attention module that adaptively weights IMU and leg-wise kinematic tokens without relying on manually defined contact or slip thresholds. The estimator is trained with direct supervision and two physics-inspired auxiliary losses that promote kinematic consistency and reliable use of leg information. To reduce policy-specific overfitting and consequently improve sim-to-real transfer, simulation training incorporates policy randomization, followed by partial real-world fine-tuning of the temporal encoder and prediction head. Experiments across diverse indoor and outdoor terrains demonstrate consistent reductions in position drift compared with classical filtering-based, hybrid, and purely learning-based baselines. Ablation studies further validate the contributions of the proposed training objectives, policy randomization, and real-world fine-tuning, particularly under unreliable contacts and sim-to-real mismatch.

</details>


### [210] [Beyond Flat Policies: Hierarchical Post-Training for Embodied Agents in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.05999)
*He Kong,Zengjue Chen,Qi Wang,Qianli Xing,Runliang Niu,Peidong Liu,Jiawei Li,Shiqi Wang,Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HiRoC introduces a hierarchical post-training framework that separates high-level planning from low-level action execution in vision-language-action robotic manipulation, incorporating subgoal alignment to improve long-horizon performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current post-training VLA models are treated as flat policies, which struggles with explicit task progression and robust, long-horizon manipulation. Hierarchical approaches often rely on offline data and lack online improvement through interaction, hindering real-world deployment.

Method: HiRoC comprises a high-level planner that decomposes tasks into executable subgoals and a low-level executor trained with reinforcement learning to produce subgoal-conditioned actions. An alignment step between planner-generated subgoals and the executor mitigates distribution shift before RL, enabling effective collaboration between planning and execution.

Result: Empirical experiments across diverse robotic manipulation benchmarks show that HiRoC consistently outperforms strong baselines. Ablation and analysis demonstrate the value of hierarchical post-training and the contribution of each key component.

Conclusion: Hierarchical post-training with explicit planning and subgoal-conditioned RL, plus subgoal alignment, improves long-horizon manipulation and overall performance, validating the proposed HiRoC framework.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in robotic manipulation by leveraging pretrained vision-language models. However, existing post-training methods predominantly optimize VLA models as flat policies, making it difficult to explicitly model task progression and perform robust long-horizon manipulation. Although hierarchical approaches introduce task decomposition, they mainly rely on supervised learning from offline demonstrations and cannot effectively improve execution through online interaction. To address this limitation, we propose Hierarchical Robotic Control (HiRoC), a hierarchical post-training framework that decouples high-level task planning from low-level action execution. The planner decomposes complex tasks into executable subgoals to provide explicit semantic guidance, while the executor continuously improves subgoal-conditioned action generation through reinforcement learning. To enable effective collaboration between the two modules, we further align the executor with planner-generated subgoals before reinforcement learning, mitigating the distribution misalignment between planning and execution. Extensive experiments across diverse robotic manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that HiRoC consistently outperforms strong baselines. Comprehensive analyses further validate the effectiveness of hierarchical post-training and the contribution of each key component.

</details>


### [211] [Adaptive-WAM: Quality-Guided Early-Exit Planning from Intermediate Video-Diffusion Features](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06008)
*Sining Ang,Yuguang Yang,Yan Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Adaptive-WAM reduces compute for video-based ego planning by using a multi-exit DiT backbone with trajectory heads and a quality scorer to stop early once a satisfactory trajectory is decoded, avoiding full iterative diffusion steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large video diffusion models provide rich priors for driving but incur high costs due to iterative video generation. The work asks how much video diffusion is needed to make reliable driving decisions and aims to reduce compute while maintaining planning performance.

Method: Introduce Adaptive-WAM built on Wan2.2-5B. Attach trajectory diffusion heads to selected DiT blocks and a lightweight trajectory-quality scorer to terminate inference when a trajectory meets a threshold; otherwise, continue from cached hidden states to a deeper exit. This yields a quality-aware, multi-exit planner that bypasses classifier-free denoising and VAE decoding.

Result: On NAVSIM, adaptive single-trajectory planner achieves 90.8 PDMS; fixed-exit variant achieves 92.6 PDMS with 64 proposals. NAVSIM v2: 89.9 EPDMS, best among front-view video world-model planners. Zero-shot transfer to nuScenes yields 0.88 m average L2 error and 0.08% collision. On A100, adaptive routing improves PDMS from 90.62 to 90.79 with ~170 ms end-to-end planning latency, ~10% faster than fixed block-15 and ~47% faster than fixed full-depth.

Conclusion: Adaptive-WAM demonstrates that high-quality driving trajectories can be extracted from intermediate layers of a video diffusion backbone, enabling a computation-efficient, quality-aware planning approach that transfers across domains without fine-tuning and achieves state-competitive planning performance with reduced latency.

Abstract: Large video diffusion models provide rich spatiotemporal priors for autonomous driving, but existing world-action models often inherit the cost of iterative future-video generation even though deployment only requires an ego trajectory. We ask a more basic question: how much of a video diffusion model must be executed to make a reliable driving decision? Through a controlled study of video denoising timesteps and Diffusion Transformer (DiT) depth, we find that planning performance is largely insensitive to the tested video-noise levels, whereas strong trajectories can already be decoded from intermediate layers. Based on this observation, we introduce Adaptive-WAM, a quality-aware multi-exit planner built on a Wan2.2-5B backbone. Trajectory diffusion heads are attached to selected DiT blocks, and a lightweight trajectory-quality scorer terminates inference once the best trajectory decoded so far satisfies a quality threshold; otherwise, computation continues from the cached hidden state to a deeper exit. The deployed planner therefore avoids the iterative classifier-free denoising loop and VAE decoding required for future-video synthesis, while dynamically allocating backbone depth according to trajectory quality. On NAVSIM, the adaptive single-trajectory planner achieves 90.8 PDMS; a separate fixed-exit variant reaches 92.6 PDMS with 64 proposals. It further obtains 89.9 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2, yielding the best reported results among the compared front-view video world-model planners. Without target-domain fine-tuning, Adaptive-WAM transfers to nuScenes with 0.88 m average L2 error and a 0.08\% collision rate. On an A100, adaptive routing improves PDMS from 90.62 to 90.79 while averaging 170 ms end-to-end planning latency, approximately 10\% below the 190 ms fixed block-15 planner and 47\% below the 320 ms fixed full-depth planner. Code will be released.

</details>


### [212] [Topometric Autonomous Vehicle Localization by Combining Visual Embeddings and Feed-Forward 3D Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06021)
*Eulogio Quemada-Torres,Alberto Jaenal,Francisco-Angel Moreno,Javier Gonzalez-Jimenez*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A topometric localization framework that fuses probabilistic VPR with FF3D-based metric poses via offline pose-appearance mapping and online particle filtering; achieves improved accuracy and robustness over appearance-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the limitations of compact, low-dimensional VPR maps (robust to appearance changes) by incorporating accurate metric information from FF3D, enabling scalable yet precise visual localization.

Method: An offline mapping tool models the topometric pose-appearance interaction across scene regions. An online particle filter uses odometry and a belief over places to guide FF3D inference, integrating neural metric estimates into probabilistic appearance-based localization. The system is modular, allowing interchangeable descriptor extractors and FF3D models, and sequential belief mitigates perceptual aliasing.

Result: Evaluations on three benchmarks show substantial improvements over existing appearance-based methods. The architecture’s modularity is validated, and analysis indicates that sequential belief reduces severe failures under perceptual aliasing.

Conclusion: The approach effectively combines appearance-based localization with neural metric estimation to form a scalable, robust topometric VL framework. Sequential belief enhances reliability under perceptual aliasing while maintaining modularity for components.

Abstract: Effective Visual Localization (VL) requires a map of the environment that combines compactness for efficient scalability with robustness against visual appearance changes and metric precision. Through low-dimensional image embeddings, Visual Place Recognition (VPR) is able to successfully meet the first two requirements, but its low metric accuracy makes it less suitable than standard VL approaches based on local features or neural representations. This limitation can be overcome by integrating VPR with the accurate local trajectory estimates produced by feed-forward neural 3D geometry (FF3D) models. In this paper, we address sequential appearance-based localization through a topometric framework that iteratively combines probabilistic VPR with FF3D metric pose estimation in controlled image sets. Our approach proposes an automatic offline mapping tool that models the topometric pose-appearance interaction in the different parts of the scene. This map is later employed by an online particle filter that estimates the pose from odometry and belief over places for FF3D inference, successfully incorporating neural metric estimation into probabilistic appearance-based localization. We extensively evaluate the framework on three known benchmarks, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing appearance-based methods. The modularity of our approach allows the descriptor extractor and FF3D model to remain interchangeable, and a focused analysis further shows that sequential belief can mitigate severe failures under perceptual aliasing.

</details>


### [213] [IcFuzz: Fuzzing Isaac Sim with Semantic Stage Guidance and Multi-level Mutation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06088)
*Zhixiang Chen,Zhuangbin Chen,Ruoxi Jia,Zeqin Liao,Wei Li,Jinyang Liu,Zibin Zheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IcFuzz is a domain-aware fuzzing framework for NVIDIA Isaac Sim that uses LLM-driven semantic stage segmentation, hierarchical mutation operators, and a bandit-based mutation scheduler to improve code coverage and bug detection, uncovering multiple crashes and bugs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Isaac Sim's GPU-accelerated, photorealistic simulation presents a large, context-rich state space with complex object semantics. Existing fuzzers struggle due to lacking context, hierarchical control, and scalability, risking unreliability in a widely used simulator.

Method: 1) LLM-based semantic stage segmentation to decompose simulation programs into structured stages capturing context-aware object semantics. 2) Multi-level mutation operators designed for hierarchical granularities to exercise the simulator across different levels. 3) Multi-armed bandit algorithm to adaptively schedule mutation operators and navigate the large state space.

Result: IcFuzz outperforms baselines in code coverage and bug detection. It reaches ~1.9–2.05× the code coverage of baselines and detects an average of 3.7 unique crashes over three rounds of 12-hour tests (baselines detect no crashes). It also uncovered 11 bugs over ~4 months, 9 of which were confirmed or fixed by developers.

Conclusion: IcFuzz effectively enables high-coverage, bug-rich fuzzing for Isaac Sim by leveraging semantic segmentation, hierarchical mutation, and adaptive scheduling, demonstrating potential applicability to other complex, state-rich simulators.

Abstract: Robotics simulators serve as a foundational infrastructure for embodied AI, facilitating safe and scalable robotic system development. NVIDIA Isaac Sim has emerged as one of the most popular simulators, distinguished by its GPU-accelerated physics engine and photorealistic rendering, which enable high-fidelity modeling of complex environments. However, its inherent complexity inevitably introduces software bugs that can compromise simulation reliability. Existing fuzzing approaches struggle to test Isaac Sim effectively due to challenges of context-aware object semantics, hierarchical simulation control, and a vast simulation state space.
  In this paper, we propose IcFuzz, the first fuzzing approach for Isaac Sim. IcFuzz first performs an LLM-based semantic stage segmentation, decomposing simulation programs into structured stages that capture context-aware object semantics. Guided by this information, IcFuzz designs multi-level mutation operators to systematically exercise the simulator across hierarchical granularities. To efficiently navigate the vast simulation state space, IcFuzz employs a multi-armed bandit algorithm to adaptively schedule mutation operators. Experimental results show that IcFuzz outperforms the baselines in terms of both code coverage and bug detection. Specifically, IcFuzz achieves approximately 190\%--205\% of the code coverage of the baselines and detects an average of 3.7 unique crashes over three rounds of 12-hour tests, while no crashes are detected by the baselines. Moreover, IcFuzz has uncovered 11 bugs over approximately four months, 9 of which have been confirmed or fixed by the developers.

</details>


### [214] [Visual Grounding in Zero-Shot Vision-Language Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06154)
*J. de Curtò,Dayani Plasencia,Diego Sánchez,I. de Zarzà*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VLMs fail to ground decisions to visual input in direct-control tasks; many are image-invariant; a symmetry-based guardian and modular replay can recover high accuracy, but overall VLMs act as bounded hazard assistants rather than universal zero-shot controllers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle whether VLMs rely on true visual grounding or simulator dynamics and priors by subjecting them to targeted ablations and cross-model tests across multiple simulators.

Method: An input-ablation battery (blind-image controls, repeated inputs, lane reflection, non-visual baselines, integrity checks) tested on 9 direct-action models, 6 local VLMs, and an exploratory VLM-MPC across 2 embodiments and 3 simulators; 32,874 scored calls; additional post-hoc symmetry-consensus guardian; offline modular replay; evaluation via balanced accuracy, MAE, symmetry equivariance, and leave-one-episode-out analyses.

Result: Direct-control performance largely negative: constant-SLOW beats scripted geometric controller; many models are image-invariant; hazard recognition does not translate LEFT/RIGHT under reflection; no local VLM achieves joint longitudinal and lateral grounding. An image-only deterministic perception yields MAE 0.090 m and exact mirror equivariance. A leakage-controlled guardian yields 0.954 balanced accuracy on held-out frames; nested cross-validation confirms the result; abstaining on ties improves to 0.973 at 0.824 coverage; offline replay achieves 0.934 action agreement and exact mirror equivariance.

Conclusion: Current VLMs function as bounded, selective hazard assistants rather than universal zero-shot controllers; modular safeguards and perception-enabled lateral authority can yield strong but limited grounding, suggesting a path toward hybrid systems leveraging perception with guardrails.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as zero-shot controllers, but successful trajectories do not necessarily show that decisions are grounded in visual input: simulator dynamics and conservative action priors can produce favourable scores without meaningful perception. We investigate this with an input-ablation battery: blind-image controls, repeated identical inputs, lane-axis reflection, non-visual baselines, and pipeline-integrity checks. Across nine direct-action models, six structured local VLMs, and an exploratory VLM-MPC hierarchy, we analyse 32,874 scored calls over two embodiments and three simulators. The direct-control results are largely negative: a constant-SLOW policy outperforms a scripted geometric controller, several models are image-invariant or nearly constant, and models that recognize longitudinal hazards still fail to transform LEFT and RIGHT under reflection. No local VLM meets the joint longitudinal and lateral grounding criteria. However, an image-only deterministic positive control estimates the lead gap with 0.090 m MAE and exact mirror equivariance, confirming the stimuli carry sufficient visual information; the failures are modular, not universal. A post-hoc, leakage-controlled symmetry-consensus guardian selects two models from 16 calibration frames and freezes a 2-of-4 hazard vote across original and reflected views. On 272 held-out frames it reaches 0.954 balanced accuracy (episode-cluster bootstrap 95% CI [0.895,0.990]); nested leave-one-episode-out recovers the same pair and threshold in all 12 folds. Abstaining on ties raises committed balanced accuracy to 0.973 at 0.824 coverage. With deterministic perception retaining lateral authority, offline modular replay achieves 0.934 action agreement and exact mirror equivariance. These results support current VLMs as bounded, selective hazard assistants, not monolithic zero-shot controllers.

</details>


### [215] [Prior-SG: Task and Prior Driven Region Segmentation for Scene Graphs in Arbitrarily-Structured Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06170)
*Giorgio Tonetti,Laurent Kneip,Abel Gawel,Marco Hutter*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Prior-SG frames 3D scene graph generation as probabilistic alignment using a Prior Graph and LLM-derived vocabulary to fuse RGB-D streams into a globally consistent Instance Graph via MAP with a Markov Random Field, achieving state-of-the-art semantic region segmentation and zero-shot ontological restructuring in open-plan environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D scene graph extraction relies on local clustering or strict geometric heuristics, which fail in open-plan or arbitrarily-structured environments. A task- and prior-driven approach is needed to achieve robust high-level spatial reasoning for autonomous mobile platforms.

Method: Continuously aggregate RGB-D streams into a physically grounded Instance Graph with multi-scale open-vocabulary feature fusion. Use a Prior Graph (topological priors) and dynamically synthesized task vocabulary via a Large Language Model. Perform MAP estimation by optimizing a Markov Random Field that fuses visual, geometric, and discrete object experts with the priors to resolve perceptual ambiguities.

Result: Validated on diverse simulated residential datasets and large open-plan real-world environments. The approach achieves state-of-the-art semantic region segmentation, robustly delineates distant functional boundaries without physical walls, and provides zero-shot ontological flexibility allowing complete restructuring of spatial partitions per task.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a principled integration of perception, geometry, priors, and language-model guidance to produce flexible, task-aware 3D scene graphs. This enables improved planning and interaction in complex environments but may raise considerations about computational efficiency, prior/LLM reliability, and scalability to real-time operation.

Abstract: Hierarchical 3D scene graphs are a promising representation for high-level spatial reasoning in autonomous mobile platforms. However, existing extraction frameworks typically rely on purely local visual clustering or strict geometric heuristics, such as wall-separated rooms, which fail in open-plan or arbitrarily-structured environments. We propose Prior-SG, a task- and prior-driven framework that casts scene graph generation fundamentally as a probabilistic alignment problem. As the robot explores, it continuously aggregates an incoming RGB-D sensor stream into a physically grounded Instance Graph utilizing a multi-scale, open-vocabulary feature fusion strategy. The system then infers the high-level functional semantics of this map through a Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) estimate, guided by a Prior Graph-a logical expectation of the environment's structure and task-relevant vocabulary synthesized dynamically by a Large Language Model. By optimizing a Markov Random Field that fuses heterogeneous experts (visual, geometric, and discrete objects) with these topological priors, the system resolves local perceptual ambiguities. We validate this approach across diverse simulated residential datasets and large, open-plan real-world environments. Prior-SG achieves state-of-the-art semantic region segmentation accuracy compared to recent baselines, robustly delineates distant functional boundaries in the absence of physical walls, and uniquely provides zero-shot ontological flexibility, enabling the robot to entirely restructure its spatial partitioning based on a given high-level task.

</details>


### [216] [ErgoSurf: Ergodic Control for the Coverage of Unknown Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06208)
*Stefan Schneyer,Timo Bachmann,Maged Iskandar,Korbinian Nottensteiner,Alin Albu-Schäffer,Freek Stulp,João Silvério*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Online ergodic control with GPIS-based tactile learning enables simultaneous surface coverage and geometry reconstruction on unknown surfaces via local tangent-plane approximations and heat-diffusion guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ergodic control methods typically require prior knowledge of surface geometry or vision input, limiting applicability to unknown or dynamic surfaces. This work aims to enable online, tactile-driven surface coverage with concurrent geometry learning.

Method: Integrate Gaussian Process Implicit Surfaces (GPIS) to learn surface geometry from tactile sensing during execution. Use local tangent-plane-based point clouds to approximate the surface and define the sampling domain for both target and coverage distributions. Apply a heat-diffusion analogy to compute smooth potential fields guiding ergodic exploration, with iterative GPIS fitting.

Result: Demonstrated in both simulation and real-robot experiments, achieving simultaneous ergodic coverage and online surface geometry learning with reconstruction errors approaching ground truth.

Conclusion: Extends ergodic control to unknown surfaces by enabling concurrent surface learning, enabling robust, adaptive contact-centric exploration under real-time conditions; potential for broader surface-centric manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Contact-centric tasks on surfaces, ranging from inspection and cleaning to sanding and polishing, require robots to systematically cover the surface while maintaining stable contact. Ergodic control generates trajectories that spend time at a location proportional to a desired, task-specific spatial distribution, enabling efficient information gathering and coverage. However, traditional ergodic control methods rely on prior knowledge of surface geometry or require a vision sensory input to scan the geometry beforehand, limiting their applicability in real-world scenarios with unknown or dynamic environments. This paper introduces a novel online ergodic control framework that achieves systematic surface coverage while simultaneously reconstructing unknown surface geometry. We employ a Gaussian Process Implicit Surface (GPIS) model that learns global surface geometry from intrinsic tactile sensing during execution. For efficient online planning, we approximate the surface locally using point clouds sampled from tangent planes at observed contact points and iteratively fit them to the Gaussian Process. This approximation simultaneously serves as the sampling domain for both the target and the coverage distributions. We employ a heat-diffusion analogy to compute potential fields that guide ergodic exploration, translating spatial coverage objectives into smooth robot trajectories. We demonstrate our framework through simulation and real-robot experiments, validating simultaneous ergodic coverage and online surface geometry learning with reconstruction errors approaching the ground truth.

</details>


### [217] [VIDP: Variable Impedance Diffusion Policy for Compliant Robot Manipulation from Diverse Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06210)
*Hisham Khalil,Neil Fernandes,Thomas M. Kwok,Hsiu-Chin Lin,Yue Hu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Imitation-learning-based variable impedance control VIDP uses TP-DAMM to extract physically consistent trajectory distributions from diverse demonstrations and map them to stiffness profiles, enabling pose actions with task-compliant impedance without force sensors; real-world tests outperform fixed-impedance baselines in success and force/tracking metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust contact-rich manipulation requires compliant behavior that adapts to changing contact constraints. Static compliance is insufficient, and conventional force-free trajectory data do not reveal implicit compliance. Prior methods infer compliance from motion variations that may reflect geometry rather than intentional impedance.

Method: Introduce Task-Parameterized Directionality-Aware Mixture Model (TP-DAMM) to model multi-task trajectories from demonstrations. VIDP maps these trajectory distributions to stiffness profiles and jointly predicts pose actions and variable impedance without relying on force sensors, enabling real-time adjustment of stiffness as tasks progress.

Result: Real-world experiments show VIDP achieves higher task success rates than fixed-impedance controllers, while reducing interaction forces compared to high-stiffness baselines and reducing tracking errors compared to low-stiffness baselines.

Conclusion: VIDP demonstrates that generative imitation-learning with task-parameterized, directionality-aware trajectory distributions can yield physically consistent variable impedance policies from demonstrations, improving robustness and safety in contact-rich manipulation without force sensing.

Abstract: Contact-rich manipulation requires precise tracking and mechanical compliance, where variable impedance control can improve robustness in task success, whereas static compliance cannot adapt to varying contact constraints. Variable impedance skills can be learned from demonstrations, avoiding complex modeling, but compliance is a hidden variable in force-agnostic kinematic data. While existing methods infer compliance from trajectory variations, these variations may reflect geometric adaptation and not intentional compliance when subject to changing spatial layouts. Therefore, this letter introduces Variable Impedance Diffusion Policy (VIDP), an imitation learning-based variable impedance control framework leveraging a Task-Parameterized Directionality-Aware Mixture Model (TP-DAMM) to extract physically consistent trajectory distributions from diverse demonstrations. By mapping distributions to stiffness profiles, VIDP jointly predicts pose actions and task compliance without force sensors. Real-world experiments show that VIDP significantly outperforms fixed-impedance baselines in task success rate while reducing interaction forces with respect to high stiffness controllers and tracking errors with respect to low stiffness baselines.

</details>


### [218] [Design and Evaluation of a Touchscreen-Based Teleoperation Interface for Robotic Manipulators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06219)
*Juan José García Cárdenas,Alperen Kenan,Hamidreza Raei,Paul Bremner,Manuel Giuliani,Arash Ajoudani,Adriana Tapus*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Touchscreen teleoperation maps finger movements to a Panda arm, outperforming a joystick in speed, accuracy, and cognitive load; autonomous mode further reduces workload.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve intuitive, precise surface-contact teleoperation in challenging environments (e.g., nuclear industry) where conventional controllers are inadequate.

Method: Design and test a touchscreen interface with continuous finger-to-robot mapping and integrated visualization. Conduct a within-subject user study (n=20) comparing touchscreen, joystick, and single-click autonomous control using a Franka Emika Panda. Collect kinematic, physiological, and behavioral data to assess task performance, cognitive load, and trust.

Result: Touchscreen yields faster task completion (median 2.50 min vs 5.38 min), higher path coverage (90.7% vs 84.1%), and lower overshoot vs joystick; NASA-TLX decreases from 52 to 43; autonomous mode achieves the lowest workload (31).

Conclusion: The touchscreen interface enables more natural, precise surface interaction with improved performance and lower cognitive load; autonomous mode offers further reduction in workload. The approach is simple to implement and potentially generalizable, but warrants validation across more tasks and settings and assessment of safety in nuclear-like environments.

Abstract: Intuitive teleoperation interfaces are crucial for the safe and effective operation of robotic manipulators in challenging environments. In the nuclear industry, surface contact tasks such as swab sampling require precise path and force tracking, obstacle avoidance, and sustained operator attention, which conventional joystick interfaces struggle to support effectively.
  This study designs and evaluates a novel touchscreen teleoperation interface that maps continuous finger movements directly to robotic manipulator motions, provides finer velocity control, and integrates control with visualization, enabling more natural, precise, and intuitive surface interaction than conventional controllers. A comparative user study with 20 participants evaluated task performance and workload using the proposed touchscreen, a conventional joystick, and a single-click autonomous mode. Tasks simulated realistic surface manipulation using a Franka Emika Panda arm, remotely controlled from another country. Kinematic, physiological, and behavioral data were recorded to comprehensively assess task performance, cognitive load, and operator trust across each control condition.
  Participants completed teleoperation tasks more efficiently and accurately with the touchscreen interface, achieving a 53.5% reduction in completion time (median: 2.50 vs. 5.38 min), higher in-area coverage on the sinusoidal path (90.7% vs. 84.1%), and lower overshoot on both path geometries compared with the joystick. Cognitive load, quantified via NASA-TLX (0-100), decreased from joystick to touchscreen (mean TLX 52 to 43; -9 points, -17.3%) and was lowest under the autonomous one-click mode (31; -21 points vs. joystick, -40.4%; -12 vs. touchscreen, -27.9%). This research presents an easy-to-implement touchscreen interface that improves performance in teleoperated surface tasks while reducing cognitive load.

</details>


### [219] [Robot Learning from Human Demonstrations: Handwritten Alphabet Trajectories and Human-Likeness Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06221)
*Alperen Kenan,Paul Bremner,Manuel Giuliani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A probabilistic learning-from-demonstration framework extends Gaussian Mixture Models/Regression to learn human-like robot handwriting motion by incorporating force and time, evaluated with user studies showing moderately high perceived human-likeness; dataset released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge gap between robot and human-like motion to enhance trust and collaboration; provide reproducible benchmark.

Method: Data collection of 3,142 handwriting demonstrations from 22 participants across 52 character-case combinations via touchscreen interface; metrics: planar position, force, timing; model: extended GMM/ GMR with force and normalized time, handling non-continuous multi-segment trajectories; evaluation: perceptual user study with 21 participants rating human-likeness on 0-100 scale.

Result: Average human-likeness score 71.50 (SD 22.56); majority perceived as more human-like; identified geometric positioning and trajectory sequence as main perceptual factors; positive attitudes towards human-like behavior; dataset released open-source.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of learning human-like robot motion with richer dynamics; provides reproducible benchmark; suggests design factors affecting perception; potential to improve HRI trust.

Abstract: Learning from demonstration (LfD) provides a developmental framework through which robots can develop motor skills by observing and imitating human dynamics, reducing reliance on explicit programming to teach a skill to a robot. The resulting human-like robot motion is recognised as a key factor in building trust and enabling natural collaboration in human-robot interaction. This paper presents a framework for learning human-like robot motion from demonstration, including data collection, probabilistic trajectory learning, and perceptual user evaluation. A dataset of 3,142 handwriting demonstrations was collected from 22 participants across all 52 Latin alphabet character-case combinations via a touchscreen teleoperation interface, capturing planar position, contact force, and timing. Building on the widely used Gaussian Mixture Model and Gaussian Mixture Regression approach for learning from demonstration, the framework is extended in this work by incorporating force and normalised time dimensions to enable richer representation of human dynamics, and adapting it to handle non-continuous, multi-segment trajectories, enabling generalisation across demonstrations. A user study with 21 participants evaluated the perceived human-likeness of the generated trajectories using a continuous scale anchored between robotic and human-like motion, normalised to 0-100 where 50 represents the neutral midpoint. The generated trajectories achieved an overall human-likeness score of 71.50 (SD=22.56), indicating that the majority of trajectories were perceived as more human-like. Participants identified geometric positioning and trajectory sequence as the most influential perceptual factors, and reported positive attitudes toward human-like robot behaviour. The datasets are released as open-source, providing a reproducible benchmark for developing and evaluating human-like robot motion methods.

</details>


### [220] [GeniWorld: A Generalizable Interactive World Model for Robotic Manipulation via Visual Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06332)
*Chenghao Gu,Hanyang Yu,Jingbo Zhang,Haitao Lin,Wenyao Zhang,Jinghe Wang,Hanglei Jin,Shuzhao Xie,Jingyan Jiang,Zhi Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GeniWorld is an interactive, action-conditioned video world model that decouples embodiment from environment dynamics, enabling robust zero-shot generalization and scalable policy evaluation and data-efficient improvement in complex, unseen environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalist robot policies struggle with robustness in unseen, complex real-world environments; action-conditioned world models often suffer from limited action controllability and poor OOD generalization; a scalable, robust world model is needed for evaluation and policy improvement.

Method: Build on pretrained video generative models; use URDF-based rendering to convert numerical actions into visual action representations; decouple embodiment kinematics from environmental dynamics; develop an autoregressive video prediction model with high-frequency robot kinematic control for closed-loop control; supports interaction with policies and human teleoperators.

Result: Trains on limited fixed-scene data yet achieves strong in-domain performance and robust zero-shot generalization to highly randomized unseen environments; provides a scalable policy evaluator robust to environmental perturbations; enables generation of diverse manipulation trajectories within the world model with limited real-world demonstrations, improving downstream policy performance and robustness.

Conclusion: GeniWorld offers a robust, scalable framework for evaluating and improving robot policies by grounding action representations in visual kinematics and decoupling embodiment from dynamics, yielding strong generalization and data-efficient policy enhancement in complex, unseen environments.
